# STUDIES IN SAIVA-SIDDHANTA
<term>Saiva-Siddhanta: A major school of South Indian Hindu philosophy and theology. The name translates to "the settled conclusion of Shaivism," focusing on the path to liberation through the grace of the god Shiva.</term>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of J. M. Nallasvami Pillai's "Studies in Saiva-Siddhanta." It contains a note from the publisher, J. N. Ramanathan, explaining the origin and purpose of this collection of essays.</meta>

## PUBLISHER'S NOTE.

---

The following essays originally appeared in the *Siddhānta Dīpikā* <note>original: Siddhānta Dīpikā; translated as "The Light of Truth," a monthly journal dedicated to religion, philosophy, and science</note>, the *Madras Review*, and the *New Reformer*. They represent my father’s contribution to the study of Siddhānta <note>The "established conclusion" or doctrine, specifically the Saiva-Siddhanta school of South Indian Hinduism.</note> during the last fourteen years, in addition to his translations of *Śivajñānabōdham* <note>original: Śivajñānabōdham; "Instruction in the Knowledge of Shiva," a foundational 13th-century Tamil scripture</note>, *Śivajñāna Siddhiyār* <note>original: Śivajñāna Siddhiyār; a comprehensive philosophical commentary clarifying the Śivajñānabōdham</note>, *Tiruvaruṭpayan* <note>original: Tiruvaruṭpayan; "The Fruit of Divine Grace"</note>, Tirumūlar’s *Tirumantiram* <note>original: Tirumantiram; "The Holy Incantation," a classic poetic work on Yoga and mysticism</note>, and other works. These essays embody his critical research and deep learning in the fields of Indian religion and philosophy.

As the earlier volumes of the *Siddhānta Dīpikā* are out of print, these essays are now published in a collected form for the first time at the pressing request of many readers of that journal. I hope to publish my father's other works as soon as possible. I trust that my father’s great labors in the fields of South Indian literature, philosophy, and religion will be fully appreciated through the steady sale of this edition.

MADRAS
1911

J. N. RAMANATHAN.

<summary>In this preface, the publisher J. N. Ramanathan explains that the book is a collection of his father's essays on Saiva-Siddhanta philosophy originally published in various journals over fourteen years. He highlights his father's role as a translator of key Tamil scriptures and expresses a desire to preserve these works for a wider audience.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction's discussion on the value of Tamil Shaiva literature and the specific spiritual insights found in the Agamas.</meta>

-># INTRODUCTION<-

...which hardly left the mature soul without the spiritual nourishment <note>original: "pabulum"</note> that was essential for its upward growth, development, and eventual spiritual freedom. The goal of this book is to reveal some of the veins of the purest <term>Agamic</term> <note>Agamic: relating to the Agamas, a vast collection of scriptures focused on philosophy, ritual, and yoga</note> gold through a pleasant instructional style and a varied presentation. These veins of wisdom, which the author mostly attributes to the Tamil sources of <term>Shaiva</term> <note>Shaiva: relating to the tradition that reveres the god Shiva as the Supreme Being</note> literature, actually belong to a system of layers that are older, more widespread, and more valuable in their composition and structure. 

The gold dug from these veins is of remarkable quality, whether in the form of raw ore, nuggets, or ingots. Readers will be richly rewarded for delving into this book, as each essay focuses on a central idea that is consistently developed and explained with grace and clear language. Consequently, readers can be sure to emerge from their reading clearly enlightened on many of the debated points of Hindu philosophy. These points are examined using the insights of the <term>Agamic</term> doctrines preserved for us in ancient and medieval Tamil. 

It is not easy to grasp the true spirit of the <term>Agamanta</term> <note>Agamanta: the philosophical conclusion or essence of the Agamic scriptures</note> if one is not familiar with its authentic traditions. Because of how these traditions are preserved and shared in India, they are not easily accessible to European scholars. A notable example of failing to understand the spirit of the Agamic teaching because of this lack of access is seen in the faulty interpretation given by the Reverend Dr. G. U. Pope regarding the central doctrine of Agamic mysticism, <term>Shakti-nipata</term> <note>Shakti-nipata: literally the "descent of power," referring to the descent of divine grace upon a soul</note>. The late Oxford professor of Tamil, as clever and skilled as he was in translating the *Kural*, the *Naladiyar*, and the *Tiruvāçagam*, was completely wrong when he explained <term>Shakti-nipata</term> as "the cessation of energy" in the introductory essay of his edition of the *Tiruvāçagam*. This explanation brings to mind a similar instance where a...

<summary>This page introduces the spiritual value of Agamic literature and critiques Western scholars, specifically G.U. Pope, for misinterpreting key theological concepts like the "descent of grace."</summary>
<keywords>Agamas, Shaivism, Tamil literature, Shakti-nipata, G.U. Pope, Hindu philosophy, spiritual growth, Agamanta</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction’s critique of Western academic interpretations of Indian philosophy, focusing on the need for deep traditional knowledge to avoid superficial translations.</meta>

-># INTRODUCTION<-

...interpretation. For example, a European Sanskrit scholar, perhaps unaware of the deeper implications of the phrase, translated the term ‘*Parama-hamsa*’ <term>Parama-hamsa</term> <note>original: "Parama-hamsa"; literally "Supreme Swan," a title for a high-ranking ascetic who has attained enlightenment</note> as "great goose." A strict academic purist might try to justify such childish <note>original: "puerile"</note> translations based on the origin of the words, but they are clearly failures—misinterpretations that reflect everything except the true meaning and soul of the original. One should never expect such lapses into unintentional ignorance in any of the essays in this collection. Our author is not only a diligent and tireless researcher of Tamil philosophical literature that clarifies the <term>Agamic</term> religion, but has also, in his search for Truth, freely used the expertise of local scholars <note>original: "indigenous savans"</note> who represent the pinnacle of Hindu traditional learning and spiritual heritage today.

It is a striking irony that, despite scattered attempts of questionable value, no serious effort has yet been made to provide the educated public with a connected overview <note>original: "conspectus"</note> of the full range of teachings found in the *Shaiva Agamas*. The Reverend Dr. G. U. Pope, the Reverend H. R. Hoisington, the Reverend T. Foulkes, and Dr. Karl Graul from an earlier generation—along with some more recent English clergymen such as the Reverend H. A. Popley, the Reverend G. E. Phillips, the Reverend W. Goudie, the Reverend A. C. Clayton, and a few others—have occasionally tried to explain the Tamil phase of this philosophy to the best of their abilities. However, they were unable to fully free themselves from their Christian biases and preconceived notions. The foundation of Agamic philosophy and mysticism must be explored through Sanskrit, and researchers for that purpose have, until now, been very rare. Even in the otherwise insightful work recently published in German by Dr. M. Winternitz on the *History of Indian Literature, Volume One* <note>original: "Geschichte der indischen Litteratur, Erster Band"</note>, the only mention made of the Agamas is in relation to the *Shakta-tantras* <term>Shakta-tantras</term> <note>Shakta-tantras: scriptures focused on the worship of the Divine Mother or Goddess (Shakti)</note>.

<summary>This page critiques superficial Western translations of Indian spiritual terms and highlights the lack of comprehensive English scholarship on the Shaiva Agamas, noting that previous efforts were often colored by missionary biases.</summary>
<keywords>Parama-hamsa, Agamic religion, Shaiva Agamas, Tamil philosophy, Sanskrit scholarship, Western orientalists, G.U. Pope, M. Winternitz</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction’s discussion of Western Indologists and their limited or emerging awareness of Agamic literature, specifically mentioning scholars like Moritz Winternitz and Paul Deussen.</meta>

-># INTRODUCTION<-

...which he simply calls ‘Tantras’ <term>Tantras</term> <note>original: "Tantras"; a category of esoteric Indian scriptures focusing on ritual, meditation, and yoga</note>. In other words, he describes a few Tantras that are Shaktic <note>original: "Śāktic"; relating to Shaktism, the worship of the Hindu goddess (Shakti)</note> in nature. Although the Shaiva Agamas <term>Shaiva Agamas</term> <note>original: "Śaivāgamas"; the primary scriptures of the Shaiva tradition, focusing on the worship of Shiva</note> are not related to these Shaktic Tantras by any shared philosophy or historical origin, the fact that he mentions them at all indicates that influential European scholars are beginning to expand the field of Indology <term>Indology</term> <note>The academic study of the history, languages, and cultures of India</note>. Until now, this field has been strictly limited to a very narrow, orthodox scope of study. 

It is to hope that when a second edition of that German work is requested, Dr. Moritz Winternitz will be quick to use the materials provided by the Agamas to expand the post-Vedic chapters of his book. At the same time, it is clear that Dr. Paul Deussen—another German Sanskrit scholar and philosopher of superb talent and achievement—shows signs of knowing something about Shaiva philosophy <note>original: "Śaiva-darśana"</note>. In his masterful summary of the monistic idealism of Shankara <note>original: "Śaṅkara"; an influential 8th-century philosopher of the Advaita Vedanta school</note>, published in German as *The System of Vedanta, Second Edition* <note>original: "Das System des Vedānta, Zweite Auflage"</note>, he refers to the commentary <term>Bhashya</term> <note>original: "Bhāshya"; a technical commentary on a primary text</note> written by Srikantha on the *Brahma-Sutras* <term>Brahma-Sutras</term> <note>A fundamental text of the Vedanta school of Hindu philosophy</note>. (I translated the relevant portions of this work from German into English for the journal *Brahmavadin* in 1907-08). 

Furthermore, in his more recent work on post-Vedic philosophy, also published in German—*General History of Philosophy, Volume One, Part Three: The Post-Vedic Philosophy of India* <note>original: "Allgemeine Geschichte der Philosophie, Erster Band, Dritte Abteilung, Die nachvedische Philosophie der Inder"</note>—he devotes a chapter to Shaiva philosophy. However, there is nothing to show that Professor Deussen has studied the Agamic literature directly <note>original: "at first-hand"</note>, as he has done, for example, with the Upanishads <note>original: "Aupanishadic"</note> during his research into the depths of ancient Indo-Aryan Monism <term>Monism</term> <note>The philosophical view that all of reality is essentially one single substance or principle</note>. 

Furthermore, the Agamas have their own unique interpretations of the fundamental teachings and precepts of the ancient Upanishads. Therefore, a thorough foundation in the Agamas—and in the Puranas <term>Puranas</term> <note>original: "Purāṇas"; a vast genre of Indian literature consisting of legends and traditional lore</note> that were clearly influenced by or developed alongside them—will provide a student with an entirely new perspective on the teachings of the Upanishads.

<summary>The author critiques European scholars Moritz Winternitz and Paul Deussen for their limited first-hand knowledge of Agamic literature, arguing that these texts are essential for a complete understanding of Indian philosophy and the Upanishads.</summary>
<keywords>Indology, Moritz Winternitz, Paul Deussen, Shaiva Agamas, Upanishads, Vedanta, Srikantha, Monism, German Sanskritists</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction’s discussion of how European scholars are beginning to engage with Shaiva Agamic literature, specifically mentioning the limitations of earlier scholarship and the contributions of Dr. L. D. Barnett.</meta>

<page-num>v</page-num>
-># INTRODUCTION v<-

...from a new perspective. That is to say, it offers a setting different from what orthodox European Orientalists <term>Orientalists</term> <note>Scholars who study the languages and cultures of the East, particularly Asia</note> have previously considered the purely Vedantic <note>original: "Vēdāntic"; relating to Vedanta, one of the six orthodox schools of Hindu philosophy</note> view of the entire mystery <note>original: "arcanum"</note> or structure of Indian metaphysics. Consequently, an independent study of the Agamas <term>Agamas</term> <note>original: "Āgamas"; a collection of scriptures in several Hindu traditions</note>, free from any prior biases, will be of priceless value to those scholars who wish to investigate anew <note>original: "de novo"</note> whether the Upanishadic <note>original: "Aupanishadic"; relating to the Upanishads, the final part of the Vedas</note> teachings might support a different philosophical interpretation than the one previously given by the so-called "accepted" schools of Hindu philosophy. 

Furthermore, in the final important work published by Max Müller <note>A famous 19th-century German-born philologist and Orientalist</note> before his death, *The Six Systems of Indian Philosophy*, there are signs he was aware of the Agamanta <note>original: "Āgamānta"; the philosophical conclusion or end of the Agamas, often synonymous with Shaiva Siddhanta</note> in both Sanskrit and Tamil. However, nothing suggests he studied or was familiar with the details of the Shaiva system <note>original: "Śaiva-darśana"; the philosophy or worldview of the Shaiva tradition</note> as developed in the Divine Agamas <note>original: "Divyāgamas"</note>. 

It is said that Dr. Georg Bühler intended to conduct a thorough study of the Sanskrit treatises based on the Agamas, specifically regarding the Spanda <term>Spanda</term> <note>A branch of Kashmir Shaivism focusing on "divine vibration"</note> and Pratyabhijna <term>Pratyabhijna</term> <note>original: "Pratyabhijñā"; a school of Shaiva philosophy emphasizing the "recognition" of the divine</note> phases of Shaiva philosophy. Unfortunately, he passed away too soon in 1898. 

Consequently, Dr. L. D. Barnett is perhaps the only living European Orientalist who has maintained a long-term interest in the Sanskrit literature related to Shaiva philosophy. To his great credit, he is not only a thorough Sanskrit scholar but also an accomplished student of the Dravidian languages <term>Dravidian vernaculars</term> <note>A family of languages spoken primarily in Southern India, including Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, and Malayalam</note>. His writings clearly show a deep familiarity with works concerning most phases of the Agamanta—namely, Pratyabhijna, Vira Shaiva <note>original: "Vira Śaiva"; a distinct Shaiva tradition also known as Lingayatism</note>, and Suddha Shaiva <note>original: "Suddha Śaiva"; Pure Shaivism</note> (the precursor to the system developed by Meykandan <note>A 13th-century Tamil saint and philosopher</note> in Tamil). He has translated Abhinavagupta’s *Paramarthasara* <note>original: "Paramārthasāra"; "The Essence of the Supreme Truth," a foundational text of Kashmir Shaivism</note> into English (a Pratyabhijna...

<summary>This page argues that studying the Agamas provides a fresh perspective on Indian metaphysics beyond traditional Vedanta. It notes that while earlier scholars like Max Müller and Georg Bühler had limited or unfulfilled plans to study these texts, Dr. L. D. Barnett has made significant contributions to the field.</summary>
<keywords>Vedanta, Agamas, Indian metaphysics, Max Müller, Georg Bühler, L. D. Barnett, Shaiva-darśana, Pratyabhijna, Agamanta, Dravidian languages</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction’s survey of contemporary scholarship on Shaiva literature, moving from European contributions to specific translation projects and the work of prominent interpreters like P. Ramanathan.</meta>

<page-num>vi</page-num>
-># INTRODUCTION vi<-

...work), and edited other Shaiva <term>Shaiva</term> <note>original: "Śaiva"; relating to the worship of the god Shiva</note> works in Sanskrit. Another Pratyabhijna <term>Pratyabhijna</term> <note>original: "Pratyabhijñā"; a school of Shaiva philosophy centered on "recognition" of the divine</note> work, titled *Shivasutravimarshini*, has recently been translated into English <note>original: "englished"</note> by Mr. P. T. Srinivasa Iyengar. Dr. Wilhelm Jahn seems to take a lively interest in Agamic <note>Relating to the Agamas, a collection of scriptures in several Hindu traditions</note> research (see the *Journal of the German Oriental Society* <note>original: "Zeitschrift der Deutschen Morgenländischen Gesellschaft"</note>, Volume 65, pages 380 and following <note>original: "et seq."</note>; refer to this <note>original: "q. v."</note>). This interest suggests great future possibilities for the field through his work. Additionally, Dr. F. Otto Schrader will soon publish an edition of the *Maharthamanjari* (a Pratyabhijna work); I have been asked to provide an English translation for it, along with critical and explanatory <note>original: "exegetical"</note> notes. 

The task of continuing the translation of the *Mrigendra-Agama* from the point where Mr. M. K. Narayanasvami Aiyar left off has fallen to me as a favor to a friend <note>original: "devolved on me as a matter of friendly office"</note>. Although I have not been able to make much progress with the continuation due to unexpected interruptions, I hope that the entire translation may soon be ready. I have also undertaken a completely new translation into English of Nilakantha's *Brahma-Sutra-bhashya*, along with Appaya's *Shivarkamanidipika*, which is its detailed scholarly commentary <note>original: "scholium"</note> in Sanskrit. In any case, it will take some time to finish. That translation will be supported by extensive scholarly references <note>original: "critical apparatus"</note>, illustrative and explanatory notes, and special introductions. For the first time, these introductions will openly incorporate a summary in English of the essential portions of most of the Agamas currently available. The projects mentioned above represent all that has been achieved, or is nearly completed <note>original: "near within an ace of achievement"</note>, regarding the explanation of Shaiva philosophy <note>original: "Śaiva-darśana"</note>.

On the purely explanatory side, the doctrines of the Agamas have found a respectful and talented interpreter in the scholar-sage Mr. P. Ramanathan. It is impossible to surpass his writings—either in India <note>original: "this peninsula"</note> or abroad—for their clarity of thought...

<summary>The author lists several ongoing scholarly efforts to translate and explain Shaiva and Agamic texts, including his own upcoming translation of Nilakantha's Brahma-Sutra-bhashya. He also praises P. Ramanathan as a leading modern interpreter of these philosophical doctrines.</summary>
<keywords>Shaiva-darshana, Agamic research, translation, Pratyabhijna, P. Ramanathan, Brahma-Sutra-bhashya, Mrigendra-Agama, Sanskrit scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction’s evaluation of contemporary scholars, transitioning from the praise of some to a sharp critique of Mr. P. T. Šrīnivāsa Ayyaṅgār’s recent work on Indian philosophy.</meta>

<page-num>vii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

...or directness of appeal. But, unfortunately for scholars, he has not chosen to write on the subject more often or extensively than his writings would lead the reader to expect. On the other hand, the literature and the mysticism of the <term>Āgamas</term> <note>A collection of scriptures in several Hindu traditions</note> have also had their share of parody and mockery in a newly-published work on Indian Philosophy, recently released by Mr. P. T. Šrīnivāsa Ayyaṅgār. This recent production is a strange mixture of intensive learning and deceptive horseplay that will appeal neither to the scholarly philosopher nor to the humor-loving general reader. Except for some bibliographical fragments of various types and questionable authority, the book fails as a genuine *summary* of the factors that make up the many mystic and metaphysical cults that have spread across post-Vedic India; and worst of all, the chapters of the book regarding the <term>Āgamas</term> and the <term>Šaiva-daršana</term> <note>original: "Śaiva-darśana"; the philosophical systems associated with Shaivism</note> are spoiled in some places by gross misinterpretations and in others by factual errors born of outright ignorance. As a work, the book is obviously inspired by a desire to synthetically imitate, within the field of Hindu philosophical investigation, the differing achievements of Westerners like Dr. Paul Carus and Prof. David Masson. And how little the author has succeeded in his endeavor would be clear to anyone who takes the time to read carefully the chapters concerning Shaiva doctrines and the <term>Šaivāgamas</term> <note>The scriptures specific to Shaivism</note>. The Christian Literature Society is constantly engaged in attacks against various aspects of Indian Thought; therefore, an occasional devil's advocate from within the tradition certainly fulfills an important role in the landscape of academic investigation. In that sense, at least, an author like Mr. P. T. Šrīnivāsa Ayyaṅgār should be welcomed rather than dismissed as unworthy of a detailed examination and serious analysis.

The Āgamas claim that they represent the truest interpretation of

<page-num>viii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

the <term>Vedas</term>, and their origins are certainly as ancient as those of some of the classical <term>Upanishads</term> <note>Original: "Upanishats"</note>. If fire-worship is regarded as the ritual taught in the Vedas—the outer symbolism of spiritual truths—then temple-worship may also be said to assume a similar importance regarding the <term>Āgamas</term>. The Āgamas introduce temple-worship only as a further companion to fire-worship, with one regarded as a supporting addition to the other. The only difference they introduce to the elements of fire-worship is the removal of animals as objects of sacrifice. The higher interpretations of the sacrificial act found in many of the Upanishads are all present in the Āgamas, though the latter lead to those interpretations through the symbolism of fire-worship as developed through the channel of temple-worship. For the rest, it can be seen that in India today, there is hardly a Hindu who does not observe some form of temple-worship or another. This suggests that the Āgamas have had, in one form or another, a universal influence across the continent of Hindu India, and that their impact remains significant. 

It may be easy to point to specific passages of the Vedas to argue that they do not intend for temple-worship. Be that as it may, it will be equally easy to demonstrate that the Āgamas are the legitimate outcome of the teachings promoted by the Vedas, and that their more important portions—namely, the purely mystic and philosophical ones—were in every way earlier than those dealing with the rites of temple-worship and the technique of sacred architecture. Therefore, the course of development along Āgamantic lines suggests that the beginning of the <term>Vidyā</term> and the <term>Yoga pādas</term> <note>The sections of the Āgamas dealing with knowledge (Vidyā) and spiritual practice (Yoga)</note> of the Āgamas was the next great step after the formation of the earlier Upanishads. In turn, the <term>Vidyā</term> and the <term>Yoga pādas</term> gradually necessitated outer symbolic rites to facilitate a congregational worship adapted to the needs of the average man with

a heart within him. Those liturgical rites were enshrined in the remaining <term>pādas</term> of the <term>Āgamas</term>, and the places for performing such rites became the temples. For instance, there are <term>Āgamas</term> in which the order of the arrangement of the <term>pādas</term> follows exactly the chronology explained here; while there are also others in which the arrangement is reversed, possibly due to a later deliberate desire to follow the logic of theoretical sequence instead of the order of natural evolution. Temples are very ancient institutions, though only slightly less ancient than the <term>Upanishads</term> <note>Original: "Upanishats"</note> of undoubted antiquity. And there is no doubt that, although the first impulse for temple worship came from the Kashmirian Region, the institution flourished in South India with considerable pomp and ceremony. The construction of the sacrificial pavilion for performing the <term>Śrauta</term> rites is, as understood from the <term>Śulba-Sūtras</term>, primarily astronomical in design and meaning. The same is true for the temple, every part of which has a parallel with either an astronomical phenomenon or a zodiacal convention. And this astronomical significance of temple symbolism runs, in some of the <term>Āgamas</term>, side by side with the spiritual meaning that we have learned to associate with the same symbolism. There are also phases of the <term>Śaiva-darśana</term> in which temple worship is not viewed with favor, either because it is not considered to contribute directly to one's spiritual uplift and eventual emancipation, or because it proves, at a certain stage, to be an outworn and dull observance unsuited to the spiritual needs of the devotee.

The <term>Āgamas</term> branched out from the same stem of the Vedic tree that produced the earlier <term>Upanishads</term> <note>Original: "Upanishats"</note>, and they were once as widespread in India as the <term>Upanishads</term> themselves. Like the <term>Upanishads</term>, the <term>Āgamas</term> also became, over the course of centuries, the basis for a number of creeds which, though unanimous in accepting

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

the essentials of the <term>Āgamic</term> teaching were divergent regarding rituals, observances, and minor, non-essential details. The earliest crystallization of the <term>Āgamic</term> doctrines into a system of formal theology originated in Kashmir under the names of the <term>Spanda</term> and <term>Pratyabhijñā</term> <term>darśanas</term>, which gradually influenced the whole of trans-Vindhyan Upper India. It is not a safe approach to associate, as some do, the early origins of the <term>Lakulīśa-Pāśupata</term> with any of the phases of the <term>Śaiva-darśana</term> that recognizes the <term>Śaivāgamas</term> as its infallible and authoritative scriptures. The dividing line between the two forms of faith is established by the fact that the <term>Lakulīśa-Pāśupata</term> (which is currently limited to the upper parts of the Bombay Presidency) does not base its teachings on the <term>Śaivāgamas</term>. The current of the <term>Pratyabhijñā</term> and the <term>Spanda</term> flowed south and became the foundation of the <term>Vira-Śaiva</term> system, which in turn grew influential in and around the Deccan. Meanwhile, an earlier current of the <term>Pratyabhijñā</term> and the <term>Spanda</term> had found its way into South India, forming the core of what later—during the time of medieval theologians—became the unified system of the <term>Śuddhaśaivadarśana</term>. The philosophy underlying all three of these <term>darśanas</term> is the <term>Āgamānta</term>, which is known by various names, the primary one being the title <term>Śaiva-Siddhānta</term> (meaning "the logical conclusion established by the <term>Śaiva-darśana</term>").

<margin>|</margin>The three philosophical categories recognized by the <term>Āgamānta</term> are Nature, Soul, and Spirit. The entire organization of the present creation is under the active control of the Spirit and is specifically designed by Him for the purpose of the emancipation of the soul. Nature is multicolored and manifold in its forms; it is the material cause not only of the outer universe—which contains countless star systems within its vast depths—but also of our body, with all its grosser and subtler

<page-num>xi</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

divisions and components, its instruments of knowledge and action, its proclivities and tendencies, in which the Soul lives as if in a cottage. The Spirit is immanent in both Nature and Soul and is, in fact, their Guiding Principle. He is thus the Soul’s Soul. It is not within the power of the Soul to lead an independent existence; it must either remain in unwitting communion with Nature, overpowered by her charms, or in conscious fellowship with the Spirit. An intermediate state is thus practically denied to it. If it ceases to gravitate toward Nature, it must lean toward the Spirit. The <term>*saṁsāra-chakra*</term> is the Soul’s orbit, which represents the result of two forces continually acting upon it. The orbit certainly shrinks toward the Spirit when the Soul is no longer attracted by Nature. The Soul has the ability to know both Nature and Spirit because it possesses sentience—an attribute it shares only with the Spirit. But it cannot be perceived by Nature because she lacks sentience; and for the same reason, the senses and the mind, which are fashioned out of insentient Nature, cannot perceive the Soul. Nor does the Soul usually have an opportunity to perceive its own true features as such, because of its ceaseless and indistinguishable communion with either Nature or Spirit—a communion that prevents the Soul from identifying its own genuine characteristics. In other words, the Soul possesses a remarkable tendency to always appear in the colors of whichever of the two other Categories happens to be in association with it for the moment. As we have shown, it is rarely, if ever, in a state of complete detachment from both Nature and Spirit, and it cannot associate with either of those Categories without being indistinguishably merged with or becoming one with it. Consequently, the Soul ordinarily sees within itself either Nature or Spirit, but not its own form. It is entangled from the beginning in the fascinations of Nature, and the Spirit

<page-num>xii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

carries on His fivefold operations with a "body of pure sentient Energy"—the outcome of His own free will—solely to disentangle it from those ruinous fascinations. The universe that we see around us has Nature for its material cause, the Spirit for its efficient cause, and His "body of pure sentient Energy" for its instrumental cause. Nature is specifically superintended by the Spirit so that she, although insentient, may more rigorously and consistently exhibit the law of desert and causality in relation to the Soul. The law of causation is really the inherent and eternal property of Nature. As long as the Soul chooses to enjoy the company of Nature, her law of causality and desert will hold the Soul tight within its meshes. But her connection with the Soul is, after all, only temporary, even though she is, in herself, eternal. It is also possessed of an ingrained perversity that is inherited from Nature, and is therefore eventually eradicable, through which it mistakes its sensuous or sensual wallowing in the "lap of Nature" for its appointed goal, and thus converts its Spirit-given instruments of emancipation, formed out of Nature, into effective engines of its own perdition. The award of spiritual freedom is always made by the Spirit to the Soul by an act of Grace, and when the moment for that award (which involves a complete emancipation from its bondage to Nature) has arrived, the Spirit reveals Himself to the Soul in any manner He pleases, and blesses it with *His Eternal Fellowship* of ineffable power and joy. The above, in short, is the plainest summary of the central truths of the <term>Āgamānta</term>, when stripped of all learned technicalities, and it will not be difficult to see how simply the whole teaching runs.

We shall now look at some of the <term>Āgamic</term> teachings a little more closely. The three categories—Nature, Soul, and Spirit—are, as we have already seen, eternal; that is to say, they are without either beginning or end. However, the Soul and Nature are under the control of

It describes how the Spirit’s Grace eventually grants the Soul spiritual freedom and eternal fellowship.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the relationship between the Spirit, the Soul, and Nature, specifically detailing the cycles of manifestation and how the law of causality serves the Soul's eventual emancipation.</meta>

<page-num>xiii</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

the Spirit, and have no absolute independence of action, which the Spirit alone enjoys to the full. The Spirit is an embodiment of love and compassion, or, as it is sometimes expressed, is nothing but Life, Light, and Love. The Souls are infinite in number, but a broad categorization organizes them into three classes, based on the varying degrees of their bondage to Nature. Nature is governed by ceaseless cycles of periodic manifestation and dissolution—cycles which prove, however, to be of many types and conditions, when considering not only the duration of or the interval between the periods, but also the specific character, phase, or grade of the manifestations and dissolutions. Manifestation is simply a process of becoming evident, while Dissolution is that of becoming hidden. Nature always endures, oscillating between a state of density and weight on the one hand, and subtlety and imperceptibility on the other. She is *in herself* inert, and every cycle of Her activity is only made possible by the unique impact She receives from the Spirit and His presence within Her. The essential active attribute of insentient Nature is Her rigid adherence to the law of causation and merit, both physically and morally; if it is stated that She is the Spirit-appointed material instrument of the Soul's salvation, all we should understand from that is that the Spirit requires the Soul to seek its emancipation only by uniting with Nature, and thereby passing through the ordeal of causality. But the elaborate processes that Nature daily employs to attract more and more Souls as Her suitors—so that they may be educated under the law of causation—are indeed very mysterious, though exceedingly seductive. She first seduces the Soul into Her company through Her irresistible fascinations, and finally exhausts it through Her relentless law of causality, which simultaneously reveals Her internal horror to the deceived Soul. The Soul then judges Her at Her true...

It describes Nature as an inert but cyclical force that uses the law of causation to both seduce and eventually educate the Soul toward its spiritual emancipation.</summary>

<page-num>xiv</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

worth, when She also, in Her turn, becomes a penitent and obedient instrument at its hands, by releasing Her hold of causality on the Soul. And thus Nature proves successively to be a seducer, a taskmaster, and a servant in relation to the Soul, according to the degree of spiritual progress the Soul has attained. The Soul is originally stupefied by the darkness of involved or inchoate Nature and, in that condition, remains tossed about in Her unfathomable womb until the Spirit brings it to life, so that it may take its chance toward permanent Spiritual Freedom by consciously interacting with Nature. At each Dissolution, the unemancipated Soul reverts to the “womb of Nature” and awaits its return to the highway of *samsāra* with Her next Manifestation. The Salvation of the Soul, once attained, is permanent and irrevocable, but the unconscious stupor in which it is primarily plunged has no beginning. How the Soul comes by that oblivion—or, what amounts to the same thing, how it becomes entangled in Nature without a beginning—cannot be satisfactorily explained; any effort to do so, however skillfully managed, will simply land one in a vicious circle of *ad infinitum regressus* <note>infinite regression</note>. In other words, the Soul’s state of bondage has no beginning but has an end, while the Soul’s Spiritual Freedom has a definite beginning but no end. It is at this point that the doctrine of the Āgamānta becomes difficult to understand for those who cannot accept it solely on the testimony of the saints who “know” the “mysteries of the Spirit.” Let it be remarked, however, *en passant* <note>in passing</note> that similar difficulties face us when we endeavor to examine other systems of philosophy put forth in India. There is hardly a philosophy or reasoned system without a trapping difficulty that is many-headed and ever-changing; if it is skillfully avoided in one part of our discussion, it certainly threatens us with a paralysis, if not the total extinction, of thought in another.

<summary>This page describes how Nature transitions from a seducer to a servant of the Soul and explores the paradox that while the Soul's bondage is beginningless, its salvation is permanent. The author notes that the ultimate origins of this bondage remain a mystery beyond logic, a difficulty common to all Indian philosophical systems.</summary>

<page-num>xv</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

Agamic mysticism specializes in the subjective processes connected with the Soul's Emancipation. Based on the principle that the “cottage” in which the Soul lives is a miniature copy or replica of outer Nature, and that the active Spirit behind Nature is also the Soul's Soul, a graduated course of spiritual discipline is prescribed. This course is full of appropriate methods to suit the Soul at every one of its stages, whereby it is first trained to conduct a minute examination of the constitution and functions of Nature through a detailed and searching inspection of its own “cottage.” It is then taught to slowly and steadily disentangle itself from the entanglements of Nature, and is finally left in a condition fit for the Grace of Emancipation from the Spirit.

The disentanglement from the meshes of Nature is briefly organized as ten-fold (*<term>daśa-kāryāṇi</term>*); the condition of the Soul in its different grades of bondage to Nature is identified as eighteen-fold (*<term>aṣṭādaśa-avasthāḥ</term>*); the course of Nature's manifestation is regarded as six-fold (*<term>ṣaḍ-adhvānaḥ</term>*); the mood of Nature is proclaimed as five-fold (*<term>pañcha-kalāḥ</term>*); and so on. In this fashion, many precious hints are provided in the Agamas, not only regarding the progression of Nature in her manifestation and her recession in her withdrawal, but also in connection with her unexpected methods of seducing the unwary Soul, and the only ways of keeping her in her proper role—namely, as an obedient handmaiden of the spirit-bound Soul. All these, however, only serve as preparation to await the appearance of the Spirit, who—at the right moment known only to Him—suddenly opens the door of His Kingdom (*<term>Śaṅkarapura</term>*) to the ever-expectant Soul and admits it to His never-ending Fellowship (*<term>Ananya-sāyujya</term>*).

This concludes an imperfect summary of a system of ancient thought, philosophy, and mysticism. To explain this system, various papers have now been brought together for the first time in book form, from

This systematic discipline prepares the Soul for the eventual gift of divine Grace and eternal fellowship with the Spirit.</summary>

# FLOWER AND FRAGRANCE.

## A FLORAL WREATH.

---

What is there in Nature so full of beauty and so symbolic of the heart’s purity, innocence, love, and joy, as the tiniest flower of the field? What reflects the great Divine Beauty, the Divine Loveliness, and the Divine Harmony more than the lowliest blossom of the valley? The freshness, the symmetry, and the delicate tracery of those flowers—how they appeal to human nature and how they inspire us! Should we wonder, therefore, that they have attracted the attention and love of the Oriental—no more than they deserve, we should say—and that they play a large part in his enjoyments, his religion, and his philosophy? They hold a significant place in Oriental symbolism, and the Indian has loved to illustrate great truths using flowers. No ceremonies can be performed without flowers; he loves to adorn the presence of his Heavenly Father with them, and he calls out to his brothers:

-> சித்தந் தெளிவீர்காள் <-
-> அத்தனாரூரனை <-
-> பத்திமலர்தூவ <-
-> முத்தியாகுமே. <-

> O you who wish to attain peace of mind,
> If you worship our Father of <term>Ārūr</term>
> With flowers of <term>Bhakti</term>,
> Then you will attain <term>Mukti</term>.—(<term>Devāram</term>).

The flower, in its threefold character of form, color, and fragrance, appeals to him as the visible presence of That which is <term>Sat</term>, <term>Chit</term>, and <term>Ānanda</term>.

<summary>This text explores the spiritual symbolism of flowers in Indian philosophy and religion, illustrating how their beauty and fragrance represent divine qualities. It emphasizes the essential role of flowers in worship and quotes the Devāram to show how devotion leads to spiritual liberation.</summary>

# FLOWER AND FRAGRANCE.

> “ பூவண்ணம் பூவின்மணம் போல மெய்ப்போதவின்ப
> மாவண்ண மெய்க்கொண்டவன், ”
>
> “ Like the flower, its color and its fragrance,
> The Lord as <term>Sat</term>, <term>Chit</term>, and <term>Ānanda</term> assumes form, ”

says the author of “<term>Tiruvilaiyāḍal Purāṇ</term>,” a work, by the way, noted for its charming language and great ability for clear description.

Our Saint <term>Appar</term> addresses this Divine Form as: “O! You cow, the five products of the cow <note>These are milk, curd, ghee, urine, and dung, collectively known as Panchagavya, used in sacred rituals.</note>, O! You intelligence, You <term>Agni</term>, You sacrificial food, You tongue, and the words proceeding from the tongue, You Lord, present in the heart of the four <term>Vedas</term>, *You flower, the fragrance present in the flower, and the joy of the flower* present in the hearts of the liberated, You <term>Deva</term>, <term>Deva</term> of <term>Devas</term>, You Effulgent Sun; behold! Such is Your Divine Presence.”

To the philosophical and highly devout <term>Māṇikka vāchaka</term>, the delicate connection between the flower and its fragrance appealed in a different light, and he sings of “*His greatness in filling all inseparably and surpassingly, like the fragrance of the flower*”:

> “ பூவினாற்றம் போன்றயர்ந்தெங்கு
> மோழிவறநிறைந்து மேவியபெருமை. ”

In another place, he compares this very connection to the relationship between body and soul; and in comparing both to the connection of the <term>Param</term>, he distinguishes them at the same time:

> உற்ற வாக்கையி னுறுபொருள் நறுமல
> ரேழுதரு நாற்றம்போல்
> பற்ற லாவதோர் நிலையிலாப் பரம்பொருள்
> அப்பொருள் பாராதே
> பெற்றவா பெற்ற பயனது நுகர்ந்திடும்
> பித்தர்சொற் றெளியாமே
> யத்த னாண்டுதன் னடியரிற் கூட்டிய
> அதிசயம் கண்டாமே,          ∴

> “Like the soul present in the body and the fragrance in the flower, the Supreme (<term>Param</term>) pervades them and *surpasses* all.”

<summary>This page quotes Tamil saints and the Tiruvilaiyāḍal Purāṇ to demonstrate how the union of a flower and its fragrance serves as a metaphor for the Divine pervading all things. It highlights the works of Appar and Māṇikka vāchaka, who use this floral imagery to describe the omnipresence and superiority of the Supreme Being.</summary>

<keywords>Tiruvilaiyāḍal Purāṇ, Appar, Māṇikka vāchaka, Sat-Chit-Ānanda, Vedas, Param, floral metaphor, Tamil literature, Shaivism, omnipresence</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the metaphorical relationship between a flower and its fragrance as a representation of the Divine, transitioning from the verses of Māṇikka vāchaka to the teachings of Tirumūlar and Tāyumānavar.</meta>

# FLOWER AND FRAGRANCE.

Fools, not perceiving this truth, simply delight in enjoying the fruits of their own <term>Karma</term>. My Father has taught me not to listen to their words; He has made me His slave and has drawn me into the society of His <term>Bhaktas</term>. I have been permitted to see this miracle!”

Although God’s connection with us is compared to the connection between the soul and the body, in that case, the soul's omnipresence is still confined to the body. That connection brings the soul only an imagined pleasure rather than a real and lasting one. This differs from the Supreme, who pervades and surpasses everything. He is all Love and all Bliss, ready to impart this Love and Bliss to those who understand Him as such. When this undying love (அயரா அன்பு) is possessed, then, in that very moment, “the fragrance of <term>Śivam</term> (Love, <term>Ānanda</term>) will blossom out of the flower of the <term>Jiva</term>,”

->“ சிவனுக்குள்ளே சிவமணம் பூத்தது.”—(*<term>Tirumūlar</term>.*)<-

That great Yogin, <term>Tirumūlar</term>, frequently uses the simile of the flower. From a variety of such examples, we select one in which he piles his "flowers" (of rhetoric) thick upon one another to express the omnipresence of the most Supreme:

> “My Lord and my King is present, united with all, like feeling in the air, sugar in the cane, butter in milk, the sweet juice in the fruit, and the *fragrance in the blossom*,”
>
> “ காலினூறுங் கரும்பினிற் கட்டியும்
> பாலினுள் நெய்யும், பழத்து ளிரதமும்
> பூவினுள் நாற்றமும் டோலுளன் எம்மிறை
> காவல னெங்கும் கலந்து நின்றானே.”

Our Saint <term>Tāyumānavar</term>—whose skill with epithets and phrasing we will one day illustrate—uses most joyful language in this regard when invoking that Rock of Love:

> “O! You support of the devoted, who attain to limitless <term>Yoga-Samādhi</term> by the *one word* (of their Divine Guru), when they view this vast world as Supreme Bliss! O!

<summary>This page elaborates on the floral metaphor in Shaivite philosophy, using the works of Tirumūlar and Tāyumānavar to illustrate how God's presence in the world is as natural and pervasive as fragrance in a flower. It contrasts the temporary pleasures of bodily existence with the eternal bliss found in Divine Love.</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Bhaktas, Śivam, Ānanda, Jiva, Tirumūlar, Tāyumānavar, Yoga-Samādhi, Shaivism, omnipresence, Divine Love</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "flower and fragrance" metaphor, moving from the poetry of Tāyumānavar to the writings of Nakkīrar and the teachings of the Dharmapura Maṭṭ regarding the nature of Advaita.</meta>

# FLOWER AND FRAGRANCE.

Oh, loving friend of even my humble self! O! You, the Rock of joy, uniting with and appearing in all bodies, the world, and souls, like the *fragrance playing upon the half-bloomed flower* shaped like the half-opened, elegant, and sweet-toned tinkling bells on children's feet.

The comparison of the half-opened flower (in the jasmine, for instance), in which the fragrance is the sweetest and most intense, to the sweet bells with half-opened mouths tied around children's feet, is most fortunate and delightful.

Nakkīrar is a very ancient author said to belong to the last <term>Saṅgham</term>, or College of <term>Paṇḍits</term>, in Madura, and he has written:

> "Look! my Lord of <term>Kailāsa</term>, who soars high above all, with none other higher than Himself, is present in all things, like the meaning in a word, the soul in a body, *and the fragrance in a flower*."

We will weave into this growing wreath one more flower gathered from the garden (*<term>Šivabhogasāram</term>*) of the founder of the <term>Dharmapura Maṭṭ</term>, as it clearly illustrates the meaning of '<term>Advaita</term>.'

> "The *advaita* relationship between God and the perfected Soul in <term>Mukti</term> is like the advaita relationship always existing between fire and wood, heat and water, sweetness and honey, *fragrance and flower*, and <term>ākāś</term> (the ether) and wind."

Greatly doubting our own ability to achieve anything without the Grace (<term>Aruḷ</term>) of the Most High, and without the spirits of the sanctified filling our inmost souls, we have helped ourselves to these holy flowers from His <term>Bhaktas</term> to make a wreath and lay it at the fragrant <term>Lotus-Feet</term> of Him, who has never been known to forsake His devotees. We pray to Him in all love and humility to crown our humble efforts with success.

<summary>This page concludes the exploration of the flower and fragrance metaphor for the Divine, citing the poet Nakkīrar and the Šivabhogasāram to explain the inseparable Advaita relationship between God and the soul. The author offers these gathered poetic "flowers" as a humble tribute at the feet of the Divine.</summary>
<keywords>Nakkīrar, Saṅgham, Madura, Kailāsa, Šivabhogasāram, Dharmapura Maṭṭ, Advaita, Mukti, ākāś, Aruḷ, Bhaktas, Lotus-Feet, symbolism, divine omnipresence</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of a new section, introducing the "Uṇmai Viḷakkam" (The Light of Truth) by Tiruvadigai Manavāšagam Kaḍandār, following the previous discussion on the nature of Advaita and divine fragrance.</meta>

# THE LIGHT OF TRUTH
### OR
# UṆMAI VIḶAKKAM
### BY
# TIRUVADIGAI MANAVĀŠAGAM KAḌANDĀR.

This short treatise, consisting of 54 stanzas, is one of the fourteen <term>Siddhānta Śāstras</term>. Its author is said to be Tiruvadigai Manavāšagam Kaḍandār, one of the forty-nine disciples of Saint <term>Meykaṇḍān</term>. It is certain that he was a native of Tiruvadigai and a pupil of Saint Meykaṇḍān, but there are no other details available about his life history. That he must have been an advanced sage is evident from the name (a <term>kāraṇappeyar</term> or descriptive name) he bears, which means "he who has passed beyond thought and speech."

The author attempts to expound in these few pages the truth of the Sacred <term>Āgamas</term> without engaging in argumentation, providing only what is sufficient for the seeker of spiritual Truth to bring the teaching into actual daily practice. The text is written in the form of questions addressed to the teacher Saint Meykaṇḍān and the answers received from him. The latter part of the treatise explains the truth of the <term>Pañchākshara</term> and <term>Śrī Naṭarāja</term> symbols. We hope the book will be of use to many.

I. வண்மைதரு மாகமநூல் வைத்தபொருள் வழுவா
   வுண்மை விளக்கமுரை செய்யத்—திணமதஞ்சேர்
   அந்திநிறத் தந்திமுகத் தொந்திவயிற்றைங்கரனைப்
   பந்தமறப் புந்தியுள் வைப்பாம்.

We place Him in our heart—the five-armed God in strong rut, of russet color, with a tusked mouth and a pot-belly—so that we may be freed from our ignorance and enabled, without fault, to spread the Light of Truth gathered from the Sacred <term>Āgamas</term>. . .

<summary>This page serves as the title and introduction to the "Uṇmai Viḷakkam," a core Śaiva Siddhānta text written by a disciple of Saint Meykaṇḍān to provide practical spiritual guidance.</summary>
<keywords>Uṇmai Viḷakkam, Siddhānta Śāstra, Tiruvadigai Manavāšagam Kaḍandār, Saint Meykaṇḍān, Āgamas, Ganesha, Pañchākshara, Śrī Naṭarāja</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Uṇmai Viḷakkam," moving from the invocatory verse to the student's formal petition and the teacher's initial explanation of the five elements and their divine associations.</meta>

# UNMAI VILAKKAM.

2. பொய்காட்டிப் பொய்யகற்றிப் போதாந்தப் பொருளா
மெய்காட்டி மெய்கண்டாய் விண்ணப்பம்—பொய்காட்டா
மெய்யா திருவெண்ணெய் வித்தகா சுத்தவினா
வைய்யா நீதான் கேட்டருள்.

O you, my teacher, who, perceiving the truth, showed the truth of Supreme Knowledge and Bliss after removing falsehood by proving it to be false!

->O You, Truth, who will not speak falsehood,<-
->O You, who reside in <term>Thiruvenṇainallūr</term>,<-
->Hear, O You, my humble petition, and please answer my questions!<-

3. **O, my Teacher, explain the following to me!**
What are the 36 <term>tatvas</term>? What is <term>Āṇava</term>?
What is that <term>Karma</term> which arose even then?
What am I, who seem to differ from these?
Who are You? What is the Lord’s Sacred Dance
and what is the truth of the <term>Pañchākshara</term>?

4. O my son, who is immersed in blissful Yoga, hear what I am now teaching you in accordance with the teachings of the Supreme <term>Āgamas</term>, graciously spoken long ago by the Supreme <term>Śiva</term>.

5. The earth’s form is a four-sided figure. The water is the form of a crescent. The fire is always the form of a triangle. The air is a six-sided figure. The <term>Ākāś</term> is a circle. And the soul receives a body formed of these.

6. The colors of these are golden, white, red, black, and smoky-colored, respectively, and their letters are ல, வ, ர, ய, அ.

7. Their symbols are the diamond-sword, the lotus flower, the *svastika*, the six spots, and the <term>Amṛita-bindu</term> respectively. So the ancient <term>Āgamas</term> declare, O my Son.

8. The Gods for the elements—Earth, etc.—are <term>Brahmā</term>, <term>Vishṇu</term>, <term>Rudra</term>, <term>Maheśvara</term>, and <term>Sadāśiva</term>; and their functions are respectively Creation, Sustenance, Regeneration, giving Rest (<term>Tirobhava</term>), and showing Grace (<term>Anugraha</term>).

<summary>This page contains the student's formal inquiry into the nature of reality, the soul, and God, followed by the teacher's explanation of the five elements, their geometric forms, colors, and presiding deities.</summary>
<keywords>Unmai Vilakkam, tatvas, Āṇava, Karma, Pañchākshara, Āgamas, Śiva, Ākāś, Amṛita-bindu, Brahmā, Vishṇu, Rudra, Maheśvara, Sadāśiva, Tirobhava, Anugraha, Thiruvenṇainallūr, five elements</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the teacher's explanation of the 36 tattvas, moving from the physical characteristics of the five elements to their associated deities, the sensory and motor organs, and the internal mental faculties that constitute the 24 Atma-tattvas.</meta>

# UNMAI VILAKKAM.

9. <term>Brahmā</term> creates; the lotus-eyed <term>Vishṇu</term> protects; <term>Rudra</term> destroys, and <term>Īśa</term> gives them rest; and <term>Sadāśiva</term> always shows grace.

10. The Earth is hard, water is cool, and fire is hot; air flows here and there, and <term>Ākāś</term> provides space for all.

11. We have now explained the number and quality of the elements. If we are to tell you about the five deceitful Perceptions, they are the desire-producing Sound, Touch, Sight, Taste, and Smell.

12. Hear the list of the <term>*Jñānendriyas*</term>! Know them to be the ear, the skin, the eye, the tongue, and the nose, which perceive the lowly sensations in this lowly world.

13 and 14. The ear perceives sound through <term>Ākāś</term>. The body perceives touch through the air. The eye perceives light through fire. The tongue perceives taste through water. And the nose perceives smell through the earth. So the <term>Āgamas</term> declare. Those who conquer these senses secure the blissful <term>Nirvāṇa</term>.

15. The <term>*Karmendriyas*</term>, which give rise to speech and other actions, are the mouth, feet, hands, anus, and the genital organs.

16. The mouth speaks through the aid of <term>Ākāś</term>; the feet move through the aid of air; the hands work through the aid of fire; the anus excretes through the aid of water; the genital organs give pleasure through the aid of earth.

17. Hear now the list of the <term>*Antaḥkaraṇas*</term>! They are <term>Manas</term>, <term>Buddhi</term>, <term>Ahaṅkāra</term>, and <term>Chitta</term>. They respectively perceive, reason, linger, and reflect.

18. The preceding 24 <term>tattvas</term> are stated by the ancient <term>Āgamas</term> to be the <term>Ātma-tattvas</term>. Hear now the <term>Vidyā-tattvas</term> explained by me.

19. Time, <term>Niyati</term>, <term>Kalā</term>, <term>Vidyā</term>, <term>Rāga</term>, <term>Purusha</term>, and <term>Māyā</term>—this is their order. Hear now their nature with attention.

<summary>This page describes the divine functions of the five deities and details how the five elements correspond to the sensory organs, motor organs, and the four internal faculties of the mind. These twenty-four components are classified as the Atma-tattvas, followed by an introduction to the seven Vidya-tattvas.</summary>
<keywords>Brahmā, Vishṇu, Rudra, Īśa, Sadāśiva, Ākāś, Jñānendriyas, Karmendriyas, Āgamas, Nirvāṇa, Antaḥkaraṇas, Ātma-tattvas, Vidyā-tattvas, Manas, Buddhi, Ahaṅkāra, Chitta, Māyā</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the teacher's explanation of the 36 tattvas, specifically focusing on the Vidyā-tattvas and Śuddha-tattvas, and introduces the concepts of Mala and the nature of the soul.</meta>

# UNMAI VILAKKAM.

20. *Time* measures the past, provides enjoyment in the present, and contains new store for the future. <term>Niyati-tattva</term> fixes the order and sequence of Karma. <term>Kalā-tattva</term> induces action. <term>Vidyā-tattva</term> induces intelligence. The <term>Purusha-tattva</term> induces perception of the five senses. And <term>Māyā</term> induces doubt and ignorance.

21. We have now stated the Vidyā-tattvas. Hear now the <term>Śuddha-tattvas</term>! They are Śuddha-Vidyā, Iśvara, Sadāśiva, Śakti and Śiva tattvas.

22. * <term>Śuddha-Vidyā</term> induces more intelligence than action. <term>Iśvara-tattva</term> induces more action than intelligence. <term>Sadāśiva-tattva</term> induces them both in equal proportion. <term>Śakti-tattva</term> induces action, and <term>Śiva-tattva</term> induces <term>Jñāna</term> alone.

23. † We have now fully stated the 36 Tattvas. Hear now about the two kinds of Mala: Āṇava and Karma. As stated, <term>Āṇava</term> induces ignorance; Karma Mala induces you to identify yourself thoroughly with the chain of pleasures and pains.

24. O rare Teacher, you have explained to me the nature of the 36 Tattvas, and Āṇava and Karma. Please now show me my own nature, for I seem both to differ and not to differ from these.

25. Listen well to what I say! <term>Achit</term> cannot exist in the presence of pure <term>Chit</term>. Chit cannot perceive Achit. The <term>Ātmā</term> (Soul) is what distinguishes and perceives both Chit and Achit. So the <term>Vedas</term> declare without doubt.

---

* <note>All these 36 tattvas are component parts of the universe of matter (<term>Māyā</term>), all-powerful and all-intelligent, in union with which the soul is freed from its darkness and regains its light. This <term>Śiva-tattva</term> and <term>Śakti-tattva</term>, etc., forming only matter, should not be confused with the Supreme Śiva and His Śakti.</note>

† <note>This Karma, as defined here, is exactly what the Buddhists understand as the Individual Ego or Individuality, which of course exists from moment to moment and is not anything that exists permanently.</note>

<summary>The text concludes the categorization of the 36 Tattvas, detailing the Vidyā-tattvas and Śuddha-tattvas, and introduces the soul's role in perceiving both pure consciousness and matter. It also defines the impurities of Āṇava and Karma that bind the soul to ignorance and sensation.</summary>
<keywords>Vidyā-tattvas, Śuddha-tattvas, Āṇava, Karma, Achit, Chit, Ātmā, Shaiva Siddhanta, cosmogony, spiritual evolution</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the teacher's explanation of the 36 Tattvas, explaining why these material categories lack consciousness and how the soul, guided by Divine grace, perceives them. It moves into a discussion of the nature of God and the symbolism of the Sacred Dance.</meta>

# UNMAI VILAKKAM.

26. Hear now how the 36 Tattvas cannot be conscious of themselves. The six kinds of taste cannot perceive themselves. In the same way, the Tattvas do not know themselves.

27. Just as a person must taste these six kinds of taste to perceive them, you are the intelligent person who, by uniting with these Tattvas, perceives each and all of them.

28. “By your undiminished grace, you have shown me my own nature. Explain to me Your own Imperishable Form.” “As the Sun enables the eye to see, so will We enlighten you and your intelligence.”

29. Know more: The senses cannot understand without the soul, and they cannot understand the soul. Similarly, We enlighten you without you being able to perceive *Us*.

30. “As the vowel letter ‘A’ is to the rest of the letters, so do We stand as the Life of all life. When We are not present in any soul, then there will be no light. So the good Āgamās declare.”

31. O Meykaṇḍa Nāthā, graciously explain so that I may understand the nature of the Sacred Dance with the sound of the five letters seen by the sages.

32. “O my son, listen; The Supreme Intelligence dances in the soul formed of the letter *ya*, with a Form composed of the five letters *Ši*, *va*, *ya*, *na*, *ma*, for the purpose of removing our sins.

33. Hear now how the Dance is performed! In His feet is *na*; in His navel is *ma*; in His shoulders is *Ši*; in His face is *va*; in His head is *ya*.*

34. ‡ The hand holding out protection is *ya*; the hand holding the fire is *na*; the foot holding down *muyalaka* is *ma*.

---

\* <note>These letters are to be contemplated in those specific parts of the body.</note>

‡ <note>This is another method of contemplating the Pañchākshara.</note>

<sig>2</sig>

<vocab>Tattvas, Āgamās, Meykaṇḍa Nāthā, Sacred Dance, five letters, Ši, va, ya, na, ma, muyalaka, Pañchākshara</vocab>

<summary>The teacher explains that the Tattvas are unconscious tools used by the soul, which is itself enlightened by God, much like the sun enables the eye to see. The text introduces the Sacred Dance of Shiva, mapping the five sacred letters (Panchakshara) onto the divine form and the human soul.</summary>
<keywords>Tattvas, soul consciousness, divine enlightenment, Sacred Dance, Panchakshara, Ši-va-ya-na-ma, Meykaṇḍa Nāthā, spiritual anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the teacher’s explanation of the Sacred Dance of Śiva, specifically identifying the symbolic meaning of Lord Naṭarājā’s form and the mystical significance of the five letters (Pañchākshara).</meta>

# UNMAI VILAKKAM.

35. The arch (Tiruvāśi) over Śrī Naṭarājā is *Omkāra*; and the *akshara* (syllable) which is never separate from the *omkāra* is the Filling Splendor. This is the Dance of the Lord of Chitambara. Those who have lost their ego (*Ahaṅkāra*) understand this. By understanding, they leave their births behind.

36. Creation starts from the Drum. Protection proceeds from the Hand of Hope. The Fire produces Destruction. From the Foot holding down proceeds *Tirobhava*; the Foot held aloft gives *mukti* (liberation).

37. By these means, our Father scatters the darkness of *māyā*, burns the strong *karma*, stamps down *mala* (Āṇava) and showers grace, and lovingly plunges the soul into the Ocean of Bliss. This is the nature of His Dance.

38. The silent *Jñānis* (sages), destroying the three kinds of *mala*, establish themselves where their egos are destroyed. There they witness the Sacred Dance filled with Bliss. This is the Dance of the Sabhānātha whose very form is Grace.

39. The One who is beyond thought and speech graciously assumes the Form composed of the *Pañchākshara* in the Dancing Hall of *Parāśakti*, so as to be seen by His Consort, Umā, Haimavatī. Those who see this mystic Dance never experience rebirth.”

40. O my gracious Guru! You have explained to me beautifully the nature of the *Nadānta* Dance. Let me now know the nature of the Pañchākshara. Can they be the same as the letters which are perishable?

41. “The symbols of these letters may be perishable, but not their meaning in any language. The meanings of the five letters respectively are God, His grace (Śakti), the Soul, Tirobhava, and Mala.

42. God, Grace, the Soul, Tirobhava, and Mala are the meaning of the five letters (*Śivāyanama*). If pronounced beginning with *na*, you will not obtain Grace. You will obtain it when you pronounce it beginning with *Śi*.

<vocab>Naṭarājā, Omkāra, akshara, Chitambara, Ahaṅkāra, Tirobhava, mukti, māyā, karma, mala, Āṇava, Jñānis, Sabhānātha, Pañchākshara, Parāśakti, Umā, Haimavatī, Nadānta, Śakti, Śivāyanama</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the symbolic attributes of Lord Naṭarājā's dance, where his form represents the five divine acts (creation, protection, destruction, concealment, and liberation). It also details the meaning of the five-letter mantra, Śivāyanama, and the spiritual necessity of beginning the mantra with the letter representing God to obtain grace.</summary>
<keywords>Nataraja, Omkara, Panchakshara, Chidambaram, Tirobhava, Mukti, Shivayanama, divine dance, Saiva Siddhanta</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the teacher’s explanation of the spiritual significance of the Pañchākshara (the five-letter mantra) and transitions into describing the nature of the soul’s liberation (Mukti) and its union with God.</meta>

# UNMAI VILAKKAM.

43. If this beautiful *Pañchākshara* is meditated upon, the soul, shedding its *Āṇavamala*, will arrive in the region where there is neither light nor darkness; there, God’s Grace (*Śakti*) will unite it to *Śivam*.

44. If the *Pañchākshara* is pronounced with the letters representing the two *malas*, then one will not get rid of their three *malas* nor obtain Bliss. If it is pronounced otherwise according to the law, your *jñāna* will be boundless and you can live in Bliss.

45. Within the *Pañchākshara* are found the *Āgamas* and the *Vedas*, revealed by the gracious God. Within it are found the *Purāṇas*. In it is the Blissful Dance. And in it is found the silent *Mukti*, which transcends everything.

46. The *Āgamas* declare that the nature of the union achieved by the *Muktas* is like that of a fruit and its taste, fire and its heat, or a musical composition and its tune.

47. The *Vedas* truthfully declare that just as the various *Tattvas* are found inseparably united in the state of bondage, so the souls in the state of freedom will dwell as one with God.

48. As the moon’s light is indistinguishable from the light of the sun, so will the soul unite itself to the foot of the Supreme Lord and plunge into Bliss.

49. If it is said that the soul had to travel and unite itself to God, then the omnipresence of *Śiva* would be denied. If God is said to have united Himself to the soul, then they must be separate entities. What, then, is the truth? The situation is like that of the sun, which surrounds a person who has been cured of blindness.

50. You tell me that the Supreme One, who is beyond thought and speech, is gracious and suffers no taint, and that like this *Pati*, the *Paśu* and *Pāśa* are also eternal. Prove how this is so in the state of *Mukti* as well.

51. “O my son, hear how they exist in *Mukti*! He who enjoys the Supreme Bliss is the soul. He who imparts this—

<summary>This section explains how meditation on the Pañchākshara leads the soul to union with God, comparing the nature of this liberation to the inseparable relationship between a fruit and its taste or the sun’s light surrounding a person who has regained their sight.</summary>

<header>UNMAI VILAKKAM.</header>

Supreme Bliss is the First Cause. That which increases this happiness is *Mala*. Understand this with all love."

52. "O my Father, let me know the unfailing means of securing this *Mukti*?" "Hear me state this! Those who regard and worship the *Guru*, *Linga*, and GOD'S DEVOTEES as the incomparable God will not suffer births and deaths."

53. "Melting in love, like a cow that has recently calved, the *Jivan-muktas* will take a strong hold of the *Guru*, *Linga*, and *Bhaktas*, and will be filled with great love for them, which will destroy their sins."

54. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;வாழ்ந்தேன் அருட்கடலே வற்றாப் பவக்கடலில்

# THE HOUSE OF GOD

> O You, the Imperishable Triple Form and the Formless!
> O You, Supreme Intelligence working steadfastly in the six forms of Religion!
> Who could know You after raising the curtain of <term>Māyā</term>?
> <margin>s/</margin>You dance in the hearts of those who think of You;
> You are the Priceless Jewel; You are my eye;
> You are the Supreme Panacea;
> You, the Ocean of <term>Chinmudrā</term> Wisdom,
> Who taught the four ancient sons
> <term>Mauna Jñāna</term> from under the Sacred Banyan Tree;
> You, the <term>Deva</term> of <term>Devas</term>.

The first two verses we quote from Saint <term>Šekkilār</term>'s <term>Periyapurāṇ</term> and the last from Saint <term>Tāyumānavar</term>, in praise of the famous Temple at <term>Chidambaram</term> and the sacred mysteries contained there. We have observed elsewhere that even if we lost our books on the <term>Veda</term> and <term>Vedānta</term>, we could reconstruct the whole thing again from the symbols we possess, provided we had the tiny key to unlock these sacred mysteries. The most ancient wisdom is thus enshrined in these unmistakable symbols, and when we understand them correctly, we are enabled to test and know which is the true Philosophy and which is the true Religion—surrounded as we are today by a multitude of Religions and Philosophies conflicting among themselves and yet claiming to be the most ancient and the truest. It is a most unfortunate thing in India and in Indian Religion that the same books and the same texts provide the authority and the sanction for every existing phase of belief and thought. When this fact is coupled with such a blind ignoring of what is past and what is modern, and when the materials for applying such a historical test are not very extensive, the task of deciding which interpretation is true and which is false is rendered very difficult, though not impossible; the value of a test as indicated above cannot be ignored. In interpreting documents, the rule should no doubt be that where the words are plain and unambiguous, the plain meaning of the words ought to prevail, and no clever reasoning should be allowed to ruin the effects of its plain meaning. It is only when the

# THE HOUSE OF GOD.

words are ambiguous, any interpretation as to its real meaning by other evidence is permissible at all. Then, again, when we begin to inquire into the truth of any particular custom and tradition, we find how difficult it is to arrive at a uniform conclusion when we have to rely on mere oral evidence; and any documentary evidence (we use it in the strictly legal sense), if available, is of the utmost importance, and the older the document, the greater its value. Then, again, consider the difference between the verbal accounts of a dozen people who witnessed a particular scene all at the same time, and the actual scene photographed by an ordinary *Kodak*. We might be sure to discover discrepancies and contradictions in the oral testimony, though it might be perfectly honest. Of course, there might be exceptionally trustworthy witnesses, as there might be untrustworthy cameras. The test we have proposed above may, as such, be seen to possess all the elements of an ancient document and a trusty camera. And the more so, when we know, as a matter of fact, that the written language of primitive mankind consisted of pictures only. The most ancient Sumerian, Chaldean, Egyptian, and Chinese were all pictorial languages; and it is well known that these were the people who have tried to leave their highest thoughts on religion and philosophy behind them in pictures, statues, and monuments.

In proceeding, therefore, to unravel the mysteries connected with our symbolism, we must confess that the task is not one which we can conscientiously think of adequately discharging. In attempting the impossible, therefore, we have no other excuse than the one which Sage *Śekkiḷār* had before him:

> “ அளவு கூட வுரைப் பரிதாயினும்
>   அளவிலாசை தூப்ப வறைருவேன் .”
> “ Though it is impossible to reach its limits,
>   Insatiable love drives me to the task.”

Before we do so, however, we have to get clear of two sets of men who often pester us with their cant. One such will

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of their inquiry into religious symbolism, shifting from the methodology of using ancient "pictorial" evidence to addressing potential critics who might dismiss the work as sectarian or revivalist.</meta>

# THE HOUSE OF OF GOD.

...raise the cry of sectarianism, and the other, with the catch-word, revivalism. There are some very respected people belonging to both these classes, we admit, as well as their sincerity, but with most it is all mere hypocrisy, pure and unmitigated hypocrisy. They believe in neither the one nor the other; they have neither the inclination nor the wish to study and think, or to pause and inquire into the truth of things. They are themselves sectarians, so blind that they will not acknowledge themselves to be such. They start with the inborn conviction that this is trash, and they have no patience with those who honestly differ from them, and they clutch at a word or a phrase to kick up a dust, with the evident object of slandering the other side. No doubt, there is a sort of skepticism which we prize much—a skepticism which will lead one to doubt and inquire into the truth of things and not to scorn and scoff at everything. And in our inmost heart, we do not wish to wound the feelings of a single person, of whatever shade of opinion he may belong to. And is not the present inquiry solely devoted to reaching "the region of universalism," "பொதுமன்றூ," where, in the words of our Sage Tāyumānavar,

> "பகரரிய தில்லை மன்றுட் பார்த்த போதுங்கு
> என்மார்க்க மிருக்கு தெல்லாம், வெளியே யென்ன
> எச்ச மயத்தவர்களும், வந்திறைஞ் சாநிற்பர்"?

every religious person comes and bows in adoration of the One Supreme, saying they see no symbols of any creed but all <term>Ākāś</term>? And he states in the previous lines that he reached this region after looking in vain in every creed and in every path for that Pure Spirit which seeks to reconcile all the bitter conflicting creeds and religions with the path of noblest knowledge.

> "சன்மார்க்க ஞானமதின் பொருளாம்வீறு

> பன்மார்க்க நெறியினிலும் கண்டதில்லை."

<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual universalism, focusing on Chidambaram's symbolic and physical significance as the "center-spot" of the world and its parallel in the human body's anatomy and spiritual centers.</meta>

# THE HOUSE OF GOD.

...heart melted into love and bliss the moment he saw the Holy Presence?

> -> " கன்மார்க்க நெஞ்சமுள வெனக்கும் தானே <-
> -> கண்டவுட னானந்தம் காண்ட லாகும். " <-

This has not been his experience alone, nor that of believers only. Ages ago, scoffers and atheists felt the power of this Presence; it is recorded of the great atheist teacher, Jaimini, that when he approached, all his disbelief left him, and he composed his song of *Vedapādastava*. Although there are thousands of temples all over the land, the heart of every true believer has always turned with love and longing toward this *central spot*. It is believed that Chidambaram occupies a central geographical position between the northern and southern extremes of India, including Ceylon. Corresponding to this position in the macrocosm, Ārumukha Nāvalar observes that, in the human microcosm as well, the location points to the region of *Sushumnā* between the *Iḍā* and *Pingaḷā* *nāḍis*.

There is another center of heat, vitality, and light in the human body: the heart. The heart is the most vital and delicate organ in the entire system. Every other organ requires its help for nourishment and maintenance. It is saved and protected from many ailments by its position, while every other organ remains exposed; this is because, while life can be prolonged even after injury to any other organ, life ebbs away the instant the heart is injured. And then, is the heart not the seat of love—love pure and undefiled? Pity, kindness, mercy, and grace are all different shades of this one Love, அன்பு, Bhakti, and faith. Is there anything else that can compete with this Supreme Principle? "Knowledge," you may exclaim, "with its seat in the brain." We venture to say, "It cannot." The slightest injury to the heart completely paralyzes the brain. Even the pulsation in the brain itself rises and falls with the beat of the heart. It is the one organ in the body that is always active and knows no rest, even when everything else, including the brain, is resting. And in human nature as well, what is there that love

# THE HOUSE OF GOD.

...cannot bring to life? It can give life to the despairing and the lifeless, strength to the weak, and courage to the coward; and there have been many instances that show what extraordinary feats of intellect love has caused. The whole world is bound by the heart much more than by the intellect alone. And Mrs. Humphrey Ward has portrayed in glowing words the difference between the man of the intellect and the man of the heart in her *Robert Elsmere*. There, the man of intellect longs, in secret and in his pride, for that very touch which makes the whole world kin. And it is in this heart that all mankind has liked to build a temple for the Most High. The only requirement is that this heart be pure. And the moment this heart is pure, the light from the invisible <term>Ākāś</term> will shine there, dispelling the darkness that blinds the eye and enabling it to see.

->“ வெளியான நீஎன்னை வெளியாக்கி விரவினையா<-

> “As large as this <term>Ākāśa</term> is, so large is that <term>Ākāśa</term> within the heart. Both heaven and earth are contained in it; both
