# THE HOUSE OF GOD.

Fire and Air; both Sun and Moon; both Lightning and Stars; and whatever of Him is in this world, and whatever is not, all that is contained within it." (VIII, 1. 123) In an earlier chapter, this Supreme Being is called "The Intelligent One, whose body is <term>Prāṇa</term>, whose form is Light (<term>Jyotis</term>), whose thoughts are true, who is like the <term>Ākāśa</term> (omnipresent and invisible), from whom all works, all desires, all sweet odors, and tastes proceed; the <term>Ātmā</term> within the heart, smaller than a grain of rice, smaller than a grain of barley, smaller than a mustard seed, smaller than a canary seed, or the kernel of a canary seed; also the <term>Ātmā</term> within the heart, greater than the Earth, greater than the Sky, greater than the Heaven, greater than all these Worlds." (III. 14. 223). In a later passage, the <term>Upanishad</term> says that "He who is called <term>Ākāśa</term> is the revealer of all forms and names; That within which these forms and names are contained is the <term>Brahman</term>, the Immortal, the <term>Ātmā</term>." (VIII. 13. 1.) The following verse occurs in the <term>Kaṭha</term> (I. 2. 20.), the <term>Śvetāśvatara</term> (III. 20.) and the <term>Taittirīya-mahopanishat</term>, and the same is reproduced in the <term>Śivamahāpurāṇa</term>.

> "Smaller than small, yet greater than great, in the heart (<term>Guhā</term>) of this creature, <term>Ātmā</term> or <term>Īśa</term> reposes: He who is free from desire sees the Lord and His might by His favor, with his grief gone."

In the <term>Kaivalyopanishat</term>, the same is reproduced in the following words: "Beyond the heavens, yet shining in the heart (<term>Guhā</term>) of his creatures, Him the sages, free from desire, reach." <term>Śri Kṛishṇa</term> also imparts this most secret of secrets to his pupil: "that <term>Īśvara</term> dwells in the hearts of all beings, O <term>Arjuna</term>, by his <term>māyā</term>, causing all beings to revolve, as though mounted on a potter's wheel," and entreats him to seek refuge in Him to secure Supreme Peace by His Grace. The manner of occupying this seat or dwelling place is elsewhere referred to in the 13th and 9th discourses, 32nd and 6th verses respectively, and these three or four verses bring out the whole of the <term>Upanishad</term> thoughts. "*As the Omnipresent <term>Ākāśa</term> is not soiled, by reason of its subtlety, so, seated everywhere in the body, the Self is not soiled.*" "*The support of beings, and not*"

# THE HOUSE OF GOD.

...rooted in beings, my <term>Ātmā</term> is their *efficient cause*; as rooted in the <term>Ākāśa</term>, the mighty air moves everywhere, so all things rest, rooted in me." This Supporter, Permitter, Spectator, and Enjoyer is styled <term>Māheśvara</term>, <term>Paramātman</term>, and <term>Parama-Purusha</term> in verse 22, chapter XIII. Another verse in the <term>Chhāndogya</term> says that <term>Gāyatrī</term> is the body and the heart, because in it all the spirits are established. No wonder, therefore, that on almost every page of the *Tamil* Veda and the writings of the later *Tamil* Saints, God's truest dwelling place—His house, His palace, His seat—is universally referred to as the human heart. “நினைப்பவர் மனமே கோயிலாகக் கொண்டவர்.” And so it is that the famous shrine we are speaking of is, by preeminence, called “திருக்கோயில்,”* “The Beautiful House,” since it is also called the “<term>Pundarika Vidu</term>” (“புண்டரீகவீடு”), “the House of the Lotus,” or “<term>Dahara Vidu</term>” as well. And today, we will stop after identifying this *Golden Palace* in <term>Chidambaram</term> with the “*Human Heart*” spoken of in the most ancient writings; we will speak of the *Great King and Lord*, who is the *Dweller* in this palace, and His characteristics in a future issue.

<insert>
[* It is interesting to note that the chief temple in Mecca is called ‘al Caaba,’ literally meaning ‘The House,’ and the Hebrew word for the great Temple at Jerusalem also meant simply ‘The House,’ or “The House of God.”]
</insert>

---

### LOTUS OF THE HEART.

If the real nature of the *Lotus of the Heart* is examined, its stalk consists of the *24 <term>tattvas</term>*, beginning with earth; its petals are the *<term>vidyā-tattvas</term>* and *<term>śuddha-vidyā</term>*; its pollen represents the *64 <term>kalās</term> of <term>Īśvara</term> and <term>Sadāśiva</term>*; its ovary is *<term>Śakti</term>*, the *essence of <term>kalās</term>*; its seeds are the *51 forms of <term>nādam</term>*; and the *<term>arul-śakti</term> of the Lord <term>Śiva</term>* rests upon it (as fragrance).

(Sivajn̄ānabodham IX. 3. c)

# ON ANOTHER SIDE.

We refer to an article entitled 'Wisdom and Worship' in an issue of the *Brahmavādin* dated June 5, 1897. The first paragraph is devoted to the statement and explanation of the two postulates of existence according to the <term>Sāṅkhyas</term>, namely Nature and Souls. The next paragraph shows how untenable this theory is in the view of the <term>Vedāntin</term>, and the article proceeds in its first half to expound the <term>Vedāntin's</term> view on the same subject. As the article deals with some of the most fundamental questions connected with Hindu Philosophy, we proceed today to examine some of these statements contained in the first part of the article only, leaving the question of worship to be discussed later. According to <term>Sāṅkhya</term>, there is Nature (<term>Pradhāna</term>), which changes and manifests all phenomena, and there are an infinite number of Souls, which, being simple, cannot change and must, therefore, be different from Nature. Nature works out all phenomena for the liberation of the Soul, and liberation consists of the Soul recognizing that it is not Nature (<term>Pradhāna</term>). The Soul is also omnipresent. The <term>Vedāntin</term> answers that this is not a perfect system. If Nature is simple and the Soul is also simple, there will be two simples; and the Soul being omnipresent, Nature must be omnipresent also. In that case, Nature would be beyond time, space, and all causation, and no change would be possible within Nature as such.

There is thus an impossibility of having two simples and two absolutes. How does the <term>Vedāntin</term> solve this problem? His solution is this:

> "Because, according to the <term>Sāṅkhyan</term>, there must be a Soul apart from Nature, for the reason that Nature in all her modifications, from gross matter up to <term>chitta</term> (or the intellect), is simply insentient—even the mind-stuff being insentient—so, there must be some sentient being as the motive power behind Nature, making the mind think and Nature"

<summary>This text critiques the Sāṅkhya philosophical system, arguing that its dualistic view of an omnipresent Nature and Soul is logically inconsistent. It begins to present the Vedāntic solution, which posits a sentient motive power behind otherwise insentient Nature.</summary>
<keywords>Sāṅkhya, Vedānta, Pradhāna, chitta, Hindu Philosophy, dualism, Soul, Nature, Brahmavādin</keywords>

Now, says the <term>Vedāntin</term>, this sentient being, which is behind the whole universe, is what we call God, and consequently this universe is not *wholly* (the italics are ours) different or apart from Him. It is but Himself, Who has *some-how* (the italics are ours) become this universe. He is not only the <term>instrumental cause</term> of the universe, but also the *material* <term>cause</term> thereof. A cause is never *altogether* different from its effect, and an effect is but its own cause reproduced in another form.” All <term>Vedāntins</term> accept these propositions, it is stated, namely first, that God is both the instrumental and material cause of this universe, and that everything that exists is He; and secondly, that Souls are also part of God, sparks of that Infinite Fire, and an <term>Upanishat</term> Text is quoted in proof of this. No, it is said further down, it is no spark, but the burning log itself, inasmuch as <term>Brahman</term> can have no parts. ‘Then how can there be so many souls?’ We are led into another simile, the oft-repeated simile of the Sun and its myriad reflections in different particles of water: “so all these Souls are but reflections of <term>Brahman</term> and are not real. They are not the real ‘*I*,’ the One undivided Being; men, women, and animals are mere reflections of Him, and are unreal.” There is but one Infinite Being, and He appears as ‘you’ and ‘me’, and the appearance of distinctions is all a delusion. This apparent division of Him is caused by looking at Him through the network of time and space and causation. The Ego is He, the Non-Ego is He. They are not part of Him, but the whole of Him. “It is the Eternal Knower Who stands behind all phenomena; He Himself is the phenomena. He is both the subject and object, He is the Ego and the Non-Ego.” Here we might pause, before we proceed to the rest of the paragraphs.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical critique of how technical terms in Sāṅkhya and Vedānta—such as "Avyakta" (the unmanifest) and "omnipresence"—are often misinterpreted. # ON ANOTHER SIDE.

The word occurs in the <term>Gītā</term>. The entire mistake is, no doubt, due to not remembering that this word—and others like <term>Prāṇa</term>, <term>Purusha</term>, <term>Ātmā</term>, <term>Kshetra</term>, etc.—are used in the older works with a variety of meanings. Any argument based on such a superficial verbal similarity is sure to end in a fatal error. Now, in regard to this word ‘<term>Avyakta</term>’, it is used in the 10th sūtra of the <term>Sāṅkhya-Kārikā</term> to distinguish <term>Mūlaprakṛiti</term> from its own products; and the commentator no doubt says that the distinction might apply to the <term>Soul</term> also. The word itself might be applied to the <term>Soul</term>, but in that context, it only means ‘uncaused’ and ‘causeless’. And <term>Colebrooke</term> translates it as ‘undiscrete’. The 3rd Sūtra clarifies this distinction at the very beginning: “Nature is not a production; seven principles are both productions and productive; sixteen are productions (unproductive). The <term>Soul</term> is neither a production nor productive.” 

This is where the entire difference lies between the <term>Soul</term> as <term>Avyakta</term> and Nature (<term>Pradhāna</term>) as <term>Avyakta</term>, and the mental and sensory planes. Nature itself occupies a higher position and is more pervasive than the <term>Intellect</term>, and the <term>Intellect</term> is more pervasive than the senses, and so on. That is to say, the <term>Intellect</term> is omnipresent, and the senses are not, when compared to the senses themselves. But the <term>Intellect</term> is not omnipresent when compared to <term>Pradhāna</term>, and <term>Pradhāna</term> is omnipresent regarding its own products; yet its omnipresence is nothing in the presence of the <term>Soul</term>, since the latter is the superintendent and the enjoyer, and the former ceases to exist when the <term>Soul</term> is in a state of abstraction. 

As such, the word ‘omnipresence’ itself is a relative term, just as ‘space’ is, and it is absurd to conclude that because both are called simple and omnipresent, they must therefore be two absolutes and two such impossible things. We will explain ourselves more fully. Take, for instance, the five senses: the eye, the ear, etc. The eye covers a certain sphere in its operation, but it is limited; it cannot comprehend what the ear can perceive, and the ear cannot do what the nose can feel, and so on. Each sense, in fact, is limited and lacks pervasiveness; but consider the <term>Intellect</term> in connection with this. The <term>Intellect</term> is omnipresent. It both sees and hears and

<summary>This page argues that philosophical terms like "Avyakta" and "omnipresence" must be understood within their specific contexts and hierarchies. It explains that while Nature (Pradhāna) is pervasive compared to the mind, it is subordinate to the Soul, which is the ultimate enjoyer and superintendent.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relative pervasiveness of the intellect, nature, and the soul, specifically addressing the Sāṅkhyan perspective on omnipresence versus the Vedāntin objection.</meta>

# AN ANOTHER SIDE.

...smells, etc. It covers a greater sphere, and all the spheres covered by its own productions: the senses. But consider the intellect (*<term>Buddhi</term>*) itself in its relation to the <term>Soul</term>. The <term>Soul</term> is sentient, and *<term>Buddhi</term>* is insentient. The latter is nowhere when the <term>Soul</term> is in itself. As such, the <term>Soul</term> is more truly omnipresent than *<term>Pradhāna</term>* or Nature. That is to say, there are different planes of existence and different grades of *<term>Vyāpaka</term>* (pervader) and *<term>Vyāpya</term>* (pervaded). The lower one is *<term>Vyāpya</term>*, and the higher one is *<term>Vyāpaka</term>*; this higher one is itself *<term>Vyāpya</term>* when compared with something even higher than itself, and so on, until we arrive at a Being who is the most omnipresent and beyond whom our thought and mind cannot penetrate. 

This view of the <term>Sāṅkhyan</term> has certainly not presented itself to the <term>Vedāntin</term>; what the latter, however, has in his mind is the old riddle: how can two things coexist if one is omnipresent? Like all such riddles, this is based on a fallacy by not taking note of the facts presented above about the essential difference between *<term>Pradhāna</term>* and the <term>Soul</term>. The riddle supposes that two things are of the same kind, of the same quantity, length, breadth, width, and of the same density or thinness, etc. If they were so, it would undoubtedly be an impossibility. But we contend that things of different densities and thinness can fill and overlap one another—all the more so when one is sentient and *<term>Chit</term>* (consciousness), and the other is non-sentient and *<term>Achit</term>* (non-consciousness). 

For instance, there can be no two things as contrary in nature as Light and Darkness. Do they coexist or not, or are they one and the same? To the objection of the <term>Vedāntin</term> that darkness is no <term>padārtha</term> (distinct substance), we need only point to the recent discoveries of our own Hindu scientist—I mean Dr. <term>Bose</term> <note>Jagadish Chandra Bose</note>—who could demonstrate the presence of invisible rays of light in a pitch-dark room by means of his instrument. What does this mean? The ray of light was so thin that it was swallowed up in the grosser darkness. When a lamp is brought, it can dispel the darkness itself, but only within a certain radius. Then a bigger light—a gas light, or an electric light of a vast number of candle-powers—is used; but all these pale before the brilliant light of the Sun. There is, thus, such a merger of one—the less powerful—into one more...

<summary>The author argues that different grades of existence can coexist in the same space based on their varying densities and natures, specifically comparing the sentient Soul to insentient Nature. This hierarchy of pervasiveness is illustrated using the coexistence of light and darkness, referencing the scientific work of Jagadish Chandra Bose.</summary>

Saint <term>Meykaṇḍa Deva</term> adds, “Just as before the Perfect and Eternal Intelligence, the imperfect and acquired intelligence (falsehood) is shorn of its light, it is therefore established that in the presence of the <term>Sat</term>, <term>Asat</term> loses its light.” And the illustration implied in this is amplified in the following verse: “Evil (<term>Asat</term>) ceases to exist before Him, just as darkness does before the Sun.” The term <term>Asat</term> has itself been the source of many misconceptions in both the East and the West, and different interpreters of <term>Šaṅkara</term> explain it in different ways. Here is what a critic of <term>Paul Deussen</term> says: “Kant is mostly credited with having proved that there is something behind or beneath the ‘reality’ of our senses which these cannot fathom (அசத்தறியா). European scientists say sneeringly: ‘What of that? If we cannot reach it, let us ignore it!’ On the other hand, the Neo-Kantian Metaphysicians say: ‘No, this is the only reality; therefore, all the rest is useless rubbish, only fit for momentary amusement,’ and that is all.

“That is the Western conception of the Indian term <term>Māyā</term> (<term>Asat</term>)—indeed, a rubbish conception. Mistaken by this illusion, Western philosophers have declared that Eastern philosophy, and particularly <term>Vedāntism</term> and <term>Buddhism</term>, are ‘*Akosmism*’—that is, they deny the existence of the universe altogether. An incredible absurdity! Is not the real meaning of <term>Šaṅkara</term> easy enough to understand? Everyone knows that there are different states of consciousness: that of an animal is different from that of a human, that of a savage different from that of a scholar, that of a waking man different from that of a dreaming man, and all these are different from that of a sage in <term>Samādhi</term>. All of these are, again, different "realities" than the one that the <term>Muktā</term> "realizes" as <term>Ātman</term>. Viewed from the perspective of any of these different states of consciousness, all other conceptions of "reality" appear as <term>Māyā</term>—as illusion or as unreality. Material scientists, along with most European philosophers, would not hesitate for a moment to declare this alleged realization of <term>Ātman</term> an illusion, although they would not deny that it might be some state of consciousness.

That is, <term>Asat</term> does not mean non-existent, but rather not-<term>Sat</term> or other-than-<term>Sat</term>. This is <term>Šaňkara</term>'s view according to Dr. Hubbe Schleiden, and this is the view we have taken the trouble to explain above. Yet how many followers of <term>Šaňkara</term> hesitate to interpret <term>Māyā</term> as illusion and delusion, and <term>Avidyā</term> as ignorance and nescience? 

In the very article being reviewed, we read in one sentence that each soul is a spark, a part; in the next sentence, we are told no, it is not a part, but the whole of *Brahman*. In the very next sentence, all these souls are described as mere *reflections of Brahman* and are *not real*. "Men, women, and animals, etc., are but reflections of Him, and are *unreal in themselves*." If they are mere reflections and unreal, how can this be reconciled with the statement that each soul is not even a part, but the whole of <term>Brahman</term>? The entire argument is constructed using similes; instead of sticking to one, it jumps from one to another to address the difficulties arising from the previous one. Either the argument must proceed based on simple facts and inferences without the use of similes, or, when an attempt is made to prove it solely through figures of speech, then no excuse should be offered that "it is only a figure," and the metaphor should not be stretched too far. The simile was specifically used to demonstrate to the unlearned how the concept is possible and conceivable; yet when the unlearned person, following the simile, asks if the same...

It argues that while these terms are often translated as "illusion" or "ignorance," they are better understood as states of consciousness or "other-than-wisdom."</summary>

## ANOTHER SIDE.

Since the necessary conditions are present in the comparison to justify the conclusion, what answer does the <term>Vedāntin</term> give him? "This apparent division of Him (as 'you,' 'me,' and the dog) is caused by looking at Him through the network of time, space, and causality." "Looking at Him," indeed! When? And by whom? How is this "looking at Him" and this delusion possible before the actual division itself? The process of dividing Him into "you," "me," and animal must precede the process of "you," "me," etc., looking upon each other and Him delusively. Does the delusion occur before the evolution of <term>Brahman</term> into "you," "me," and "animal," or after such evolution? To any thinking being, it must occur that this delusion must have occurred before, and not after; and the *<term>Brahmavādin</term>* recognizes this and states below that there will be a final duality in the universe: <term>Ātman</term> and delusion. <note>Note here and elsewhere that the word "delusion" is used simply as a synonym for <term>Māyā</term>.</note> This objection is brushed aside on the grounds that delusion is non-existence, and that to call it anything else is idle sophistry! 

And yet "you," "me," and others were under a delusion all this while! Were we, or were we not? Is that a fact, or is it a delusion itself? Is the evolution of God into men, women, and animals a fact or not? If it is a fact, is the question "how is this evolution brought about?" a possible or an impossible question? If it is not a fact, why is the statement made in another paragraph that there are perfect men and imperfect men—men like <term>Christ</term>, <term>Buddha</term>, and <term>Kṛishṇa</term> who must be worshiped, and men like ourselves who must worship them? 

In one place, this evolution of God into humans and animals is presented on a supposedly rational basis: that God wants to know Himself, to see Himself, and to realize Himself through His reflections (the reason why is not stated), because He cannot know and see Himself otherwise—just as we on earth cannot see our own faces except in a mirror! It was our brother's fault for not choosing a good mirror. And does he mean to attribute such a fault to the Most Intelligent, for not choosing a vessel in which He can see Himself and know Himself to the best advantage? The Perfect cannot seek to know Himself in the imperfect and the ignorant, the wicked and the sinful, the sorrowing and the suffering. If all this is His play and no such distinction exists—such as the imperfect, the wicked, the sinful, the sorrowing, and the suffering—and if all this is a hallucination, myth, and non-existence (to use his own chosen words), why should any man aspire to be a good man, a perfect man, or a <term>Jīvan-mukta</term>? Why should he realize *his identity with the Absolute*? 

God, in trying to realize Himself (for His sport or for what?), became man, woman, and brute; and look at the trouble of this man, woman, or brute, performing good acts, acts without attachment, real <term>tapas</term>, <term>yoga</term>, and <term>jñāna</term> to realize his identity with the Absolute! What guarantee is there that, after all this trouble, a <term>Jīvan-mukta</term> might not again be differentiated from the Absolute into a man, woman, or animal? How senseless and vain all these efforts seem, and how ignoble the purpose of creation and evolution? 

To the question of why the Perfect becomes the imperfect—a question our brother states in all its various forms, from the vulgar to the highly philosophical—our brother's answer is that this question is impossible, and it should not be asked at all! We have already pointed out how inconsequential this question and answer are. But the same question has been asked, and answers attempted, by learned men of our brother's kind; and these answers are varied and conflicting among themselves. Of these, Svāmi Vivekānanda receives the most glory. His answer is, "I do not know." Mr. Mukhopādhyāya replies that the Svāmi is wrong, and that the Perfect does not become the imperfect; God does not become man. Man is only a reflection and, as such, cannot be God. According to the <term>Brahmavādin</term>, man is a reflection and is unreal;

According to Paul Deussen, the answer is that "the never-ceasing new creation of the world is a moral necessity connected with the doctrine of <term>samsāra</term>." "A moral necessity for <term>Ātman</term>? What a *contradictio in adjecto* [contradiction in terms]!" exclaims his critic. "<term>Ātman</term>, as we all agree, is that which is beyond all necessity and causality—that is, causality reigns or exists only in our manifested world of *individual consciousness of any sort*." And the critic's own explanation is that existence is the manifestation of the "will to exist," and this will is <term>trishṇā</term> or <term>tanha</term>—the desire for enjoyment. Well, whose will, we ask? Who desires enjoyment? The Absolute, the <term>Sachchidānanda</term>, or some other? What? Do they call this hell of an earth an enjoyment for Him? We leave our learned Doctor to fight Professor Deussen by himself and proceed to state another learned lady's opinion. If we remember correctly, she said that <term>Ishwara</term> evolves into man and brute to gather experience and to improve himself by means of his animal sheaths, and that there could be no perfect <term>Brahman</term> at any time; It goes on improving Itself day after day. She suggests that if the Veda repeats the cry that there is a "Bourne from which there is no return, no return," it is a mere make-believe. And all these are learned expounders of <term>Śaṅkara's</term> school; who is right? Can we ask this question, or is our question merely captious? The Siddhāntin's answer is that the question itself is based on a fallacy—an assumption. The fact assumed is that the Perfect becomes the imperfect. Is this a proven fact? Does God really become man and brute? What is the proof of this, putting aside Vedic texts and the desire to reach a high-sounding philosophic unity? It is this fancied desire to generalize everything into "One" that led the Greek philosophers to postulate number, water, and fire as the final and ultimate cause of all things. Why not leave bad, good, and evil as they are? Why should you refer the evil to the good, or the impure to the pure? Will not

### ANOTHER SIDE.

Should silence in this respect be golden? Will not *maunam* <note>silence</note> in this case be real *jñānam* <note>knowledge</note>?

Well, we will now return to our statement of what the <term>Sāṅkhyan</term> meant when he postulated a <term>Pradhāna</term> and a Soul or souls. The learned Editor of the “*Light of the East*” has evidently fallen into an error when, in his account of the ancient <term>Sāṅkhya</term> system, he suggests that according to the ancient <term>Sāṅkhya</term> and the <term>Gītā</term>, there is only one <term>Purusha</term> and not many <term>Purushas</term>. The mistake is due to the fact that, in the enumeration of the <term>padārthas</term> <note>categories of existence</note>, the singular form only is used; this is a mere technical usage, as in the phrases <term>Jīva</term>-<term>Īśvara</term>-<term>Jagat</term>, <term>Chit</term>-<term>Achit</term>-<term>Īśwara</term>, and <term>Pati</term>-<term>Paśu</term>-<term>Pāśa</term>. All the words used are in the singular, but it cannot mean that the respective schools intend to postulate only one <term>Jiva</term>, one <term>Chit</term>, or one <term>Pāśa</term>. In explaining each, the explanation will be given that the <term>Jivas</term>, or souls, are many. In the same way, in the earlier <term>sūtras</term> of the <term>Sāṅkhya</term>, <term>Purusha</term> is used in the singular, but the subsequent <term>sūtras</term> proceed to state that the <term>Purushas</term> are multitudinous. <term>Pradhāna</term> is real and it is the cause, and its effects—the phenomena—are also real, because the effect already exists within the cause. As our learned brother approvingly puts it, an effect is its own cause reproduced in another form; and we hope the following sentence from <term>Dr. Brown’s</term> lectures will equally meet with our brother’s approval.

> “That the form of the body is only another name for the relative position of the parts that constitute it, and that the forms of the body are nothing but the body itself.”

If so, why should the cause be considered real and the effect unreal, contrary to the <term>Sāṅkhyan</term> view held by <term>Vedāntins</term>? If <term>Māyā</term> is phenomenon and effect, why should it be unreal when the substance and cause are real? The relationship of cause and effect, however, must be kept separate from the relationship of substance and phenomenon, and both of these must be distinguished from the questions of reality and delusion. In the second paragraph, however, our brother identifies the <term>Sāṅkhyan’s</term> <term>Pradhāna</term> with his own <term>Māyā</term> and the <term>Sāṅkhyan’s</term> <term>Purusha</term> with his own God or <term>Brahman</term>. If so, why attempt any criticism of the <term>Sāṅkhya</term>? It is all a quibble about words. They practically

Then, why is it that the <term>Sāṅkhya</term> is called by <term>Śaṅkara</term> ‘<term>Niriśvara Sāṅkhya</term>’ (‘Godless or Atheistic <term>Sāṅkhya</term>’), and the philosophy of the <term>Gītā</term> is called ‘<term>Seśvara Sāṅkhya</term>’ or the ‘Theistic <term>Sāṅkhya</term>’? The word <term>Sāṅkhya</term>, primarily meaning "number," signified a theory or philosophy to <term>Kapila</term> and <term>Kṛṣhṇa</term>. Compare, for instance, a similar change in the Tamil word ‘எண்’ (*eṇ*), meaning "number," and in the verse “எண்ணும் எழுத்தும் கண்ணெனத்தகும்,” where ‘எண்’ means logic and philosophy. The following quotation from the <term>Gītā</term> itself will explain the difference between the two schools:

> *“There are ‘two <term>Purushas</term>’ in this world, one destructible and the other indestructible; the destructible is <term>sarvabhūtāni</term> (all things), and the indestructible is called the <term>Kūṭastha</term>.” (Chapter XV. 16.)*

Notice how this verse runs; it mentions only two <term>Purushas</term> instead of mentioning three, as appears in the next verse. However, there is a purpose in mentioning only two <term>Purushas</term> in this way; it is seemingly to reiterate the accepted postulate of the <term>pūrva-paksha</term> <note>the preliminary or opposing argument</note> school, to enable it to state the <term>siddhānta</term> <note>established doctrine or final conclusion</note> view in the next verse, which is:

> *“The ‘<term>parama Purusha</term>’ is truly another, declared as the ‘<term>Paramātman</term>’—He who pervades and sustains the three worlds, the indestructible <term>Īśvara</term>.”*

Consider again the steps that follow one after another in the next verse:

> *“Since I excel the destructible (the first <term>Purusha</term>), and am more excellent than the indestructible (the second <term>Purusha</term>), in the world and in the Veda, I am proclaimed <term>Purushottama</term>” (the third <term>Purusha</term>).*

Note here that the word <term>Purusha</term> simply means a category, a <term>padārtha</term>, as when we speak of the <term>Tripadārtha</term> or <term>Tattvatrayam</term> <note>the triad of categories: God, soul, and matter</note>. Note again how in verse 19, chapter 13, the first two <term>Purushas</term> are mentioned by their more appropriate names, <term>Prakṛiti</term> and <term>Purusha</term>; and the same definition of these two is given in verses 20 and 21 as provided by the <term>Sāṅkhya</term>. A further step beyond <term>Kapila</term> is taken by <term>Śrī Kṛṣhṇa</term> in postulating...

# ANOTHER SIDE.

> “*A spectator and permitter, supporter and enjoyer, <term>Maheśhvara</term>, thus is styled the <term>Paramātman</term>, in this body, the <term>Paramapurusha</term>.*”

And then occurs a most beautiful passage about the distinction of these three <term>Padārthas</term>, and of the different <term>Jñānas</term>: <term>pāśajñāna</term>, <term>Paśujñāna</term>, and <term>patijñāna</term>. The <term>Lokāyata</term> only knows his body and has no knowledge of his own self or anything higher. According to the <term>Nirīshvara Sāṅkhyan</term> or the <term>Vedāntin</term>, there are—or seem to exist—only two things: <term>Prakṛiti</term> and Soul, <term>Māyā</term> and <term>Ātman</term>; and liberation consists in distinguishing one's own self as different from <term>Prakṛiti</term> or <term>Māyā</term> (delusions). This is <term>Paśujñāna</term> or <term>Ātmajñāna</term>. According to the <term>Seśvara</term> Sāṅkhyan, he sees and learns to distinguish <term>Prakṛiti</term> from his self, and his self from the Highest One (verse 29), as <term>Akarta</term> and <term>Karta</term>; and by knowing the nature of this One, he reaches <term>Brahman-hood</term> (verse 30 of Chapter 13). It is also particularly noteworthy that in the whole <term>Gītā</term>, in innumerable passages—as in the one cited above—the knowledge of the Supreme and total devotion to Him is put forward as the highest path for attaining Liberation, rather than the <term>Ātmajñāna</term> doctrine that knowledge of the individual self, as implied in the phrase ‘know Thyself,’ is the highest attainment. We take the liberty again to quote <term>Dr. Hubbe Schleiden</term>, simply to show how this latter theory is repugnant to the followers of <term>Śaṅkara</term>.

> “Indeed, there can be no more fatal error than to believe, with those most advanced Western philosophers, that *<term>Jñānam</term>*, or *<term>Moksha</term>*, means nothing else but the intellectual conception—<term>Monism</term> (<term>Advaita</term>) nothing else but the intellectual enjoyment of a proud theory.”

What we have said until now will convince our readers that there is another side to these questions, and that they do not remain only where the Sāṅkhyans and the Vedāntins left them. According to this view, the Sāṅkhyans are undoubtedly correct, as far as they go, in postulating <term>Prakṛiti</term> and <term>Purusha</term>, and the <term>Vedāntin</term> is quite correct in his identification of these two with

### ANOTHER SIDE.

...his Māyā and Brahman. There is but a thin partition between the soul or man of the Sāṅkhya, and the Brahman of the latter. In fact, man is God. In such an identification of man with God, the result is that man's intelligence does not pass on to the postulating and realizing of a Higher Being than himself; and the Brahman of the Vedāntin is only so in name. The third school postulates this third <term>Padārtha</term>, differing from the soul or <term>Ātman</term> of either school, whom the latter cannot know except with the grace of the third <term>Padārtha</term>. And though it might be correct to say that man cannot know himself, it would be blasphemous to say that God cannot know Himself. This would be attributing a human imperfection to the Most High and limiting His nature. How do we know that He cannot know Himself, when we cannot know our own selves, nor Him, without His Grace? Consider the following passage from Saint <term>Meykaṇḍa Deva</term>:

> "When the soul unites itself to God, and feels His <term>Arul</term> (Love), God covers it with His Supreme Bliss and becomes *one with it. Will He not know Himself, who is understood by the soul through the intelligence of the soul?*"

The next passage we are going to quote will show clearly that God has not manifested His glorious Truth to one people and in one region alone.

> "Why may not the Absolute Being be self-conscious?" asks a Christian Divine in almost the same words. "To deny this to Him would be to deny to Him one of the perfections which even finite beings may have." <note>Rev. J. Iverach's 'Is God knowable?' page 225.</note>

The question remains: what then is the necessity for all this evolution and resolution? The answer is contained in a simple sentence in the first sūtra of the *<term>Śivajñānabodha</term>*, namely, ‘மலத் தளதாம்’ <note>Bound by the impurities of Mala.</note>. The second <term>Padārtha</term> in our categories, and not the third, is imperfect—or more correctly, is shrouded by dross—which has to be removed like the colors on a crystal, so that its own original purity may be apparent, and it can reflect and realize the Glory and Presence of God in all Its brightest radiance. ### ANOTHER SIDE.

...this lower being, the <term>Ātman</term>, seeks to perfect itself, and the Will of the Highest comes into play to enable the soul to work out its own salvation. The *<term>Ichchā</term>*, *<term>Jñāna</term>*, and *<term>Kriyā</term> Śaktis* of the Lord induce the *ichchā*, *jñāna*, and *kriyā* śaktis of the individual soul; herein, God's Grace, Love, and Omnipotence are manifested. The exercise of the Divine Will is not for the purpose of enabling Itself to exist free from <term>samsāra</term>, nor for perfecting Itself, nor for knowing, seeing, or realizing Itself. It is not for His own sport or pleasure, nor is it without purpose; rather, it is simply to help and aid the poor soul in its attempt to achieve all these things. How well our Saint <term>Tāyumānavar</term> realizes this conception of God's great beneficence in the following lines:

> “ இமையளவும் உபகார மல்லால் வேறொன்று
> இயக்கநிற் குணக்கடலாய் இருந்தவொன்றே. ”

This view postulates three <term>Padārthas</term>, and it may be called Dualism, or <term>Dvaita</term>, or anything of that sort; however, we will demonstrate in a future article how this view is also strict <term>Advaita</term>, or true monism.*

# THE TATTVAS AND BEYOND.

> "God is not this, not this."—*Brihad Ār. Up.*
> "God is other than the known, and other than the unknown too."—*Śwetāś. Up.*

Today we present our readers with a table of the 36 <term>tattvas</term> derived from <term>Māyā</term>, together with the other postulates of the <term>Siddhānta</term> school, with which they are connected in an *advaita* relation. In Tamil, small tracts called <term>kaṭṭaḷai</term> (கட்டளை) exist, which describe and define these <term>tattvas</term>. These <term>tattvas</term> are variously numbered as 19, 25, 36, or 96. Both <term>Siddhāntins</term> and <term>Vedāntins</term> (Idealists) accept the number 36 or 96, but they differ in several particulars. When analyzed further, the "thirty-six" become "ninety-six." The simpler form of the table is given here, and it requires careful study. A precise definition of these <term>tattvas</term> must follow, but we do not attempt it here for want of space. Rev. Hoisington has translated one of these tracts, as has Rev. Foulkes of Salem. Unfortunately, both of these books are out of print. 

We will proceed to explain the table briefly, stating at the same time its points of difference from other schools. We must first state that the <term>tattvas</term> enumerated here are all produced from and form only subdivisions of <term>Māyā</term>; the term as such does not cover <term>Āṇava</term>, <term>Karman</term>, <term>Ātman</term>, or God. These <term>tattvas</term> form, as it were, different coats or garments of different textures at different times and at different stages for the soul undergoing evolution, with the intent to rid itself of its coil (<term>Āṇava</term>) in strict accordance with the Law of <term>Karma</term>. These, however, form no garments for the Supreme Being, and He is accordingly addressed as '<term>Tattvātīta</term>,' or 'Beyond the tattvas.' The soul is also sometimes called this, as it lies outside the category of the thirty-six <term>tattvas</term>. However, a distinction must be made between the two. The soul, a subject, when united to the objective

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the 36 tattvas, specifically discussing the inductive method of philosophical inquiry and how different schools of thought—ranging from materialists to theists—interpret the levels of existence.</meta>

# THE TATTVAS AND BEYOND.

(material) body, becomes in a sense objective. But the supreme subject can never become objective. The enumeration of the <term>tattvas</term> begins from the lowest and the grossest, which is the earth. Philosophic inquiry also proceeds, and ought to proceed, from the lowest—the things known—to the Highest—the Unknown. This is the pure inductive method. When we come to inquire into the manner in which this inquiry has proceeded, we find that each school clings to one or another of the <term>tattvas</term>, or something else, as the highest and truest existence, and refuses to recognize that anything else can be real or true.

As such, we find that the <term>lokāyatas</term> (materialists) occupy the lowest rung of the ladder. We say lowest from our standpoint, and we beg their pardon for saying so. In their own estimation, they are postulating the highest possible existence, and every other postulate is only a hoax. The <term>Lokāyata</term> will only recognize the first four <term>tattvas</term>—earth, water, fire, and air—and will not recognize even <term>Ākāś</term> (ether) as a real element. The Buddhists and Jains also recognize only these four elements. If you point to the existence of mental powers, the <term>Lokāyatas</term> will categorize all of them as being merely functions of the brain or other organs of the body, and assert that all these functions are mere phenomena produced by and caused by bodily powers.

We proceed a step higher, and we come to those who admit the mental powers to be substance; they would reduce all bodily functions and powers to mere phenomena and assert that beyond this mind (<term>Buddhi</term>), nothing can exist. If you assert that there is such a thing as an <term>Ātman</term> (soul), they will think you are a fool; however, if they want to win you over, they will simply assert that what we have all along believed in as <term>Ātman</term> and God cannot be anything but this <term>Buddhi</term>. They will apply every name you have learned to use for higher things to this mind.

Passing beyond this <term>Buddhi</term>, we reach its immediate cause, the <term>Mūlaprakṛiti</term>. Most Indian theistic schools do not carry their notion of matter beyond this <term>Mūlaprakṛiti</term>, which stands at the head of the first twenty-four <term>tattvas</term>. They fail to see that matter can assume even finer

# THE TATTVAS AND BEYOND.

and more intelligent forms than these 24 <term>tattvas</term>. Just as people at lower levels have mistaken the gross forms for <term>Manas</term>, and <term>Buddhi</term> itself for the soul and God, these higher forms of *matter* have also been mistaken for the soul and God. This mistake is made more natural because the souls whose coverings are formed out of these rarer forms of matter are increasingly advanced spiritually and intellectually. It will be seen that what is called *<term>Guṇa</term>* (meaning merely quality) is the special essence of *<term>Mūlaprakṛiti</term>* or matter at this stage; and this <term>Guṇa</term>, which divides itself as <term>Sattva</term>, <term>Rajas</term>, and <term>Tamas</term>, does not belong to any higher forms of matter than *<term>Mūlaprakṛiti</term>*. 

And this *<term>Mūlaprakṛiti</term>* forms the special vesture of the lowest classes of souls called *<term>Sa-kala</term>*. These souls range from the greatest Gods to the smallest living germ; each is clothed with the <term>Guṇas</term>: <term>Sattva</term>, <term>Rajas</term>, and <term>Tamas</term>. The highest of these classes of souls are endowed with very great powers, and they become the lords of this universe in different <term>manvantaras</term>. These three beings are <term>Rudra</term>, <term>Vishṇu</term>, and <term>Brahmā</term>. 

Considering the greatness of these *<term>jivas</term>* from our own low position, we need not wonder why people have often mistaken these *<term>jivas</term>* for the Supreme God Himself. And an even grosser mistake was never made than when it is (foolishly) asserted that this *<term>Saguṇa-Rudra-Jīva</term>* is the *<term>Pati</term>* postulated by the <term>Śaiva-Siddhāntins</term>. Some of this latter class of people boast over the former, and say that the worship of this lower Brahm (<term>Saguṇa-Rudra</term> or *<term>Īśvara</term>*) is all well for a time, but that it is no good and cannot secure any *<term>Moksha Sādhana</term>*, and that the belief in the <term>Nirguṇa</term> Brahm alone is capable of freeing one from one’s bonds. 

But that is placing too much importance on the difference between <term>Saguṇa</term> and <term>Nirguṇa</term> beings. That this is not, in fact, any very important factor will be made manifest from the fact that instead of one <term>Nirguṇa</term> Being, as believed in by the Hindu Idealists, there are a host of such Beings who possess no vestures formed of the three <term>guṇas</term>. The higher orders of *<term>Pralayākalas</term>* and *<term>Vijñānakalas</term>* are all <term>Nirguṇa</term> Beings, and they can never be born again as mortals or human beings.

<summary>This page explains that higher forms of matter are often mistaken for God and clarifies that beings like Rudra, Vishnu, and Brahma are powerful souls (jivas) rather than the Supreme Lord (Pati) himself. It distinguishes between Saguṇa beings, who are bound by the three qualities of matter, and Nirguṇa beings like Pralayākalas who have transcended those bonds.</summary>

# TATTVAS AND BEYOND.

The Śāṅkhyas and Hindu Idealists postulate <term>Mūlaprakṛiti</term> and the twenty-four <term>tattvas</term> derived from it, and for a twenty-fifth they postulate <term>Jīva</term> (souls) or <term>Ātman</term>. When the <term>Ātman</term> (<term>Brahm</term>), otherwise <term>Nirguṇa</term>, becomes clothed with a <term>Saguṇa</term> body, it becomes a lower <term>Brahm</term> or <term>Jīva</term>; but when the question is asked how this is possible, some answer honestly that they do not know, and others practice wordplay with phrases, saying that there is no such occurrence as the <term>Nirguṇa Brahm</term> becoming a <term>Jīva</term>, and if it appears so, it is entirely a delusion. But the other side argues that if this is not a delusion, but rather that there is a <term>Jīva</term> clothed in darkness, and if the other side will not acknowledge any being other than the Being who falsely appeared as <term>Jīva</term>, then the <term>Ātmā</term> they believe in cannot be the highest, but only one of the lower <term>Jīvas</term>. The same mistaken identity is manifest here as in the positions of those who took matter, mind (<term>Buddhi</term>), Indra, Brahmā, Vishṇu, or Rudra as the Highest Being. 

Before we pass on, we have to notice one class of <term>Saguṇa-Vādins</term>, who will not admit that God is <term>Nirguṇa</term> at all, and who seek to explain away all texts which refer to God as a <term>Nirguṇa</term> Being by saying that <term>Nirguṇa</term> simply means the absence of bad qualities, and <term>Saguṇa</term>, the presence of good qualities, or <term>Sattva</term>. When one is confronted with a text of the <term>Gitā</term> itself—one's highest authority—stating that God is devoid of all the three <term>Guṇas</term>, he does not hesitate to take the plunge and claim that the absence of the three <term>Guṇas</term> does not negate the presence of the <term>Sattva-Guṇa</term>! There is a wholesale misreading of the texts, and all this quibbling is made necessary simply because they cannot tolerate the idea that the <term>Saguṇa</term> Being, in whose worship they have become such strong adherents, should turn out after all not to be the Highest. 

Next above the <term>Sa-kalas</term> (<term>Jīvas</term>) come the <term>Pralayākalas</term> who have a special body (<term>Nirguṇa</term>) formed out of the <term>tattvas</term> No. 26 to No. 30. It is so distinctive in kind, form, and powers that it has been regarded almost as a separate <term>tattva</term>, called <term>Purusha-tattva</term> or <term>Ātma-tattva</term>. This will clarify passages which assert that <term>Avyakta</term> (unmanifested

(What this comparative greatness and smallness mean, we have explained in our article titled 'Another Side' when explaining the meaning of Omnipresence, <term>Vibhutva</term>). The next five, the highest <term>tattvas</term>, constitute a different, highly spiritual body for the highest order of souls called <term>Vijñānakalas</term>, and they proceed from <term>Šuddhā-Māyā</term>. The foremost in rank among these <term>Vijñānakalas</term> become Lords, <term>Išvaras</term> of the Universe, and they are variously called <term>Mahešvaras</term>, <term>Sadāšivas</term>, <term>Bindu</term>, and <term>Nāda</term>. And yet all these seven <term>Išvaras</term>—three of whom are <term>Saguṇas</term> (<term>Brahma</term>, <term>Vishṇu</term>, <term>Rudra</term>) and four of whom are <term>Nirguṇa</term> (<term>Mahešvara-Brahm</term>, <term>Sadāšiva-Brahm</term>, <term>Bindu-Brahm</term>, and <term>Nāda-Brahm</term>)—are all souls united to <term>Ašuddha-Māyā</term> and <term>Šuddhā-Māyā</term> bodies. In the <term>Vedas</term> and <term>Upanishats</term>, all these <term>Saguṇa</term> and <term>Nirguṇa</term> Gods are spoken of as the Highest God, and specific <term>Upanishats</term> are devoted to the praise of one or another of these Gods. Great confusion arises from the fact that from <term>Rudra</term> (one of the Trinity) upwards, all the different <term>Išvaras</term> are called by all the names of the Most High: <term>Rudra</term>, <term>Šiva</term>, <term>Šankara</term>, <term>Šambhu</term>, <term>Bhava</term>, <term>Sarva</term>, <term>Pašupati</term>, etc. The reason for this identity in form and name appears to be that these <term>Išvaras</term> are, in a sense, immortal and are not subject to human rebirths like <term>Sa-kalas</term>. Furthermore, there are no possibilities of regression among them, and they approach the Majesty of the Most High much more closely than other lower beings.

To classify God, the <term>Param</term>, as being in the <term>Turiya-avasthā</term> condition <note>See the table on page 7 in "Theosophy of the Vedas," Volume I.</note> is sheer blasphemy. He also notes that <term>Vemana</term> uses the term <term>Śiva</term> to denote the Highest as well, and the writer fails to understand how this can possibly be when, today, the term ‘<term>Nārāyaṇa</term> or <term>Vishṇu</term>’ is used throughout the Telugu country as the name of the Most High. He suggests a probable explanation that it might be due to <term>Liṅgāyit</term> influence. 

But in the days of the author of the <term>Atharvaśikha Upanishat</term> and the <term>Mahimnastotra</term>—not to mention many others which we quoted on page 36—no <term>Liṅgāyit</term> sect had yet come into being, and yet their belief is exactly similar to that of <term>Vemana</term>. These two parties occupy positions which seem almost as distant as the poles and are altogether irreconcilable. The Rev. Father asks, ‘Why this mockery? Say with the fool that there is no God: that the existence of God is a sham, a bubble, a false show, a cheat, a daydream, a chimera: because an Impersonal God is all this.’ The learned <term>Svāmi</term>, on the other hand, says, “The monistic theory has this merit: it is the nearest to a demonstrable truth in theology that we can get. The idea that the Impersonal Being is in nature, and that nature is the evolution of that Impersonal, is the nearest we can get to any truth that is demonstrable; and every conception of God which is partial, small, and *Personal* is comparatively irrational.” In the editorial note on ‘God and <term>Brahman</term>,’ a novel and very presumptuous and misleading distinction in the use of the words God and <term>Brahman</term> is attempted, and the article concludes by saying that the worship of God, in all truth and in all love, will never lead one to <term>Moksha</term>. “God is for such, and the <term>Brahman</term>

"...is for those whose goal is perfect rest in perfect freedom."

The presumption lies in assuming that all other religious people, except those of our learned brother’s kind, do not postulate a <term>Brahman</term>, and that their path—not being the ‘Soham’ path (<term>Paramahamsa</term>)—will not lead one to <term>Moksha</term>. It is also an unwarranted presumption to try to restrict the use of the word "God" to what these people have, until now, been calling the "lower <term>Brahman</term>," <term>Saguṇa Brahman</term>, or "Personal God."

The so-called <term>Vedāntists</term> have an insidious way of seeking favor with others by judiciously offering praise here and there, while simultaneously trying to raise themselves above everyone else at their expense.

They profess to be full of the milk of human kindness toward the followers of all creeds and sects, and would willingly take them into their fold. For what purpose? Only so that these people may see that what they profess to teach is the only true path containing the only truth, and that the other paths are—well—not paths at all. They claim these other paths will only bring people back to the same cycle of birth and death, containing only a so-called "phenomenal truth."

And after all, what is the truth of these people worth? In itself, it is so shaky, or they distort it so badly in their attempt to please everyone, that their "truth" (substance) becomes indistinguishable from untruth (phenomena); and this is exactly what the <term>Svāmī’s</term> Guru, the <term>Paramahamsa</term>, the <term>Mahātman</term>, says.

God—the <term>Saguṇa</term>—the Personal God is <term>Māyā</term> or <term>Śakti</term>, as indistinguishable from <term>Brahman</term> as heat is from fire; and this God or <term>Māyā</term> is, as such, one with <term>Brahman</term>. Therefore, the distinction between a Personal and Impersonal God is a distinction without a difference. (*Prabhudda Bhārata p. 109*)!!

It will be seen from a reading of the Reverend Father’s article, and from how these words are used in the *Brahmavādin* and the *Prabhudda Bhārata*, that all these parties use the word <term>Saguṇa</term> as fully equivalent to "Personal," and <term>Nirguṇa</term> as equivalent to "Impersonal Being." It has never even occurred to these learned people to wonder whether such a translation is actually true.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of God, specifically focusing on the definitions of Nirguṇa, Guṇa, and the relationship between Prakṛiti (nature) and Purusha (the spirit or soul) as described in the Bhagavad Gītā.</meta>

# THE NATURE OF THE DIVINE PERSONALITY.

In our last discussion, we quoted a <term>Švetāšvatara Mantra</term> in which the One God is called *Nirguṇa*. Today we quote a <term>Gītā</term> verse in which God is called *Nirguṇa*. “Beginningless, without qualities (*Nirguṇa*), the Supreme Self (*Paramātman*), Imperishable, though seated in the body, O Kaunteya, works not, nor is soiled.”<note>Chapter xiii. 31.</note> And the whole of chapters 13 and 14 must be read to know the precise meanings of *Guṇa*, *Saguṇa*, and *Nirguṇa*. Verses 5 to 18 (chapter 14) define and describe the *Guṇas* and their varieties—*Sattva*, *Rajas*, and *Tamas*. The three *Guṇas* are born of *Prakṛiti* (14. 5., and 13. 19), from which come all action, causes, and effects (13. 23), and from which all bodies are produced (14. 20.). 

*Sattva* is simply bodily (and mental) purity leading one to the desire for wisdom and bliss (14. 6); the light of wisdom streams forth from the *Sattvic* man; and when he dies, he goes to the worlds of the Gods (*Vijñānaloka*) and rises upward. The *Sattvic* man is still clothed in the material (*Prakṛitic*) body and is not yet released from his bonds; he is not a *Mukta*. He is simply what the world esteems as a wise and great man. On the other hand, *Rajas* engenders passion, engenders a thirst for life, and is united to action—greed, outgoing energy, the undertaking of actions, restlessness, and desire—and he is born again and again among people attached to action. *Tamas* engenders ignorance, delusion, sloth, indolence, darkness, negligence, etc., and he is born and enveloped in the vilest qualities. 

From this *Prakṛiti* and the three *Guṇas* born of *Prakṛiti*, the *Purusha* is distinguished.<note>On page 582 of the *Brahmavādin*, Purusha, Brahman, and Spirit are called synonymous terms. On page 217 of Mr. Mahādeva Šāstrin’s <term>Gītā</term> translation, <term>Šaṅkara</term> says that Purusha, Jiva, <term>Kshetrajña</term>, and <term>Bhokta</term> are all synonymous terms. So Brahman and Jiva are synonymous!!!</note> *Prakṛiti* is the cause of causes, effects, and instruments; and *Purusha* is the origin of pleasure and pain—that is, experiences—and is attached to the qualities (*guṇa*) born of *Prakṛiti*. By this attachment or *Pāša*, it undergoes birth and death. So the reason for its undergoing birth and death is its attachment to the *Guṇas*.

<summary>This page defines the three Guṇas (Sattva, Rajas, and Tamas) born of Prakṛiti and explains how the Purusha becomes bound to the cycle of birth and death through its attachment to these qualities. It also critiques contemporary interpretations that equate Brahman with the individual soul (Jiva).</summary>

*Sattva* is included. And the only way this *Purusha* (the *Brahman* of our *Brahma-vādin*), the Dweller in the body, can be freed from death into everlasting life is by crossing over the three *Guṇas* (14. 20), and by realizing that all action and change is the result of the three *Guṇas* (14. 19), and that he himself (*Purusha*) is actionless or flawless (13. 29), and that there is One higher than the three *Guṇas* (*Prakṛiti*) (14. 19), who is distinct from himself. 

The Highest *Purusha*, the *Paramātman*, He who pervades and sustains the three worlds, the indestructible *Išvara* (15. 17), the Spectator, Permitter, Supporter, and Enjoyer, the *Mahešvara*, and this beginningless, *Nirguṇa Paramātman* cannot perish; though He is also seated in the body, just as the *Purusha* or *Ātmā* is seated, He is not attached to the three *Guṇas* from which the bodies are created, and is not tainted nor soiled—as the *Purusha* was declared to be in verses 19, 20, and 21 of the 13th chapter—just as *Ākāša* (ether) is not soiled, though present in each and every thing. 

The *Purusha* (the *Brahma-vādin's Brahman*, and our *Jīvātma*) must also realize, to achieve his freedom, that he and *Prakṛiti* are all rooted in this One and proceed from it (13. 30), although the One is neither rooted in *Prakṛiti* nor *Purusha*, being their efficient cause (9. 5). This one God, as the *Švetašvatara* says (the passage is worth repeating), is:

> "hidden in every *Bhūta*, pervading all, the inner *Ātmā* of every *ātmā*, the Inspector of all deeds (the spectator) in whom everything dwells (the support), the Witness, the Pure Intelligence and *Nirguṇa* Being; The *Išvara* of *Išvaras*, the *Mahešvara*, the Supreme God of Gods; the king of kings, the Supreme of the Supreme, the *Īša* of the Universe."

> "The Eternal of eternals, the consciousness which every being's consciousness contains, who, as one among many, dispenses their desires—the Cause."

> "The sun does not shine there, nor the moon and stars, nor do these lightnings shine, much less this fire. When He shines forth, all things shine after Him; by *Brahman's* shining, everything here below shines."

This same Being is described below as the all-creator and protector, the refuge of all, who created *Brahmā* himself and taught him his craft. This same Being is described by the

# THE NATURE OF THE DIVINE PERSONALITY.

&emsp;&emsp;The Taittiriya Upanishat describes God as the only true and endless Intelligence, whose head is surely Love, Joy His right wing, Delight His left; Bliss His very self; and Who is distinct from the Ātman whom we know to be also *Sat*, *Chit*, and *Ānanda*. The Gītā expressly speaks of God as being distinct from Purusha and Prakṛiti. The Švetāšvatara also does the same. The Vedānta sūtras sum up the teaching of the Upanishats beyond all doubt in sūtras 17 and 21 of the first pāda of the first chapter; and in the preceding sūtras, God is described as Love, Intelligence, the-inside-of (*antas*) everything, the Light, the Person, the Powerful One. It is of Him that it is said by the Muṇḍaka that He perceives all and knows all, and whose penance consists of knowledge; of whom the Švetāšvatara and Gītā speak as having hands and feet on all sides, and eyes and faces on all sides. Now this is the God who is described as the creator, protector, and destroyer, and the refuge, the Truth, the Intelligence, and Love and Bliss; Who is described as the supporter, spectator, seer, and person, and Who is declared at the same time to be Nirguṇa, transcending both Prakṛiti and Purusha and Gods and Išvaras. Now we will ask our Reverend Father Bartoli if he will accept this Nirguṇa Being as the true God, or the Saguṇa God, or Išvara (the lower one referred to in Mantra 7, section vi of the Švetāšvatara, whom we showed in our table as forming the Sakala Jivas). And, in fact, the personal God whom our learned contributor defines and describes is indeed none other than this Nirguṇa Being. The Christian ideal of God is also that He is the Creator of heaven and earth, the only one Truth and Light and Intelligence and changeless Substance who loves and cherishes His creatures, and Who is the bridge to immortality, and Who is distinct from His creatures.

&emsp;&emsp;The Personal God of Christian Theology does not mean a Being who undergoes change, is clothed in a material body like ourselves, who is born and dies repeatedly (though they speak of one incarnation for all time to come), who has eyes, hands, and senses as we have, and whose intelligence, will, and power are finite and limited like ours. Of course, we must also point out that

<summary>This page defines the Supreme Being as Nirguṇa, an infinite intelligence of love and bliss that transcends nature and individual souls, while drawing parallels between this Vedantic concept and the Christian definition of a personal, changeless God.</summary>

# THE NATURE OF THE DIVINE PERSONALITY.

&emsp;&emsp;We do not agree with those who falsely suppose that of the *Nirguṇa* Being, even *Sachchidānanda* cannot be predicated (if so, where is the Being itself and what remains of it at all? In that case, all our Reverend Father's denunciations of the Impersonal God would apply with even greater force). We do not agree that It is not Knowledge (consciousness) and Power (*Jñāna, Kriyā Svarūpam*), nor that It is not the author of creation, destruction, and grace, nor that this *Nirguṇa* God can neither know and love us, nor that we can love and know Him either. All these and more are no doubt stated as an article of faith by the so-called Vedāntists; however, the Editor of the *Light of the East* (a staunch Vedāntist) ranks them as gross materialists and atheists, and we have quoted direct texts to show otherwise. Some of these so-called Vedāntists also claim to have reached the knowledge of the highest simply by learning to speak of God in the neuter—as "It," "That," and "*Brahman*"—and by regarding Him as formless and nameless. Nothing can be a greater delusion than this. This "It" of theirs is actually nothing but *Jīva* after all, and is one with the Universe. The *Svāmi* says,

> "So the whole is the absolute, but within it, every particle is in a constant state of flux and change; it is unchangeable and changeable at the same time, Impersonal and Personal in one. This is our conception of the Universe, of motion, and of God, and this is what is meant by 'Thou art That.'"

This may be what the *Svāmi* holds as true, but this is what we hold to be *Pāša* and *Pašujñānam*—Materialism and Anthropomorphism. The *Svāmi* talks glibly enough of the *absolute and its particles* and the unchangeable and changeable *Brahman*. But did he forget the Vedic mantra that God is "partless, actionless, and tranquil"? And the *Svāmi’s* guru, appropriately enough, speaks of *Māyā* and *Brahman* as one. And what, I ask, is Materialism? Furthermore, what is this much-vaunted attribute of *Achala* and *Nišchala* (unchangeability) worth, when every one of its particles is undergoing change? A man may be seated and at perfect rest. Yet so many of his muscles and nerves are in an extremely active condition and undergoing change and destruction, and the particles of his whole body are also under-

A pool of water may be at perfect rest, but a single breath of wind can cause motion in every particle, and we do not call water a stable element; and we do not ourselves aspire to the condition of rest and freedom described above. This is only a make-believe rest and stability. So, we must rate the Brahman (unchangeable and changing) of the *Svāmi* as only a being—since every *chalana* <note>vibration or motion</note> being undergoes rest at short or long intervals out of sheer exhaustion—willful, inconstant, and unstable, the mere toy of every passing whim, every passing breath. The Infinite and Limitless God whom the *Brahmavādin* portrays in such glowing colors to mislead the credulous few—whose throne is Space, and whose queen is Time, and who is as limitless and infinite as space and time are limitless—must also share a similar ignoble fate. We never thought that we would have to correct our learned brother regarding such a simple thing: that the very notion of time and space implies both limitation and finiteness. We have no need to turn over big treatises to find authorities for this statement. There is lying before us a small and well-written pamphlet by Dr. Peebles of America, entitled *The Soul*. In the very opening paragraph, we find the following lines; we quote them only to show what a trite notion it has now become:

> “All beginnings in show, time, and space necessarily have their endings. A creature which has its beginning in time is incapable of perpetuating itself or of being perpetuated through eternity. A line projected from a point in space has a further limit which no logic can carry to infinity.”

We have, on another occasion, pointed out that infinite space and limitless time are contradictions in terms. The absolute can never involve itself in space and time. If it does, there is no use in calling it the absolute and unconditioned. And our brother is quite right in saying that knowledge of this Brahman is only a misnomer (a myth, we should say). Then again (on the same page, 587), our brother says that “the Brahman (It) is formless, for all forms imply a boundary.”

# THE NATURE OF THE DIVINE PERSONALITY.
<sig>7</sig>

Most futile of delusions! But does formlessness imply an absence of boundaries? Many things in nature are invisible and have no form. If by "formless" is meant something without extension, such as the mind, etc., we know that the mind, as a product of <term>Māyā</term>, is also limited. But by "formless," people generally mean "<term>Arūpi</term>," or "invisible"; and invisibility is not a great attribute after all, since matter can also be formless and invisible. We have elsewhere pointed out the error of taking "Form" and "formless" as being respectively equivalent to "Personal" and "Impersonal." To deny that God can take form is to deny His omnipotence and to limit His nature. The distinction exists from our own perspective. When we begin to identify Him with anything we know, from the lowest <term>tattva</term> to ourselves (<term>Ātmā</term>), then that conception is anthropomorphic.

The distinction does not rest on calling the Supreme " <term>Śiva</term>," "<term>Śivāḥ</term>," or "<term>Śivam</term>," or by the pronouns "He," "She," or "It." God has form; the <term>Śrutis</term> declare this to be so. God is formless; the <term>Śrutis</term> say this as well. He has form and has no form. This is because His body is not formed of matter but is pure <term>Chit</term>, or Intelligence. It is only when we make God enter a material body, and say that He is born and dies, that we blaspheme Him and humanize Him, making our conception anthropomorphic. Some of the self-styled <term>Vedāntists</term>, who are unable to distinguish between what constitutes God’s real nature and anthropomorphism or Hindu symbolism, misunderstand the ideal of God according to <term>Śaiva Siddhānta</term>.

Do they bother to understand why, when describing God, the scriptures say He is neither male, nor female, nor neuter—neither he, she, nor it—neither <term>Rūpi</term>, <term>Arūpi</term>, nor <term>Rūpārūpi</term>? And yet, when they address God, He is called <term>Śiva</term>, <term>Śivāḥ</term>, or <term>Śivam</term>, and "<term>Rūpam Kṛishṇa Piṅgaḷam</term>," and is worshipped as the invisible air and <term>Ākās</term>. Professor Max Muller points out, with bewildering perplexity, how the gender frequently varies from the masculine to the neuter in the <term>Śvetāśvatara</term>. Well, in the passage "it has feet and hands everywhere," if the neuter <term>Brahman</term> can have feet, why could the Being with the feet, etc., not be described as "He" also? We describe all inanimate creation as "it," and when we proceed to call the Supreme "It" as well, we transcend from

<term>Saguṇa</term> to <term>Nirguṇa</term> !!! We have already cautioned against mistaking the <term>Śakti</term> of <term>Śaiva-Siddhānta</term> for <term>Māyā</term>. It is this mistake that has been the fruitful source of all the degradation and vice of the northern <term>Vāmāchāra</term>. This <term>Śakti</term> is called most frequently in Tamil '<term>Arul Śakti</term>' (God's manifestation as Love or Grace) and the greatness of this '<term>Arul</term>' is thus beautifully described by <term>Tirumūlar</term>.—

> "அருளெங்குமானவளவையறியார்
> அருளையமுதானதுந்தேறார்
> அருளெங்கருமத்திருந்தமுன்னார்
> அருளெங்கட்கண்ணெனவறிவாரே."

"Who knows the Power of this <term>Aruḷ</term> by which Omnipresence is secured?
Who understands that this Love transmuted Herself into flavorful ambrosia?
Who thinks that this Love—permeates subtly the five great operations (<term>Pañchakṛitya</term>)?
Who knows that this Love has eyes on all sides (is Omniscient)?"

> அருளிற் பிறந்திட்டருளில் வளர்ந்திட்ட
> அருளி லழிந்திளைப் பாறி, மறைந்திட்டு,
> அருளான வானந்த வாரமுதூட்டி
> அருளாலென்னந்தி யகம்புகுந்தானே.

"Born in Love, raised in Love,
Changing, and resting in Love,
Fed in the Supreme ambrosia-like Love,
The <term>Nandi</term> entered me as Love."

He says elsewhere that no one knows that Love and God are the same. To go and identify this Supreme Love of God—which, like the emerald, covers everything with Her own Love, and imparts to each and every one its own peculiar beauty and power and grace and will—with <term>Māyā</term>—which, like darkness, plunges everything into ignorance and death—is real blasphemy and prostitution indeed. We will stop the discussion here as far as <term>Saguṇa</term> and <term>Nirguṇa</term> are concerned, and glance at the controversy regarding a Personal and Impersonal God. It is not very easy to get at the precise definition of these terms, and the dispute seems to be more often a quarrel over words. One author, for instance, says that by Personality is implied and involved mortality, corporeality (materiality), and human volition. Another says that personality involves limitation. Is this so?

<keywords>Saguṇa, Nirguṇa, Śakti, Śaiva-Siddhānta, Māyā, Arul Śakti, Tirumūlar, Pañchakṛitya, Nandi, Divine Personality, Impersonal God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Divine Personality, moving from the definition of the word 'person' to the arguments of modern and ancient thinkers regarding God as a self-conscious, absolute Being.</meta>

<page-num>51</page-num>

# THE NATURE OF THE DIVINE PERSONALITY.

And is this the proper connotation and denotation of the word? If so, no one need hesitate to conclude that God cannot be personal. But eminent men like Emerson and others say that it does not mean any such thing. To quote Dr. Peebles again: "Personality in its common and outward sense is usually associated with appearance and outward character; but for writers such as Emerson, James Freeman Clarke, Frohschammer, Elisha Mulford, Lotze, etc., Personality has a far deeper meaning. The Latins used *persona* to signify personating, counterfeiting, or wearing a mask. But personality, in the sense in which Emerson employs it, signifies true Being—both concrete and spiritual. It alone is original Being. It is not limited. Personality is that universal element that pervades every human soul, and which is at once its container and fount of Being. Distinction from others and limitation by them results from Individuality, not Personality."

Personality, therefore, pertains to the substance of the soul, and individuality to its form. The Reverend J. Iverach also very ably disputes the idea of personality as first stated in his work, *Is God Knowable?* He argues that to say the absolute and unconditioned Being is personal is not a contradiction in terms (such as a "round square"), but rather it is true in the same way we might say a "white" or "crimson square." "When we speak of the absolute, we speak of it as a predicate of pure Being; what we mean simply is that the absolute is complete in itself, having no conditions except the conditions contained within itself. When we speak of personality, we ascribe to it Being, regarded as pure spiritual Being; we simply mean that absolute personal being is and must be self-conscious, rational, and ethical; it must correspond to the idea of spirit. Why may the absolute Being not be self-conscious? To deny this to Him would be to deny Him one of the perfections which even finite beings may have?" Saint Meykaṇḍa Deva asked the same question several centuries earlier (*Šivajñānabotham*, XI. Sūtra 1 b), and our Saint Tirumūlar also states the question in similar terms.

<summary>The author argues for a deeper definition of "Personality" as a self-conscious, spiritual substance rather than an outward mask, allowing God to be understood as both Absolute and Personal. This view is supported by referencing both Western philosophers like Emerson and Iverach and Tamil saints like Meykaṇḍa Deva.</summary>

->நானறிந்தன்றே இருக்கின்ற தீசனை<-
->வானறிந்தா ரறியாது மயங்கினர்<-

> "On that day I knew my God; the same was not understood by the Devas. The Bright Effulgence, shining within my body and soul, it is said, does not know itself. Who else can know them?"

We will stop here for the present. We accept the view of personality as set forth by Emerson and others, in which case we must reject the notion of an impersonal, unintelligent and unconscious, unknown, unknowable, unlovable, and unloving nothingness. Christians and Muslims (there are some Saguṇavātis among them as well) have no need to be wary of the Nirguṇa conception, though the followers of Rāmānuja and Madhwa—whose God is identified with Prakṛiti itself (*Vasudeva Para Prakṛiti*)—never rise above the Saguṇa *Sattvic* conception. 

Some Vedantists, wavering between two positions, manage to fail most miserably, and we have no adequate language to describe their view of a God who is both Nirguṇa and Saguṇa, Personal and Impersonal. No one should be ashamed to proclaim the truth, if it is indeed the truth. Why go to the trouble of praising Krishna and his teaching to the skies, only to say, after all, that Krishna (as the late Mr. T. Subba Rao stated more plainly, he cannot be the incarnation of the absolute) is only for those who wish to be born again and again and who consider the service of God as their highest happiness, while Brahman is for those whose goal is perfect rest in freedom? 

These very people will raise a protest if a Śaiva were to state the same truth—which, incidentally, was stated long ago by Śrī-Kṛishṇa himself—that the worship of Śiva or Śivam alone would secure Sāyujya (Moksha), and the worship of other gods (Īśvara, Brahma, Vishṇu, Rudra, etc.) would only secure their respective realms (Pada). There are some more questions which arise out of this discussion, and we reserve them for a future occasion.

# VOWELS AND CONSONANTS.

->(MIND AND BODY.)<-

---

> “Of letters, I am the letter A,” Gitā.

> “There is an alliance with matter, with the object or extended world; but the thing allied, the mind proper, has itself no extension and cannot be joined in local union. Now, we have a difficulty in providing *any form of language, any familiar analogy*, suited to this unique conjunction; in comparison with all ordinary unions, it is a *paradox* or *contradiction*”—Bain.

The quotation we provide above is from Dr. Bain’s remarkable book ‘Mind and Body,’ and the several chapters comprising the book are worth close study, even though we are not bound to accept the learned Doctor’s conclusions, nor share in his hope that the philosophy of the future will be a sort of qualified materialism. The important thing is to get at his facts, as far as they can be arrived at by close observation and experiment, and such inferences as are warranted by strict logic, which have been most thoroughly sifted, and about which therefore there can be no doubt. We will inquire, therefore, what the proved facts are concerning the nature of mind and body and their characteristics, and the nature of their connection, so far as they can be ascertained. Now as regards Mind, it is analyzed into Feelings (including emotions), Will, and Intellect.

> “These are a trinity in unity; they are characteristic in their several manifestations, yet so dependent among themselves that no one could subsist alone; neither Will nor Intellect could be present in the absence of Feeling; and Feeling manifested in its completeness carries with it the germs of the two others.”

The ultimate analysis of a Feeling being either a pleasure or a pain, it is seen, however, that volition or thought could not, in any
