<meta>This page continues the discussion of Dr. Alexander Bain’s psychological theories, specifically focusing on how feelings serve as the foundation for volition and intellect, while comparing these Western ideas with Tamil and Hindu philosophical concepts regarding the nature of human intelligence.</meta>

# VOWELS AND CONSONANTS.

sense, be confused with feelings. However, what Dr. Bain means in the above quotation is that without the acquisition of feelings, no volition or thought could arise first; that feelings are primarily all derived through the sensory organs and centers. Pleasure is seen to be connected with an activity which tends to promote life (உயிர்த்தல் செய்தல்) and pain with an activity that destroys life (உயிர்த்தல் ஒப்பது); these also determine the nature of right (good) and wrong, <term>Pāpam</term> and <term>Punyam</term>, in ethics. This principle is stated as the law of self-conservation. But there is a limit to all pleasures; even a pleasure may become painful if it is carried to excess. Another law exhibited in feelings, which also applies to thought, is what is called the law of relativity—namely, that “change of impression is necessary to our being conscious.” If either a feeling or a thought is prolonged for too long, it becomes weaker and weaker until it is blotted out altogether, and we are no longer conscious of such a feeling or thought; to become conscious again, we soon change this train of thought and then return to it. The Tamil philosophers state this principle in the axiom, ‘நிணைப்புண்டேல், மறப் புண்டாம்’—“If there is thought, there is forgetfulness also.” Dr. Bain almost admits that the reason for this law is not very easy to explain, either on the mental or the physical side. But Hindu philosophers take this fact as showing that man’s intelligence (அறிவு) is weak (சிற்றறிவு) and that it can become stronger and stronger, becoming "all thought" through practice (<term>Sādana</term>). In Yogic practice, what comes first is more darkness and oblivion than light; but by continuing in the same path, true light dawns in the end. The nature of the light is often mistaken in the interval because so many different shades of it break out. And our volition (இச்சை—<term>Ichcha</term>) determines our actions as they are impelled by feeling or intellect. Intellect is analyzed into a sense of difference, a sense of similarity, and retentiveness or memory. What are variously called memory, reason, judgment, imagination, conception, and others are all reducible to these three kinds. And difference lies at the very basis of our intellect. No knowledge and no intellectual operation is

<meta>This page continues the analysis of mental functions based on the law of relativity, moving from the necessity of "difference" in perception to the limitations of human consciousness in processing multiple sensations simultaneously.</meta>

# VOWELS AND CONSONANTS.

possible if there is no difference in the constituent elements, if there is a mere sameness. If there were only one color, the art of painting would be an impossibility; if there were only one sound or tune, we could never hear music. As it is, the law of relativity governs our very being. Sameness could provide knowledge only if there were difference; hence, the sense of similarity is also considered an intellectual function, and it performs a great function in the field of invention. Furthermore, no high degree of intellectual power is possible if we do not possess the power of remembering our past experiences and impressions. 

One peculiarity of the human mind—which we might call a defect—may also be noted here, as it is based on the law of relativity already stated. The mind is not conscious of all impressions through all the sense organs all at once. A person does not become conscious of a sight, a touch, a sound, or a smell all at once. There must be a transition from one to the other, however momentary it might be. The case of an *Ashtāvādāni* <note>A person capable of performing eight different mental tasks or "avadānams" simultaneously through intense concentration.</note> is no exception to this. Assisted by a good memory, the more *avadānams* he performs, the more time he takes. 

It will be noted that in this analysis of the mind, no distinction is drawn between a feeling and the consciousness of a feeling, a volition and the consciousness of a volition, or reasoning and the consciousness of reasoning. Both are considered identical and therefore require no distinction. In Hindu philosophy, they are distinguished: a mere feeling, willing, or thinking is separated from the consciousness of such functions. Pure consciousness is regarded as the soul or *Sat*, and the rest is classified with the body and the world as the non-soul or *Asat* (that which is other than *Sat*). We will speak more of this distinction further on. 

However, the body and its functions, along with the so-called external world, are contrasted with these mental functions. This, collectively called matter, the non-ego, or the object, possesses certain characteristics and properties that are not found in the mind at all—such as breadth and length (order in place), extension, hardness and softness (inertia), weight (gravity), color, heat, light, electricity, organized properties, chemical properties, etc.

<summary>The author explains that knowledge requires difference and relativity, noting that human consciousness transitions between singular sensations rather than processing them all at once. He contrasts Western psychology with Hindu philosophy regarding the nature of pure consciousness (Sat) and concludes by defining the physical properties of matter.</summary>
<keywords>law of relativity, sameness, intellectual function, memory, Ashtāvādāni, consciousness, Hindu philosophy, Sat, Asat, non-ego, properties of matter, inertia, gravity</keywords>

# VOWELS AND CONSONANTS.

and the most important of this is *extension*. Matter is *extended*, Mind is *unextended*. Says Dr. Bain,

> “We are in this fix; mental states and bodily states are utterly contrasted; they cannot be compared, they have nothing in common except the most general of all attributes—degree and order in time; when engaged with one, we must be oblivious of all that distinguishes the other. When I am studying brain and nerve communications, I am engrossed with properties exclusively belonging to the *object* or material world, and I am unable at that moment (except by very rapid transitions or alterations) to conceive a truly mental consciousness. Our mental experience, our feelings and thoughts, have *no extension*, no place, no form or outline, no mechanical division of parts; and we are incapable of attending to anything mental until we shut off the view of all that. Walking in the country in spring, our mind is occupied with the foliage, the bloom, and the grassy meadows—all purely objective things. We are suddenly and strongly arrested by the odor of the May-blossom; we give way for a moment to the sensation of sweetness; for that moment objective considerations cease; we think of nothing extended, we are in a state where extension has no footing; there is to us place no longer. Such states are of short duration—mere fits, glimpses; they are constantly shifted and alternated with objective states, and while they last and have their full power, we are in a different world; the material world is blotted out, eclipsed, for the instant unthinkable. These *subject-movements* are studied to advantage in bursts of intense pleasure or intense pain, in fits of engrossed reflection, especially reflection on mental facts; but they are seldom sustained in purity beyond a very short interval; we are constantly returning to the objective side of things—to the world whose basis is extension and place.”

However widely these may differ, there is this remarkable fact about them: they are found united together in a sentient being—man or animal. And the exact correlation, correspondence, or concomitance in these two sets of phenomena is what Dr. Bain takes very great trouble to show in several chapters. This we need not deny, as Dr. Bain fully admits that this conjunction and correspondence do not warrant us in stating that mind causes body o <unclear>r bod</unclear> <unclear>m i n d</unclear> ; but <unclear>h i s</unclear> position is that mind-body causes mind-body. This latter circumstance again causes no difficulty to the Siddhānti who postulates "முத்தியிலும் மும்முதலுண்டு," meaning "even in Mukti, none of the three *padārthas* are destroyed," and who no more believes in a disembodied mind than Dr. Bain does, unless a body or an organism is understood to be the body composed of all the 25 lower *tattvas*. From the table given in No. 10 of the first volume of the *Siddhānta Dīpikā*, it will be seen that even the most spiritual beings have a body composed of Asudda or Sudda Māyā; and we have also remarked, cautioning against the common mistake of calling matter "dead," that these higher aspects of matter are so potent and active as to be often mistaken for God Himself.

Passing from this point, however, we now come to the question of the nature of the union between this mind and body. When we talk of union, the suggestion that it is *union in place* is most predominant. And Dr. Bain lays great stress on the fact that such a local conjunction is unthinkable and impossible. There can be no union in place between an unextended thing (such as Chit) and an extended thing (such as Achit); and all such expressions as "external" and "internal," "container" and "contained," are also misleading and harmful. The connection is not a causal connection. It is wrong to call such a conjunction one where one acts on the other, or where one uses the other as an instrument. (The theories of occasional causes and of pre-established harmony are also antiquated now). The phenomenon is a most unique one in nature; there is no single similar conjunction in nature that we may compare it to by analogy, and there is no fitting language to express such

Language fails and analogy fails to explain this union, even though it is a fact in itself; it remains a mystery in a sense, although seeking an explanation for an ultimate fact can, in no sense, be logical. All that we can do has been done when we have tried to generalize the various sets of phenomena into the fewest possible number; if we cannot pass to a higher generalization than two, we can only rest and be thankful.

We are sure that this is a perfectly safe position to hold, and our object in writing this article is in no way to differ from this view. However, we believe we have an *analogy* in Tamil that will exactly answer the point and make the union more intelligible, besides bringing out the nature of mind and matter in a much more favorable light than from the standpoint of a mere materialist, whether qualified or otherwise. We believe we have been using language almost every day to describe this union, even though the name itself is a *puzzle* and embodies both a *paradox* and a *contradiction*. Before we state them, however, we will mention one or two facts as they bear upon the relation of mind and matter, which Dr. Bain states more fully in his *Mental Science*. One fact is that all objectivity implies the subject-mind at the same time. "All objective states are, in a sense, also mental." Unless the mind is present, even if unconscious, you cannot have knowledge of an object at all. We cannot have a purely objective condition at all without the subject supporting it, as it were, though for the time being it is non-apparent and entirely blotted out (*Sūnyam*). Or rather, shall we say that, though they are dissimilar, the mind has become thoroughly identified with matter. But the mind can ascend to pure subjectivity, and this does not imply the presence of objects in the same way the object implies the subject; and in such a pure subjective state, where is the object? It has also become non-apparent (*Sunyam*). Regarding the possibility, however, of matter being the primary element, there is this fact: matter is found both as

organic and inorganic; and what a world of difference is there between these conditions of matter? Is the peculiar organization given to it by the temporary presence of mind within it, or is it derived solely by its inherent power? We have admitted that so-called dead matter might possess countless potentialities. Still, is there any sort of similarity between the inorganic properties exhibited by matter and the organic or vital properties? However this may be, we will now proceed to state our analogy. It is the analogy of vowels and consonants. We have quoted the *Gītā* verse, but we look in vain even in Śaṅkara's commentary for the meaning we have tried to give it. Possibly Śaṅkara would not give such an explanation, as it would conflict with his preconceived theory. So, if there was truth in it, it remained locked, and the key, ultimately, remained with the Siddhānta writers. The most familiar example of the analogy occurs in the sacred *Kural*, in its very first verse:

> "அகர முதல வெழுத்தெல்லா மாதி
> பகவன் முதற்றே யுலகு."
> "As 'A' is the first of all letters,
> So the ancient Bagaván is the first in this world."

We might imagine an alphabet in which the letter "A" is not the first, and if the point of comparison were merely to denote God's order in place as the first, many other analogies might be thought of. And Parimelalaḷagar accordingly notes that the order is not an order of position, but an order of origin. It is the most primary and first sound that the human voice can utter, and it is also the one sound which is present in every other sound, whether vowel or consonant. All other vowels are formed by modifications of this sound. And what, pray, are vowels and consonants? A vowel is defined as a sound that can be pronounced by itself, without the aid of any other sound. And a consonant is one which cannot be sounded except with the aid of the vowel. Let us look more carefully into the nature of these sounds. We utter these sounds every day, and yet we

<meta>This page continues the linguistic analogy comparing the relationship between vowels and consonants to the union of mind and body, or God and the world, specifically through the lens of Tamil grammar and Hindu symbolism.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>

<header>VOWELS AND CONSONANTS.</header>

[We] fail to recognize the mystery in their connection, solely because of their familiarity. We try to utter 'A.' It comes forth pure and simple, by the mere opening of the mouth, without any modification whatever, and requires no other aid. But let us pronounce, for example, 'K.' It is 'Kê' in English; in Tamil it is 'Ka' ('க'), 'Ik' ('க்'), or 'Ich' ('ச்'). There is a vowel sound present in it: 'ê', 'a', or 'i.' Let us eliminate this vowel sound and try to pronounce the consonant alone. Well, the task is impossible; you do not get any consonant sound at all. In the consonant, therefore, there is always a vowel sound present, though we never consciously recognize its presence; however, in Tamil, the symbolism is so highly philosophical that we invariably mark its presence even when we write pure consonants. We dot all our consonants as 'க்,' 'ச்,' etc., and the dot or circle represents the letter 'அ' ('A') in Hindu symbolism. This dot or circle begins almost every one of the twelve vowels in the Tamil alphabet, and we will take another opportunity to explain what the other curved, horizontal, and perpendicular lines mean. Therefore, when we write 'க்,' the framers of the alphabet meant to represent how the vowel sound underlies the consonant, supports it, and gives it its very being and existence. Such a mark is unnecessary when we write the vowel-consonant 'Ka' ('க'), as we are fully aware of its presence. In the pure consonant, therefore, the vowel is implied and understood, though for the time being its presence is not detected, and it is completely identified with the consonant itself.

We have been considering at scholarly length the nature of the union between mind and body, but have we ever paused to consider the nature of the union of the vowel and consonant? Is there any other such unique conjunction anywhere else in nature, where one cannot exist except in conjunction with the other? Aside from the inseparable conjunction stated above, we see that the pure consonant is no more derived from the vowel than the vowel is from the consonant. There is a much wider contrast between these than between any other two things in the world. Their places of origin are distinct. 'A' is pronounced by the

<summary>The author explains that consonants cannot be pronounced without an underlying vowel sound, using the Tamil alphabet's "pulli" (dot) as a philosophical symbol for how the divine supports existence. This linguistic relationship serves as an analogy for the inseparable union between mind and body.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the linguistic analogy between vowels and consonants and their metaphysical counterparts, mind and body, by exploring why these two categories are inseparable yet distinct.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>VOWELS AND CONSONANTS.</header>

mere opening of the mouth. The tongue has to be brought into contact with the palate to pronounce ‘k,’ and this same act cannot produce the vowel. So, the vowel cannot be said to cause the consonant, nor the consonant the vowel. Nor can we call the consonant and the connection themselves false, or a mere illusion or delusion. So, neither the principle of <term>*Pariṇāma*</term> <note>transformation or evolution</note> nor <term>*Vivartana*</term> <note>illusory appearance or superimposition</note> can apply to this connection. All that we can say of it is that they are so connected and inseparable that no language is possible by vowels alone or by consonants alone. Every consonant is, at the same time, a vowel-consonant, in which the vowel is either apparent or non-apparent; and though we can conceive of vowels standing alone, to think of consonants as existing by themselves is an utter impossibility.

Now apply all of this to the case of mind and body. Mind is the vowel, and the body (matter) is the consonant. Mind and body are as widely contrasted as vowels and consonants are. One cannot be derived from the other by <term>*Pariṇāma*</term> or <term>*Vivartana*</term>. Yet both are inseparably united, and though the mind occupies an independent position and can be a pure subject at times, the body cannot subsist unless it is in conjunction with the mind. Mind is always implied in the body; mind underlies it, supports it, and sustains it (if all this language derived from material cognition is permissible). When the mind is pure mind, the body is not; it is <term>*asat*</term> (<term>*Sūnyam*</term>). When it is pure body, the mind is present but non-apparent; it has become one with the body. The mind is there, but it conceals its very self, its very identity, and it is effectively absent. And except at rare intervals, our whole existence is passed in pure objectivity, without recognizing the presence of the true self, the mind. The whole truth of these two analogous cases—the only two—are brought out in Tamil in the most beautiful manner, by using the same words to denote vowel and consonant as are used for mind and body. See what light breaks forth when we name ‘உயிர்’ and ‘மெய்’ (உடல்). The word ‘உயிர்’ means both a vowel and a mind (soul); and ‘மெய்’ means both a body and a consonant. Dr. Bain observes that the sense of similarity is the sense of invention and true discovery. The

<summary>This page argues that vowels and consonants are inseparably linked like the mind and body, using Tamil linguistic terminology to show that the soul (vowel) underlies and sustains the physical form (consonant).</summary>

The greatest discoveries in science have been made by identifying such resemblances at rare intervals. And when the very first Tamil man called his vowels and consonants 'உயிர்' <note>Uyir; meaning "life" or "soul"</note> and 'மெய்' <note>Mey; meaning "body" or "truth"</note>, was he not a born philosopher? Had he not comprehended the true nature of the union between mind and body, and vowels and consonants? This simile receives its best exposition for the first time in the hands of Saint Meykaṇḍa Deva (see *Śivajñānabotham*, II. 1. b. and notes pp. 12, 19, and 20) and his followers (see *Light of Grace* pp. 7 and 8). Saint Meykaṇḍān gives a name in the same verse to denote this connection. This one word is <term>*Advaita*</term>. This word has been a real puzzle to many, and so many interpretations of it have been given. The Tamil philosopher, however, explains it as meaning "neither one, nor two, nor neither" (ஒன்றாகாமல், இரண்டாகாமல், ஒன்றுமிரண்டு மின்றாகாமல்), which fully and beautifully brings out the meaning of Dr. Bain's words: that the connection is both a *paradox* and a *contradiction*. Very few outside the circle of the <term>Siddhānta School</term> could be made to comprehend the truth of this paradox—especially when their minds are biased by their own views. But we have always used the analogy of vowels and consonants with great effect, and it has tended to make the subject much clearer than many a more learned argument. In this article, we have confined ourselves to dealing with the last two sets of phenomena in nature: Mind and Matter. We will reserve for a future article the nature of the higher powers we postulate and their connection with the lower ones, as well as a further expansion of the subject and the history of the question in Indian systems of thought.

# GOD AND THE WORLD.

### THE ADVAITA.

The Vedic Texts *Ekam evādvitiyam Brahma* and *Ekam Eva Rudro Nadvitiyāya thas teh* mean that there is only one Supreme Being without a second. And this One is the <term>Pathi</term> and not the soul. You, who ignorantly say you are one with the Lord, are the soul and are bound up with <term>Pāśa</term>. Just as we say that without the primary sound "A," all other letters will not sound, so the Vedas say that without the Lord, no other things will exist. *Sivajñānabotham* (ii. 1. b).

->“ ஆணவத்தோடத்து யிதமானபடி மெய்ஞ்ஞானத்<-
->தானுவினோ டத்துவித மாகுநாளெந் நாளோ.”<-

"Oh, for the day when I will be in <term>Advaita</term> union with the unchangeable True Intelligence, just as I am now in union with <term>Āṇava</term> (<term>Pāśa</term>)!"

Count Tolstoy says, "Religion is a certain relation established by man between his separate personality and the endless universe or its source; morality is the perpetual guiding of life which flows from this relation." And as we have explained in our previous article, even the knowledge of a thing means knowledge of its difference and similarity compared to other things—its relation to things which are dissimilar and to things which are similar. From the knowledge of such relations, our further acts are determined. For example, if the object is a new fruit we had not seen before, if we find it related to edible species, we try to eat it; if not, we throw it away. However, if one should make a mistake in the identification—whether from imperfect experience or knowledge, or by being misled by the attractive and tempting appearance of the fruit—woe befalls him when he partakes of it. Accordingly, all our good and evil flows from our understanding, rightly or wrongly, our relation to

Accordingly, the highest philosophy and religion also signify knowledge, specifically knowledge of the relationship between the highest postulates of existence; different systems arise as different kinds of relationships are proposed. In determining these respective views, imperfect observation and experience, passion and prejudice, and the constraints created by heredity and society all play their part. Consequently, we see different moral standards followed by men, consciously or unconsciously, resulting from their pre-existing convictions.

Proceeding along our own lines to discover these relationships, we looked to Dr. Bain to help us reach a particular stage. He is a most uncompromising agnostic and materialist (with qualifications), yet we were in perfect agreement with him as far as he led us. If he refuses to go further with us, seeing pitfalls and dangers in such a path and being unwilling to brave them, we can quite understand his motives and can only admire his honesty. As far as we traveled with him, it was smooth sailing. We were well aware of the things we were discussing; there was no mistaking them. The facts were all within our experience, nothing in them contradicted our experience, and we were not asked to believe anything on faith by appealing to intuition or authority. When reason failed, we were not referred to <term>Šruti</term>; when <term>Šruti</term> failed, we were not referred to someone’s individual yogic experience; and when all these failed, no verbal trickery was employed. Nothing was made to appear grand by turning it into a mystery. Our meaning is quite unmistakable, and we use plain language; if it is not plain enough, we shall try to make it even more so.

We found, accordingly, that our present experiences and the facts of cognition resolve themselves into two sets of facts—two grand divisions—totally distinct and yet in an inseparable relationship. We called them, respectively, mind and matter, ego and non-ego, subject and object, <term>ātmā</term> and <term>pāśā</term>, <term>chit</term> and <term>achit</term>, <term>sat</term>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between God and the world, specifically defining the nature of existence as "sat" (being) and "asat" (non-being/changing world) and exploring the "Advaita" relationship as understood in Siddhanta philosophy.</meta>

# GOD AND THE WORLD.

and *asat*. We noted their interdependence and interrelation. Regarding the nature of the relation itself, it was in a sense inexplicable. We could say positively that the relation is not one of causation or succession, nor merely an order in place. It could not be that of the whole to its part, nor one acting on the other, or using the other as its instrument, nor that of container and contained, nor a total lack of relation; we could not picture this relation in any of the modes known to us in our actual experience. The only analogy available to us in nature—namely, that of vowels and consonants—helped us a great deal in gaining some idea of this relation. It is not one, it is not two, and our Āchārya asks us to remain quiet: “ஏகமிரண் டென் னாமற் சும்மாவிரு” <note>Tamil: "Without saying one or two, stay still."</note>. 

But still, even this position requires a name; and for want of a better term, we use the word ‘Advaita’ for such a relation. The word Advaitam implies the existence of two things and does not negate the reality or the existence of one of the two. It simply postulates a relation between these two. The relation is one in which an identity is perceived, and a difference in substance is also felt. It is this relation which could not easily be expressed in words, but which perhaps may be conceived and is seen as two (*Dvaitam*) and at the same time as not two (*Na Dvaitam*); it is this relation which is called Advaitam (a unity or identity in duality). The philosophy which postulates such a relation is called the Advaita Philosophy; and because it is the highest truth, it is also called the *Siddhānta* (The true end). 

This view, therefore, must be distinguished from the monism of the materialist and idealist, and from the dualism of Dr. Reid and Hamilton. However, Dr. Bain and others of his school would regard themselves as monists; but in that case, the distinction between this monism—which we might call qualified monism—and the monism of writers before the advent of the present agnostic school must be carefully observed. There is no harm in using any name for anything, but when particular associations have already been established, it serves no purpose except to confound and confuse to use old words with new meanings introduced into

<sig>9</sig>

<summary>This page defines the "Advaita" relationship in Siddhanta philosophy as a unique "unity in duality" that is neither strictly one nor two, using the analogy of vowels and consonants. It distinguishes this perspective from Western monism and dualism, emphasizing that Advaita does not negate the existence of either entity but describes their inexplicable connection.</summary>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>GOD AND THE WORLD.</header>

them. In a sense, this view is also the true monistic view. Say from the individual standpoint, when a person is in a purely objective condition, his mind becomes merged in the body; the mind identifies itself thoroughly with the body and is not conscious of its own distinction from the body. By this process of merger and complete identification, the apparent existence is only one—that of the object. When the mind is free from all consciousness of objects, the objective world vanishes, as it were, and there is only one fact present, and that is the mind, and nothing else. Without the mind, however, nothing else can subsist, and when the mind is in its own place, nothing else is *seen* to subsist. And how appropriate the interpretation of that often-quoted and often-abused Vedic text, ‘*Ekam evādvitīyam Brahma*’ <note>Sanskrit: "One only, without a second, is Brahman."</note> by Saint Meykaṇḍān seems now! When we arrive at the postulate of God, we arrive at the third <term>padārtha</term> <note>category or substance</note>, and nobody has yet been found to postulate an existence higher than these three. And these constitute the <term>tri-padārtha</term> <note>the three categories: God, Soul, and Matter</note> of most of the Hindu schools. They differ, no doubt, in the definition and description of these three entities, as also in the description of their relationships. This third postulate could not be arrived at by direct perception, observation, and experiment. We think, however, it can be proved by strict logical methods—by such proof as is possible—and we are at liberty to postulate it to explain the remaining facts left unexplained by the Materialists and Idealists. If this postulate will explain facts left unexplained by these people, and if it will not contradict any of the facts of human nature and probabilities, there is no harm in having it as a workable hypothesis. We believe also that the Materialists and Idealists leave many facts unexplained and that this third postulate is necessary to explain these facts. We, however, do not propose to go into this wide question now. We only propose to discuss God’s relation to the mind (soul) and matter just at present. And the relation we postulate is the same as between the mind and body which we have already proposed, and we call it by the same name ‘*advaita*’. And the couplet we have quoted from

Tāyumānavar conveys the idea most beautifully, and the credit for explaining this beautiful view of '*advaita*' must in the first place be given to *Saint Meykaṇḍān*, whom *Saint Tāyumānavar* himself praises as பொய்கண்டார் காணாப்புனித மாமத்துவித மெய்கண்டான், "The holy Seer of *Advaita* Truth, whom those who see only the false cannot perceive." God is related to the soul as the soul is related to the world. God is the pure subject, the Pure Ego, and the soul is the pure object, the non-ego. God is *Sat* (true existence); the soul is *Asat*. However, because we have already called the world *Asat*, we are reluctant to apply the same term to the soul; besides, the soul occupies a unique position between God and *Sat* on one hand, and the world and *Asat* on the other. Therefore, the term *Satasat* has been applied to it. This term means that which is neither God nor the world (*māyā*), but which, when joined to either, becomes completely identified with it. When united to the body, it is completely identified with the body, and when united to God, it is completely identified with God. We have already observed that when the soul is united to the body, it is completely identified with it; the soul has not ceased to exist just as the body ceased to be when the soul was in its own plane. The very existence of the body implied the existence of the soul, even though, for the time being, the soul was not conscious of its own separateness, individuality, and distinction from the object or body.

Just in the same way, when the *Jiva* <note>individual soul</note> is in the highest union with *Śivam* <note>God</note>, the *Jiva* is not conscious of its separateness, individuality, and distinction from God. If this consciousness were present, there would be no union; and if the soul itself were not present, speaking of union in *Moksha* <note>liberation</note>, *Anubhava* <note>experience</note>, and *Ānanda* <note>bliss</note> would be using language without meaning. This characteristic of the soul is very unique. It is named சார்ந்ததன்வண்ண மாதல் or அது அது வாதல், "becoming one with that to which it is attached." The Hindu Idealists try to arrive at the postulate of the soul by precisely the same mode of proof as is provided in sutras 3 and 4 of *Śivajñānabodham*. Arriving at this postulate, which is found to be above the 24 <term>tattvas</term> <note>principles of nature</note>, above the elements, above the <term>tanmātras</term> <note>subtle elements</note>, above the <term>Jñāna</term> <note>knowledge-related organs</note> and <term>Karmendriyas</term> <note>action-related organs</note>, above

<summary>This page defines the soul as 'Satasat,' a unique entity that identifies completely with whatever it attaches to, whether the material body or God. It explains that in the state of liberation (Moksha), the soul remains present to experience bliss even as it loses its individual consciousness in union with the Divine.</summary>

All the absurdities of the <term>Māyāvāda</term> school are clearly traceable to its failure to understand the nature of the soul correctly. He is not different from the soul, pervading its understanding entirely. The soul so feeling itself is also <term>Śivam</term>.”

Chapter II of *Light of Grace* must also be read in this connection; and Saint Umāpati Śivāchārya asks a question to highlight the importance of this great characteristic of the soul. “Are there not objects in this world which become dark in darkness and light in light?” he asks, and the answer given by himself elsewhere is, “the eye, the mirror, and <term>ākāś</term> <note>ether or space</note> are such objects.” The eye loses its power of seeing in darkness and recovers it in light; and the others become dark or bright as darkness or light surrounds them. Saint Tāyumānavar also refers to this peculiarity in several places and calls the soul யாதொன்று பற்றின தனியல்பாய் நின்று பந்தமறும்பளிங்கனை யசிந்து நீ. “You who are like the mirror or crystal from which dust has been removed, becoming of the very same nature as that to which [you are attached]...”

it is joined.' Here, the Light is God, darkness is <term>Māyā</term>, and the Mirror, Eye, or <term>Ākāś</term> is the soul. We all feel that there is a sentience that undergoes this change from light to darkness. If this sentience is identified with God Himself, then surely the change must fall upon Him. We have not yet been able to understand (of course, we are ready to confess that we do not belong to the superior class of mortals said to possess 'the sharpest intellects, a bold understanding'—ranks to which our brother of the *Brahmavādin* elevates himself; see page 749 of the current volume) how, when they postulate only one <term>padārtha</term> (substance), one self, and no <term>Jīva</term> (individual soul), God can be saved from all the impurity, sins, and ignorance present in nature. To say that the <term>Śruti</term> says God cannot be tainted by such contact is merely begging the question and is no answer at all. To assert that the Infinite God, through this false imposition of <term>Avidyā</term>, had become divided into millions and millions of finite beings—without proving this statement except by an analogy (which is found to fail most miserably in its most important details)—and then to assert in the same breath that this subdivision is false, a mere myth or a dream, and that there is no universe, no men or Gods, no "you" or "I"; and then to further say that you and I, Gods and men, and the world are all God, seems to be the height of absurdity and not the result of "the sharpest intellect" or "a bold understanding." If that is the case, we must have altogether different definitions for these terms. We will close this paper by quoting two verses from Saint Tirumūlar, and we challenge comparison with them against anything else found in any writing, ancient or modern, to express the truth of the double aspect and relation we have been describing above with the same aptness and richness of illustration.

The wood was concealed in the mad elephant;

The Supreme was concealed in the world;

<note>Here "wood" (rendered as "tree" in the original) refers to a wooden toy elephant.</note>

The gold was concealed in the golden ornament;
The gold concealed the golden ornament.
The ‘I’ was concealed in its own senses;
In the ‘I,’ its own senses were concealed.

These two verses, though they look similar, are not the same, and we will explain their meaning in our next article.

# THE TWO GEMS.

## (SAT AND SAT-ASAT).

---

->“ எவ்வெவர் தன்மையும் தன்வயிற்படுத்தும்<-
->தானேயாகிய தயாபரன் எம்மிறை.”<-

->“To each and every one, His own nature imparting,<-
->Our Lord stands alone, Supreme, full of Grace.”<-
->*Tiruvāchakam.*<-

We proceed to explain the two verses quoted from Tirumūlar at the close of our last article. The two verses seem so alike that unless they are looked into more closely, their meaning is likely to be lost. These verses explain, in fact, the *Bhanda* (bondage) and *Moksha* (liberation) conditions of the soul, and the soul's ascent through various stages called *Tattva Darśanam*, *Ātma Darśanam*, and *Šiva* or *Parā Darśanam*. The verse, "The gold was concealed in the golden ornament, etc." must be considered first. The object before us is a golden ornament. The thing can be looked at from two different points of view, in two different aspects. It can be viewed as merely gold, and then we are solely engaged in looking at its color, its fineness, specific gravity, and so on; while we are so engaged, the other view of it—whether it is a brooch, a medal, or a bracelet—is altogether lost to view. In the same manner, when we are viewing the object as a mere ornament, then all idea of the gold, its fineness, and so forth, is lost. This happens even when the object before us is one and the same; neither the gold as gold, nor the ornament as an ornament, can be said to be non-existent. In either case, they cannot be said to be unreal or a mere delusion. We merely change our point of view, and we ourselves are under no delusion at either moment. The delusion is neither in the gold, nor in the ornament, nor in ourselves. The object before us is so

<summary>The author explains Saint Tirumūlar's verses on the soul's states of bondage and liberation, using the analogy of a golden ornament to illustrate how perception shifts between substance and form without either being a delusion.</summary>
<keywords>Tirumūlar, Tiruvāchakam, Bhanda, Moksha, Tattva Darśanam, Ātma Darśanam, gold analogy, perception, Saivism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of Saint Tirumūlar’s verses, specifically focusing on the relationship between the individual soul and the objective world through the analogy of gold and jewelry.</meta>

# THE TWO GEMS.

is made such that it possesses this double nature or aspect, so to speak, and our own psychological structure is such that we can change from one point of view to the other. Each point of view has its own vantage ground. A person going to a jeweler’s shop cannot afford to lose sight of either point; if he does, he is sure to make a bad bargain. What would we think of this man if he entered the shop with the firm idea that, regarding the jewel he is going to buy, the gold is a mere name and delusion, or the ornament is a mere name and delusion? When bargaining, however, once he has tested the fineness and color of the gold, he need worry no more about it, and he can proceed to examine the shape, size, and other qualities of the ornament.

Taking this analogy, Tirumūlar proceeds to point out the same relation between the individual ego (the subject) and its objective senses. The word used is <term>தன்</term> (*than*), standing as it does for the individual ego, <term>jiva</term>, soul, <term>paśu</term>, or <term>chit</term>. The phrase <term>தன் கரணங்களால்</term> (*than karanangalāl*) also brings out the meaning of <term>தன்</term>, and it cannot refer to the Supreme <term>Brahman</term>, as was interpreted by a Hindu Idealist. Of course, he could not help saying so, as the being which he postulates above "its senses" (<term>தன்கரணங்கள்</term>) is God, the Supreme. Saint Tirumūlar was prophetic enough to foresee such a misinterpretation of his words; that is why he sang the next verse, <gloss>“மரத்தை மறைத்தது”</gloss> ("the tree was concealed," etc.). In our article on "Mind and Body," we have fully discussed the relation which Saint Tirumūlar perceives between the individual ego (the soul) and its body and senses. When the individual <term>paśu</term> lives a purely objective existence—by caring for his body, his comforts, his wealth, his pleasures, and so on—his true self (the mind) is altogether identified with the world, and he himself lies buried and concealed. Look at the words our Saint has selected. He does not cry "false, false" or "delusion, delusion" at every turn. He actually uses <term>மறைத்தது</term> (*maraittathu*) and <term>மறைந்தது</term> (*marainthathu*), meaning "concealed" and "is concealed"! Neither the soul nor the world is a myth or a delusion; rather, when the mind was in an objective condition, it was concealed by the object. When the

<summary>The author uses the analogy of a gold ornament to explain that neither the soul nor the physical world are delusions, but rather that one can "conceal" the other depending on one's perspective. He critiques idealist interpretations, asserting that Saint Tirumūlar's use of the word "concealed" proves the reality of both the individual ego and the objective world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion regarding the relationship between the individual soul and the objective world, moving from the analogy of gold and jewelry to the definitions of the soul’s bound and liberated states.</meta>

# THE TWO GEMS.

The soul regains its own self by forgetting the world; the world has not become a myth, it only lies concealed, merged in the soul itself. The thoughtless critic is likely to consider such distinctions as mere wordplay, but no student of philosophy can easily afford to ignore the first principles of correct reasoning—choosing words so that each one expresses one particular idea and no other. Many a specious and delusive argument has begun in such an ignorant and ambiguous use of words. 

To proceed: when the soul lies so concealed in the world, this constitutes its <term>bandha</term> (bound condition), and the thing doing the concealing is called <term>bandha</term> or <term>Pāša</term>. When the soul learns to discriminate between its own nature and the nature of the world, and to value the lower world at its true worth, then it attains <term>Tattva Daršanam</term> and <term>Ātma Daršanam</term>. And the whole field of ethics evolves from our correct perception of these relations. When a person perceives that the more they are attached to the world, the more their own faculties become clouded and they are led into more sorrow and suffering, and the more they free themselves from such attachment, the more they free themselves from sin and sorrow and develop their higher spiritual nature—it is then that their moral faculties are developed and, in time, strengthened by constant practice. 

But then, there is this peculiarity about the human mind which is not noticed in any other system we know of, and which we have already referred to in our last article: its intermediate nature between <term>Sat</term> and <term>Asat</term>. This gives it the name <term>Satasat</term>, a peculiarity that <term>Kaṇṇudaiya Vaḷḷalār</term> (author of <term>Oḷiviloḍukkam</term>) emphasizes by using the expressive name of அல, or hermaphrodite—neither male nor female, neither <term>Sat</term> nor <term>Asat</term>. But the rule in Tamil grammar for determining the sex of the hermaphrodite is “ஆணுறுப்புமிகுந்தால் ஆணாகும், பெண்ணுறுப்புமிகுந்தால் பெண்ணாகும்.” “The sex follows the more predominant organs present.” And so, a hermaphrodite person will always be called either "he" or "she" and not "it." The life of the individual soul is, as such, passed as either <term>Asat</term> or as <term>Sat</term>, and it has no life of

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul's nature, focusing on its dependency on external supports—either the world or God—illustrated through analogies of a ripening fruit and a rushing flood.</meta>

# THE TWO GEMS.

...its own. That is, it cannot exist by itself, independent of its relation with either <term>Padārtha</term>. If either God or the world did not exist, the existence of the soul would be an impossibility. Saint <term>Meykaṇḍān</term> uses two analogies to illustrate this position. The soul is compared to an object suspended in air, and to a flood of water. We cannot imagine an object suspended in air without a support. If the support is removed, the object falls to the ground. Saint <term>Meykaṇḍān</term> thus had distinctly before him the question, "why does an apple fall to the ground?" The actual example he had before him was a swing attached by a rope to a tree. The tree holds up the object by its own force. When this force is weakened and loosened, another force is brought into place: the force of the earth, gravity. The object was, in fact, held between these two forces. The object must either be attached to the tree or to the earth. In spite of the enormous power of gravitation exerted by the earth, the tree was able to hold up the object for a time. Only for a time; for when the fruit matures, the tree cannot hold it up, however much it may desire to do so. The same act accomplishes the severance from the tree and the bringing of it to the earth. 

Just so, in the case of the soul: it is bound to <term>Māyā</term> and <term>Mala</term> as long as the soul is not ripe. Before it is ripe, we do not perceive its brightness and sweetness. When the soul perfects itself, fed by the juices from the earth (the Grace of God), it finds its resting place in God. When it so finds itself united, it becomes one with God, just as the fruit itself, when left alone, becomes one with the earth. The flood, again, cannot stand still unless it is held up by an embankment. When this embankment is breached, it will run on until it finds its resting place in the broad arms of the ocean. Without either of these means of support, it will be difficult to restrain the fleeting soul. The embankment or the floodgates are the <term>Māyā</term> support of the soul. The ocean is God. This support is called in Tamil பற்று—a support, a bond of attachment, a rest, desire, love. It is this peculiarity which Saint <term>Tiruvaḷḷuvar</term> expresses in the following couplet,

<vocab>Padārtha, Meykaṇḍān, Māyā, Mala, Tiruvaḷḷuvar, Tamil, soul, God, gravity, analogies, பற்று</vocab>

<summary>The author explains that the soul cannot exist independently but must be attached to either the material world or God, using analogies of gravity and water to describe spiritual maturation and liberation. This essential attachment is identified by the Tamil term "patru."</summary>

->பற்றுக பற்றற்றான் பற்றினை , யப்பற்றினைப்<-
->பற்றுக , பற்றுவிடற்கு ,<-

which again is the mere echo of our Saint <term>Māṇikkavāchakar</term>'s words:

->" சுற்றிய சுற்றித் தொடர் வறுப்பான்றொல் புகழே<-
->பற்றி யிப் பாசத்தைப் பற்ற நாப்பற்றவான்<-
->பற்றிய பேரானந்தம் பாடுதும் காண்மானாய்."<-

This peculiarity of the soul that we have been discussing above has a tremendous bearing on various philosophical schools. The ancient <term>Buddha</term> and the modern <term>Agnostic</term> would not propose this other support and resting place for the soul. And we find that they accordingly end up in <term>Nihilism</term>. The moment of perfection is the moment of annihilation for the Buddhist. Indeed, according to his modern apostles—Mrs. <term>Annie Besant</term>, for example—the cry of the <term>Vedas</term>, "from which there is no return, there is no return," is merely a hollow cry. There is no such thing as final perfection, beatitude, or <term>Moksha</term>. The soul must continue forever, subject to the never-ceasing and ever-recurring evolution due to "the moral necessity connected with the central and most precious doctrine of the exoteric <term>Vedānta</term>, the doctrine of <term>Saṃsāra</term>." Here, of course, we see the phenomenon of extremes meeting. The <term>Vedāntist</term> could not deny the possibility of the soul, after attaining so-called <term>Moksha</term>, returning to the cycle of evolution, since the original descent of <term>Brahman</term> into gods and men, brutes and worms, is not explainable by him. The Agnostic, not believing in God, examines the nature of the mind or soul; perceiving how intimately it is connected with matter, he naturally denies its separate personality and independent existence. Hence, he denies the soul’s immortality and future existence once its mortal coil is broken. In the case of the <term>Vedāntist</term>, however, this peculiarity of the soul alone provides the justification for his theory. And we have heard honest <term>Vedāntists</term> admit this as the only explanation for <term>Śrī Śaṅkara</term>'s otherwise untenable position. When in union with God, the

<summary>The author argues that the soul requires a spiritual support (God) to avoid falling into Nihilism, contrasting this view with Buddhist, Agnostic, and Vedāntic theories of reincarnation and annihilation.</summary>

# THE TWO GEMS.

The soul has lost not merely the consciousness of the world, the <term>Asat</term>; it also loses its self-consciousness (not, it should be noted, its self-being). It also loses its consciousness of difference from God, etc., and the only perception that remains is the bare perception—the bare enjoyment of God. The full manifestation and presence of God, as Love and Bliss, alone is felt; and in such a condition, <term>Śaṅkara</term> could say there is no second thing.

->சென்று சென்றணுவாய்த் தேய்ந்து<-
->தேய்ந்தொன்றாம்<-

<term>Śaṅkara</term>’s experience will therefore be only a one-sided one, and the statement cannot stand as a matter of proof. The state of union with God is called <term>Turiya</term> or <term>Para-Avasta</term>, and in this condition, though the conscious perception of the world and soul may not be possible, *be-ness* (existence) is not gone. Saint <term>Tirumūlar</term> expounds this condition in his next verse.

->மரத்தை மறைத்தது மாமதையானை,<-
->மரத்தின் மறைந்தது மாமதையானை,<-
->பரத்தை மறைத்தது பார்முதற்பூதம்,<-
->பரத்தை மறைந்தது பார்முதற்பூதம்.<-

->The tree was concealed in the mad elephant;<-
->The tree concealed the mad elephant.<-
->The world concealed the Supreme,<-
->In the Supreme was concealed the world.<-

The Supreme is concealed in the world (not non-existent); the world is concealed in the Supreme (not non-existent). In the sentences, "I was concealed by the world, the world was concealed in me," note the fact that there are only two names, two categories involved: namely, "I" and "the world"—soul and <term>māyā</term>. For an intelligent understanding of the proposition, no other category is required. But consider well the propositions, "the world concealed the Supreme, the world is concealed by the Supreme." These propositions could not be true as they stand, unless both of these stand as objective to the observer, as in the illustration of the wooden toy

itself. The wood of the toy cannot be conscious of being concealed or not by the elephant form, nor can the elephant be conscious of the wood. In human language, expression, and argument, there is always an ellipsis and the suppression of the middle term. The first two propositions relating to the wooden toy cannot be true as they stand but are only intelligible when we supply the factor of the observer. So also, the propositions that follow, though they only contain the two categories <term>Sat</term> and <term>Asat</term>, involve the presence of a third, the <term>Satasat</term>. What we have stated above will explain the <term>Sivajnanabotha Sutra</term>,

<margin>* Paul Deussen's *Elements of Metaphysics*, p. 334.

That we are concealed by our <term>Maya</term> covering is a fact; the sharpest intellect and the boldest understanding cannot get over it, however it may quibble and juggle. This being a fact, "we are here in ignorance, sin, and misery, and we know the way out of them, but the question of a cause for them is senseless." * For nothing can be more senseless than to ask for an explanation when the fact to be explained is itself an ultimate fact. An ounce of fact outweighs a pound of probabilities, say the lawyers; and they only express a logical truth. But the proposition advanced by the <term>Purvapakshi</term> is that the <term>jiva</term>, being neither a part, nor a different thing, nor a variation of <term>Brahman</term>, must be the <term>Paramatman</term> fully and totally himself. As such, he is clothed with such attributes as all-pervadingness, eternity, almightiness, and exemption from time, space, and causality; and this <term>jiva</term> is hidden by the world † (<term>maya</term> and <term>avidya</term>) as fire is in wood (or as Saint <term>Tirumular</term> would put it, wood in the elephant). He then asks, what is the cause of this concealment? Why should the perfect become deluded into the imperfect by <term>avidya</term> and ignorance? Dr. <term>Paul Deussen</term> admits that here all philosophers

We say "the same" as well, regardless of what the learned Doctor’s understanding of Kant might be. The whole question is inadmissible; indeed, the entire proposition of the <term>Pūrvapakshi</term> on which this question is based is inadmissible—it is untrue and is not a fact. It is not a fact that the Supreme <term>Brahman</term> is concealed by <term>Māyā</term> and <term>Avidyā</term>. Dr. Deussen would put his unfortunate <term>Brahman</term> into the dock and accuse him of high crimes and misdemeanors (our friends are never conscious of what gross blasphemy they are guilty of—it pains us greatly that we should even write this for the sake of argument); and before proving the guilt with which he charges him, he would indulge in irrelevant and irreverent talk as to why and for what reason this <term>Brahman</term> committed these crimes. 

Any ordinary judge would rule his talk as senseless; likewise, such talk from the accused's counsel—kindly appointed by the state—would be ruled as senseless when the accused admits the charge and there is, besides, overwhelming testimony as to his guilt, leaving no room for doubt. The case envisioned by the learned Doctor finds a parallel in those occasional instances of judicial murder <note>A legal term for the execution of an innocent person through the due process of law.</note>. A great crime has been committed, there is a great outcry, and someone ought to be punished and suffer for the unknown criminal. The police chase down someone they have long known, an old offender; witnesses (pseudo-<term>jñānis</term>, with their <term>Svānubhūti</term> and esoteric experience) flock in overwhelming numbers only to prove the prisoner's guilt. The weight of testimony is crushing; the poor prisoner at the bar is simply dumbfounded and cannot find the words to clear himself, however innocent he might be, and his silence is taken as a confession, and he is condemned to die. 

Before his bones are whitened, however, the real criminal turns up and confesses his crime, and the first conviction is found, after all, to be based on a case of mistaken identity. We have already shown how liable the soul is to be mistaken for God, or to mistake itself for God. Saint Meykanḍān, even where he teaches the

The text initiates one into the practice of <term>Sohambāvana</term> and cautions him both before and after not to mistake himself for God.

‘யாவையும் சூனியம் சத்தெதிர்.’ "In the Presence of the <term>Sat</term>, all else is <term>Śūnyam</term>." Why? Our Saint answers that "before the Perfect and Eternal Intelligence (Truth), the imperfect and acquired intelligence (the appearance) is stripped of its light." He illustrates this by saying that the evil <term>Asat</term> ceases to exist before Him, just as darkness vanishes before the sun. He explains that <term>Hara</term> cannot perceive them as objects, as nothing exists outside of Him. How well this explanation fits with the Vedic text: "Neither the sun, nor the moon, nor the stars shine there, nor these lightnings, much less this earthly fire. After Him, the Shining One, all things shine; by His light, this whole world is lit."* And when even the suns and moons pale before this Shining One, they still dare to assert that darkness, <term>māyā</term>, and <term>avidyā</term> can dare to lift their heads to veil, conceal, and dim His brightness. They claim that because of this veiling, the Shining One can become deluded and imagine Himself to be <term>Asat</term>—this body, these senses, and this world. The <term>Siddhānti</term> rightly asks: can you show me a sun covered by darkness, that I might believe in a <term>Brahman</term> veiled by <term>Māyā</term> or <term>Upādhi</term>?† No doubt the blind man says the sun is hidden by darkness; he will not admit his own blindness and darkness, but instead attributes his infirmity to the Radiant Sun. "After Him, all things shine; by His light, this whole world is lit." Yes, O Lord, we are but broken lights of You. The

->எவ்வெவர் தன்மையும் தன்வயிற் படுத்தும்<-

The *diamond crystal* (சார்ந்ததன் வண்ணமாகும்) and the gem *emerald* (சார்ந்ததுதன் வண்ணமாக்கும்)—these are the symbols used by the <term>Siddhāntis</term> for the Soul and <term>Śivam</term>. Students of science know the structural difference between those two bodies as mediums or distributors of light. This divine light is <term>Umā</term> (literally wisdom or light), that wondrously fair lady who showed her royal consort to the astonished immortals. Her color is green emerald, and we will close this article by invoking her aid and quoting this passage from <term>Kumara-gurupara</term>, which is poetic and philosophic at the same time.

->பண்ணுறு கிளியொழிப் பாவைநின் திருமேனி<-

# SOME ASPECTS OF THE GOD-HEAD.

> “ பெண்ண  ணவியெனும் பெற்றியன் காண்க. ”
> “ Behold! He is the male, the female and the neuter. ”
->Tiruvāchakam.<-

> “ Śivaṁ śāntam, advaitam chaturthaṁ manyante ”.
->Rāmatapinī Upanishat.<-

It often happens that we have to write about the same subject over and over again, and no one should wonder why this should be so. We eat the same kind of rice and dish of vegetables over and over again, day after day, from one year’s end to the next, and yet we never ask why this should be so. The answer is plain: this is the best, safest, and most wholesome food we require every day of our lives for its sustenance, nurture, and growth. What applies to the body applies to the mind as well. The mind also requires some wholesome, safe, and healthy <term>pabulum</term> to feed upon every day—no, every hour. You can starve the mind just as you can overfeed it, and you may feed it on unwholesome and unhealthy food; these are irregularities which we should avoid, just as we should avoid irregularities in diet. Wholesome food, however often we may repeat it, ought not to tire anyone. And this is necessary for another reason also. A person is influenced more or less by their environment; all sorts of influences are brought to bear on them, and these create doubts and misgivings even in the most well-regulated minds; the mind wavers from one extreme to the other. It is therefore good that the mind is made to face the same truth continually. And then, indeed, our memory is so weak that we forget what we learned only yesterday; and what fails to strike our imagination at

# SOME ASPECTS OF THE GOD-HEAD.

one time may attract it another time. Furthermore, errors and fallacies are repeated day after day, and it becomes necessary to repeat what we regard as truths just as often. As such, we make no further apology for going fully into a subject which we touched upon in our review of “the Minor Upanishats,” in our introduction to the Kaivalya Upanishat, and in our article on the “Personality of God.” Very often, a controversy is carried on by means of names and words, and the entire fallacy lies in the different parties to the controversy understanding the word in many different ways. We have seen how European writers differed in defining the words “*Personal*” and “Impersonal”; and we have accepted the word “*Personal*” free from all implication of limitation or anthropomorphism and in the manner defined by Emerson, Lotze, Dr. Iverach, etc. We have also noted the different ways of interpreting the words *<term>Saguṇa</term>* and *<term>Nirguṇa</term>*. One person calls God *<term>Saguṇa</term>*, and interprets *<term>Nirguṇa</term>* in undoubtedly authoritative passages as meaning merely “*devoid of bad qualities*.” In this sense *<term>Saguṇa</term>* must mean *full of bad qualities*; and yet this person will only call his God *<term>Saguṇa</term>* and not *<term>Nirguṇa</term>*; and he exhibits a clear prejudice against the word “*<term>Nirguṇa</term>*,” thus clearly showing that his interpretation is, after all, only a doubtful tactic for avoiding an inconvenient corner. We have, however, referred to its technical, original, and philosophical meaning, in that *<term>Guṇa</term>* means the *<term>Guṇa tattva</term>* which is the name and characteristic of *<term>Mūlaprakriti</term>*; and this *<term>Guṇa</term>* comprises the three *<term>Guṇas</term>*: *<term>Sattva</term>*, *<term>Rajas</term>*, and *<term>Tamas</term>*. *<term>Saguṇa</term>* accordingly means being clothed with *<term>Sattva</term>*, *<term>Rajas</term>*, and *<term>Tamas</term>*—gross material qualities—and *<term>Nirguṇa</term>* means freedom from these three qualities or gross material veils. Therefore, the definition of God as *<term>Nirguṇa</term>*, and not as *<term>Saguṇa</term>*, does not conflict with the literal and consistent meaning of the two words or our concept of God’s Supreme Nature. Incidentally, additional proof that our interpretation is correct is provided by the fact that the *<term>Saguṇa</term>* philosopher actually clothes his God with *<term>Sattva-Guṇa</term>*. # SOME ASPECTS OF THE GOD-HEAD.

Comparisons are distasteful, but where principles are at stake they cannot be avoided altogether; and we merely invite our readers' attention to the two descriptions of the Godhead, given in the appendix to Dr. Muir's "Metrical Translations from Sanskrit Writers," which are summarized respectively from the *Śvetāśvatara Upanishat* and *Uttara-Rāmāyaṇa*. You may omit the names, for they are incidental details due to our ancient religious history, and you may give the bare descriptions to our artist; we have no doubt he will draw two totally different pictures. No doubt, we admit their <term>Saguṇa</term> (with attributes) conception of God, and as for that, any <term>bhāvanā</term> (mental visualization) of God serves the purpose of the seeker after a higher path to a great extent, on the well-known principle laid down by *St. Meykaṇḍān*: "Choose the form which attracts your love most." But as we have pointed out already, we do not remember at times that this is only a form, a symbol, and not the truth itself—that truth is beyond one's ordinary perception ("கண்முதற் புலனாற் காட்சியுமில்லோன்—உள்ளத்துணர்ச்சியிற் கொள்ளவும்படன்."), and yet this vision is possible ("கண்ணால் யானும் கண்டேன்"), ("புறையற்றிருந்தான் புரிசடையோனே") when, leaving our feeling of 'I' and 'Mine', and destroying and annihilating our <term>Paśu</term> (soul) and <term>Pāśa</term> (bondage) nature ("ஊன் கெட்டு உயிர் கெட்டு—நான் கெட்டு") and assisted by His Supreme Grace ("அவனருளால்"), we reach the place of peace, <term>Nirvāṇa</term> (literally, "non-flowing-as-air") (திரையற்ற நீர்போற் சிந்தை தெளிவாற்கு).

What we, therefore, wish to establish and emphasize to our readers here is that, whatever names we may use ("ஒருநாமம் ஒருருவம் ஒன்றுமில்லாற்கு, ஆயிரம் திருநாமம்பாடி நாம் தெள்ளேணம் கொட்டாமோ"—"Let us sing the thousand names of the One who has no name, no form, nothing"), and though we may accept this form and that symbol for worship and practice (<term>Sādhana</term>), yet we hold rigidly to the principle that God is not man, covered by ignorance and matter, and God cannot be born as man, nor clothed with <term>Prakṛiti</term> (material) qualities. The rigid acceptance of this one principle alone, that God is <term>Aja</term> (unborn),

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Godhead, emphasizing that God's unborn nature is a central tenet of the Siddhānta, distinguishing it from other religious traditions and preventing it from falling into idolatry.</meta>

# SOME ASPECTS OF THE GOD-HEAD.

The fact that God **cannot be born** should distinguish and elevate the <term>Siddhānta</term> from all other forms of religion. The rigid acceptance of this single principle alone must prevent it from degenerating into superstition, base idolatry, and the worship of men or fetishes. One great obstacle to the proper recognition of the excellence of the <term>Siddhānta</term> is the difficulty posed by certain names. We use specific names to denote God and to encompass the characteristic attributes we assign to Him. But how can we avoid it? We cannot forget our language and its past traditions; we cannot forget our religious history, no matter how hard we might try; and we therefore cannot coin new names simply because others want us to do so. And what need is there for doing so? If we use certain names, they have been used by ninety percent of the Indian population for at least the last thirty centuries; they were used in the days of the <term>Purāṇas</term> and <term>Itihāsas</term>, they were used in the days of the <term>Upanishad</term> writers, and they were used in the days of the Vedic writers. Some of these <term>Mantras</term> and texts have been used in the daily prayers of everyone. The publishers of *The Theosophy of the Upanishads* recommend the following <term>mantra</term> from the <term>Taittirīya Upanishad</term> for our daily prayer:

> “Satyam Jñānam Anantam Brahma Ānandarūpam Amṛitam yad vibhūti Śāntam Śivam, Advaitam.”

And what is sectarian about the word “Śivam” here? Even an <term>Upanishad</term> of the <term>Rāmatāpinī</term> type contains this text with the same word (quoting, of course, the <term>Mantra</term> mentioned above):

> “Śivam, Śāntam, Advaitam, Chaturtham manyante”.

There is one thing to note about the word “Śivam.” Sanskrit scholars say that the word in this form is not a neuter noun but simply an adjective, and they accordingly translate it as *gracious, benignant, etc.*; but it is remarkable that this word is always used in the <term>Ṛig-Veda</term> and other Vedas and <term>Upanishads</term> alongside the words <term>Rudra</term>, <term>Śaṅkara</term>,

<summary>This page argues that the Siddhānta's belief in an unborn God prevents it from becoming a base superstition and defends the use of traditional names like "Śivam" as being rooted in ancient, non-sectarian Vedic and Upanishadic traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Siddhānta, Upanishads, Śivam, Mantra, Vedic writers, religious nomenclature, idolatry, Sanskrit philology, Rudra, Śaṅkara</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the various names of the Godhead, specifically addressing the sectarian or universal nature of terms like "Śivam" and their appearance in sacred texts like the Upanishads.</meta>

# SOME ASPECTS OF THE GOD-HEAD.

Bhava, etc., and these denote the same personality and no other. However this may be, the word (சிவம்) <term>Śivam</term> is clearly used in Tamil as the neuter form of <term>Śiva</term> or <term>Śivan</term> (சிவன்), just as <term>Param</term> (பரம்) is the neuter of <term>Para</term> or <term>Paran</term> (பரன்), and <term>Brahmam</term> (பிரமம்) is the neuter of <term>Brahman</term> (பிரமன்), with no change of meaning in either form. We have already pointed out that this accounts for the frequent change from one gender to another when describing the Supreme Being—even within the same <term>Mantra</term>, as in the <term>Śvetāśvatara</term>. We have also pointed out elsewhere that all these names can also be declined in the feminine gender without a change in meaning. Whether we say <term>Śiva</term>, <term>Śivam</term>, or <term>Śivā</term>; <term>Śaṅkara</term>, <term>Śaṅkaram</term>, or <term>Śaṅkarī</term>; <term>Para</term>, <term>Param</term>, or <term>Parā</term>; we denote the same Supreme Personality. We use these words and these gendered forms because these are all the forms or symbols we perceive in the material universe. To us, therefore, these names are merely names and nothing more; consequently, we assign no greater importance to one form over another. Though Professor Max Müller would prefer to call God "It" in the neuter and considers it a higher name, we are completely indifferent as to whether the Supreme is called *He, She*, or *It*. Accordingly, along with <term>St. Māṇikkavāchaka</term>, we praise God as:

->பெண்ணணண் அலியெனும் பெற்றியன் காண்க" *<-

> "Behold! He is the male, the female, and the neuter."

---

* And yet, consider the following lines from the same 'utterance':

> அச்சன் ஆண் பெண் அலியா காசமாகி
> யாரழலா யந்தமாயப்பால் நின்ற
> செச்சை மாமலர் புரையுமேனி யெங்கள்
> சிவபெருமான் எம்பெருமான் தேவர் கோவே."

> "My Father! He became man, woman, and hermaphrodite, the <term>Ākāś</term>, and Fire, and this final Cause; and, transcending all these forms, stands the Supreme <term>Śiva</term>, with a Body glowing like the flame of the forest. He is my Lord and the King of Gods."

> "பெண்ணாகி யாணாய லியாய்ப் பிறங்கொளிசேர்
> விண்ணாகி மண்ணாகி யித்தனையும் வேறாகி
> கண்ணார மூதாய் நின்றான்."

> "He became 'He' and 'She' and 'It,' and the Earth and Heaven, and is *different from all these* and stands as my dear Blessedness."

These lines are frequently repeated in the <term>Tiruvāchakam</term>, <term>Tevāram</term>, and every other sacred Tamil scripture. Can similar lines be cited from writers of any other school? We venture to say, no. However, the older <term>Upanishats</term> contain similar thoughts, and that only proves our argument that the <term>Siddhānta</term> school faithfully represents the oldest traditions today and is the heir to the most ancient philosophy. Among all Indian preachers, it was the late *<term>matakhaṇḍana</term>* Veńkaṭagiri Sāstrin who used to emphasize this universal aspect of the <term>Siddhānta</term> regarding naming Him as ‘He,’ ‘She,’ and ‘It.’ He used to point out that all names of <term>Śiva</term> can be declined in all three genders without a change in meaning, whereas other names do not allow this change; even if they do, the word becomes meaningless or means something else. We do not know why some people prefer the neuter form over the masculine or feminine when, in fact, it is logical that the male and female forms each represent the perfection of organized and organic form much more than neuter forms do. If by calling Him<note>The nature of the English language, reflecting Christian religion as it does, does not allow us to refer to God except in the masculine gender, though of course we have heard that they do not mean to say that God is a male like a man.</note> ‘It,’ we mean to emphasize that God is sexless, we must also insist that God is genderless, and that He cannot be spoken of only in the neuter gender. And the phrase, “அவனவளது” “<term>Stripunnapumsaka</term>,” ‘He, She, It,’ has become a technical phrase with us (see the first <term>sūtra</term> of <term>Śivajñānabodham</term>) to mean the whole of the material manifested universe and its various forms; and in naming God with words and forms borrowed from matter, we cannot avoid using these words. But then, the difference between principle and symbol, truth and dogmatism, has to be perceived. We tried to make ourselves clear about this distinction regarding the “<term>Soham</term> or <term>Tattvamasi</term>” doctrine in our last entry; and in the subject we have been elucidating above, a similar distinction has to be perceived. One says, ‘address God always as He’; and if you call Him ‘It,’ he says you are addressing a cold abstraction. Another

Probably, if this aspect of <term>Siddhānta</term> had been present to the mind of Prof. <term>Max Muller</term> when he wrote his introduction to the <term>Śvetāśvatara Upanishad</term>, in refuting the argument that the <term>Upanishad</term> was a sectarian one, he would not have needed to go to the extreme of trying to establish an illusory identity between a <term>Nirguṇa</term> and a <term>Saguṇa</term> God. For we do not, in any case, accept the <term>Saguṇa</term> God as God, the Supreme, at all; for the <term>Saguṇa</term> God is only a God in name, but a <term>Paśu</term> or Soul in reality. And here we come to a great fallacy which is the source of a very serious error. The error consists in interpreting words such as <term>Iśvara</term>, <term>Māheśvara</term>, <term>Parameśvara</term>, <term>Iśā</term>, <term>Iśāna</term>, <term>Maheśa</term>, <term>Deva</term>, <term>Mahādeva</term>, <term>Hara</term>, <term>Rudra</term>, <term>Śiva</term>, and <term>Purusha</term>, wherever they occur in the <term>Upanishads</term>, <term>Gita</term>, etc., as meaning the lower or <term>Saguṇa</term> Brahman, apparently because these names are also applied to a God who is one of the Trinity or <term>Trimurtis</term>: <term>Brahma</term>, <term>Vishnu</term>, and <term>Rudra</term>. But any ordinary student of the <term>Śaiva Siddhānta</term> will perceive that the God they worship is not one of the Trinity, although called by the same name, and that their system speaks of Him as the *Turiyam* and *Chaturtham*, both meaning "fourth," and these

# SOME ASPECTS OF THE GOD-HEAD.

Thoughts can be gathered from the most ancient and the most recent books in Tamil and in Sanskrit. The typical passage in the <term>Upanishads</term> is the one in the <term>Atharvashikha</term>.

Dhyāyeetesānam pradhyāyithavyam Sarvam idam Brahma Vishṇū Rudrèndrāste sarvè samprasùyantè sarvāni chèndriyānicha saha bhutais, nakāranam Kāranānām dhātā dhyātā Karanāhtu dhyèyas Sarvaisvarya Sampannas Sarvèsvaras Śambhurākasa madhyè............Śiva éko dhyéya : Śivankara : Śarvam annyat Parityaja Samāpta atharvasikhā.

Taking another book at random—for example, the <term>Mahimnastotra</term>, which is reputed to be written by a very ancient sage in praise of <term>Śiva</term>—we also find the following passage:

> “The mystical and immutable One which, being composed of the three letters A, U, and M, signifies, successively, the three <term>Vedas</term>, the three states of life (waking, dreaming, and sleeping), the three worlds (heaven, earth, and hell), and the three Gods (<term>Brahma</term>, <term>Vishṇu</term>, and <term>Rudra</term>), and which by its nasal sound (<term>ardhamātrā</term>) is indicative of your fourth office as Supreme Lord of All (<term>Paramešvara</term>), ever expresses and sets forth your collective and single Forms.”

And today we only propose to quote similar passages from one book, the <term>Tiruvāchakam</term>.

The first passage is the one occurring on page 26, which we have already quoted in reviewing the Minor <term>Upanishads</term>. Lest the ‘three’ in this verse be taken to mean <term>Brahma</term>, <term>Vishṇu</term>, and some God other than <term>Rudra</term>, our saint himself expressly sets forth his meaning more clearly in the following verse.

தேவர்கோ அறியாத தேவ தேவன்
செழும்பொழில்கள் பயந்துகாத்தழிக்கும் மற்றை
மூவர்கோ னாய்நின்ற முதல்வன் மூர்த்தி
மூதாதை. மாதாளும் பாகத்தெந்தை.”

As a Christian writer points out, the Doctrine of the Immanence of God in all nature is quite consistent with our idea of the Transcendence of God.

His pure flower-like feet my head seeks and glorifies.”)

> தேவரு காணச் சிவபெருமான்—மாவேறி
> வையகத்தே வந்திழிந்து வார் கழல்கள் வந்திக்க
> மெய்பக்தத்தே யின்ப மிகும்.
