# SOME ASPECTS OF THE GOD-HEAD.

> (“Lord Shiva, unknown by all the Devas,
> The Three and Thirty-three—
> He who rides the Bull—
> If we seek and praise His holy feet here,
> Our bliss will surely increase.”)

Yes, nothing can be truer than the thought expressed in this verse.

The highest conception of God that we can ever reach, describing as it does His inmost nature—and of course the only way we can know Him—is that God is Love and Blessedness, *Shivam.*<note>The word *Nandi*, a favorite word with Saint Tirumular and others, also literally means the Blissful; and our readers have to consider why we now call the Great Bull (*Pasu*) in front of God by the same name, Nandi.</note>

And such a great scientist as the late Prof. Romanes has asked with truth: “What has all the science or all the philosophy of the world done for the thought of mankind to be compared with the one doctrine ‘God is Love’?”

<summary>This page defines the highest conception of God as being Love and Blessedness (Shivam), a concept echoed by both ancient saints and modern scientists like Professor Romanes. It also explains that the name Nandi, given to the sacred bull, literally signifies this state of bliss.</summary>

<keywords>Shiva, Devas, God-head, Shivam, Prof. Romanes, Nandi, Saint Tirumular, Pasu, Divine Love, Bliss</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the God-head, specifically emphasizing the relationship between God and the world as one of soul and body, and exploring the concept of God as the "Eight-Formed" Being.</meta>

# ASHTAMUHURTAM.

-> "Antarichchanti Tamsena Rudram Promanishaya Krinanti Chikbahya Chacham. (Rig-Veda.)" <-

-> "Those who meditate with love on the Supreme Rudra who is within all, they receive nourishment." <-

It is a noteworthy fact that our sages have often compressed an entire philosophy into a single word or phrase. We previously illustrated how significant the naming of vowels and consonants as <term>uyir</term> and <term>mey</term> (or <term>Sariri</term> and <term>Sarira</term>) was regarding the question of the relationship between God and the world. Today, we take up another word that expands upon the same subject. This word is "<term>Ashta Murti</term>." It means a Being having Eight Forms and is a synonym for Shiva or Rudra. These Eight Forms are: Earth, Water, Fire, Air, Akash (Ether), the Sun, the Moon, and the Soul (also known as <term>Jiva</term> or <term>Pasu</term>).

The entire universe, both animate and inanimate, is comprised of these eight names. The only substance these terms do not include is God. Therefore, when God is spoken of as having these eight forms as His Body, the relationship between God and the world is clearly demonstrated: specifically, that of soul and body. We have, of course, fully explained this relationship in our article on "Mind and Body." As the soul within a body, He is in everything and is therefore called <term>Visvantaryami</term> (the Inner Ruler of all). We have quoted a verse from the <term>Rig</term> above in which God (Rudra) is called <term>Antaryami</term>; innumerable passages are also scattered throughout the various Upanishads. Because He has the universe as His form, God is called <term>Visvasvarupi</term>.

-> "Viśvarūpāya vi Namo Namaḥ." <-

Because He brings forth the entire universe from Himself, He is called <term>Visvakarana</term> (the cause of the universe) or <term>Visvayonih</term> (the womb of the universe). In the same way, as we

<summary>This page explains the concept of "Ashta Murti" (Eight-Formed), a name for Shiva/Rudra that describes the universe—consisting of the five elements, the sun, the moon, and the soul—as the body of God. This relationship defines God as the Inner Ruler (Visvantaryami) who is both the form of the universe and its ultimate source.</summary>

<keywords>Ashta Murti, Rudra, Shiva, Visvantaryami, Visvasvarupi, Rig-Veda, Jiva, Pasu, soul and body, five elements, Visvakarana, Antaryami</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Godhead, clarifying that while God is described through the eight forms of the universe, He remains a distinct, transcendent "Inner Ruler."</meta>

# ASHTAMUHURTAM.

Just as we often identify our own body with ourselves, God is frequently spoken of as the universe itself and is accordingly addressed as Earth, Water, Fire, Air, Sky, the Sun, the Moon, and the Soul.

But there are clear passages to show that He is none of these. No one today could seriously contend that where these <term>Upanishads</term> identify God with some of these inanimate forms, it is the earth, fire, or any of these elements—and not the Ruler within or the "Puller," as He is called in the <term>Brihadaranyaka</term>—that is really God. However, the texts identifying the <term>Jiva</term> (individual soul) with God have caused a great deal of confusion, and these texts are cited as established authorities by an entire school of Indian philosophers, even though other texts can be cited just as frequently in which God is spoken of as being different from the <term>Jiva</term>. Because He is none of these eight and transcends them all, He is called <term>Visvadika</term>.

->“<term>Visvadiko Rudra</term>,” (<term>Shvetashvatara</term>).<-

> “He who is both the source and the origin of the Gods, *the lord of all, Rudra,* the mighty seer; whoever sees the shining germ (Hiranyagarbha) come into birth—may he unite us with pure understanding.”

> “He who is the overlord of the Gods, in whom the worlds are based, who rules over his creatures of two feet and four; let us offer worship with our oblation to the God known as 'Who.'”

These follow naturally the text: “That surely is fire; That is the sun; That is the air; That is surely the moon; That is verily the Bright; That is <term>Brahman</term>; That is the waters; That is the Creator.”

In the previous <term>adhyaya</term> (chapter), this passage occurs: “What is all this, far, far beyond; That which is Formless and without grief.” “To the God who is in fire and in water, who pervades the entire universe, who is in the plants and in the lords of the forest—to Him, to God, all hail!”

“This God, in truth, is in all directions; indeed, he was born long, long ago. He truly exists now within the embryo. He has been born, and he will be born; he stands behind all who are born, with his face on every side.”

<summary>The text explains that while God is identified with the eight elements of the universe, He is actually the transcendent "Inner Ruler" distinct from those elements and the individual soul (Jiva). It supports this with various verses from the Shvetashvatara Upanishad describing Rudra as the omnipresent and supreme source of all.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of God's eight forms by examining the Brihadaranyaka Upanishat, where various elements and the soul are described as the body of the Inner Ruler.</meta>

# ASHTAMUHURTAM.

The famous passage in the seventh <term>Brahmana</term> of the third chapter of the <term>Brihadaranyaka Upanishat</term> provides a full exposition of these eight forms of God. In the third <term>Mantra</term>, Earth is said to be His body—

->“Yasyaprithivi sariram”<-

> “He who dwells in the earth, and within (or distinct from) the earth, whom the earth does not know, *whose body the earth is*, and who directs (rules) the earth from within—He is your Self, the inner director (ruler), the immortal.”

And in <term>Mantras</term> 4, 5, 7, 9, 11, 12, and 22, water, fire, air, the sun, the moon, <term>Akasha</term>, and <term>Vijnana</term> are respectively said to be His bodies.

The passages are all similar to the one relating to the earth; however, we quote the last one in full.

> “He who dwells in <term>Vijnana</term>, and within (or distinct from) <term>Vijnana</term>, whom <term>Vijnana</term> does not know, *whose body <term>Vijnana</term> is*, and who directs (rules) <term>Vijnana</term> from within—He is your Self, the inner director (ruler), the immortal.”

Professor Max Muller translates <term>Vijnana</term> as knowledge, but he notes at the same time that those of the <term>Madhyandina school</term> interpret it as meaning the <term>Atma</term> or the soul. According to the text in the *samana prakarana*—“yasyatma sariram”—and from the <term>Upa-Brahmanas</term> we will quote below, it will be seen that this is the correct interpretation.

The other text in the <term>Brihadaranyaka</term> makes it much clearer: “God is to be seen, heard, contemplated, and enjoyed in the soul. He is beyond the soul. His body is the soul; He penetrates into the depths of the soul.” Nothing can be clearer than this text. This Soul and soul, this <term>Atma</term> and <term>atma</term>, this Self and self (the confusion in thought arises because the name, which originally meant the human spirit, is also applied to the Supreme Spirit), are the two birds that dwell in the tree (the <term>human body</term>); these are the two that “enter into the heart, the excellent divine abode,” and these are the two which

<summary>This page analyzes the Brihadaranyaka Upanishat to show how the physical elements and the individual soul (Vijnana/Atma) serve as the body for the immortal Inner Ruler. It emphasizes the distinction between the Supreme Spirit and the individual soul, often compared to two birds inhabiting the same tree.</summary>

# ASHTAMUHURTAM.

...are in the "inside" of the human eye. The confusion of using the same word to denote and imply two different things is truly flawed, and later writings and present-day systems have dropped such uses altogether. The beginning of such change in terminology and precision in the use of words is seen in the <term>Gita</term>, where <term>Atma</term> is distinguished from <term>Paramatma</term>, and <term>Purusha</term> from <term>Purushottama</term> or <term>Parama Purusha</term>. Verse 22 of Chapter 13 is a characteristic verse in this respect, as it gives all these names and the true definition of <term>Sat</term> as distinguished from <term>Sat-asat</term>.

> "Spectator, and Permitter, Supporter, Enjoyer,
> *Maheshvara*, thus is called *Paramatman*;
> In this body, *Parama Purusha*."

We have elsewhere observed how the sole purpose of the <term>Puranas</term> and <term>Itihasas</term> is merely to explain the particular text of the <term>Veda</term> or <term>Upanishad</term>. The passage in the <term>Upa Brahmana</term> embodies the particular text and explains it.

See how this passage from the <term>Parasara Purana</term> reproduces the words and meaning of the <term>Rich text</term> quoted above:

> "Antarichchandiyā Rudram Sadhā Vantayam Manishyā
> Kruḥnanti Sihvaya tāhirasa pūrno Amṛitodakam
> Antar Nāchchantiyā Rudram Bahvānu Sahitam Śivam
> Purusha Māvagr̥iḥnanti Sikvayātānasamśayaḥ."

The following passage from the <term>Skanda Purana</term> also says that the <term>Jiva</term> is the body of God:

> "Antaryami Sa Avisha Jivanam Parameshvarah"
> "That same <term>Parameshvara</term> is the <term>Antaryami</term> in all <term>jivas</term>."

Turning to the <term>Mahabharata</term>, the statement that God has these eight objects for His body, that the universe is His form, and that He is distinct from the universe occurs very frequently.

We cite the following passages from the <term>Anusasana Parva</term>, <term>P. C. Roy</term>'s edition:—

> "Him that has the universe for His form," page 49.

> <term>Isha</term> preserve you! He who is revealed

> Of all material essences and things,’

> “ Prithivyo Bava, Apach Sarvah, Agne Rudrah, Vāyur Bhimah, Ākāšasya Mahādevah, Sūryas Yograh, Chandrasya Somah, Ātmānah Pašupatih ”

Note here that the word <term>Hotri</term>, meaning the sacrificer or the <term>Yajamān</term> (master) of the sacrifice, stands for <term>ātmā</term>, <term>Jīva</term>, or <term>Pašu</term>. Hence the Lord of the <term>pašu</term> is called <term>Pašupati</term>. (Meda Pati)

We quote a few more passages from <term>Mahābhārata</term>.

> “ You are the eight <term>Prakṛitis</term>; You are again above the eight <term>Prakṛitis</term>, everything that exists represents a portion of Your divine Self ”. page 99.

The following passage explains why God should multiply Himself, why He should manifest Himself into these eight forms; *i.e.*, why God should bring about the evolution and creation of this world; not, of course, from any moral necessity connected with the doctrine of <term>samsāra</term>; not, of course, from His Will to exist and desire for enjoyment; not, of course, from a desire to see His own reflection; not, of course, from a necessity to seek His own salvation; but that this evolution is necessitated for the improvement and salvation of the sin-covered soul.

> “ Know O <term>Kesava</term>, that this all, consisting of animate and inanimate existences, with heaven and other unseen entities, which occurs in these worlds, and which has the All-pervading Lord for its soul, has flowed from <term>Mahešvara</term>, and has been created by Him for the enjoyment of <term>Jiva</term>.” page 70.

The soul, in its <term>Kevala</term> condition, lies in utter and hopeless oblivion, and helplessness. The Lord wills (<term>Ichchā šakti</term>) that these souls should reach salvation out of His pure Grace (<term>Aruḷ Šakti</term>); and by means of His own Energy (<term>Kriyā šakti</term>), He agitates and puts motion and life into <term>Prakṛiti</term> (<term>Māyā šakti</term>); and <term>Purusha</term> (souls) and the whole of the manifested universe is brought forth from His womb. The souls in these material bodies act, and gain experience and knowledge, and finally

achieve freedom from the bondage of birth and death. Thus, the soul passes through its <term>sakala</term> and <term>athitha</term> conditions; and it is the fundamental tenet of every school of Hindu philosophy that unless the soul enters the cycle of <term>samsāra</term>—that wheel of birth and death—the soul cannot reach <term>Mukti</term>.

We close this paper with a few quotations from the <term>Drāviḍa Śruti</term> bearing on the question under discussion. Our saint <term>Tirumūlar</term> says:

> (1) ஊனு யுயிரா யுணர்வங்கி யாய்முன்னஞ்

> தாணுவு ஞாயிறுந் தண்மதி யுங்கடந்

> Air, space, and earth are His form;
> Transcending the fixed sun and cool moon,

> மொட்டி யுயிர்கிலை யென்னுமிக்காயப்பை
> கட்டியவிழ்க்கின்ற கண்ணுதல் காணுமே.

> He is the wind that blows in the eight directions.
> The whirling flood and fire, the huge earth and space;
> He joins the sentient soul to these, His bodily frame,
> And then unbinds it—the God with the eye on His forehead.

> With the soul, these are His eight forms

->ஒன்றியதுபுற இருவகையாகி<-
->முத்திறக்குணத்து நால்வகைப்பிறவி<-
->அத்திறத்தைம்பொறி அறுவகைச் சமயமோடே<-
->ஏழுலகாகி எண்வகை மூர்த்தியோடு<-
->ஊழியோடுஏழி யெண்ணிறந்தோங்கி<-

<vocab>Saint Meykaṇḍa Deva, Pattinattār, Agaval, Sivajñānabotham, Chit, Ashtamuhurtam, Shaivism, Tamil literature</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the poetic and philosophical description of the Divine as manifested in the material world, specifically detailing the "eight forms" (Ashtamuhurtam) of God as described by the Saint Pattinattār and the Tiruvāchakam.</meta>

# ASHTAMUHURTAM.

எவ்வகையளவினிற் கூடினின்
றவ்வகைப்பொருளுநீ யாகியவிடத்தே.

O You Dweller in *Vorri*, which beams
As the face of the sea-girt Earth!
Who possesses Your Form beyond compare?
The lightning's flash Your locks show,
The teeming Earth forms Your Head.
The Sun, Moon, and Fire—these three—
Are eyes that light Your Divine Face.
Your cool bright wreaths are the countless stars.
The sky wherein the gods dwell
Forms Your broad chest; the eight quarters,
Your strong shoulders. The broad sea is Your vest.
Your organ, the Earth; Your feet the worlds below.
The flowing wind is Your constant breath,
The flawless sounds are all Your words.
The faultless wisdom that is found together
In Gods and men is all Your own.
The teeming world lives and develops,
Vanishes and reappears—these are Your acts.
The world, in life or death, awake
Or asleep, shows Your true nature.
With these as Your Form, Your one true spirit
Becomes dual; clothed in the three <term>Gunas</term>,
You are born as four; You have five senses,
You become the six religions and seven worlds,
And are the <term>Eight Gods</term>.
And thus, progressing through ages and ages,
Whatever You unite with,
That You surely become.

The following is the favorite quotation from <term>Tiruvāchakam</term>.

நிலநீர் நெருப்புயிர் நீள்விசும்பு நிலாப்பகலோன்
புலனா யமைந்தனோ டெண்வகையாய்ப் புணர்ந்துநின்றா
னுலகே ழெனத்திசைபத் தெனத்தா னொருவனுமே
பலவாகி நின்றவா தோணோக்க மாடாமோ.

Earth, water, air, fire, sky, the Sun and Moon,
And the sentient man—these eight forms He pervades.

<vocab>Ashtamuhurtam, Vorri, Gunas, Tiruvāchakam, Eight Gods</vocab>

<summary>This page contains a poetic description by Saint Pattinattār of God's physical manifestation as the universe itself, followed by a verse from the Tiruvāchakam regarding the eight forms of the Divine.</summary>
<keywords>Ashtamuhurtam, Pattinattār, Vorri, Tiruvāchakam, eight forms of God, Gunas, Shaivite philosophy</keywords>

# ASHTAMUHURTAM.

->The seven worlds and ten directions, He the One,<-
->And the Many, He stands; so let us sing.<-

Saint Tāyumānavar selects the following verse from Saint Appar's *Devāram* for special praise in his அறிஞுரை.

> இருநிலனாய்த் தீயாகி நீருமாகி
> இயமான னாயெறியுங் காற்றுமாகி
> அருநிலையம் திங்களாய் நாயிறாகி
> ஆகாசமா யட்ட மூர்த்தியாகிப்
> பெருநலமுங் குற்றமும் பெண்ணுமாணும்
> பிறருருவுந் தம்முருவுந் தாமேயாகி
> நெருநலையா யின்றாகி நாளையாகி
> நிமிர்புன் சடையடிகள் நின்றவாறே.

> As earth, fire, water, air, and the Soul (<term>Ejaman</term>),
> As the moon, the sun, and space, as <term>Ashta Murti</term>,
> As goodness and evil, as male and female,
> Himself the Form of every form,
> As yesterday, today, and tomorrow,
> My Lord with the braided hair stands Supreme.

The following verse of Saint Appar also explains how this Being, who is the greatest of the great, is also so small as to be confined within ourselves.

> எட்டுமூர்த்தியாய் நின்றி பலுந்தொழில்
> எட்டுவான்குணத் தீசனெம்மான் றன்னை
> எட்டுமூர்த்தியு மெம்மிறையெம்முளே
> எட்டுமூர்த்தியு மெம்முளொடுங்குமே.

> As <term>Ashta Murti</term>, He performs all functions;
> He, my Father and God, possessed of eight attributes;
> He, the <term>Ashta Murti</term>, is my Lord and Master;
> He, the <term>Ashta Murti</term>, is confined within me.

Saint Jñāna Sambanda has the following verse.

> பாரு நீராகி பல்கதிரிரவியும் பனிமதி யாகாச
> மோரும் வாயுவு மொண்கனல் வேள்வியிற் றலைவனுமாய் நின்றார்
> சேருஞ்சந்தன மகிலொடு வந்திழிசெழும்புனற் கோட்டாறு
> வாருந் தண்புனல் சூழ்சிரபுரந் தொழுமடியவர் வருந்தாரே.

# ASHTAMUHURTAM.

> As Earth, Water, the Sun and Moon and Sky,
> The flowing Wind, bright Fire, and the Priest (<term>Hotri</term>), He stands.
> In Sirapuram, washed by the scented waters of Kôttâr,
> Those who praise Him will suffer no pain.

And Saint Tāyumānavar himself appropriately asks why, when the earth, air, etc., are spoken of by the Vedas as God Himself, he should not also be spoken of as God.

(1) பாராதி நீயாய்ப் பகர்ந்தா லதுவெனவும்
    ஆராயுஞ் சீவனும் நீயாம்காண் பராபரமே.
(2) வானாதி நீயெனவே வைத்தமறை யென்னையுநீ
    தானாகச் சொல்லாதோ சாற்றாய் பராபரமே.

Śiva is also called *Digvāsas*, *Digambara*, and *Nirvāṇi*, and He dances in *Chitambara*. His person and limbs, as we have seen, each represent an element or portion of the universe. We notice this description of Him even from the Rig Veda onwards. The translator of the Mahābhārata frequently remarks that Śiva is identified in those passages as the Supreme Brahman, but this identification has been ongoing since the very beginning. We can speak of an "identification" only when there is an original difference. Would it not, therefore, be more appropriate to say that the words Śiva and Rudra are merely the names, and His Form is the Form, of the Supreme Brahman?

We cannot fail to note here that there are also temples in India in which God (Śiva) is worshipped in one or another of these eight forms.

As Earth, He is worshipped in Kāñchi (Conjeevaram); as Water, in Jambukeśvaram (Trichinopoly); as Air, in Kāḷahasti; as Fire, in Tiruvaṇṇāmalai; as Space (<term>Ākāśa</term>), in Chidambaram; as the Sun, when everyone performs *Sūrya Namaskāram*;* as the Moon, in Somnāth; and as the Soul (<term>Pašu</term> or <term>Ātmā</term>), in the Pašupati Temple in Nepal.

---

<note>*Even now, in her extreme old age, my grandmother is very regular in her *Sūrya Namaskāram*, but she speaks of Him as ‘Śiva Sūrya-Kaṇṇē’ (சிவசூரியக்கண்ணே).</note>

# AN <term>UPANISHAT</term> TEXT.

->Ātmānam araṇim kritvā, pranavamcha Uttarāraṇim Jñāna<-
->nirmathanābhyasātl, pāśam dahatipanditaḥ.<-

In our <term>Tamiḷ</term> edition, there appeared an excellent translation of the <term>Kaivalyopanishat</term> by that great <term>Tamiḷ</term> and Sanskrit scholar of Jaffna, Srimath <term>Senthināthier</term>, who is now staying in <term>Benares</term>. His commentary is most valuable, as it traces the passages in the <term>Kaivalyopanishat</term> to similar passages in various other <term>Upanishats</term>. Some call this <term>Upanishat</term> sectarian and modern. We deny this, and we will take another, fuller opportunity to explain our views on the age of the <term>Upanishats</term>. At the very least, it is older than the time of <term>Śrī Śaṅkara</term>, who included it among the <term>Pañcharudram</term> on which he commented. The <term>Mantra</term>, "Ātmanam araṇim kritva, pranavamcha uttararaṇim Jñāna nirmathanābhyasath, pāśam dahati panditah," <note>Making the self the lower friction-stick and the syllable Om the upper friction-stick, by the practice of churning with knowledge, the wise man burns away his bonds.</note> following as it does <term>Mantras</term> 13 and 14 of Part I of the <term>Śvetāśvatara</term> <term>Upanishat</term>, and together with <term>Mantra</term> 11 above, would completely demolish the theory of that talented lady <term>Mrs. Besant</term>—that <term>Iśvara</term> evolves, and that the sole purpose of His evolution is to make Himself manifest from His unmanifest condition like butter from cream, fire from sticks, etc. The passage as it occurs in her recent, beautiful Adyar lecture is as follows:

> "As salt in the water in which it is dissolved (<term>Chandogya</term> VI, 14); as fire in the wood before the fire-sticks are rubbed together; as butter in the milk that is brought forth by churning (<term>Śvetāś</term> I, 14 to 19); as cream in clarified butter (Ibid IV, 14); so is <term>Brahman</term> concealed as the self of every creature" (Hinduism, page 16).

No doubt the form in which she has quoted herself has misled her. The passages themselves are these (we quote from Mr. Mead’s translation and from no other):

<summary>This page introduces a commentary on the Kaivalya Upanishad by the scholar Senthināthier and refutes Annie Besant's interpretation of Brahman's manifestation as an evolutionary process. The author argues that the Divine does not "evolve" into manifestation but is realized through the practice of spiritual knowledge.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Śvetāśvatara Upanishat, specifically focusing on Mantras 11 through 16, to explain how knowledge of God is realized within the soul through meditation, challenging modern interpretations of Brahman's manifestation.</meta>

# AN <term>UPANISHAT</term> TEXT.

> “By the knowledge of God, all bonds fall away;
> as sorrows perish, the cessation of birth and death arrives.
> By contemplating him, with the body left behind,
> He is found to be all-encompassing Lordship, Pure and Passionless.”—Mantra 11,

How is this knowledge of God to be obtained?

The next verse says,

> “This is to be known as always surely settled in the (self, soul); beyond this, surely nothing else is knowable at all. When one has reflected upon what tastes, what is tasted, and what does ordain, all has been said. This is the threefold Brahman (*Sat*, *Chit*, and *Ānanda*) (Mantra 12).” The unbeliever may ask, “How do you say God is concealed in our soul and body? We do not see Him. He is not these things.” The answer is given, illustrating it at the same time and explaining the method of realization, in the next Mantra, No. 13.

> “Just as the (outer) form of fire, withdrawn into its source, cannot be seen, yet there is no destruction of its subtle form—and once more it can indeed be drawn out of the upper and lower sticks—so both indeed are to be found by means of the word’s power within the body.”

This is more fully explained in the next Mantra.

> “Taking one’s body as the lower stick and the word (the Om) for the upper stick, by the well-sustained friction of meditation, let me behold the God lurking there, as it were.”

In the next Mantra, several similes are gathered together to illustrate the same subject.

> “As oil is in seeds, butter in cream, water in springs, and fire in the fire-sticks, so is that Supreme Self (<term>Paramātmā</term>) found in the individual self (<term>jivātmā</term>) by him who seeks for Him with truth and meditation. The Self pervades all, as butter pervades milk, rooted in meditation and self-knowledge—that Brahman, the sublime theme of sacred teaching, the sublime theme of sacred teaching.”

We will quote again Mantra 16 in part IV, relied on by

<meta>This page concludes the commentary on the Śvetāśvatara Upanishad, specifically concluding the analysis of the final Mantras and critiquing the interpretations of Annie Besant and G.R.S. Mead regarding the nature of God and the soul's liberation.</meta>

# AN <term>UPANISHAT</term> TEXT.

Mrs. Besant, as well as the Mantra preceding it, before we finish our comments.

> “Surely He is the guardian of all, hidden in every creature, in whom the seers of <term>Brahman</term> and divine powers are (all) conjoined. Thus knowing Him, one cuts the bonds of death. Most rare—as if it were that essence far rarer than clarified butter—knowing Him (in His benign form as Šiva) hidden in every creature, though One (yet) all-embracing; knowing Him, God, one is free from every bond.”

Anyone reading these verses together, as we have read them, will not fail to see that Mrs. Besant’s theory finds no footing here at all. This simply explains the way of salvation for the bound soul (<term>Jīvātmā</term>), and the nature of the Supreme. The bound soul, which cannot see “the subtler than subtle Šiva” (IV. 14) by pursuing the <term>Sādana</term> indicated here—namely, the search after Him with all one’s heart and with all one’s soul in all love and in all truth, with the aid of the divine Word—will surely behold the Supreme hidden in himself, not the Supreme *as* himself; then his bonds will be cut off, and the darkness will vanish as the sun rises on one's horizon. Butter is butter, whether it remains in the milk or is separated. It gains little itself in one condition or the other, but it makes a vast deal of difference to the person who has to eat it. No sane person will think that it matters at all to the Supreme whether He remains manifest or unmanifest, but it matters a great deal to His creatures who are wallowing in the murky darkness of sin and misery. There are those, again, who think <term>Pāšatchaya</term> is the only thing that occurs in <term>Moksha</term>, and that the freed soul exists in itself, with no knowledge or enjoyment of any sort. No doubt the moment of <term>Pāšatchaya</term> is also the moment when he recovers his own self (one of the two comprised in “both,”* of Mantra 13, the other being God).

---

<note>Mr. Mead absurdly supposes that “both” refers to the lower Brahman and higher Brahman; that the God of Mantra 14 is the lower Brahman or <term>Īšvara</term>, and the “self” of Mantras 15 and 16 is the higher Brahman. Reading</note>

<summary>This page concludes a commentary on the Śvetāśvatara Upanishad, arguing that salvation involves the soul (Jīvātmā) perceiving the Supreme (Šiva) within itself rather than being identical to it. The author critiques the interpretations of Annie Besant and G.R.S. Mead regarding the nature of Brahman and the state of liberation (Moksha).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Śvetāśvatara Upanishad, comparing its teachings on the soul's union with God to the devotional poetry of the Tiruvāchakam.</meta>

# AN <term>UPANISHAT</term> TEXT.

...and at the same moment, the Divine Radiance is cast fully upon him, enveloping him on all sides and swallowing him up completely. “I know the great Purusha, sun-like and beyond darkness. Knowing Him and Him alone, one crosses over death; there is no other path at all to take.” Mantra 8, Part III.

Nothing can be clearer than this passage regarding the person seeking salvation, the object of the search, the mode of attainment, and the only path to securing it. But are one's powers all-sufficient? No—“smaller than small, yet greater than great, in the heart of this creature the <term>Ātmā</term> (God) reposes: That, free from desire, he (the creature) sees, with his grief gone, the mighty <term>Īša</term>, by His grace.” (Mantra 20, Part III.)

These two mantras are reproduced in the famous verse number 7 in “House of God” in <term>Tiruvāchakam</term>, a valuable translation of which was printed in Volume I, page 49, of the <term>Siddhānta Dīpikā</term>.

> “Light of Truth that, entering body and soul, has melted all faults and driven away the false darkness.” (Verse 3.)

> “O Splendor that rises in my heart, as I melt with asking and asking.” (Verse 6.)

> “This day in Your mercy toward me, You drove away the darkness and stood in my heart as the Rising Sun,
> Of this Your way of rising—there being nothing else but You—I thought without Thought,
> I drew nearer and nearer to You, wearing away atom by atom
> Till I was one with You, O <term>Šiva</term>, Dweller in the great holy shrine.
> You are not anything in the universe; nothing exists except You.
> Who can know You?” (Verse 7.)

And let the reader ponder well again on the whole of verse 7. Every blind man’s heart’s desire is to regain his eyesight (his own self—<term>Ātmā</term>), but suppose he regained his eyesight; will the darkness be removed that formerly pressed on his eye? Surely not, unless the glorious Sun (God) deigns to show Himself to him

---

again these verses together, could anyone discover any difference in the nature of the Godhead in these Mantras?

<summary>This page discusses the soul's path to salvation through the realization of the Divine Radiance, drawing parallels between the Upanishads and the Tiruvāchakam. It emphasizes that liberation from darkness and union with God are made possible only through Divine grace.</summary>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the soul's journey toward God, comparing the realization of the Divine to a blind man regaining his sight and the process of lighting a fire through friction.</meta>

# AN <term>UPANISHAD</term> TEXT.

...in His Supreme Mercy. Of course, the Sun is of no use to the blind man as long as his blindness lasts. So, he has to realize himself by remaining balanced in pleasure and pain (the removal of his egoism) and realize his Maker, who until now was hidden in his heart. People have always asked, and will always ask, whether there is pleasure in this passage from bondage to freedom. Saint <term>Meykaṇḍa Deva</term> asks us to consider the case of the blind man passing from darkness to sudden light. Will there be pleasure or not? Did it ever matter to the Sun, in any way, when it was hidden from the blind man, and now when it shines fully upon his newly opened eyes?

> “It was Yourself You gave, and me You took,
> Beneficent Lord, who is the gainer?
> I have gained endless bliss. What have You gained from me?
> O Lord, who has made my heart Your temple,
> <term>Śiva</term>, dweller in the great holy shrine,
> O Father, Sovereign, You have made Your home in my body.
> For it, I have nothing to give in return.” <note>Verse 10 of the same <term>Tiruvāchakam</term> hymn, “The House of God.”</note>

To remove all doubts that the Being to be sought after is not one’s own self, the passage “<term>Ātmānam Araṇim Kṛitvā</term>” refers to the self (<term>Ātmā</term>) itself as the lower piece of firewood. In the <term>Śvetāśvatara</term>, it was the body that was the lower piece, in which case both the Soul and God could be realized; but generally, the phrases "in my body," "in my eye," "in my heart," "in my mind," and "in my soul" mean almost the same thing, including the soul and everything below it. Our Saint <term>Appar</term> puts the idea conveyed in these <term>Upanishad</term> texts into beautiful and unmistakable Tamil:

->விறகிற் றீயினன் பாலிற் படு நெய்போல்<-
->மறைப நின்றனன் மாமணிச் சோதியான்<-
->உறவு கோ னட் டுணர்வு கயிற்றினான்<-
->முறுக வாங்கிக் கடையமுன் னிற்குமே.<-

->Like the fire latent in firewood and ghee in milk,<-
->The great Light remains hidden;<-
->With the churner of love and the rope of knowledge,<-
->When one creates friction, He will manifest before him.<-

<summary>This page discusses the soul's transition from egoism to the realization of God, using analogies like the blind man seeing light and fire being churned from wood. It emphasizes that while God is hidden within, He is revealed through the "friction" of love and spiritual knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Meykaṇḍa Deva, Śiva, Ātmā, Śvetāśvatara Upanishad, Saint Appar, Tiruvāchakam, spiritual enlightenment, egoism, divine grace</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly introduction to the Svetasvatara Upanishad, focusing on defending its antiquity and its place within the Black Yajur Veda against modern criticisms.</meta>

# THE <term>SVETASVATARA</term>
# <term>UPANISHAD</term>.

---

We are glad to say that Professor <term>Max Müller</term> has cleared the way before us of many misconceptions and fallacies that were held concerning this <term>Upanishad</term>. He addresses in his own way the arguments presented to show that this is a modern <term>Upanishad</term> and that it is a sectarian <term>Upanishad</term>—an <term>Upanishad</term> of the <term>Sāṅkhya</term> and <term>Bhakti</term> schools, and so on—and his conclusions are that “no real argument has ever been brought forward to invalidate the tradition which represents it as belonging to the <term>Taittirīya</term> or Black <term>Yajur Veda</term>.” He also points out that it “holds a very high rank among <term>Upanishads</term>” and that its true meaning is the same as the Doctrine of the <term>Vedānta Philosophy</term>.

Professor <term>Garbe</term> and <term>Macdonnell</term>, however, in their recent works,* speak of this as a *<term>Śivite</term>* compilation, and the latter scholar refers to the <term>Upanishad</term> itself ascribing the authorship to a sage called <term>Śvetāśvatara</term>, unlike other <term>Upanishads</term>. But this is not characteristic of this <term>Upanishad</term> alone. The fifteenth <term>khāṇḍa</term> of the last *<term>Prapāṭhaka</term>* of the <term>Chhāndogya Upanishad</term> also traces the lineage of teachers in a similar way, and there is a similar statement in the <term>Māṇ訂ūkya Upanishad</term> and others. Since each hymn of the <term>Ṛig Veda</term> has its own author, it should not be a surprise that each particular <term>Upanishad</term> should have an individual author; and we do not suppose the Professor leans toward the orthodox view that the <term>Veda</term> and the <term>Upanishads</term> had no human authors and were revealed.

---

<note>\* Garbe’s Philosophy of Ancient India (1897) and Macdonnell’s History of Sanskrit Literature (1900).</note>

<summary>This page discusses scholarly defenses of the Svetasvatara Upanishad's antiquity, specifically citing Max Müller's conclusion that it belongs to the Black Yajur Veda. The author argues that having a named human author does not disqualify it from being an authentic and ancient Upanishad.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly introduction to the Svetasvatara Upanishad, specifically addressing and refuting the claim that it is a modern, sectarian text.</meta>

# THE <term>ŚVETAŚVATARA UPANISHAD</term>.

In regard to the other deep-rooted fallacy about it being a sectarian <term>Upanishad</term>, we shall speak here at length.

By raising this objection, they mean to imply also that it is modern. Curiously enough, we read of scholars ascribing dates for the rise of these sects starting from the tenth and twelfth centuries. Sir <term>W. W. Hunter</term> seriously contends that <term>Śankara</term> was the great apostle of <term>Shaivism</term>. But these writers do not see that the history of the Hindu religion is as ancient as the history of Hindu philosophy, and that the people must have had a popular religion even in the very days these <term>Upanishads</term> were composed. They also fail to see that the <term>Purāṇas</term>, which embodied the essence of the <term>Upanishad</term> teaching, existed in a popular form even in those ancient days, and the words <term>Itihāsa</term> and <term>Purāṇa</term> occur even in the oldest <term>Upanishads</term>.* These <term>Upanishads</term> are quoted by name in the <term>Purāṇas</term>, and particular passages are also commented on.

And it will be an interesting study to determine what the religion of the people was in the days of the <term>Upanishads</term>, the <term>Mahābhārata</term>, the <term>Rāmāyaṇa</term>, and the <term>Purāṇas</term>, and to compare it with the existing phases of the Hindu religion. We may briefly indicate our own conclusions on the subject, though we cannot give our reasons in detail—namely, that so far as any room for comparison exists, the traditions, beliefs, ceremonies, and faith of modern-day <term>Shaivas</term> (among whom may be included all <term>Shaktas</term>, <term>Ganapatyas</term>, and <term>Smartas</term>), who now form the bulk of the Hindu race, were exactly the same as those of the people in the days of the oldest <term>Upanishads</term>, the <term>Mahābhārata</term>, and the <term>Rāmāyaṇa</term>. According to the opinions of many old scholars like <term>Lassen</term>, <term>Wilson</term>, <term>Muir</term>, and others, the worship of <term>Shiva</term> represented the cult of the higher castes—the <term>Brahmans</term> and <term>Kshatriyas</term>—and a text of <term>Manu</term> mentions that <term>Shiva</term> is the God of the <term>Brahmans</term>. It is remarkable how the picture of <term>Shiva</term> is exactly the same as that of any ancient

---

<note>* <term>Bṛihadāraṇyaka-Up.</term> 2-4-10 and 4-1-2; <term>Maittṛaya-Up.</term> 6-32 and 33; <term>Chhāndogya-Up.</term> VII. 1-2.</note>

<summary>This page argues against the view that the Śvetaśvatara Upanishad is a modern or sectarian work, asserting that Shaivism and the Purāṇas have roots as ancient as the oldest Upanishads themselves. The author concludes that modern Shaiva traditions align with the religious practices of the higher castes from the era of the early scriptures.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Śvetāśvatara Upanishad, transitioning from a discussion of Shaivism's antiquity to the historical development of the deity Rudra-Shiva.</meta>

# THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>.

<term>Rishi</term> (see some of <term>Ravi Varma's</term> pictures). Dr. <term>W. W. Hunter</term> remarks that <term>Shankara</term>, in adopting <term>Shaivism</term>, combined in the system the highest philosophy of the ancients and the most popular form of religion.

Regarding the conception of <term>Shiva</term> and its growth from Vedic times, scholars like to tell us that <term>Rudra</term> was nowhere called <term>Shiva</term> in the <term>Rig Veda</term> and that he merely represented the storm god, with his thunder, lightning, and the rains rushing down from the snow-capped hills; and that this <term>Rudra</term> slowly grew into the <term>Shiva</term> of the <term>Hindu Triad</term>, and scholars have not failed to remark on his composite and contradictory aspects.

There is considerable truth in this, and we can clearly trace that in his person is slowly built up the conception of the various Vedic deities: <term>Indra</term> and <term>Agni</term>, <term>Varuna</term> and <term>Vayu</term>, <term>Surya</term> and <term>Soma</term>, <term>Vishnu</term> and <term>Brahma</term>. By the time the <term>Vedas</term> were organized into the <term>Rig</term>, <term>Yajur</term>, <term>Sama</term>, and <term>Atharvan</term>, <term>Rudra's</term> position as the God of gods had become assured; and by the time of the earliest <term>Upanishads</term>, when the purely sacrificial <term>yajnas</term> were being given up, the worship of <term>Rudra-Shiva</term> supplanted the worship of the Vedic deities. Instead of a blind worship of the elements, a marked distinction was drawn between the Supreme God who dwelt in these elements and gave them special power and glory. This conception was formalized later on by <term>Shiva</term> being called the <term>Ashtamurti</term>, the God who had for his body the five elements—earth, air, water, fire, and <term>akasha</term>—as well as the sun, the moon, and the soul; and <term>Shiva</term> has temples dedicated to him in which he is worshipped in these eight forms.

<term>Rudra</term> is derived by <term>Sayana</term> from the roots <term>Rudravayita</term>, meaning "he who drives away sorrow." Consistent with this derivation, <term>Rudra</term> is called in the <term>Rig Veda</term> itself the "bountiful" and the "Healer" possessed of various remedies (the later <term>Vaidyanath</term>), "benign" and "gracious." And the term <term>Shiva</term> clearly appears in the following text of the <term>Rig Veda</term> (X. 92-9.)

<summary>This page discusses the historical evolution of Rudra from a Vedic storm god into Shiva, the supreme deity who incorporates the attributes of all other Vedic gods. It explains how this conception was eventually formalized in the form of the Ashtamurti, where Shiva embodies the elements and the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Rudra, Shiva, Shaivism, Rig Veda, Ashtamurti, Vedic deities, Sayana, evolution of religion</keywords>

# THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>

> “Stomanvā adya Rudrāya śikvase kshyadvirāya namasā didhishṭāna yebhiḥ Śivaḥ svavān eva yāvabhirdivaḥ śikshati svāyaśāḥ nikāmabhi.” *

Those who are familiar with the actual performance of <term>yajñas</term> will know how the positions of the respective priests—the <term>Adhvaryu</term>, <term>Hotri</term>, <term>Udgātri</term>, and <term>Brahman</term>—are fixed, as well as the positions of the various gods. The chief place is assigned to <term>Rudra</term>, and it is apart from the other gods. This clearly explains the significance of the title “<term>Medhapati</term>” in the <term>Rig Veda</term>, I-43-4: “<term>Gādhapatiṁ</term>, *<term>Medhapatim</term>* <term>Rudram Jale-shabheshajam</term>, <term>tat samyoḥ sumnam imehi</term>.” <note>We seek from Rudra, the lord of songs, the lord of sacrifices who possesses healing remedies, his auspicious favor</note>, as well as “king of sacrifices” (<term>Rig Veda</term> 4-3). <term>Medhapati</term> is the same word as the more popular term <term>Paśupati</term>; <term>Paśu</term> refers to the animal offered in sacrifice (<term>Yajña-Paśu</term>) and symbolically represents the bound soul, the <term>jiva</term>. 

As the Lord (<term>Pati</term>) of all sacrifices, He is the fulfiller of sacrifices: “<term>Yajña sadham</term>” (<term>Rig Veda</term> I. 114-4) and “<term>Rudram yajñānaṁ sa dadhishṭim apasam</term>” (III. 2-5). As the God of gods, He is said to “derive His renown from Himself” (<term>Rudrāya Svāyaśase</term>). His glory is said to be inherent, independent, or self-dependent: “<term>Svādhavane</term>” (<term>Rig Veda</term> VII. 46-1). He is also called <term>Svapivāta</term>, which is variously explained as meaning “readily understanding,” “accessible,” “gracious,” “he by whom life is conquered,” “he whose command cannot be transgressed,” or “you by whom prayers (words) are readily received.” He is called the father of the worlds, “*<term>Bhuvanasya Pitaraṁ</term>*” (VI. 49-10). The <term>Rig Vedic</term> story of His becoming the Father of the fatherless <term>Maruts</term> can be recalled in many a <term>Purāṇic</term> story, local legend, and common folklore.

* <note>With reverence, present your hymn today to the mighty Rudra, the ruler of heroes, [and to the Maruts], those rapid and ardent deities with whom the gracious (Shiva) and opulent (Rudra), who derives his renown from himself, protects us from the sky.</note> <term>Sāyana</term> takes “Śivaḥ” as a noun and interprets it as meaning <term>Parameśvara</term>; it seems strange that <term>Muir</term> should take it as an adjective.

# THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>.
->113<-

He is ‘*antar ichchanti*’—beyond all thought (VIII. 61-3). His form as described in the <term>Ṛig Veda</term> is almost the same as the image of later days. He is called the <term>Kapardin</term>, with ‘spirally braided hair.’ He is of <term>Hiraṇya Rūpam</term>, ‘golden formed,’ and brilliant like the sun, and ‘shining like gold’ “*Yaḥ śukra iva Sūryo hiraṇyam iva ro chati*” (I. 43-5).* And in <term>Ṛig Veda</term> X. 136-1 to 7, He is the long-haired being who sustains the fire, water, and the two worlds; who is, in appearance, the entire sky; and who is called this ‘Light.’ He is *wind-clad* (naked) and drinks <term>Visha</term> (water or poison), and a <term>Muni</term> is identified with <term>Rudra</term> in this aspect.

When we come to the <term>Yajur Veda</term>, His supreme majesty is fully developed, and He is expressly called <term>Śiva</term> by name ‘*Śiva nama śi*’ (<term>Yaj. S.</term> 3-63), and the famous mantra, the <term>Pañchākshara</term>, is said to be placed in the very heart of the three <term>Vedas</term> (the name occurs in <term>Tait. S.</term> IV. 5, 1-41 “*namaḥ śāmbave cha mayobave cha namaḥ Śaṅkarāya cha mayaskārāya cha* NAMAḤ ŚIVĀYA *cha Śivātaraya cha*”). And the famous <term>Śatarudriyam</term>, which is praised in the <term>Upanishats</term> and in the <term>Mahābhārat</term>, also forms a central portion of this central <term>Veda</term>. And this is a description of God as the all, the all in all, and transcending all: ‘<term>Viśvadevo</term>, <term>Viśvasvarūpo</term>, <term>Viśvadhiko</term>’; and anybody can see that the famous passage in the *Gītā* in chapters 10 and 11 merely parodies this other passage. These two chapters are respectively called <term>Vibhūti Vistāra Yoga</term> and <term>Viśvarūpa Sandarśana Yoga</term>, which is exactly the character of the <term>Śatarudriya</term>. The <term>Yogi</term> who has reached the highest state “sees all in God and God in all.” In the <term>Śatarudriya</term> and in the whole <term>Veda</term>, <term>Rudra</term> is called <term>Śiva</term>, <term>Śaṅkara</term>, <term>Śambhu</term>, <term>Īśāna</term>, <term>Īśa</term>, <term>Bhagavān</term>, <term>Bhava</term>, <term>Sarva</term>, <term>Ugra</term>, <term>Soma</term>, <term>Paśupati</term>, <term>Nilagrīva</term>, <term>Giriśa</term>, <term>Mahādeva</term>, and <term>Maheśvara</term>. And the most famous mantra ‘*Ekam Eva Rudronadvitīyāya taste*’ whose very existence in the <term>Vedas</term> and <term>Upanishats</term> scholars doubted at one

time, occurs in the <term>Yajur Saṃhitā</term> ('<term>Tait.</term>') in 1 Canto, 8 <term>Praśna</term>, 6 <term>Anuvāka</term>, 1 <term>Pañchāśat</term>. This very mantra is repeated in our <term>Upaniṣhat</term> (III. 2), and if the <term>Upaniṣhats</term> did not precede the <term>Vedas</term>, it will be seen how this mantra is the original source of the other famous <term>Upaniṣhat</term> mantra, "*Ekamevadvitīyam Brahma*" <note>Brahman is one without a second</note>. In fact, we doubt if the word '<term>Brahman</term>' occurs even once in the <term>Ṛig Veda</term> as meaning God, or in the <term>Yajur</term> as meaning the Supreme Being. Professor <term>Max Müller</term> is undoubtedly correct in drawing attention to the fact that the conception of a mere Impersonal Self may be later than the conception of God as <term>Śiva</term>, <term>Rudra</term>, and <term>Agni</term>. The texts we have quoted above will for once prove the danger of guessing the date of an <term>Upaniṣhat</term> for the sole reason that it uses the words <term>Śiva</term>, <term>Īśa</term>, <term>Īśāna</term>, and <term>Rudra</term>.

In the days of the <term>Veda</term> and the <term>Upaniṣhats</term>, these names—<term>Rudra</term>, <term>Śiva</term>, <term>Śambhu</term>, <term>Mahādeva</term>, <term>Īśa</term>, <term>Īśāna</term>, <term>Hara</term>, and <term>Viṣhṇu</term>—only meant the same as <term>Deva</term> or <term>Brahman</term> or <term>Ātman</term> or <term>Paramātman</term>. They had no prejudice against the use of the former set of words, as some sectarians today seem to have. In the <term>Gītā</term> itself, the words <term>Īśvara</term>, <term>Īśa</term>, <term>Maheśvara</term>, <term>Mahādeva</term>, and <term>Parameśvara</term> are freely used, and <term>Śiva</term> is used in the <term>Uttara Gītā</term>, even though the modern <term>Vaishṇava</term> exhibits the greatest prejudice toward these names.

One word about the different aspects of <term>Śiva</term>. As we pointed out before, as the idea of <term>Rudra</term>—representing all the gods or the powers of Nature—was fully evolved, in Him were also centralized the various aspects of Nature as both good and bad, awful and beneficent. <term>Kālidāsa</term> playfully brings out this idea in the following lines:

> "The gods, like clouds, are fierce and gentle too;
> Now hurl the bolt, now drop sweet heavenly dew.
> In summer heat the streamlet dies away
> Beneath the fury of the God of day,
> Then in due season comes the pleasant rain
> And all is fresh and fair and full again."

<summary>This page argues that divine names like Rudra and Shiva were originally synonymous with Brahman and the Supreme Being in Vedic texts, rather than sectarian markers. It also notes that the character of Shiva evolved to centralize both the terrifying and benevolent aspects of nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine nature of the Lord, transitioning from the scriptural names used in the Vedas and Upanishads to the dual nature of the deity as both fierce and auspicious, specifically focusing on the character of Śiva/Rudra.</meta>

# THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;No matter how terrifying a fierce storm may appear with its thunder and lightning, no one can appreciate its benefits more than those who live on Indian soil, a land that has suffered through so many famines. However fierce the sun may be, its existence is absolutely essential for the growth and ripening of all vegetation in the tropics. It should be noted that not only in the case of <term>Rudra</term>, but also with other gods, both their beneficial and harmful powers are highlighted in the <term>Vedas</term>. The Supreme Dual Personality of <term>Śiva</term> is explained in the <term>Mahābhārata</term> by Lord <term>Krishna</term> himself:

> "Mighty-armed <term>Yudhiṣṭhira</term>, learn from me the greatness of the glorious, *multiform*, and many-named <term>Rudra</term>. They call him <term>Mahādeva</term>, <term>Agni</term>, <term>Sthāṇu</term>, <term>Maheśvara</term>, the one-eyed, <term>Tryambaka</term>, the *Universal-formed*, and <term>Śiva</term>. <term>Brahmins</term> skilled in the <term>Veda</term> know two bodies of this God: one terrifying and one auspicious; and these two bodies, in turn, have many forms. The dreadful and terrifying body is fire, lightning, and the sun; the auspicious and beautiful body is virtue, water, and the moon. Half of his essence is fire, and the other half is called the moon. The half that is his auspicious body practices chastity, while the other, which is his most dreadful body, destroys the world. Because he is the Lord and Great, he is called <term>Maheśvara</term>. Since he consumes, since he is fiery, fierce, glorious, and an eater of flesh, blood, and marrow—he is called <term>Rudra</term>. As he is the greatest of the gods, as his domain is vast, and as he preserves the vast Universe, he is called <term>Mahādeva</term>. Because of his smoky color, he is called <term>Dhūrjaṭi</term>. Since he constantly grants prosperity to all people in all their actions, seeking their welfare (<term>Śiva</term>), he is therefore called <term>Śiva</term>."<note>'Śiva' is derived from 'Vasi,' which appears in the Katha Upanishad; see the Lalitā Sahasranāma commentary under 'Śiva.'</note>

And in this, we see him not only as the destroyer but also as the Reproducer and Preserver; as such, the conception of <term>Śiva</term> transcends the conception of <term>Rudra</term> as merely one member of the Trinity.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It can also be shown that the depiction of God as fierce and terrible is not an entirely unchristian idea.

<summary>This page explores the dual nature of Śiva, describing him as both a terrifying destroyer (Rudra) and a benevolent preserver, drawing on a discourse from the Mahābhārata. It argues that these opposing qualities are essential to the divine nature, much like the sun or a storm are necessary for life despite their ferocity.</summary>

<keywords>Śiva, Rudra, Mahābhārata, dual nature, Mahādeva, Maheśvara, Vedic gods, Krishna, Yudhiṣṭhira, destroyer and preserver</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the dual nature of the Divine, specifically focusing on the character of Śiva as both a terrifying and an auspicious force, moving from scriptural descriptions to natural metaphors that illustrate the unity of destruction and preservation.</meta>

# THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>.

The following paragraphs we take from a book called “The Woodlands in Europe,” intended for Christian readers; we could not provide better arguments for the truth of our conception of the Supreme <term>Śiva</term>, the Destroyer, the Creator, and the Preserver (<note>see page 6, English Edition of the Śivajñānabotham</note>).

> “And what about the dead leaves which, season after season, strew the ground beneath the trees? Is their work done because, when their bright summer life is over, they lie softly down to rest under the wintry boughs? Is it only death, and nothing beyond? No; if it is death, it is death giving place to life. Let us call it rather change, progress, transformation. It must be progress when the last year’s leaves make the soil for the next year’s flowers, and in so doing serve a set purpose and fulfill a given mission. *It must be transformation, when one thing passes into another, and instead of being annihilated, begins life again in a new shape and form.*

> “It is interesting to remember that the same snow which weighs down and breaks those fir branches is the nursing mother of the flowers. Softly it comes down upon the tiny seeds and the tender buds and covers them up lovingly, so that they are safely sheltered from all the stern rigour of the world outside. Thus they are moving forward, as it were, and life is already swelling within them; so that when the sun shines and the snow melts, they are ready to burst forth with a rapidity which seems almost miraculous.

> “*It is not the only force gifted with both preserving and destroying power, according to the aspect in which we view it.* The fire refines and purifies, but it also destroys; and the same water which rushes down in the cataract with such overwhelming power falls in the gentlest of drops upon the thirsty flower cup and fills the hollow of the leaf with just the quantity of dew which it needs for its refreshment and sustenance. And in those higher things of which nature is but the type and shadow, the same grand truth holds good; and from our Bibles—

<summary>This page uses metaphors from nature, such as decaying leaves and the protective quality of snow, to argue that destruction is a necessary precursor to new life. These natural processes are presented as evidence for the dual nature of Śiva as both a Destroyer and a Preserver.</summary>

<keywords>Śiva, Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣhat, Śivajñānabotham, transformation, nature metaphors, Destroyer, Preserver, Creator</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dual nature of God as both a source of terror and mercy, moving from metaphors of nature to a formal theological analysis of the Katha Upanishad's description of the Divine as a "thunderbolt" and "breath."</meta>

# THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>.

We learn that the *consuming fire* and the *love that passes knowledge* are but different sides of the same God:—*Just and yet merciful; who will by no means clear the guilty, yet showing mercy unto thousands.*

<term>Bādarāyaṇa</term> also touches upon this subject in I., iii., 40, and we quote below the <term>Pūrvapaksha</term> and <term>Siddhhānta</term> views on this question from the commentary of <term>Śrīkaṇṭha</term>.

"Because of trembling (I, iii, 40). In the <term>Kaṭha-Vallis</term>, in the section treating of the thumb-sized <term>Purusha</term>, it is said as follows:

> 'Whatever there is, the whole world when gone forth (from the <term>Brahman</term>) trembles in the breath; (it is) a great terror, the thunderbolt uplifted; those who know it become immortal.' (cit. 6, 2).

Here a doubt arises as to whether the cause of trembling is the <term>Parameśvara</term> or some other being.

(<term>Pūrvapaksha</term>):—Here the <term>Śruti</term> speaks of the trembling of the whole universe by fear caused by the entity denoted by the word "breath." It is not right to say that the <term>Parameśvara</term>, who is so sweet-natured as to afford refuge to the whole universe and who is supremely gracious, is the cause of the trembling of the whole universe. Therefore, as the word "thunderbolt" occurs here, it is the thunderbolt that is the cause of trembling. Or it is the vital air which is the cause of the trembling, because the word "breath" occurs here. Since the vital air causes the motion of the body, this whole world, which is the body as it were, moves on account of the vital air. Then we can explain the passage "whatever there is, the whole world, when gone forth (from the <term>Brahman</term>) trembles in the breath." Then we can also explain the statement that "it is a great terror, the thunderbolt uplifted," inasmuch as like lightning, cloud, and rain, the thunderbolt which is the source of great terror is produced by the action of the air itself. It is also possible to attain immortality by a knowledge of the air, as the following <term>Śruti</term> says: ·

<summary>The text explores the dual nature of God as both merciful and terrifying, referencing Bādarāyaṇa's Brahma Sutras and Śrīkaṇṭha's commentary on the Katha Upanishad. It presents an initial argument (Pūrvapaksha) suggesting that the "breath" which causes the world to tremble refers to literal air or a thunderbolt rather than the gracious Parameśvara.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological debate regarding the nature of God as a source of "trembling" or awe, transitioning from the commentary on the Kaṭha Upaniṣhad to an analysis of the Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣhad and its interpretation by Western scholars.</meta>

# THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>.

> “Air is everything itself and the air is all things together; he who knows this conquers death” (<term>Bṛi. Up.</term> 5-3-2).

(<term>Siddhānta</term>):—As against the previous argument, we say that <term>Parameśvara</term> Himself is the cause of the trembling. It is possible that, as the Ruler, <term>Parameśvara</term> is the cause of the trembling of the whole universe; by the fear of His command, all of us abstain from prohibited actions and engage in our prescribed duties. It is by the fear of His command that <term>Vāyu</term> and others perform their respective duties, as may be learned from such passages as the following:—

> “By fear of Him, <term>Vāyu</term> (the wind) blows.” (<term>Tait. Up.</term> 2-8).

Though gracious in appearance, <term>Parameśvara</term> becomes awesome as the Ruler of all. Hence the <term>Śruti</term> says:

> ‘Therefore the King’s face must be awesome!’ (<term>Tait. Brā.</term> 3-8-23).

> “Therefore as the Master, <term>Īśvara</term> Himself is the cause of the trembling of the whole universe.”

Before we enter into the discussion of the philosophical meaning of this <term>Upaniṣhat</term>, we must note the great difficulty felt by nearly all European scholars—who are raised solely in the school of <term>Śaṅkara</term>—in interpreting this <term>Upaniṣhat</term>, a difficulty which has equally been felt regarding the philosophy of the <term>Gītā</term>. Different scholars have interpreted it as variously expounding *<term>Sāṅkhya</term> and <term>Yoga</term>, <term>Bhakti</term> and <term>Vedānta</term>, Dualism and non-Dualism*; and Professor <term>Max Müller</term> agrees with Mr. <term>Gough</term> in taking it as fully expounding the Indian idealism school of <term>Vedānta</term>. Professors <term>Garbe</term> and <term>Macdonnell</term> characterize the philosophy as ECLECTIC. The latter says (p. 405, *History of Sanskrit Literature*): “Of the eclectic movement combining <term>Sāṅkhya</term>, <term>Yoga</term>, and <term>Vedānta</term> doctrines, the oldest literary representative is the *<term>Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣhat</term>*. More famous is the <term>Bhagavad Gītā</term>.”

If there ever was such an eclectic school, have these scholars paused to inquire who their modern representatives are? Or

# THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAD</term>.

Why is it that there are no such representatives today? The real fact is that this was the only true philosophical creed of the majority of the people, and this philosophy has remained untarnished during the last 3,000 years or more. During the <term>Upaniṣhad</term> period, the schools whose existence could be distinctly marked are the *<term>Lokāyata</term>* or *<term>Nāstika</term>*, Kapila's *<term>Sāṅkhya</term>*, *<term>Mīmāṃsā</term> of <term>Jaimini</term>*, *<term>Nyāya</term>*, *<term>Vaiśeṣhika</term>*, and *<term>Yoga</term>*. The first three were atheistic and the latter were theistic. And of course, all these were professed Hindus <note>The majority of every people and nation are virtually atheistic and materialistic, though professing a belief in God and conforming to the customs of society.</note>, and none would have deviated from the rituals and practices prescribed for the Hindu, even though, academically speaking, they would have held to this or that view of philosophy. And this inconsistency is what strikes a foreigner even now in the character of the modern Hindu. Mrs. <term>Besant</term> aptly describes this as "the Hindu's principle of rigidity of conduct and freedom of thought." All these schools were based on a certain number of *<term>tattvas</term>* or categories. The *<term>Nāstika</term>* postulated four and only four *<term>tattvas</term>*—namely, earth, air, fire, and water—and would not even believe in *<term>Ākāśa</term>* or ether. <term>Kapila</term> increased the number of categories he believed in to 19, which he grouped under *<term>Puruṣha</term>* and *<term>Pradhāna</term>*. The *<term>Mīmāṃsaka</term>* believed practically in nothing more, though he emphasized the authority and eternity of the Vedas. The next three theistic schools believed in 24 or 25 *<term>tattvas</term>*, which they grouped under *<term>Puruṣha</term>*, *<term>Pradhāna</term>*, and *<term>Īśvara</term>* or God. As all these schools based their theoretical philosophy on a certain number of *<term>tattvas</term>* <note><term>Tirumūlar</term>, a Tamil Saint of about the first century A.D., thus distinguishes the schools existing in his time: "The 96 *<term>tattvas</term>* or categories are common to all. 36 categories are special to the <term>Śaivas</term>. 28 are the categories of the <term>Vedāntī</term>. 24 categories belong to <term>Vaishṇavas</term>. 26 categories are those of the <term>Māyāvādi</term>." The particular thing to be noted here is the distinction drawn between <term>Vedāntī</term> and <term>Māyāvādi</term>.</note>, *<term>Sāṅkhya</term>*, the theoretical philosophy, came to be

It also outlines how these schools were classified based on the number of tattvas (categories of existence) they recognized.</summary>

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAD</term>.<-

...called <term>Sāṅkhya</term> as distinguished from practical religion and the code of morality. During the <term>Upaniṣhad</term> period, and even in the time of the <term>Mahābhārata</term>, the word had not lost its general significance. It will be noticed when determining what these various categories are that, with the exception of the <term>Nāstika</term>, all the other five schools believed in almost the same things—though the enumerations varied—except regarding the postulating of God. Even in this idea of God, there was practically very little difference between <term>Kapila</term> and <term>Patañjali</term>. To both of them, the freed <term>Puruṣha</term> was equal to <term>Īśvara</term>; however, <term>Kapila</term> believed that no <term>Īśvara</term> was necessary for the origination, sustenance, and resolution of the worlds. But according to <term>Patañjali</term>, there existed an eternally freed Being who created these worlds and resolved them again into their original components. 

In the <term>Upaniṣhad</term> period, the <term>Yoga</term> school was the dominant cult, and these <term>Upaniṣhads</term>, including the <term>Śvetāśvatara</term> and <term>Kaivalya</term>, etc., were all books of the <term>Yoga</term> school. The theoretical or argumentative part of the philosophy or creed was called by the name of <term>Sāṅkhya</term>, and the practical part, <term>Yoga</term>. As this <term>Yoga</term> postulated the highest end achieved by a study of the <term>Vedas</term>, which were set forth in these <term>Upaniṣhads</term>, it was also slowly coming to be called <term>Vedānta</term>. We have an example in the <term>Chāndogya</term> (1-1-10) that the word <term>Upaniṣhad</term> was actually used as a synonym for <term>Yoga</term>:

> “The sacrifice which a man performs with knowledge, faith, and the <term>Upaniṣhad</term> is more powerful.”

"Knowledge" or <term>jñāna</term> here meant the knowledge of the categories and their relation, which according to <term>Kapila</term> was alone sufficient to bring about man’s freedom. The <term>Vedānta</term> held this to be insufficient unless it was accompanied by earnestness, love, and the contemplation of a Supreme Being. This contemplation brought the thinker nearer and nearer to the *object* of his thoughts, until all distinctions of object and subject were thoroughly merged (the distinction of "I" and "Mine") and the union or *oneness* was reached, and all <term>bandha</term> or <term>pāśa</term> vanished. This is the root idea in both words "<term>Upaniṣhad</term>" and "<term>Yoga</term>." <term>Yoga</term> means union, the union of

It highlights the distinction between Kapila’s atheistic Sāṅkhya and Patañjali’s theistic Yoga, emphasizing the Vedāntic requirement of devotion alongside knowledge for spiritual liberation.</summary>

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAD</term>.<-

...two things held apart and brought together, when the *bonds or fetters* which separated them *fell off or perished*. The word "<term>Upanishat</term>" is also derived from *Upa* (near), *ni* (completely), and *sat* (to perish). Here also, the bringing together of two things and the *perishing* of something is *clearly intended*. Naturally, the two things brought together are the Soul and God, and the perishable thing is certainly the *<term>Pāśa</term>* (bond); accordingly, the Soul, when bound by *<term>Pāśa</term>*, is called *<term>Paśu</term>*.

This was the state of philosophical thought until the days of the <term>Mahābhārata</term>, and we maintain that this was prior to the rise of <term>Buddhism</term> and continued for some centuries after <term>Gautama Buddha</term> and until the time of <term>Badarāyana</term>. It was during this time that the philosophy of India spread into and permeated the thought of Europe. Professor Garbe has lucidly proved in his short history, "*The Philosophy of Ancient India*," that the influence received by the Greeks up to the Neo-Platonic school was almost <term>Sāṅkhyan</term> in character. It was during this time, again, that the blending of the <term>Aryan</term> and <term>Tamilian</term> in art, civilization, and philosophy took place (though we cannot consider here how much was common to both, and how much each gained from the other). We have an exactly parallel word in Tamil for the word "<term>Sāṅkhya</term>," and this word is <term>எண்</term> (<term>eṇ</term>), which means both "number" and "to think." Both <term>Auvaiyār</term> and <term>Tiruvalluvar</term> use this word to mean *logic and metaphysics*; the primary science on which all thought was built was mathematics, or the science of numbers. A systematic and historical study of Tamil works will support our position; even today, the most dominant cult among the Tamils is <term>Sāṅkhya</term> and <term>Yoga</term> as represented in the <term>Upaniṣhads</term> or <term>Vedānta</term>. This system must have been thoroughly established in the Tamil language and literature before the time of <term>Christ</term> and before <term>Badarāyana</term>’s composition of the *<term>Śārīraka Sūtras</term>*. Indeed, when <term>Badarāyana</term>’s system came into vogue in Southern India, it was recognized as a distinct school. As <term>Badarāyana</term> expressly professed to interpret the <term>Upaniṣhat</term> or <term>Vedānta</term> texts, his school of

Philosophy was formalized by the phrase ‘*<term>Vedānta</term>*.’ By collecting all the texts in <term>Tamil</term> down even to the time of <term>Tāyumānavar</term> (16th century) that contain references to *<term>Vedānta</term>*, we could prove what the specific view of <term>Badarāyaṇa</term> was. This will also show that the exposition of <term>Badarāyaṇa</term> contained in the earliest *<term>Bhāshya</term>*, or commentary, that we possess in Sanskrit—namely, that of <term>Śrīkaṇṭha</term>, which was later adopted almost entirely by <term>Rāmānuja</term>—was the true view of <term>Badarāyaṇa</term>. This view we may sum up in <term>Dr. Thibaut</term>'s own words:—

> “If I am now briefly to sum up the results of the preceding inquiry regarding the teaching of the <term>Sūtras</term>, I must state my opinion that they do not set forth the distinction of a higher and lower knowledge of <term>Brahman</term>; that they do not acknowledge the distinction of <term>Brahman</term> and <term>Īśvara</term> in <term>Śaṅkara</term>'s sense; that they do not hold the doctrine of the unreality of the world; and that they do not, along with <term>Śaṅkara</term>, proclaim the absolute identity of the individual and the highest self.” (p. 100, Introduction to the <term>Vedānta Sūtras</term>).

And he proves also that this was consistent with the teachings of the <term>Upanishats</term> themselves.

What gave it its special mark, however, is the peculiar relation which <term>Badarāyaṇa</term> proposed between God and the world, which is the product of *<term>Māyā</term>* or *<term>Prakriti</term>*. Although he held on to the distinction between the Supreme and the Human Spirit, he strongly fought against the old <term>Śāṅkhyan</term> view (comprising nearly all the six schools we enumerated above) that Matter was an entity independent from spirit, though like Leibniz he never denied its reality. He held that God was both the efficient and material cause of the Universe. This doctrine accordingly received its name of *<term>Pariṇāma Vāda</term>* or *<term>Nimittopādāna-kāraṇa Vāda</term>*, while the Theistic <term>Śāṅkhyan</term> systems firmly maintained that God was only the efficient cause, though He was immanent in all of Nature. As there was nothing inherently harmful and destructive to all true religion and morality in this system of <term>Badarāyaṇa</term>, the <term>Tamil</term> Philosophers welcomed this

It details Badarāyaṇa's position that God is both the efficient and material cause of a real universe, a doctrine known as Pariṇāma Vāda.</summary>

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>.<-

...view also and declared they did not see much difference in the two views and goals postulated by both the old and new schools. Both <term>Śrikaṇta</term> and Saint <term>Tirumūlar</term> expressly make this declaration.

But there was another view which had been gaining ground since the days of <term>Gautama Buddha</term>, and which was connected with the peculiar theory of *<term>Māyā</term>* or illusion. <term>Buddha</term> declared that all existence was momentary—that there was no world, no mind, no soul, and no God—and that what really existed were the *<term>Skandhas</term>*. He taught that when this truth was perceived, all desire, birth, and suffering would cease, leading to the cessation of all existence, *<term>Nirvāṇa</term>*. The <term>Buddhists</term> were accordingly called *<term>Māyāvādis</term>*. But as the <term>Buddhist</term> theory destroyed the very core of Indian national beliefs, and as it also afforded no stable ground for a national existence based on morality and religion, it was pronounced heterodox. Nevertheless, the seeds sown by him were not in vain, and a Hindu school of *<term>Māyāvāda</term>* slowly emerged from the dying embers of this old, worn-out philosophy. Its greatest exponent was <term>Śaṅkara</term>.

This Hindu school of *<term>Māyāvāda</term>* existed for several centuries before <term>Śaṅkara</term>, but it appeared later than the time of St. <term>Māṇickavāchaka</term> and earlier than <term>Tirumūlar</term>, though both of them lived before <term>Śaṅkara</term>. <term>Śaṅkara</term>'s system is referred to as *<term>Māyāvāda</term>* in all the other prominent Hindu schools prevalent since his time. Although South Indian followers of <term>Śaṅkara</term> seem to hold some prejudice against the word because of its misuse by their opponents, followers of <term>Śaṅkara</term> in the North still call it the *<term>Māyāvāda</term>* today. In some of its extreme forms, it was also called “<term>Prachchanna Bauddham</term>” (disguised Buddhism). The great learning and the towering intellect of <term>Śaṅkara</term>, accompanied by his austere life, created a large following among the <term>Brahmans</term> of the <term>Śaiva</term> faith. The movement made great strides during the time of his illustrious follower <term>Sayana</term> (also known as <term>Vidyāraṇya</term>), who combined in himself both temporal and spiritual power. And since the first interpreters of Hinduism happened to be mostly

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>.<-

Among the Brahmans of this persuasion, during the century when Sanskrit oriental scholarship first emerged, this view of Hindu Philosophy gained the most currency among European scholars. However, there was no lack of scholars in the past—such as Colebrooke and Wilson—or like Colonel Jacob, Professor Kunte, and Dr. Thibaut in the present generation, who maintain that *<term>Māyāvāda</term>* is not the authentic and true exposition of the <term>Veda</term> or the <term>Vedānta</term>. Professor Max Muller—one of the most learned and earnest students of Indian Philosophy to have ever lived—initially held very firmly to the opposing view, but he slowly yielded and accepted Dr. Thibaut’s conclusions as correct. We may add that Professor Macdonell repeats the older view, and Professor Deussen is the greatest follower of <term>Śaṅkara</term> today.

There is another significant factor in the growth of Indian Religion and Philosophy that we have not taken note of all this time, and which receives no attention at all from European scholars. This is the influence of the *<term>Āgamas</term>* or *<term>Tantras</term>*. A figure as well-informed as Swami Vivekananda has declared:

> “As to their influence, apart from the <term>Shrauta</term> and <term>Smarta</term> rituals, all other forms of ritual observed from the Himalayas to Cape Comorin have been taken from the <term>Tantras</term>; and they guide the worship of the <term>Shaktas</term>, <term>Shaivas</term>, and <term>Vaishnavas</term> and all others alike.”

But who were the authors of these works, when did they become popular, and what great power did they have to dominate the religion of the whole of India? The same Swami observes:

> “The <term>Tantras</term>, as we have said, represent the <term>Vedic</term> rituals in a modified form. Before anyone jumps to the most absurd conclusions about them, I would advise them to read the *<term>Tantras</term>* in connection with the *<term>Brāhmaṇas</term>*, especially the *<term>Adhvaryu</term>* portion. Most of the *<term>Mantras</term>* used in the *<term>Tantras</term>* will be found to be taken word-for-word from these *<term>Brāhmaṇas</term>*.”

But it should be noted at the same time that while the *<term>Brāhmaṇas</term>* prescribe the use of these mantras in connection with *<term>yajñas</term>* or sacrifices, these <term>Tantras</term> prescribe their use in connection with the worship of some deity or other.
