-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>.<-

sacrifices being well known to be only the first three <term>Purushārthas</term>, by the worship of the various Powers of Nature, the object of <term>Tantric</term> or <term>Āgamic</term> worship was the attainment of the fourth <term>Purushārtha</term> or <term>Moksha</term>. By the time we reach the <term>Upaniṣhad</term> period, we can see how a new and spiritual interpretation was placed upon the old <term>Vedic</term> sacrifices, and the uselessness of sacrifice as an end in itself was strongly declared. M. Barth says:

> "Sacrifice is only an act of preparation. It is the best of acts, but it is an act and its fruit is consequently perishable. Accordingly, although whole sections of these treatises (<term>Upaniṣhads</term>) are taken up exclusively with speculations on the rites, what they teach may be summed up in the words of the <term>Muṇḍaka Upaniṣhad</term>: 'Know the <term>Ātman</term> alone and away with everything else; it alone is the bridge to immortality.' The <term>Veda</term> itself and the whole cycle of sacred science are quite as sweepingly consigned to the second place. The <term>Veda</term> is not the true <term>Brahman</term>; it is only its reflection; and the science of this imperfect <term>Brahman</term>, this <term>Śabda Brahman</term> or <term>Brahman</term> in words, is only a science of a lower order. The true science is that which has the true <term>Brahman</term>, the <term>Parabrahman</term>, for its subject."

As the story in the <term>Kena Upaniṣhad</term> will show, the most powerful of the <term>Rig Veda</term> deities—<term>Indra</term>, <term>Agni</term>, <term>Vāyu</term>, and <term>Varuṇa</term>—were also relegated to a secondary place; and the worship of the only One, without a second, the Consort of <term>Umā Haimavatī</term>, was commenced. The <term>Kena Upaniṣhad</term> story is repeated in the <term>Purāṇas</term>; the Supreme <term>Brahman</term> is mentioned there as <term>Śiva</term> and <term>Rudra</term>. And the story of <term>Rudra</term> destroying <term>Daksha’s</term> sacrifice and disgracing the Gods who took part in the sacrifice—with the sequel of His consort, named then <term>Dākshāyaṇi</term> (the fruit or spirit of sacrifice), being reborn as <term>Umā</term> (wisdom or <term>Brahmajñāna</term>) <term>Haimavatī</term>—would seem to precede the story in the <term>Kena Upaniṣhad</term>. The story of the desecration of the sacrifice of the <term>Rishis</term> of <term>Dārukāvana</term> by <term>Śiva</term> and <term>Vishṇu</term> would point to the same moral. So that, by this time, the backbone of the old unmeaning <term>Vedic</term> sacrifices

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>.<-

stagnated in the Godless school of <term>Mimāmsa</term> was truly broken; and it was here that the <term>Āgamas</term> intervened and used the same old <term>Mantras</term> again but with a new force and significance, removing whatever was meaningless and preserving only what was useful. It also substituted new symbols while keeping the old names. From this time, therefore, modern Hinduism and the Hindu system of worship may be said to have begun. However, for these beginnings, we must go far back, beyond the days of the <term>Mahābhārata</term> and the <term>Purāṇas</term>, as the <term>Āgama</term> doctrines and rituals are fully intertwined with them.

A clear advance in the use of symbols was also made, while at the same time effectively preserving the distinction between symbols and truth through the use of proper words. The <term>Śabda Brahman</term> or the <term>Praṇava</term> was only a symbol and not the truth, as imagined by the <term>Mimāmsakas</term>, and it was called a mark or <term>Liṅga</term>. And the visual mark of the <term>Praṇava</term> (the <term>Liṅga</term> is merely the <term>Praṇava</term> as represented to the eye), the <term>Liṅga</term>, became the universal symbol of God and the object of worship, just as the <term>Praṇava</term> in mantra or sound form had been before. In the new system of worship, the temples that were built were modeled more on the old <term>yajña-śāla</term>; and the <term>yūpa stambha</term> (<term>Dhvaja-stambha</term>) and <term>Balipīṭha</term>, <term>Paśu</term> (<term>Baśava</term> or <term>Nandi</term>), and the Gods in their various places were also retained; and a <term>Brahmotsava</term> virtually replaced the old sacrifice.<note>In initiating and performing a <term>Brahmotsava</term>, the priests technically observe almost the same rituals as in initiating and performing a great sacrifice. There is a <term>Yajña Śāla</term> in every <term>Śaiva</term> temple in which the fire is ignited by the <term>Dikshita</term>, and the <term>Dhvaja Ārohaṇa</term> is performed by hoisting a flag with the figure of a bull (<term>Paśu</term> or <term>Baśava</term>) on the <term>Yūpastambha</term> and tying <term>Kusa grass</term> to the post. The <term>Paśu</term> and the <term>Kusa grass</term> represent simply the soul or <term>jīva</term> that was bound and offered in sacrifice. After <term>Avarohaṇa</term>, the soul or <term>Paśu</term> becomes free and is no longer called <term>Paśu</term>, but is called God or <term>Nandi</term>—the blissful. It would require more space for us to draw out here the parallel between the <term>Yajña Śāla</term> and a Hindu temple.</note> In the field of philosophy, it did as much to systematize and build up into a whole what

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>.<-

had been in a scattered form until now, and it served a greater purpose in fully illustrating the all-pervasiveness and transcendence of God over both <term>Chetana</term> and <term>Achetana Prapañcha</term>—the world of souls and the world of matter. Establishing the postulate of God’s supreme transcendence was the specific goal of the <term>Āgama Philosophy</term>. Because this was the highest end and truth, it was called <term>Siddhānta</term> *par excellence*, as distinguished from the <term>Vedānta</term>, which led the seeker only to certain spiritual stages. It divided all philosophy and religion into four paths or <term>Mārgas</term>, respectively called <term>Chariya</term>, <term>Kriya</term>, <term>Yoga</term>, and <term>Jñāna</term>; these were otherwise known as <term>Dāsa Mārga</term>, <term>Satputra Mārga</term>, <term>Saha Mārga</term>, and <term>San Mārga</term>. In the exposition of these paths, it revealed a thoroughly reasoned system of practical philosophy, which neither contradicted our experience nor violated our most cherished moral and religious sentiments. It was a system of thought that was progressive and built on an unshakeable foundation, leading step-by-step to higher knowledge. It was a system which, by preserving and identifying the essential differences between God, Soul, and Matter, established a true relationship between them. This led to the highest monistic knowledge—a system that was simultaneously dualism and non-dualism, <term>Dvaita</term> and <term>Advaita</term>; a system which appealed alike

---

<note>Compare Garbe, *The Philosophy of Ancient India*, p. 30. "As for those who feel inclined to view a dualistic conception of the world with disdain from a monistic perspective, the words of E. Roer in the Introduction of the <term>Bhāshaparichcheda</term> (p. XVI) may be quoted: 'Though a higher development of philosophy may remove the distinctions between soul and matter—that is, may recognize matter, or what is perceived as matter, as identical to the soul (as, for instance, Leibnitz did)—it is nevertheless certain that no true knowledge of the soul is possible without first drawing a clear line of distinction between the phenomena of matter and the soul.' This sharp line of distinction between the two domains was first drawn by <term>Kapila</term>. Knowledge of the difference between body and soul is one condition, and indeed an indispensable condition, for arriving at a true monism. Any worldview that confuses this difference can provide, at best, a one-sided monism (henism), whether it be spiritualism or an equally one-sided materialism."</note>

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANIṢHAT</term>.<-

to the peasant and the philosopher. Its system of practical religion, intended to secure the highest end and bliss, was also progressive, commencing from the simplest rituals in the adoration of God and leading to the highest <term>Yoga</term>, adapted to the means and capacity of both the lowest and the highest of human beings. Readers of <term>Svāmi Vivekananda's</term> lectures would have noted how these four paths are essential to any system of thought or religion that claims to be universal; and it is the peculiar boast of the *<term>Āgama</term>* or *<term>Tantra</term>* that it was the first to systematize this fourfold teaching. And it is in modern <term>Śaivism</term> and in the <term>Siddhānta Philosophy</term> that this fourfold aspect of religion and philosophy is wholly and fully preserved. <term>Śaivism</term> is a ritual <term>mārga</term>, a <term>bhakti mārga</term>, a <term>yoga mārga</term>, and a <term>jñāna mārga</term>. And should we wonder that the <term>Siddhānta Philosophy</term> of today is as much a puzzle to outsiders as the philosophy of our <term>Upaniṣhat</term> and the <term>Gītā</term>? The <term>Siddhānti's</term> definition of *<term>Advaita</term>* as "neither one, nor two, nor neither" will bring out the puzzle more prominently. It is a system of dualism, and it is also a system of non-dualism, but it differs from the other schools of dualism and non-dualism. What was upheld in the <term>Siddhānta</term> as mere paths or *<term>mārga</term>*, or *<term>Sādhana</term>* (means to reach the highest end), had come to be individually mistaken for the end itself; what was upheld as the mere symbol of the highest truth had come to be mistaken for the truth itself. What was declared as unprovable, indescribable, unknowable, and unenjoyable as long as man was in a state of bondage was held by these sectarians as proved and seen. What was once the purest and most transcendent monotheism degenerated into a most crude anthropomorphism and blatant pantheism.

<term>Śaivism</term> is not anthropomorphic, but symbolic. How can it be otherwise, when it draws such minute distinctions between God, Soul, and Matter? And a system of symbolism is quite consistent with the highest transcendental religion and philosophy; in fact, all our real knowledge is more truly symbolic than otherwise. In the view of the <term>Siddhānti</term>, the <term>Upaniṣhats</term>, though they deal with all the four paths, are

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANISHAT</term>.<-

...especially the textbooks of the <term>Yogapāda</term> or <term>Sahamārga</term>, where certain <term>Bhāvanas</term> or <term>Vidyas</term>—intended to create and bring about the highest <term>Nirvāna</term>, union, and freedom from <term>Pāśa</term>—are more fully explained and illustrated.

The preceding brief overview of the past history of Indian philosophy will clear the ground significantly for a proper understanding of our particular <term>Upanishat</term> in question.

We may, therefore, state that the <term>Śvetāśvatara Upanishat</term> is a genuine <term>Upanishat</term> of the Black <term>Yajur Veda</term> and is one of the oldest of its kind. It is not a sectarian <term>Upanishat</term>. It belongs more properly to the <term>Yoga Pāda</term> stage of teaching, although the other <term>Pādas</term> are also briefly touched upon and alluded to. It expounds both a theoretical philosophy and a practical religion that is all-comprehensive and all-embracing—a system that was simultaneously <term>Śāṅkhya</term> and <term>Yoga</term>, dualistic and monistic, and appealing to all classes of society.

It establishes the distinction between three <term>padārthas</term> or categories in clear terms. These are: God, the many souls, and matter or <term>Pāśa</term>.

> "Two birds, inseparable friends, cling to the same tree. One of them eats the sweet fruits, while the other looks on without eating" (iv. 6), which is explained in less figurative language in the next mantra:

> "On the same tree, man (<term>Anīśa</term>) sits grieving, immersed and bewildered by his own impotence. But when he sees the other, <term>Īśa</term>, contented, and knows His glory, then his grief passes away."

That this is the highest teaching of the <term>Ṛig Veda</term> is pointed out in the next verse:

> "Of what use is the <term>Ṛig Veda</term> to him who does not know that indestructible Being (<term>Akshara</term>) of the <term>Ṛig Veda</term>, that Highest Ether (<term>Parama Vyomam</term>) wherein all the Gods reside? Those only who know It rest contented."

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANISHAT</term>.<-

Does it need to be pointed out that the 6th verse itself is found in the <term>Rig Veda</term> (I, 164-20), is repeated in the <term>Atharva Veda</term>, and is so popular a passage that the <term>Katha</term> (iii. 1) and <term>Muṇḍaka</term> (iii. 11) also quote it?

These verses highlight the distinction between God and the soul, <term>Iśa</term> and <term>Aniśa</term>, as the spectator and the enjoyer respectively. The soul enjoys and performs <term>karma</term> while encased in the body (the tree); but though God is immanent in the soul and in the body, the works and their fruit do not cling to Him or taint Him. After the soul has eaten the fruits and realizes the greatness of God and its own insignificance, its sufferings cease.

The previous mantra (iv. 5) is also a famous and much-debated passage, and it was poorly translated by Professor Max Müller. The translation by G. R. S. Mead and <term>Chattopādhyāya</term> is literal and correct. 

> “Indeed, that one unborn (<term>Aja</term>-soul) sleeps in the arms of one unborn (nature, <term>Pradhāna</term>), enjoying her (nature—red, white, and black), who brings forth numerous offspring like herself. But when her charms have been enjoyed, he (the soul) leaves her (<term>prakṛiti</term>) side—the other who is unborn, the <term>Anyata</term> (Lord).” <note>If we read “he quits her side for the other,” it makes the sense complete.</note>

There is absolutely no mistaking this plain statement of the three <term>Padārtas</term> as eternal, as well as their relationship; all three are called *Unborn*, <term>Aja</term>, or uncreated. But the word to be noted here is the word ‘other’ (‘<term>Anya</term>’), which is almost a technical term or catchphrase meaning God, the Supreme. It occurs again in (V. 1).

> “In the imperishable and infinite highest <term>Brahman</term>, wherein the two, <term>Vidya</term> (<term>Vijñāna-Ātma</term>) and <term>Avidya</term>, are hidden, the one, <term>Avidya</term>, perishes; the other, <term>Vidya</term>, is immortal; but He who controls both <term>Vidya</term> and <term>Avidya</term> is *another* (<term>Anyatha</term>).” 

And in the subsequent verses, this *another*

is clearly pointed out to be the only One God, without a second, the ruler of all, the generator of all, and the supporter (ripener) of all. This forms the subject of discussion by *<term>Bādarāyaṇa</term>* in I, ii, 21. And the famous passage in the *<term>Bṛihadāraṇyaka</term>* is referred to. "He who dwells in *<term>Ātmā</term>* (<term>Vijñāna</term>) and is *different from <term>Ātmā</term>*, whom the *<term>Ātmā</term>* does not know, whose body *<term>Ātmā</term>* is, and who controls (rules) *<term>Ātmā</term>* from within—He is your *<term>Ātmā</term>*, the inner controller, the immortal" (iii, 7, 22).

In vi. 6, God is also called the *<term>Anya</term>*—the other. It occurs again in the *<term>Gītā</term>*, xv. 17. The previous verse postulates two entities—matter and soul—and the next verse proceeds to postulate "*another*." "But there is *another*, namely, the Supreme Being, called *<term>Paramātmā</term>*, who, being the everlasting *<term>Iśvara</term>* and pervading the three worlds, sustains them." The fact that the very use of the word is solely to emphasize God's transcendence over the world of matter and of souls—as opposed to people who only postulated two *<term>Padārthas</term>*, or would identify God, the supreme *<term>Iśvara</term>*, with matter or soul—is fully brought out in the next verse.

"As I transcend the perishable (*<term>Pradhāna</term>*) and as I am higher than even the Imperishable (soul), I am celebrated in the world and sung in the Vedas as *<term>Purushottama</term>*."

The most common fallacy committed when the eternity of matter and souls is postulated is imagining that this, in any way, affects God's transcendence and immanence. Although He pervades all and envelops all, creates and sustains and takes them back again into Himself—though He is the God in the fire, the God in the water, the God who has entered the whole world, in plants and trees and in everything else (ii. 17)—yet He stands behind all time and all persons (vii. 16) and is beyond all *<term>tattvas</term>*. (Verse 15.)

"He is the one God (*<term>Eko Deva</term>*), hidden in all beings, all-pervading, the *<term>Antarātma</term>* of all things, watching over all works, dwelling in all beings, the witness, the perceiver, the"

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANISHAT</term>.<-

Only One, <term>Nirguṇa</term> (Being) vi. 11. And in Verse 16, He is called the first cause, Himself uncaused, the all-knower, the master of *Nature and Man*. And by the supreme statement “*Ekohi <term>Rudra</term> nadvittiya tasthe*,” (There is only One <term>Rudra</term>, they do not allow a second) the complete subordination of all other things to Him is clearly postulated. There is nothing else in His presence, as no <term>Asat</term> can subsist in the Presence of the <term>Sat</term>, just as no darkness can subsist in the presence of light. And He is called Light (iii, 12), the Light by which all other lights—the sun, the moon, the stars, and the lightnings—are lighted, (vi. 14) and He is the great <term>Purusha</term>, like the Sun in luster, beyond darkness. (iii. 8.)

There is only one other passage which we have to quote while we are dealing with the three eternal postulates of this <term>Upanishat</term>. These are Verses 8 and 9 in the first <term>Adhyāya</term> itself. In these also the distinctions between the Supreme God and the bound soul, as <term>Īśa</term> and <term>Anīśa</term>, <term>Jña</term> and <term>Ajña</term>, and the third, <term>Pradhāna</term>, unborn though perishable and ever-changing, are finely drawn.

In dealing with the personality of God, who is called in the <term>Upanishats</term> as <term>Deva</term>, <term>Hara</term>, <term>Vaśi</term>, <term>Śiva</term>, <term>Purusha</term>, <term>Brahman</term>, <term>Paramātmā</term>, <term>Īśa</term>, and <term>Īśvara</term>, etc., we have to remark that the <term>Upanishat</term> makes no distinction between a Higher and a Lower <term>Brahman</term>; rather, there are no statements made about the Lower God or Gods, except one verse in V. 3, where the Supreme Lord and <term>Mahātmā</term> is said to have created the Lords, and <term>Brahma</term> or <term>Hiranyagarbha</term> is referred to as such a lord. But every statement made regarding God, by any of the names we have mentioned above, clearly refers to the one*, without a second, the Highest <term>Brahman</term>, who is also

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANISHAT</term>.<-

<term>Nirguṇa</term>. In various passages, this Highest Being is said to create, sustain, and destroy the worlds. What some people would not believe is how a Being addressed as <term>Hara</term> and <term>Śiva</term>, <term>Īśa</term> and <term>Īśvara</term> could be the <term>Nirguṇa</term> Absolute <term>Brahman</term>. They frequently associate this name with the <term>Rudra</term> or <term>Śiva</term> of the Hindu Trinity. However, it will be news to these people that even the <term>Rudra</term> of the Trinity is <term>Nirguṇa</term> and not <term>Saguṇa</term>. Absolutely no passage can be found in any of the <term>Upanishats</term>, or even in the <term>Purāṇas</term> and the <term>Itihāsas</term>, in which even the <term>Śiva</term> or <term>Rudra</term> of the Trinity is called <term>Saguṇa</term>. <term>Saguṇa</term> means having bodies (qualities) formed out of <term>Prakṛiti</term>; and when <term>Prakṛiti</term> is itself resolved into its original condition and reproduced by this <term>Rudra</term> of the Trinity, this <term>Prakṛiti</term> could not act as his vestment.

But the <term>Rudra</term> and <term>Śiva</term> of our <term>Upanishat</term> are clearly set forth in other <term>Upanishats</term> as the fourth—*chaturtam* and *Turiyam*—transcending the Trinity and being without a second.

> "Satyam Jñānam, Anantam Brahma,
> Ananda Rupam, Amritam Yad Vibhuti,
> Śantam Śivam Advaitam." — (*Tait Up.*)

> "*Śivam, Śantam, Advaitam*
> Chaturtham, manyante," — (*Ramatapini*).

> "Dhyāyeteesanam, pradhyāyedavyam,
> Sarvamidam, Brahma Vishṇu Rudrendrasthe,
> Sarve Samprāsuyante, Sarvanichendryanicha;
> Sahabhutaih Nakaranam Karanam Dhāta Dhyāta
> Karanantu Dhyeyah Sarvaiswarya Sampannah
> Sarveswsrah Sambhurakasa Madhye.
> Śiva eko Dhyayet: Sivankara, Sarvam
> Anyat Parityaja. — (*Atharva Sikha*).

"Adore the most adorable <term>Īśāna</term>. <term>Brahma</term>, <term>Viṣṇu</term>, <term>Rudra</term>, <term>Indra</term>, and others have an origin. All the senses originate with the elements. The first cause and cause of causes has no origin. The Bestower of all prosperity, the Lord of all, *<term>Sambhu</term>*, He should be contemplated in the middle of the"

<meta>This page continues the argument that Śiva, as described in the Upanishats, is the supreme Nirguṇa Brahman rather than a limited deity within the Trinity. It clarifies the philosophical definitions of Nirguṇa and Saguṇa to correct common misunderstandings by outside observers.</meta>

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANISHAT</term>.<-

...<term>Ākāśa</term>... <term>Śiva</term>, the one alone, should be contemplated; the Doer of Good; all else should be given up.” (<term>Atharva Śikhā</term>)
“The mystical and immutable one, which, being composed of three letters A, U, and M, signifies successively the three Vedas, the three states of life (<term>Jāgra</term>, <term>Svapna</term>, and <term>Sushupti</term>), the three worlds (heaven, hell, and earth), and the three gods (<term>Brahma</term>, <term>Vishṇu</term>, and <term>Rudra</term>); and by its nasal sound (<term>Ardhamātra</term>), it is indicative of Your *fourth* office as the Supreme Lord of all (<term>Parameśvara</term>)<note>A Christian missionary writing to the *Christian College Magazine* wonders how <term>Vemana</term>, the famous Telugu poet, could speak of <term>Śiva</term> as other than the Hindu triad of <term>Brahma</term>, <term>Vishṇu</term>, and <term>Rudra</term>. Compare <term>Bharthari</term>’s <term>Śatakas</term> for the popular conception of <term>Śiva</term>.</note> and forever expresses and sets forth Your collective forms.” (<term>Mahimna Stotra</term>). And the same mistake is committed by outsiders in supposing that the God of the <term>Śaivas</term> is only one member of the trinity. Any book in Tamil or Sanskrit taken at random will at once correct this misunderstanding, and one will find that the only God held up for the highest worship is the highest <term>Nirguṇa</term> <term>Parama Śiva</term>, and not merely one of the trinity. Great confusion is caused in the use of the words <term>Nirguṇa</term><note>By <term>Nirguṇa</term>, we mean ‘without Prakritic qualities’ and by <term>Saguṇa</term>, ‘clothed in Prakritic qualities.’ And God could therefore be both <term>Nirguṇa</term> and Personal in Emerson’s sense.</note> and <term>Saguṇa</term> by translating them as "impersonal" and "personal," respectively. And Europeans themselves are not agreed as to the use of these words. According to Webster, the word ‘personal’ implies limitation, but other eminent persons like Emerson, Lotze, etc., say there is no such implication. Until the acceptance of these words is therefore settled, we should not make confusion worse confounded by rendering <term>Nirguṇa</term> and <term>Saguṇa</term> as Impersonal and Personal.

So far, there can be no doubt on the nature of the Godhead described in our <term>Upanishat</term>.

> “When there was no darkness, nor day nor night, nor <term>Sat</term>, nor <term>Asat</term>, then <term>Śiva</term> alone existed (<term>Śiva eva Kevalah</term>). That is the absolute, that the adorable condition of the Lord. From that too had come forth the wisdom of old—(<term>jñānāśakti</term>).” (iv, 18).

<summary>The author argues that Śiva is the supreme, absolute reality (Nirguṇa Parama Śiva) beyond the Hindu Trinity, correcting the common Western misconception that labels Śiva as merely a personal deity. He also clarifies that "Nirguṇa" refers to being free from material qualities rather than being "impersonal" in a limiting sense.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Śvetāśvatara Upanishat, focusing on the gender-transcendent nature of the Divine and the philosophical distinction between the individual soul and the Supreme Being.</meta>

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANISHAT</term>.<-

> “He is the eternal and infinite, Unborn Being, partless, action-less, tranquil, without taint, without fault, the Highest Bridge to Immortality (vi. 19). He is the causeless first cause, the all-knower, the all-pervader, the creator, sustainer, and liberator of the world, the end and aim of all religion and of all philosophy; He is the <term>Iśvara</term> of <term>Iśvaras</term>, <term>Maheśvara</term>, the God supreme of Gods, the King of kings, the Supreme of the supreme, the <term>Iśa</term> of the Universe” (vi. 7.)

There is one other matter to be considered in the nature of the Divine Personality. God is spoken of both in masculine and in neuter, and that in the same verse—a peculiarity which is noticeable in modern <term>Śaivaism</term>. And God is addressed in all forms as ‘He,’ ‘She,’ and ‘It.’ <term>Śivaḥ</term>, <term>Śivā</term>, and <term>Śivam</term>.<note><term>Śivam</term> in Sanskrit, they say, is not the neuter of <term>Śiva</term>. But somehow this neuter form is quite prevalent in Tamil.</note> And the reason is not as stated by Prof. Max Müller, in his note under Verse 16, Chapter iii, that the gender changes frequently according to whether the author thinks of either the <term>Brahman</term> or its impersonation as “<term>Iśa</term>, Lord.” To the Indian, whether he addresses his God as <term>Śiva</term> or <term>Śivam</term>, he is addressing the same Supreme Personality who is neither male nor female nor neuter, and there is no jarring to him in the sense, as there would be to a Christian, who could only think of and address God in the masculine gender.

The Upanishat does not recognize any difference between the use of ‘It’ and ‘He,’ and it does not suggest that by using ‘It’ instead of ‘He,’ a Higher Being is reached.

Coming now to the nature of the soul, as set forth in this Upanishat, the first thing to be noticed is that the <term>Jīva</term> is very often spoken of simply as <term>Ātmā</term> and distinguished from God. The other appellations it receives are <term>Purusha</term>, <term>Anīśa</term>, <term>Ajña</term>, the <term>Hamsa</term>, and <term>Vidyā</term>, and these are to distinguish it from *the other*, the <term>Paramātma</term>, the <term>Parama Purusha</term>, <term>Iśa</term>, and <term>Jña</term>.

This soul is bound because he is not God (i. 8), and because he is ignorant of himself and of the self within him...

<summary>The author explains that the Śvetāśvatara Upanishat treats God as a gender-transcendent Supreme Personality, addressed interchangeably as male, female, or neuter. It also distinguishes the individual soul (Jīva) from the Supreme Soul (Paramātma), noting that the soul's bondage is caused by its ignorance of its true nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Śvetāśvatara Upanishat, moving from the nature of the Divine to the nature and journey of the individual soul (Antaratma) toward liberation through grace.</meta>

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANISHAT</term>.<-

*Antarātma*). This soul is not self-dependent (i. 2). This soul is confined in the *Pura* (the city-body) of nine gates—that is, it is limited and “flutters about,” is changeable, and enjoys the fruits of pleasures and pains (even pains are a pleasure to him, the ignorant soul). He fondly clings to the body and performs *karma* (iii, 18. iv. 5 and 6.)

> “But he who is endowed with qualities and performs *Karma* that is to bear fruit, and enjoys the reward of whatever he has done, migrates through his own works; he is the lord of life, assuming all forms, led by the three *guṇas* and the three paths” (vi. 7).

And yet this soul is in the image of God, is infinite and brilliant like the Sun, endowed with *Ichcha* (will) and *Jñāna* (knowledge), and is sinless.

The Supreme One—who witnesses all his doings, dwelling within him without Himself being tainted by the contact—helps to secure the ripening of his *mala* (impurity). He waits until the soul attains that condition of perfect balance in good and evil (v. 5). Through the performance of *Chariya*, *Kriya*, and *Yoga* (good works, penance, and meditation) with love, knowledge, and the syllable *Praṇava*, the soul is blessed by the Lord (i. 6). God’s grace descends on him (vi. 21 and iii, 20), and he knows and sees, with *Manas* (the supreme grace of God—the spiritual eye) (v. 14) ‘The *Purusham Mahantam Adityā Varṇam, tamasaḥ parastat*’ (The Great Person, brilliant as the Sun, beyond darkness). His fetters (*Pāśa*) fall off, sufferings cease, and he enters the Bliss of the Supreme Brahman and Eternal Peace.

That *Īśvara Prasādam* (iii. 20) or *Anugraham* (grace) is necessary is a common belief of the people, and this doctrine is not peculiar to this Upanishat alone. The Katha Upanishat expresses the same doctrine in much stronger language: “That *Ātmā* (God) cannot be gained by the Veda, nor by understanding, nor by much learning. He whom *Ātmā* (God) chooses, by him the *Ātmā* (God) can be gained” (1. 2. 23). But even the supreme Almighty (God) cannot help him if he has not turned away from wickedness and is not tranquil, subdued, and at

...rest, dedicating (<term>Arpaṇam</term>) all his words, deeds, and thoughts to God (i. 24).

That the doctrine of <term>Bhakti</term> is well set forth in the oldest <term>Upanishads</term> and the <term>Vedas</term> will be apparent by reading the texts collected by Dr. Muir in his learned *Metrical translations from Sanskrit* under the heading of '<term>Śraddha</term> and <term>Bhakti</term>.' Incidentally, this <term>Śraddha</term> and <term>Bhakti</term> is not to be understood as a manifestation of feeling only—necessary at one stage of man’s spiritual evolution and unnecessary at another—but this love is essential to the aspirant, whether he is a <term>Dāsamārgi</term>, <term>Satputramārgi</term>, <term>Yogamārgi</term>, or <term>Jñānamārgi</term>. We have already pointed out above that these four paths grow out of one another and are not independent; each one of these is hardly possible to reach without going through the lower rungs of the ladder.

All of the <term>Upanishads</term> discuss the particular <term>Upāsana</term> or <term>Upāsanas</term> which are required for the salvation of the bound soul, and these <term>Upāsanas</term> are also called <term>Vidyās</term>.

Of these various <term>Vidyās</term>, what is called the <term>Dahara Upāsana</term> or <term>Vidyā</term> is the most favored of all the <term>Upāsanas</term> in the <term>Śvetāśvatara</term>, <term>Chāndogya</term>, <term>Bṛihadāraṇyaka</term>, <term>Kaṭha</term>, <term>Muṇḍaka</term>, <term>Kaivalya</term>, <term>Atharva Śikha</term>, and in the <term>Bhagavad Gītā</term>.

The references to this highest <term>Yoga</term> practice are most numerous in the <term>Upanishads</term>, and the consistency of the various references forms the subject of discussion in the <term>Vedānta Sūtras</term> (iii. 3. 23).

The famous passages are those that occur in the <term>Chāndogya Upanishad</term>, commencing with the sentences "There is the city<note>This city is exactly reproduced in modern symbolism in the Great Temple of Chidambaram.</note> of <term>Brahman</term>" (viii. 1. 1) and "All this is <term>Brahman</term>" (iii. 14. 1 to 4). This worship or <term>Yoga</term> consists of the aspirant contemplating the Supreme One in his heart as the Person of Light and

<meta>This page continues the technical and philosophical analysis of the heart as the dwelling place of the Divine, specifically defining the terms used to describe the nature of God's power and presence.</meta>

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANISHAD</term>.<-

...as <term>Ākāśa</term>, as <term>Satchidānanda Parameśvara</term>, with the particular formula that "God is in all beings and all beings are in God." And various synonyms are used to denote this *heart of man*, such as <term>Dahara</term> (subtle), <term>Guha</term> (cave), <term>Puṇḍarīka</term> (lotus), <term>Brahmapura</term> (city), and <term>Hridaya</term> (heart).

The meaning of the words <term>Ākāśa</term> and <term>Vyoma</term> must also be carefully noted. They are synonymous and do not mean the <term>Bhūta Ākāśa</term> (elemental space), nor the <term>Māyāśakti</term> or <term>Avidyā</term> (ignorance); rather, as interpreted by the <term>Purāṇas</term> themselves, they mean <term>Chit</term> or <term>Jñāna</term>—Light or Grace—which is the <term>Parāśakti</term> of the Supreme <term>Iśvara</term>. That this <term>Ākāśa</term> is <term>Chit</term> (consciousness) and not <term>Achit</term> (insentient matter) is further proved by the phrases *<term>Chitākāśa</term>* and *<term>Chidambara</term>*. This <term>Chit Śakti</term> is the <term>Devātma-Śakti</term> of our <term>Upanishad</term>, which is inherent and concealed in Him (i. 3), and the supreme <term>Śakti</term>, which is revealed as manifold, *inherent* (<term>Śiva</term>), and manifesting as <term>Kriyā</term> (action) and <term>Jñāna</term> (knowledge) (vi. 8). It is this which is called *<term>Umā</term>*, Light, *<term>Bhargas</term>*†, *<term>Sāvitrī</term>*, and *<term>Gāyatri</term>*. Therefore, when we understand this <term>Ākāśa</term> as light and knowledge—the Supreme <term>Śakti</term> of God—its description as the highest light, the revealer of all forms, and the highest object of adoration becomes clear. The description of God both as <term>Ākāśa</term> (<term>Śakti</term>) and as dwelling in <term>Ākāśa</term> (<term>Śakti</term>) is not conflicting, just as no distinction is made between the sun and its light, much less between God and His power.‡

It is this <term>Jñāna Śakti</term> who gives form, shape, life, love, and light to the <term>Chetana</term> and <term>Achetana Prapañcha</term> (the conscious and unconscious world); but the substance or <term>Upādāna</term>¶ out of which this

<meta>This page continues the technical and philosophical analysis of the nature of God's power and His relationship with the material world, focusing on the concepts of Māyā and the all-pervasive presence of the Divine as the subtle Light or consciousness.</meta>

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANISHAD</term>.<-
->139<-

That from which <term>Prapañcha</term> is evolved is the <term>Māyā</term> or <term>Pradhāna</term>, which, also dwelling in Him, is drawn out and drawn in by the Supreme Power (<term>Śakti</term>) with the same ease and dexterity as a spider that spins its web out or in; or like a magician who draws forth fruits, flowers, and sweets from an empty basket. The <term>Māyā</term> (also meaning power) is also a <term>Śakti</term> of His (<term>Māyāśakti</term>), but it differs from the other <term>Śaktis</term>—<term>Ichchā</term>, <term>Jñāna</term>, and <term>Kriyā</term>—just as darkness differs from light. As darkness is necessary for rest and recuperation, so this power of God also works for our rest, recuperation, and salvation. And God is called the Lord of <term>Māyā</term> (<term>Māyin</term>) and is "beyond" all forms of the tree, as He transcends all the <term>Tattvas</term>, <term>Kalā</term>, etc., and transcends <term>Pradhāna</term>. The reason why we are required to contemplate God as <term>Ākāśa</term>, Light, or <term>Chit</term> is that by this Light alone can we know Him, and as such Light; it is as Light and <term>Chit</term> that God is immanent in the world and omnipresent. And this brings out again the reason why this <term>Chit</term> is called <term>Ākāśa</term>: it is the most subtle, invisible, and omnipresent element we have in Nature.

God is present in all nature and pervades it, as oil is in seeds, butter in ghee, and fire in wood (i. 15). And this all-pervasiveness is thus explained in a text of the <term>Atharva Śiras Upanishad</term>: "Why is it called <term>Sarva Vyāpi</term>? It is so called because, like ghee diffusing and soaking itself through and through the <term>Rūda</term> (milk or seed), it pervades every created thing through and through as warp and woof."

And because of this pervasiveness, since nothing could be imagined as existing outside of Him, the whole is also called <term>Brahman</term>. The whole—with its parts, limbs, and bodies (iv. 10) as the <term>Chetana-Achetana Prapañcha</term>—has <term>antaḥkaraṇa</term> as <term>Chit</term>.

...the bare ground, we naturally suppose there was some seed embedded in it without our knowledge, though the earth contained it and is essential for the support and growth of the plant. <meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on the Śvetāśvatara Upanishad, specifically exploring the non-dual relationship between Brahman, His energy (Śakti), and the universe as discussed by Śrīkaṇṭha Śivāchārya.</meta>

<term>Śakti</term>, and He Himself is the *Soul* of this vast whole. And as all of us form only parts of Him, we are also instructed to be kind to one another; for whatever we do to each other will also be done to His body. We quote the following from <term>Śrīkaṇṭha Śivāchārya</term>'s commentary in which this point is discussed:

> "All this is <term>Brahman</term>, as it begins, ends, and breathes in Him; and therefore, let a person meditate on Him."

> "This passage may be explained as follows: The origin, existence, and end of all this depends on <term>Brahman</term>. All this—both sentient and insentient existence—is truly <term>Brahman</term>, and therefore let a person meditate on <term>Brahman</term> with a tranquil mind. Just as water bubbles that have their origin, existence, and end in the ocean are found to be only forms of that ocean, so too, that which depends for its origin, etc., on <term>Brahman</term> associated with <term>Śakti</term> must be made of <term>Brahman</term> and nothing else. Nothing distinct from Him is ever perceived. Accordingly, in the <term>Atharva-Śiras</term>, it has been declared by <term>Iśāna</term> as follows:
> 
> > "I alone was at first; (alone) I am and shall be.
> > There is no one else distinct from Me."

And then it was declared by Him in the words "I am <term>Brahman</term>" that the whole universe is His own form. And in the words "He entered the more hidden from (or than) the hidden one," etc., His entry into the universe is given as the reason for the whole universe being His own form. Thus, this universe, having no origin, existence, or end outside of <term>Brahman</term>, is not a thing entirely distinct from <term>Brahman</term>. Accordingly, the learned say:

> "His <term>Śaktis</term> or energies form the whole world, and <term>Maheśa</term>, or the Great Lord, is the energetic <term>Śaktimān</term>. Never can energy exist distinct from the energetic. The unity of these two is eternal, like that of fire and heat, since an inseparability always exists between energy and the energetic. Therefore, supreme energy belongs to the supreme <term>Ātman</term>, since the two are related to each other as substance and

It emphasizes that because the universe originates and ends in Brahman, it is not distinct from Him, similar to bubbles in an ocean.</summary>

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANISHAD</term>.<-
->141<-

attribute. The energy of heat is not conceived to be distinct from fire," and so on.

The <term>Vāyu-Samhitā</term> also says: (Parva 25, chapters 18 and 19).

> "From <term>Śakti</term> down to the earth, (the whole world) is born of the principle <term>Śiva</term>. By Him alone it is pervaded, as a jar, etc., is pervaded by clay. His variegated Supreme <term>Śakti</term>, whose form is knowledge and bliss, appears as one and many, like the light of the sun."

The following passages of the <term>Śruti</term> speak of <term>Para-Brahman</term> as possessing infinite powers of creating, ruling, and maintaining the world, all inherent in Him.

> "His Supreme <term>Śakti</term> is spoken of as manifold, inherent, and endowed with the activity of knowledge and life." (<term>Śvetāśvatara Upanishad</term> 6:8).

> "Truly, <term>Rudra</term> is one—they did not acknowledge a second—who rules these worlds with the powers of ruling." (3:2).

In short, on the authority of the <term>Śruti</term>, <term>Smṛiti</term>, <term>Itihāsa</term>, <term>Purāṇa</term>, and the sayings of the learned, the Supreme <term>Śakti</term>—whose manifold manifestation is this whole universe of <term>Chit</term> (sentient) and <term>Achit</term> (insentient), and whose being is composed of Supreme Existence and Intelligence and is unlimited by space and time—is inherent in the nature of <term>Śiva</term>, the Supreme <term>Brahman</term>, and constitutes His own essential form and quality. Apart from <term>Śakti</term>, He cannot be the Omniscient, the Omnipotent, the cause of all, the all-controlling, the all-adorable, the all-gracious, the means of attaining all aspirations, and the omnipresent; and, moreover, such grand designations as "<term>Maheśvara</term>," the *Supreme Lord*, "<term>Mahādeva</term>," the *Supreme Deity*, and <term>Rudra</term>, the *expeller of pain*, cannot apply to Him. Thus, it is <term>Brahman</term> whose body is the whole sentient and insentient universe, and who is denoted by all words. Just as the word 'blue' denotes not only the blue color, but also the lotus which is of a blue color, so does the word 'universe' also denote <term>Brahman</term>. Therefore, such passages as "All is truly <term>Rudra</term>" teach that <term>Brahman</term> is denoted by all words. Accordingly, the passage

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument that the entire universe—both sentient and insentient—is the body of Brahman (Shiva), and that worshiping the universe is equivalent to worshiping Him.</meta>

-># THE <term>ŚVETĀŚVATARA UPANISHAD</term>.<-
->142<-

“All this, truly, is <term>Brahman</term>” refers to <term>Brahman</term>, whose body is the whole of the sentient and insentient universe. Since the universe is thus a form of <term>Brahman</term> and is therefore not an object of hatred, etc., let everyone be peaceful at heart and worship <term>Brahman</term>. This doctrine is clearly explained even in the <term>Puranic</term> texts such as the following: “The body of the God of Gods is this universe, both moving and unmoving. This, the <term>Jivas</term> (<term>Pasus</term>) do not know, due to their powerful bondage. They say consciousness is <term>Vidya</term>, and unconsciousness is <term>Avidya</term>. The whole universe of <term>Vidya</term> and <term>Avidya</term> undoubtedly forms the body of the Lord, the first cause of all; for the whole universe is subject to Him.”

“The word ‘*sat*’ is used by the wise to denote the real and the good, and ‘*asat*’ is used by Vedic teachers to denote the contrary. The whole universe of the *sat* and the *asat* is the body of Him who is on high. Just as the branches of a tree are nourished by watering its roots, so the universe, which is His body, is nourished by the worship of <term>Shiva</term>. <term>Atman</term> is the eighth body of <term>Shiva</term>, the <term>Paramesvara</term>, pervading all other bodies.

“Therefore, the whole universe is ensouled by <term>Shiva</term>. If any embodied being whatsoever is subjected to constraint, it will be utterly offensive to the eight-bodied Lord; of this there is no doubt. Doing good to all, being kind to all, and affording shelter to all—this they hold to be the worship of <term>Shiva</term>,” and so on.

“Since <term>Brahman</term> is all-formed, it is only right to say ‘all is <term>Brahman</term>’ and that everyone should be peaceful and worship ‘<term>Brahman</term>.’ Therefore, it is <term>Brahman</term> who, in the opening passage, is stated to be the object of worship; He is also spoken of as <term>manomaya</term>, as partaking of the nature of the mind (<term>manas</term>), and so on. Nor should it be supposed that partaking of the nature of <term>manas</term> is a characteristic mark of a worldly being (<term>samsarin</term>); for <term>Brahman</term> may limit Himself by assuming a shape that can become an object of worship.”

# A CHAPTER FROM THE KURAL.

Anyone who has even the slightest insight into the pages of the sacred <term>Kuraḷ</term> will surely agree with the observation of the experienced <term>Tamil</term> scholar, <term>Rev. Dr. G. U. Pope</term>, that this is a work without equal in any language. The merits of the work are so obvious that, even at its very beginning, it received the highest praises from the most distinguished scholars of the day—the <term>Paṇḍits</term> of the famous <term>Madura College</term>, or <term>Saṅgam</term>. The tradition that the author was of humble birth only increases the value of the acclaim showered upon him. One of the scholars compares it to the <term>Veda</term>, and another says that, unlike the <term>Veda</term>, <term>Tiruvaḷḷuvar’s</term> words do not lose their value when anyone repeats them. One speaks of it as containing everything worth knowing, and another says there is nothing that is not contained in this work. One says that the words are sweeter than the <term>Heavenly Ambrosia</term>, and unlike the latter, can be enjoyed by everyone. And as the poet speaks these words, even our own mouths begin to water. Another says they are sweet food for the mind, sweet to the ear, and sweet to the tongue, and the great cure-all for the ills of <term>Karma</term>. One compares it to the sun which, by dispelling the deep darkness of ignorance, makes the lotus of the heart bloom. Another compares it to a lamp dispelling our mental darkness, with a container of *<term>Dharma</term>* (virtue) for oil, *<term>Artha</term>* (wealth) for a wick, *<term>Kāma</term>* (love) for clarified butter, words of affection for the flame, and the short meters for the lamp-stand. Its brevity—which does not lead to confusion or vagueness as do most of the <term>sūtras</term> in <term>Sanskrit</term>—its perfection of expression and style, and its depth are all qualities praised by these learned scholars. What is more, the poet <term>Kallāḍar</term> highlights its most prominent characteristic in his verse: its universality. People argue about whether this or that is the truth, and they align themselves with various schools, but all are

Since his time, all religious groups—<term>Buddhists</term> and <term>Jains</term>, <term>Shaivas</term> and <term>Vaishnavas</term>—have all claimed him as their own. We do not need to inquire from where he derived his truths. It is enough to acknowledge that it is the perfection of Truth, if one can say so—a perfect ethical and religious code, a perfection of art and thought. Indeed, a close study of the work reveals its perfect scientific basis; each part, each chapter, and each verse is placed one after the other in a perfect chain of logical arrangement and argument. And we may hope that some dedicated student of the <term>Kural</term> will derive from it a perfect theory of ethics, both private and international.

One more remark will introduce us to the chapter of the book we have chosen for translation and explanation. His own contemporaries did not believe he had done so; rather, they stated in their praises that he explained all four <term>Purusharthas</term> and that he has shown the path to <term>Moksha</term>. The <term>Rev. Dr. Pope</term>, in his short paper on the ethics of the <term>Kural</term>, maintains that <term>Tiruvaḷḷuvar</term> bases his ethics on the grand truths of <term>Tripadartha</term>: <term>Pati</term>, <term>Pasu</term>, and <term>Pasha</term>. In fact, his creed is not a godless one like that of the <term>Jains</term> or <term>Buddhists</term>. In this respect, there is a difference between the <term>Naladi</term> and this work. Our author’s God is the “First Cause and Lord” (<term>ஆதி பகவன்</term>); He is “Intelligent” (<term>வாலறிவன்</term>); He “resides in the heart of His creatures” (<term>மலர்மிசையேகினான்</term>); He is “Immaculate, untainted by likes and dislikes” (<term>வேண்டுதல் வேண்டாமையிலான்</term>); He is the “Lord of Lords” and “King of Kings” (<term>இறைவன்</term>); He is “Incomparable” (<term>தனக்குவமையில்லாதான்</term>); He is the “Source of all Dharma and Beneficent” (<term>அறவாழி அந்தணன்</term>). And these bonds are not mere myths; they are caused by our own ignorance—*Avidyā*, *Ahaṅkāra*, or *Āṇava*—which is eternal, *Anādi*. Then, the chain of causation following *karma* into endless births and suffering is worked out, and the means or *Sādana* required to be freed from these bonds are fully shown. Of all the means, the greatest *Sādana* is to reach Him who is beyond all thought and speech; unless this is done, it is useless to hope to have our cares destroyed. And as all these principles are fully explained in chapter 36 on 'மெய்யுணர்தல்', 'How to Perceive Truth,' we have translated it below, adopting mostly the language of Dr. G. U. Pope, together with the famous commentary of Parimēlaḻagar, with some running notes to show how far this is embodied in the Advaita-Siddhānta. Naturally, the language of the *Kuṛaḷ* is the language of the Śaivite writers of the past 2,000 years; it is no wonder, then, that the truths expounded by all of them should be the same.

### HOW TO PERCEIVE TRUTH?

That is, we know the truth when we know the nature of Birth and Freedom (Moksha) and their causes, free from error and doubt. Sanskrit scholars call this *Tatvajñāna*. As this knowledge arises after desiring the desire of Him who has

# A CHAPTER FROM THE KURAL.

...having no desire, this chapter is placed as a result after the chapter on ‘துறவு’ (*Renunciation*), or ‘Sanyāsa.’

1. பொருளல் லவற்றைப் பொருளென் றுணரு
மருளானா மாணாப் பிறப்பு.

> The delusion where men consider that to be the truth which is not,
> That is the cause of miserable birth.

### PARIMELALAGAR’S COMMENTARY.

This delusion consists in believing that books and doctrines which hold that there is no rebirth, no fruits for either kind of Karma, and that there is no God—and similar ideas—are the true books and doctrines. This delusive belief is the same as when one mistakes one thing for another, such as a wooden block for a man, or a shell for silver. மருள் (*delusion*), மயக்கம் (*confusion*), விபரீத உணர்வு (*misconception*), error, and அவிச்சை (*Avidyā* or *ignorance*) are all synonymous words. Since only sorrow is reaped in all four kinds of birth—as celestial beings (*Devas*), humans, animals, and astral beings—this couplet explains that birth is sorrowful and that *Avidyā*, or error, is its cause.

### NOTE.

By changing only a single letter in the first line—an ‘அ’ (‘a’) into an ‘இ’ (‘i’) (changing *porulalla* into *porulilla*)—the meaning of the whole passage would be altered, and we would have a new system of philosophy directly opposed to our author’s. Instead of it being the truth, it would then become its opposite. This is the same question that has arisen in interpreting the negative prefix in the word ‘Advaita.’ This ‘a’ or ‘na’ is interpreted in two ways: either as meaning ‘அல்ல’ (‘not’) or ‘இல்லை’ (‘no’), though the distinction in the English equivalents is not very obvious. This is its ‘அன்மைப்பொருள்’ (*attribute of non-identity*) or ‘இன்மைப்பொருள்’ (*attribute of non-existence*). Siddhāntins, of course, accept the former interpretation, while most followers of Śaṅkara prefer the latter one. This latter view involves the negation of one of the two—or perhaps both—of the postulates in ‘Advaita.’ A great conflict has raged over this question, and volumes have been written by the late Śri-la-Śri Somasundara Nayagar and his followers on one side, and the late Ratna Cheṭṭiyār and those like him on the other. In any case, Saint Tiruvaḷḷuvar’s meaning is clear. He does not mean to reject anything as unreal or non-existent. To him, delusion or error

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of truth and delusion in the Kural, expanding on how misconceptions about the soul and the physical world lead to the cycle of birth and suffering.</meta>

# A CHAPTER FROM THE KURAL.

...consists in mistaking one existing thing, such as a shell, for another existing thing, such as silver. To him, to know the truth is to understand the true nature of each individual thing. The question of reality or unreality does not enter into it. One simply must not mistake one thing for another or doubt its nature. It will be a sufficient requirement of the definition if one understands the true nature of God, man, and the world; one does not need to believe any of these to be unreal. One such truth is that birth is sorrowful. This can be proven to be true. However, one's ignorance or delusion arises when one mistakes this actual sorrow for happiness. You think that with this body there is an absolute end, when in fact there are future births. Believing that there is no future life or future birth, a person does not believe that there can be a soul; and if there is one, they think the body itself is the soul. Believing this, all of one's energies in this world are directed solely toward what would procure the greatest pleasure and gratification of the senses, and one does not care what means are adopted provided one's passions are satisfied. As it is, the entire foundation of morality will be undermined, and one need fear neither men nor God. All this is the result of a lack of knowledge regarding the true nature of one's body and oneself, and this ignorance is the cause of one's birth. This ignorance is a fact, and to believe that this ignorance is itself unreal would be an error or false knowledge. It is only when a person knows that they are ignorant that they will learn and try to remove their ignorance. But can this ignorance be removed? Yes. If so, how? This question is answered in the next couplet.

2. இருணீங்கி யின்பம் பயக்கு மருணீங்கி
மாசறு காட்சி யவர்க்கு.

> Darkness departs and rapture springs to men who see
> The mystic vision pure from all delusion free.

### PARIMELALAGAR’S COMMENTARY.

இருள், darkness, is hell. ‘The mystic vision pure’ is the supreme object of knowledge. This couplet explains that by freedom is meant *<term>Niratisayānanda</term>* <note>unsurpassable bliss</note> and the *<term>Nimitta Kāraṇa</term>* <note>the efficient cause</note> for this is the Supreme Being.

### NOTE.

Darkness and ignorance, and light and knowledge, have at all times and in all climes been used synonymously, and no two things are so analogous in

<summary>This page discusses the philosophical distinction between truth and delusion, arguing that ignorance of the soul's true nature leads to sensory obsession and the cycle of rebirth. It introduces the second couplet of the chapter, which equates the "mystic vision" of the Supreme Being with the removal of ignorance and the attainment of eternal bliss.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Kural, focusing on the removal of ignorance and doubt as the path to spiritual liberation and the attainment of a well-balanced mind.</meta>

# A CHAPTER FROM THE KURAL.

...nature as these two pairs of words. When will darkness vanish? When the sun rises. When will the sun rise? After the night has passed. When will ignorance cease? When the source of all light arises in his heart. When will this be? When he has attained a well-balanced mind (இருவினையொப்பு). The *Pāsatchayam* and *Pathijñānam* are distinct facts, though the first is not possible without the second. This couplet answers all those who say that if ignorance was eternally attached to the soul, it cannot be removed, and even if it were removed, what follows is only a blank, and no Divine Power is required to give one freedom. This couplet and verse 4 below, which gives a very distinct reply to the Buddhist view, will remove all doubts as to whether he is a *Siddhānti*, a Buddhist, or a Jain. But even when some of these truths are known to a man, doubt often oppresses him, surrounded by a host of dogmatists who each assert that their own dogma is the only truth. In the next couplet, it is stated that even this doubt is the cause of birth, and the means of getting rid of this doubt is also stated.

3. ஐயத்தினீங் கித்தெளிந் தார்க்கு வையகத்தின்
வான நணிய துடைத்து.

> When doubts disperse and clarity is gained,
> Heaven is nearer than earth to the sage's soul.

### PARIMELALAGAR’S COMMENTARY.

Doubt (ஐயம்) is knowing a thing in various ways. That is, doubting whether there is or is not a God, <term>Karma</term>, and Rebirth, and living without a definite belief in anything. This is the same as doubting whether a thing is water or a mirage, a rope or a snake. As it is natural for every system to refute other doctrines and establish its own, those sages well-practiced in <term>Yoga</term> will remove the doubts arising from such a multitude of doctrines through their <term>Svānubhūti</term> (experience) and attain real knowledge; hence, they are called ஐயத்தின் நீங்கித்தெளிந்தார். As they reach higher and higher Yogic experience, their attachment to the world grows less and less; hence, the author’s statement that "heaven is nearer," etc. By this couplet, it is explained that doubtful knowledge is a cause of birth.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on spiritual clarity, moving from the removal of doubt to the role of Yoga and the necessity of perceiving absolute Truth (Pathijñāna) beyond mere sensory control.</meta>

# A CHAPTER FROM THE KURAL.

### NOTE.

Yoga is a means and not an end. Until <term>Yoga</term> merges into knowledge, no real knowledge is gained. Even the highest Yoga is of no benefit, unless the final goal is reached from which there is no return. The attainment of Yoga is truly difficult, but this is not all. One can subdue his passions and desires, and control his senses, but unless he has the **“pure Vision,” “The only Truth,”** then this attainment will be only temporary, and the man will again fall prey to his senses. To address this specific Buddhist view—that the attainment of the mere extinction of all desires is <term>Nirvāna</term>, and that there is no such thing as <term>Brahma-Nirvāna</term>—is the special object of the next couplet.

4. ஐயுணர் வெய்தியக் கண்ணும் பயமின்றே
மெய்யுணர் வில்லா தவர்க்கு.

> When five-fold perception is gained, what benefits accrue
> To those whose spirit lacks perception of the Truth?

### PARIMELALAGAR’S COMMENTARY.

Five-fold perception refers to the <term>Manas</term>. By “gained,” the author means the control of the manas and the concentration of it in <term>Dāraṇa</term>. As training in this alone is not sufficient, the author says there is no benefit, and he emphasizes by the use of the Tamil particle “உம்” (um) how difficult a feat even this attainment of Dāraṇa is. By these two couplets, the greatness of <term>Pathijñāna</term> is explained by pointing out that without this attainment, no <term>Moksha</term> is possible. (And the nature of this Pathijñāna is the subject of the next couplet).

5. எப்பொருள் எத்தன்மைத் தாயினு மப்பொருள்
மெய்ப்பொருள் காண்ப தறிவு.

> Whatever thing it is, of whatever kind it may be,
> It is wisdom’s part to see the real truth in each.

### PARIMELALAGAR’S COMMENTARY.

That is, one must perceive the truth immanent in every thing, after discarding our ordinary notions of them. In the phrase “கோச் சேரமான் யானைக்கட் சேய்மாந்தரஞ்சொலிரும்பொறை,” the words may ordinarily mean the name of King <term>Seramān</term> of a particular description, but they may mean more specifically

<summary>This page explains that spiritual disciplines like Yoga and sensory control are only beneficial if they lead to the perception of the True (Pathijñāna). It defines wisdom as the ability to see the essential reality within all things, regardless of their outward appearance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the fifth couplet from a chapter of the Kural, focusing on the distinction between the material world (Tattvas) and the Supreme Truth. it then transitions into the sixth couplet, which discusses the importance of hearing and learning (Śravaṇa) as a means to attain liberation (Moksha).</meta>

the Tattvas from earth to Purusha. When examined and reduced to their final causes, what finally remains is none of this cause and effect, but the Highest Truth, and His knowledge is the true knowledge. This couplet explains the nature of this true knowledge.

### NOTE

This is one of the most frequently quoted couplets of the Kural, and it is put to more general uses than what is intended here. One does not have to go far to discover the Supreme Being and know Him. He is in everything; but one must lose sight of the apparent to gain the real. God is in the earth, but the earth is not God; God is in water, but water is not God, and so it is through every Tattva; and lastly, God is in the soul, but the soul is not God. When one has learned to discriminate and distinguish in this way, only then will he attain to *Patijñānam*. In the next three couplets, the Sādanā required for attaining this *Patijñānam* is given. And the first requirement is hearing or learning.

6. கற்றீண்டு மெய்ப்பொருள் கண்டார் தலைப்படுவர்
   மற்றீண்டு வாரா நெறி.

> Those who learn and here the knowledge of the truth obtain,
> Shall find the path from which they do not come again.

## PARIMELALAGAR'S COMMENTARY.

By "learn," the author means learning from everyone and at all times. By "here," the author emphasizes the greatness of human birth, from which alone one can attain Moksha.

"The path that does not come again" is the path to Moksha. The means or Sādanā for knowing the First Cause—the cause of one's attaining Moksha—are of three kinds: they are <term>கேள்வி</term> (Hearing or study), <term>விமரிசம்</term> (Reflection), and <term>பாவனை</term> (Bāvanā or Realizing). (In Sanskrit, these are *Śravaṇa*, *Manana*, and *Nididhyāsana*). This couplet explains *Śravaṇa*.

### NOTE.

Though the commentator's idea of what is to be learned is very broad, the correction conveyed in the following stanza of the Nāladiyār is important.

# A CHAPTER FROM THE KURAL.

கல்வி கரையில கற்பவர் நாள்சில
மெல்ல நினைக்கின் பிணிபல—தெள்ளிதின்
ஆராய்ந் தமைவுடைய கற்பவே நீரொழிய
பாலுண் குருகிற் றெரிந்து.

"In this matchless verse," says Dr. Pope, "not a syllable could be spared; while almost every word is common and easy, yet it *is* the very fittest and is used in its exact meaning. It is somewhat archaic; it has a fascinating air of mystery; it pleasantly exercises and amply rewards the student's ingenuity; and it seems dark at first, but once lit up, it sparkles forever.

"This கரை—shore—suggests a metaphor: 'learning is a shoreless—infinite—ocean.'

"Then comes the simple antithesis: 'the learner's days are few.' In Tamil, the use of the same root twice (in கல்வி and கற்பவர்) and again in the third line (கற்பவே) adds a certain charm.

"Into these perfectly harmonious lines (to Tamil ears) is compressed a whole chapter.

"The subject of study (கல்வி with a plural verb) is infinitely numerous, but the learner's days are few; and if it is calmly thought out, men are liable to many diseases <note>பிணி: natural infirmities or 'bonds' that enfeeble and restrict</note>. Youthful enthusiasm may lead men to anticipate great and varied triumphs; calm reflection teaches them their natural weakness. So, men should learn with discrimination (தெள்ளிது), examining closely (ஆராய்) things that are befitting (அமை: those that suit, satisfy, or gladden them) with intelligence (தெரிந்து), like that of the bird—the semi-divine Hamsa, which drinks only the milk and leaves the water when these are presented mingled together."

7. ஓர்த்துள் ளமுள்ள துணரின் ஒருதலையாய்ப்
பேர்த்து ள்ளவேண்டா பிறப்பு.

The mind that knows with certainty what the First Cause is, and ponders its thoughts well, does not need to dwell on being born again into another life.

->COMMENTARY.<-

This explains 'manana' <note>Reflection or contemplation</note>.

8. பிறப்பென்னும் பேதைமைநீங்கச் சிறப்பென்னும்
செம்பொருள் காண்ப தறிவு.

When the folly of desiring rebirth departs, the soul can view the exalted Home of the Good Being; this is true wisdom.

<meta>This page provides the commentary by Parimelalagar on the Kural couplet introduced on the previous page, focusing on the relationship between ignorance, birth, and the realization of the "True Being" to attain Moksha.</meta>

## PARIMELALAGAR'S COMMENTARY.

Birth and ignorance, and the Exalted Home and Truth, are related as effect and cause; however, they are presented in reverse order in this couplet. Of the five faults, since ignorance is the cause of all the other faults, the author has identified it as *the cause* of birth. Because <term>Moksha</term> is higher than all other things, it is described as "exalted." The First Cause is described as the "Good Being," since He is eternal, without birth or death, and because all other things are too insignificant to taint Him through contact. He remains the same, without change or taint at all times, even though He is immanent in all things. For this reason, He is also referred to above as the "True Being" (மெய்ப்பொருள்) and the "Existent" (உள்ளது). The "viewing" refers to the soul shedding its <term>Mala</term> by constantly realizing or practicing (<term>பாவித்தல்</term>, *Bāvanā*), so that it may become one with God (<term>முற்றையுணர்வு</term>). This *Bāvanā* is also called *Samādhi* or *Šukla Dhyāna*. Since it is commonly held by all schools of thought that the soul, when it leaves the body, becomes whatever it contemplated at that time (அதனால் யாதொன்று பாவிக்கப்பட்டது அஃது அதுவாய்த் தோன்றுமாதலால்; <note>meaning, it is born into a body that matches what it yearned for at the time of death</note>), and since it is necessary for people who aspire after <term>Moksha</term> to meditate on the Transcendent Being so that their thoughts of birth may cease, there is no better method than this <term>Sādana</term> for constant practice. Thus, *Bāvanā* is explained in this couplet.

### NOTE.

The commentator proves his thesis by drawing upon a common belief held by all people. Everyone believes that the form one sees, the object one pursues, or the idea that possesses one at the moment of death will determine one's form in the next birth; stories are common about a <term>rishi</term> who, having fondled a deer, was reborn as a deer, and so on. However, many do not know the principle upon which this is based; except in the <term>Siddhānta</term> works, this principle is not explained anywhere else. The principle involved concerns the nature of the Soul, which is stated briefly and concisely by St. Meykaṇḍān as 'அது அது ஆதல்' ("that, that becomes") or 'சார்ந்ததன் வண்ணமாதல்' ("that becomes the color of what it leans upon," or "that becomes that to which it is

<summary>Parimelalagar explains that birth is caused by ignorance and that liberation is achieved through constant meditation (Bāvanā) on the True Being. He references the principle that the soul's final thoughts determine its next form, a concept central to Siddhānta philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Parimelalagar, Kural, Moksha, True Being, Bāvanā, Samādhi, Siddhānta, St. Meykaṇḍān, reincarnation, soul nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the nature of the soul and the practice of Bāvanā, building on the principle that the soul identifies with what it contemplates.</meta>

# A CHAPTER FROM THE KURAL.

...attached by St. Arul Nandi, which is paraphrased again by St. Tāyumānavar as:

> “ யாதொன்று பற்றின் அதன் இயல்பாய் நின்று
> பந்தமும் பளிங்கனைய சித்தத்து.”

"Like the dirt-removed crystal which takes on the nature of that to which it is attached." St. Tiruvaḷḷuvar himself has clearly expressed this principle in the verse “பற்றுக்கற்றார்” etc. of the last chapter, and in the second verse of this chapter, and in the next verse “சார்புணர்ந்து etc.” and verses 4, 5, 7, and 8 of the first chapter. In these, he shows that unless the soul leaves its clinging to one thing, it cannot cling to another. From this is deduced the principle (பற்றுக்கோடின்றி நில்லாமை) that the soul cannot have any independent existence or form unless it is clinging to one thing (the world or body in <term>Bandha</term>) or the other (God in <term>Moksha</term>). While so attached, it identifies itself so thoroughly that it is impossible to discover its separate personality. 

Hence it was that Tyndall, Huxley, and Bain, with all their minute anatomical, biological, and psychological analysis, were not able to discover a mind in the body different from the body, though they could feel that the result was not very satisfactory. The express language used by the commentator, “Whatever is contemplated by the non-real will appear as that very thing” (அசத்தால் யாதொன்று பாவிக்கப்பட்டது அஃது அதுவாய்த் தோன்றும்), and “To destroy the contemplation that causes birth, one must contemplate the Absolute Being alone” (பிறப்பிற்கேதுவாய பாவனை கெடுதற்பொருட்டுக் கேவலப்பெருளையே பாவித்தல் வேண்டும்), as will appear from the beautiful stanza we quote below from St. Arul Nandi, will show to whom he is indebted for the explanation:

> “கண்டவிவையல்லேனானென்றகன்றுகாணாக்
> கழிபரமுநானல்லேனெனக்கருதிக்கசிந்த
> தொண்டினொடுமுளத்தவன்றமுநின்றலப்பாலே
> சோகமெனப்பாவிக்கத்தோன்றுவன்வேறின்றி
> விண்டகலும்மலங்களெல்லாம் கருடத்தியானத்தின்
> விடமழியுமதுபோல விமலತೆಯுமடையும்
> பண்டைமறைகளுமதுநானானேனென்று
> பாவிக்கச்சொல்லுவதிப்பாவத்தைக்காணே ”

The word பாவனை (<term>Bāvanā</term>) is important. <term>Bāvanā</term>, <term>Sādana</term>, <term>Dhyāna</term>, and <term>Yoga</term> are all more or less synonymous terms. It means practice through symbolic meditation or realization. You firmly visualize that you are one with that, and you become that. And this is the principle which underlies all the <term>Mahāvākyas</term>, such as ‘Tattvamasi,’ etc. For fuller treatment,

9 சார்புணர்ந்து சார்பு கெடவொழுகின் மற்றழித்துச்
சார்தரா சார்தரு நோய்.

He who knows the true support—and rejects the support he sought before—
The sorrow that clings shall cease and cling to him no more.

### PARIMELALAGAR’S COMMENTARY.

‘Conduct’ or ‘practice’ (ஒழுக்கம்) here means the practice of Yoga. This Yoga is of eight kinds: Yama, Niyama, Āsana, Prāṇāyāma, Pratyākāra, Dhāraṇa, Dhyāna, and Samādhi. Their explanations are too long to be given here; see them in the books on Yoga. ‘The sorrows that cling to us’ are the fruits of <term>Karma</term> which have yet to be experienced. These are the result of infinite <term>Karma</term> performed in births dating from eternity, and they give rise to fruits already consumed in past births and in the present birth. “Shall cease and cling no more,” as they will vanish before Yoga and <term>Jñāna</term> like darkness before light. The Jains call this ‘உவர்ப்பு.’ Because even Good <term>Karma</term> is the seed of birth, it is called a ‘disease.’ The author holds that births will cease when the Supreme is perceived by the three means mentioned above. When births cease, what harm can all the ills do? They cannot cling to these <term>jñānis</term> who are well-practiced in Yoga, and since there is no support, the ills will die. This is the meaning of the stanza.

### NOTE.

The word ‘சார்பு’ in the verse and ‘பற்று’ in the previous chapter mean a *support* or *hold*. The soul has two such supports—one in <term>Bandha</term> and one in <term>Moksha</term>—and without such supports, it cannot stand. This may be compared to a piece of iron held between two magnetic poles (one positive and one negative), or better still, to a fruit growing on a tree. The fruit is held up by the tree only as long as it is raw and immature (undeveloped); but as soon as it is ripe, it reaches the ground (by the force of gravity). A fruit, as such, must be united to either the tree or the ground. What happens is that as the fruit grows riper, the tree's sap no longer rises to the twig; the twig dies, and the fruit falls off. So too as

It uses the metaphor of a ripening fruit to illustrate how the soul shifts its dependence from worldly bonds to divine liberation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Kural's teachings regarding the soul's liberation, transitioning from the requirement of divine support to the destruction of the three primary faults that cause rebirth.</meta>

# A CHAPTER FROM THE KURAL.

As man rises higher and his desire for the world decreases, the bonds are severed and he falls at the Feet of the Lord. "பாசந் துறந்து பசுக்கிடம் பதியாம்." The author of <term>Thirukkalitruppadiyar</term> explains ‘சார்புணர்வு’ (*Sārbunardhu*) as <term>Dhyāna</term>, and ‘சார்புகெட வொழுக்கம்’ (*Sārbukeda-volukkam*) as <term>Samādhi</term>, the highest <term>Jñāna-Yoga</term> practices. In the next verse, this <term>Pāśatchaya</term> is further explained.

10. காமம் வெகுளி மயக்க மிவைமூன்ற
->ணாமங் கெடக்கெடு நோய்.<-

### PARIMELALAGAR'S COMMENTARY.

Eternal ignorance, <term>avidyā</term>; the resulting <term>ahaṅkāra</term>, the feeling of 'I' and 'mine'; the hankering which desires this or that; the eternal desire for various objects; and the dislike or hate arising from unsatisfied desire—these five faults are listed by Sanskrit scholars. The author lists only three, as '<term>Ahaṅkāra</term>' can be included under '<term>Avidyā</term>,' and 'hankering' can be comprised under 'Desire.' As these faults are burned up before <term>Jñāna-Yoga</term> practices, like cotton before a wildfire, the author speaks of the disappearance of the very names of these three faults. Since those who do not commit these faults will not perform the good or bad <term>Karma</term> caused by them, the author states accordingly in this verse that they suffer no pain from it. As a result of attaining True Knowledge, the suffering of past births and future births is destroyed, and thus these two verses find a place in this chapter. We learn from this also that what remains for those who have perceived the Truth is the present body and the ills attached to it.

#### NOTE.

And the next chapter discusses the means of even getting rid of this bare bodily infirmity and of guarding against what is called <term>Vāsanā Mala</term>.

<summary>Parimelalagar explains that by destroying the three root faults—desire, anger, and delusion—one ceases to perform Karma, thereby ending the cycle of birth and suffering. He notes that while past and future Karma are destroyed by True Knowledge, the physical body and its current ailments persist until further spiritual practice.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from a commentary on the Kural regarding the soul's liberation to a general discussion on the role and risks of using analogies in Oriental philosophy and the Bhagavad Gītā.</meta>

# THE ANALOGIES IN THE GĪTĀ.

---

Analogy is used extensively in the clarification and explanation of various principles in Oriental philosophy, with varying degrees of success. In most cases, they serve a very important function; there are many truths which, because they deal with ultimate realities, can only be demonstrated through such analogies and not by any other form of proof. However, there are also great dangers in using such analogies; an analogy may seem so plausible that one is likely to be carried away by it without noticing its inherent flaws, which a slightly closer investigation would clearly reveal. 

Care should be taken to distinguish between analogies that are merely similes or metaphors—based on a simple resemblance and intended only to make the subject matter more impressive and clear—and analogies properly so called, which are intended as proof. In the latter case, simple resemblance alone is not enough; there must be consistency between the various parts of the illustration and the thing being illustrated. Neglecting this rule often leads to great confusion and errors in thought. If, for the specific inference desired, the preceding circumstances match the circumstances in the analogy, the inference will be quite justified if it matches the outcome of the analogy. It would be simply illogical to stretch the illustration to other purposes or to extremes. 

Analogy, at its best, is only a mediocre kind of proof; where we do not take proper precautions when using it, its value in philosophical argument is almost nothing. Another source of error in the use of analogies by Indian writers is the brevity of expression characteristic of such analogies as they appear in some of the most ancient books. When the analogy is taken literally, without filling in the necessary parts and omissions, it cannot help but lead one astray.

<vocab>Gītā, analogy, Oriental philosophy, Indian writers, inference</vocab>

<summary>This text discusses the importance and potential pitfalls of using analogies in Oriental philosophy, emphasizing that they are often the only way to explain ultimate realities but must be used with logical precision. It warns against confusing simple metaphors with formal proofs and notes that the brief style of ancient Indian texts can lead to misunderstandings if taken too literally.</summary>
