<meta>This page continues the critique of using analogies as proof in philosophy, moving from a general discussion to a specific criticism of a certain Indian school and European scholars like Dr. Paul Deussen regarding the "stream and ocean" analogy for the soul's liberation.</meta>

# THE ANALOGIES IN THE GĪTĀ.

There is one school of philosophers in India who are excessively fond of these similes and who, at almost every step, seek the aid of a simile to help them justify their position. These similes have now become far too hackneyed; they pass from mouth to mouth, and even educated people repeat them like parrots—people who would easily identify the fallacy if the matter were only presented to them for a moment. We expected at least those learned in Western lore to explain their subject instead of building their entire argument on the strength of these doubtful similes; in this respect, even European scholars are not beyond reproach. For what shall we say of a scholar like Dr. Paul Deussen, if he expresses the following false analogy?

He says, "And then for him, when death comes, there is no more *Samsāra*. He enters into Brahman, like streams into the ocean: he leaves behind him *nāma* and *rūpa*, he leaves behind him *individuality*; but he does not leave behind him his Atman, his Self. It is not the falling of the drop into the infinite ocean; it is the whole ocean becoming free from the fetters of ice, returning from its frozen state to what it really is and has never ceased to be—to its own all-pervading, eternal, almighty nature."

In these few lines, he crowds together as many fallacies as there are words, and we have neither the time nor the patience to point out all of them. We will, however, point out the most glaring among them. The soul returning from its migrations to its resting place, its final goal, was likened to the stream returning to the bosom of the mighty ocean. When the stream joins the ocean, does it lose its name and form? Does it really do so? And if it did, so what? How is it changed in any way? What we generally call a stream is a small body of water flowing between two banks. The water by itself, without its local connection, cannot be called the stream. The moment the water leaves its local connection, it ceases to be called a stream. So, it is not really the stream that flows into the ocean, but rather the water of the stream that flows into and mixes with the water of the ocean. What really makes the difference between the ocean and the stream is the

He argues that this analogy is logically flawed, as it confuses the water itself with the geographical form of the stream.</summary>

difference in the size of the respective bodies and the size of the receptacle. The water in either receptacle is acted upon by the sun and wind, is tossed by storms, and is discolored and made muddy. The trick by which the learned Doctor converts the stream water—indeed, even a drop—into a mighty ocean is not evident in the illustration. The drop or the stream water remains the drop or the stream water in the heart of the ocean, even if, for the time being, we are unable to distinguish its *identity*. When its *identity* is lost, its *individuality* is not seen and is, in a sense, also lost. The water remains water and has not lost its *nāma* and *rūpa*, though this water acquires other names through external circumstances. It is the circumstance that determines the more specific name, and we will have to inquire how the thing acquired this circumstance or became separated from it. 

Then we come to the figure of the frozen ocean and the free ocean. Here is a jump from one figure to another. The bound soul was formerly the stream, and the freed soul the ocean. In either case, as we observed above, the two bodies of water were subject to the same changes and limitations, except that one was larger than the other. Now, the bound soul is the frozen ocean and the freed soul is the ocean after it has melted. And the learned Doctor speaks of the shackles of ice. What does it matter to the ocean whether it was in a frozen condition or otherwise? How does it cease to be almighty, all-pervading, and eternal more when it is frozen than when it was not? One would think that if the ocean's wishes were to be consulted, it would much prefer to be frozen than not, as it would not be subjected to the mercy of the wind, the sun, and the moon. Water is water, whether it remains a liquid, a gas, or a solid substance. And it would be pure rhetoric to attribute shackles to it. 

And this shackle is either real or imaginary, and either an evil or a good. If it is real and an evil, how did this shackle happen to be put on? If not, why try to get rid of the shackle? The shackle was put on by the ocean's own will or by another will, more powerful still. If the ocean put it on by its own will, it may do so again, and there is no inducement for

...anyone to try to get rid of this shackle, then "*the strongest support of pure morality, the greatest consolation in the sufferings of life and death,*" would surely be undermined. If it is by another's will, who is greater than this Ātman? It is no doubt the Paramātman, which ends in true dualism. In the case of the ocean itself, it did not become frozen by its own will or power. As water, its nature is unstable and changeable, and the change is brought about by other causes. If we apply heat to it, its liquid condition disappears and it becomes a gas. Withdraw the heat, and the more you do so, the more solid the water becomes; in the Arctic regions, where the sun—thousands of times more powerful than the ocean water—is altogether absent for several months, the water is affected by cold and darkness and becomes fettered in ice. The learned Doctor failed to consider the previous agent in the freezing or otherwise of the ocean, namely the sun, and hence his error. The Siddhāntins take water—whether it is that of the smallest stream or that of the ocean—as analogous to the soul, and they view the universal Ākāš, present both in the water of the stream and that of the ocean, as the Paramešvara and Paramātman: the universal Supporter and all-pervader. The glorious Sun is also God, whose pañchakṛitya is also felt in the ocean and stream water—in its creation, growth, and dissolution—and under whose powerful Šakti the minor powers of Karma (wind and moon) also find play, and the whole cycle of evolution is set in motion.

And it is this learned Doctor who spoke of the *misinterpreting* variations of Šaṅkara's *advaita*, known by the names of Visishtādvaita, Dvaita, etc. It is the frequent boast of people of his kind that Šaṅkara's Advaita is the most universal and ancient system, whereas all other forms of Indian philosophy are only partial, sectarian, and modern. In the present paper, we propose to deal with this claim to a certain extent by examining the Gītā, their most beloved Upanishad; by merely looking at the various analogies used by Lord Kṛishṇa, we will show whether or not we find among them any of the

The first simile in the book occurs in chapter II, 13.

> “Just as in this body, childhood, youth, and old age belong to the embodied man, so also does he acquire another body.”

This is a popular enough simile, and its meaning is plain; but it cannot be interpreted as Śaṅkara does—that the soul undergoes no change or is not affected by the change of <term>avastas</term> (states) or change of bodies. For it cannot be argued that the intelligence of Śaṅkara is in the same embryonic stage as that of a newborn baby, and to deny this would also conflict with all our ideas of evolutionary progress and the necessity of undergoing many births. In the previous verse, Śrī Kṛṣṇa proposed the existence of many souls by asserting, "There was never a time when I did not exist, nor you, nor these rulers of men; and none of us will ever cease to exist hereafter." He repeats the same fact in chapter IV, 5, where he refers to his own former births, a fact which is also mentioned by Śrī Kṛṣṇa himself again in the *Anuśāsana Parva* and stated by Vyāsa in the *Yuddha Parva*. By “I” and “you” and “these,” he clearly does not refer to their bodies as Śaṅkara interprets. 

The next figure occurs in verse 22 of the same chapter: “just as a man sheds worn-out clothes and puts on others which are new, so the soul sheds worn-out bodies and enters those which are new.” Similar examples are that of the serpent shedding its skin, the mind passing from the conscious into the dream condition, and the Yogi entering into another body, which are given by Saint Meykaṇḍān. The next one occurs in verse 58, where the Sage withdrawing his senses from the objects of sense is compared to the tortoise withdrawing its limbs at the approach of anyone. The same simile occurs in the *Tiruvaruṭpayan*.

There is one other passage relating to the soul and its bound condition, namely verse 21 in chapter xiii itself: “<term>Purusha</term>, as seated in <term>Prakṛiti</term>, experiences the qualities born of <term>Prakṛiti</term>; attachment to qualities is the cause of his birth in good and evil wombs.” Lo, the Supreme Self, attaching itself to qualities born of <term>Prakṛiti</term>, constrained to commit sin, deluded by co-existent darkness, having to undergo births and deaths, and getting fettered and seeking salvation—and all this “*as it were*”! What a precious excuse would it not prove, this “*as it were*,” to the murderer, the forger, the liar, the thief, etc.? It may be noted that the expression "embodied" is always used when describing the soul, <term>Jīva</term>, and never to denote God. Though God is seated in the hearts of all, He is the Soul of Souls and Light of Lights. He can never be called the "*embodied*." The expression "*embodied*" itself conveys the idea of attachment and bondage. No one reading verses 36 to 40 of chapter iii, and xiii, 21; xiv, 5, 20; and verses iv, 14; ix, 9; xiii, 31 together, can fail to observe the utter contrast between the two entities; and we appeal to common sense whether Śaṅkara’s "*as it were*" can do away with this distinction and contrast. This distinction and contrast is highlighted in different chapters, within the same chapter, and in consecutive verses (xv, 16, 17, 18), and even in the same verse (v. 15). 

The word "*another*" (<term>Anyatha</term>) is itself a technical word, like "the inside of" (<term>Antas</term>), etc., and occurs in the Gītā in other places and in a number of Vedic texts to denote the Supreme God as distinguished from souls and the world—the entities recognized by the <term>Kapila Sāṅkhyas</term>. <term>Adhikaraṇas</term> 4 to 9 of the <term>Vedānta Sūtra</term>, and the texts quoted there which appear in Vol. II of the <term>S.D.</term> <note>The Siddhanta Deepika journal.</note> pages 73 to 79, fully support our thesis. The apparent confusion caused by both the human spirit and the Supreme Spirit being spoken of as dwelling in the human body is altogether removed by the <term>Mantras</term> which speak of "the two birds entering into the cave," "Rudra, destroyer of pain, enters into me," "He who abides in the <term>Vijñāna</term>," "He who abides in the <term>Ātman</term>," and "higher than the high, higher than the imperishable" (cf. xv, 18, Gītā). 

Leaving this subject for the present, we proceed. Chapter iv also contains only one simile (verse 37): "As a kindled fire reduces fuel to ashes, O Arjuna; so does the fire of wisdom reduce all <term>Karma</term> to ashes." The next illustration occurs in chapter v, verse 16, and is a very familiar one—that of the Sun and darkness: "But in those in whom *unwisdom* is destroyed by the Wisdom of the Self, like the Sun, that wisdom illuminates That Supreme." nor the good deeds of anyone; wisdom is enveloped by unwisdom; by this, mortals are deluded."

Here, "wisdom" clearly means <term>Ātmā</term>, <term>Ātmajñān</term>, the Soul, or the Soul's intelligence. This intelligence is covered by <term>ajñāna</term>, or unwisdom. In contrast with the ignorance-covered soul, there stands <term>Parameśvara</term>, untouched by evil, though dwelling in the body. How is the Soul's wisdom to get rid of the veil of unwisdom? If it were able to get rid of this unwisdom by its own wisdom, it could have done so the moment it willed, and we would never hear of a soul in bondage. So the illustration explains how this is done. Unwisdom is destroyed not by the soul's wisdom (referred to merely as wisdom) but by <term>Ātmajñān</term>, <term>Brahmajnān</term>, or <term>Śivajnān</term>, leading to the perception and enjoyment of <term>Śivānanda</term>. This is like the darkness covering the individual eye, which flees before the rising glory of the effulgent Sun; and the Sun, while it dispels the darkness, simultaneously enables the eye to exercise its own power of seeing (the soul's wisdom) and allows it to see the Sun itself. The reader is requested to read the simile as explained, along with <term>Śaṅkara</term>'s own explanation, and form his own conclusions.

"As a lamp in a sheltered spot does not flicker" is the simile of the <term>Yogi</term> in Divine Union. "திரையற்ற நீர்போல் சிந்தை தெளிவார்." "Like the waveless seawater, the <term>jñāni</term> attains clarity and calm" is another simile. The water and the lamp are by nature changeable; any little gust of wind (<term>karma-mala</term>) can make the one flicker and the other form into ripples. But the Sun, or <term>Ākāśa</term> (God), can neither flicker nor change. And this is exactly the simile in chapter IX, verse 6. 

However, the simile in chapter VII, verse 7 demands our prior attention. "There is nothing higher than I, O <term>Dhanañjaya</term>; in me, all this is woven as a row of gems on a string." Here, the string is <term>Iśvara</term>, and the gems are other creatures and objects. The string can no more become the gems than the gems can become the string; it simply highlights the distinction between the lower and the higher <term>Padārthas</term> spoken of in verse 5, and shows how <term>Iśvara</term> supports and upholds the whole universe, just as a string supports various gems.

# THE ANALOGIES IN THE <term>GĪTĀ</term>.

The next simile already mentioned is in chapter IX, verse 6: “As the mighty wind moving everywhere rests in the <term>Ākāśā</term>, know that in this way all beings rest in me.” And Lord <term>Kṛishṇa</term> describes the truth explained by this as the Kingly science and the Kingly secret, which is immediately understandable; and he may well say so, as this explains the true nature of <term>advaita</term>. Verses 4 and 5 must be stated in full: “By me all this world is pervaded in my unmanifested form. All beings dwell in Me; and I do not dwell in them.” “Nor do beings dwell in me, behold my Divine <term>Yoga</term>! Bearing all beings and yet not dwelling in them is my Self, the cause of beings.” With this we might also read the similes in XIII, verses 32 and 33: “As the all-pervading <term>Ākāśā</term> is, by reason of its subtlety, never soiled, so God seated in the body is not soiled.” “As the one Sun illumines all these worlds, so does the <term>Kshetri</term> (not <term>Kshetrajña</term>) illumine all <term>Kshetra</term>,” and the simile in XV, verse 8: “When the Lord (the <term>jiva</term>, the lord of the aggregate of the body and the rest—<term>Śaṅkara</term>) acquires a body and when he leaves it, he takes these and goes, as the wind takes scents from their seats.” Here <term>Parameśvara</term> is compared to <term>Ākāśā</term> and the soul, <term>jiva</term>, is compared to the wind; and the relation between God and Soul is the same relation as between <term>Ākāśā</term> and the wind or things contained in <term>Ākāśā</term>. And what is this relation? Logicians and <term>Siddhāntins</term> call this relation <term>Vyāpaka Vyāpti Sambandam</term>: the relation of container and contained. We explained in our article on ‘Mind and Body’ that this was not a very appropriate comparison, as it refers to quantity; yet it is the best synonym and illustration of the <term>Advaita</term> relation—not <term>Bheda</term> (<term>Madhva</term>), not <term>Abheda</term>, not <term>Bhedābheda</term> (<term>Rāmānuja</term>), not <term>Pariṇāma</term> (<term>Vallabha</term>), not <term>Vivarta</term> (<term>Śaṅkara</term>), but the <term>Vyāpaka Vyāpti</term> relation. Taking the five elements and the order of their evolution and involution, it is seen how all four evolve from and resolve into <term>Ākāśā</term>. But earth is not water, nor water earth; water is not fire, nor fire water; fire is not air, nor air fire; none of these is <term>Ākāśā</term>, nor is <term>Ākāśā</term> any of these. And yet all solids can be reduced to liquids, and liquids into a gaseous condition, and all disappear into <term>Ākāśā</term>. The one

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between God and the soul, specifically focusing on the analogy of Ākāśa (ether or space) to explain how God pervades all things without being affected by them, contrasting this with the analogies used by the Māyāvāda school.</meta>

# THE ANALOGIES IN THE <term>GĪTĀ</term>.

The lower element is contained within the one above it, and all are contained within <term>Ākāśa</term>; however, <term>Ākāśa</term> cannot be said to be *contained* in any of these, though it is present in each. Each element is more subtle and more vast than the lower element, and <term>Ākāśa</term> is the most subtle, vastest, most pervasive, and invisible ("my form unmanifested"). <term>Ākāśa</term> is not capable of any change, though the wind, water, fire, and earth contained within it can be contaminated by that to which they become attached. Wind carries off scents and is subjected to all the forces of the sun and moon. The water of the ocean becomes salty, becomes frozen, and becomes tempest-tossed. The lamp flickers and becomes smoky or bright, or spreads a fragrant smell or otherwise, depending on the nature of the oil or wood it is burning. This very illustration of the sea (space), water, and winds is used by Saint <term>Meykaṇḍān</term> in VII, 3-3 to illustrate that ignorance does not attach itself to God, but rather to the Soul.

> "Ignorance will not arise from God, who is the True Intelligence, as it is <term>Asat</term> (like darkness before the sun). The soul, which is ever united to God, is co-eternal with Him. The connection of ignorance with the soul is like the connection of salt with the water of the sea."

The word "<term>Ākāśa</term>," by the way, is a technical term, like "another," "<term>antas</term>," "<term>jyotis</term>," etc., and is a synonym for God (see <term>Vedānta Sūtras</term> I, 1-22 and the texts quoted there, as well as the article "House of God," "<term>Chit Ambara</term>" in *The Siddhānta Dīpikā*, Vol. I. p. 153).

The simile of streams and the sea occurs in XI, 28, to illustrate not the entering into <term>moksha</term>, but rather undergoing dissolution and death. The similes in XV, 1 and 2—the <term>Ashvatha</term> tree rooted above and spreading below—and in XVII, 61 <note>This verse is commonly cited as XVIII, 61 in modern editions of the Bhagavad Gītā.</note>, that 

> "the Lord dwells in the hearts of all beings (<term>jivas</term>), O Arjuna, whirling by <term>Māyā</term> all beings (as if) mounted on a machine,"

are the very last to be noted. These are nearly all the similes discovered in the <term>Gītā</term>; and do we not miss here nearly all the favorite similes of the <term>Māyāvāda</term> school? If so, how was it that the omniscient Lord <term>Kṛishṇa</term> failed to use any one of them?

<summary>This page concludes the analysis of analogies in the Gītā, focusing on Ākāśa as a metaphor for God's pervasive yet detached nature. It notes that the Gītā lacks the common similes used by the Māyāvāda school of thought.</summary>
<keywords>Ākāśa, Meykaṇḍān, Asat, Vedānta Sūtras, Siddhānta Dīpikā, moksha, Ashvatha, Māyā, Kṛishṇa, Māyāvāda, Bhagavad Gītā analogies</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from a specific theological discussion on the nature of God and the soul to a broader philosophical argument regarding the unity of knowledge and the necessity of intellectual tolerance in the Indian context.</meta>

# "THE UNION OF INDIAN PHILOSOPHIES."

> ->"All partitions of knowledge should be accepted as lines to mark and distinguish, rather than as sections to divide and separate, so that the continuity and entirety of knowledge may be preserved."—*Bacon*.<-

This saying by the greatest and wisest man of his age has even greater application today in the land of <term>Bharata</term> than it did in Bacon's own time. It clearly illustrates the purpose and full scope of all knowledge, as well as the true principles of tolerance and liberalism that should guide us in our search for knowledge and the discovery of truth. Unless we carefully sift through and understand the nature of what is presented to us as knowledge and truth for our acceptance—marking their lines of similarity and difference—we will gradually fall into a state of intellectual color-blindness. We would cease to know what is color, what is knowledge, and what is truth; the final result would be intellectual and moral atrophy and death. 

Therefore, when we engage in moral and intellectual discussions to avoid such a catastrophe and self-destruction, we must also remember that these differences in thought should never divide people in their mutual sympathies or their aspirations in the pursuit of the common good. There is no need at all for angry discussions or bitter language. Regardless of whatever capabilities of the human mind may still remain hidden, the human mind is, in a sense, limited. The laws of thought can be determined positively, and they are as fixed as possible. We can only think about a particular question in a specific number of ways and no more; the total number of these ways is fully revealed through their various permutations and combinations.

<summary>Drawing on Francis Bacon, the text argues that divisions in knowledge should be used to distinguish ideas rather than divide people. It emphasizes that while the human mind has limited modes of thought, intellectual rigor and tolerance are necessary to avoid moral and intellectual decay.</summary>
<keywords>Indian Philosophies, Francis Bacon, Bharata, epistemology, tolerance, liberalism, laws of thought, unity of knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the universality and cyclical nature of philosophical thought, emphasizing that geographical or temporal differences do not alter fundamental human inquiries. It warns against the current trend in India toward narrow-mindedness and the misuse of "universalism" to justify intellectual stagnation.</meta>

Differences in time, climate, and nationality have not affected thought in the least. People have expressed the same moral sentiments and feelings; the same beauties in nature, along with existing similarities and disparities, have been minutely noted by the poets of all lands. Therefore, it should not surprise us that the same theories about some of the grand problems of human existence have been discussed and held since man first began to ask himself those questions; these same theories will endure for ages to come. The same stories have been told and the same battles have been fought over and over again, but we also note that the honors of war have often rested on and followed the preferences of the people and the prominence of the storyteller of the time. Each theory and school of philosophy has had its own heyday of life and glory, its fall, and its subsequent resurrection. Even within a single generation, we see a thinker who is considered the greatest philosopher of the day—as one who has revolutionized all thought and philosophy—become greatly discounted and pale before rising stars whose fads capture the popular fancy. By these observations, we do not mean to discourage all theorizing, but only to show the uselessness of dogmatism on any point; we hold more than ever that all divisions of knowledge are useful and should be accepted for consideration.

We have ventured these observations because, in these days and in this land, what is considered knowledge, <term>jñānam</term>, and philosophy is all seeking a narrow groove and taking on a one-sided character. This tends to obliterate thought by ignoring the thin and delicate partitions that exist between different kinds of knowledge, and the consequences cannot be entirely beneficial. This process of ignorance and obliteration has been ongoing for some time and has been mainly assisted by false or strange notions of what constitutes tolerance and universalism. The habit of trying to defend everything and explain everything away from one’s own preconceived point of view is clearly a harmful habit.

<summary>The author argues that fundamental philosophical questions are universal across time and culture, though different schools rise and fall in popularity. He warns that contemporary Indian philosophy is becoming dangerously narrow and dogmatic by misapplying the concepts of tolerance and universalism.</summary>
<keywords>Indian Philosophies, jñānam, dogmatism, universalism, tolerance, philosophical schools, human existence, intellectual history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the critique of narrow intellectual trends in India, specifically addressing the tendency to force disparate philosophical views into a single, unified framework.</meta>

# THE UNION OF INDIAN PHILOSOPHIES.

intellectually and morally. The vain search for a fictional unity has often ended in a trap; and a similar attempt nowadays to reduce every perspective to a single view is purely a Procrustean method and extremely fallacious. What is the benefit of such a procedure? There could be neither profit nor pleasure in seeking such similarities and uniformities in things that are fundamentally different. Is there any value in such knowledge and reasoning as this: "Black is the same as red, because both are colors"; or, "A crow is the same thing as ink, because both are black"? Such an attempted unification of knowledge is purely delusive and of no importance whatsoever. Furthermore, when commentators claim and argue that a certain passage supports only their interpretation and no other, and that each person’s own interpretation is the best, common sense dictates that these views cannot all be correct, nor could the author have intended all these meanings himself.

Our <term>Hindu</term> commentators have often taken the greatest liberties with their authors, and they have often proven to be the worst offenders in forcing meanings upon words and passages which both the words themselves and the context clearly show they do not support. Yet we are often asked by some very tolerant people to accept every view as truth and to adopt their perspective as the greatest truth of all. Since many of these ancient books are written and commented upon in an obsolete language that very few have the time or take the trouble to master, this delusion has been maintained by a few, and people have often been led astray by the use of certain prestigious names. 

But the illusions are beginning to be dispelled as we come to understand what the real text says in plain, literal language—thanks to the labors of European scholars—without burdening ourselves with what this or that commentator says. Some of these scholars and translators have been quite honest and outspoken regarding what they believe to be the true view as supported by the text. No scholar has yet come forward to dispute the view taken by Dr. Thibaut regarding how far <term>Śaṅkara's</term> views are actually supported by the text of the <term>Vêdânta Sūtras</term>. We hope to discuss these in due course,

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the interpretation of Indian philosophical texts, specifically focusing on the Bhagavad Gītā and the Śrīkaṇṭha Bhāshya, contrasting traditional Indian understanding with contemporary European perspectives.</meta>

# THE UNION OF INDIAN PHILOSOPHIES.

Since the translation of the *Śrīkaṇṭha Bhāshya* that we are publishing is proceeding quickly, we are comparing and contrasting these works. It should be kept in mind that Śrīkaṇṭha was an older contemporary of Śankara, and his commentary is the oldest of all those on the *Vēdānṭa Sūtras* currently in existence. In this article, however, we intend to discuss the questions concerning the *Bhagavad Gītā* raised by Mr. Charles Johnstone in his valuable paper, "The Union of Indian Philosophies," which we reprinted in our last issue from the *Madras Mail*. 

He asks himself to which of the three schools of Indian philosophy—Sāṅkhya, Yoga, and Vēdānṭa—this book belongs. He states that his immediate answer would be that it is undoubtedly one of the textbooks of the Vēdānṭa school, and one of the most significant of them. Yet, despite this, he thinks there are other aspects of the *Gītā*, and that much in them belongs to the Sāṅkhya, and even more that is the property of the Yoga school. He explains below how the *Gītā*, beginning as a ballad about Kṛishṇa and Arjuna, gradually expanded into its present form. He suggests it incorporated all the teachings of the *Upanishats* and the teachings of the Sāṅkhya and Yoga schools, along with Purāṇic episodes of the transfiguration. In the opinion of this writer, that transfiguration "reproduces all that grim and gruesome ugliness of many-armed gods with terrible teeth, which the *Purāṇas* have preserved, most probably from the wild faiths of the dark aboriginals and demon-worshippers of Southern India."

We will deal with this last statement—which is pure fiction—later on. The point we wish to draw particular attention to is that it struck the writer as a novelty, and he presents it as news to an uninformed world, that the *Gītā* does not represent Vēdānṭa alone. To any Indian who knows anything about Indian philosophy, this is not news at all, as all the modern Indian schools—including Dvaita, Visishṭādvaita, and Suddhādvaita—claim the book as an authority and have written commentaries on it as well. However, the European who has learned to read the books of only one school of philosophy (all...

We well remember an Indian graduate in arts and law asking us if there was any such thing as a special school of Śaiva Siddhānta philosophy. Of course, he wears *Vibhūti* and *Rudrāksha* and worships Śiva, and he knows that the Great Guru Śankara was an incarnation of Śiva Himself; however, because all the English books that treated Hinduism only spoke of the Vedānta philosophy, his surprise and ignorance were quite natural. 

But as a result of the great upheaval currently occurring, and the greater attention being paid to the study of our philosophical and religious literature, even our own people have been slowly waking up to the truth of the matter. That most devoted adherent of Vedānta, the editor of the *Light of the East*, was the first to concede and point out in his articles on the ‘Ancient Śāṅkhya System’ that the *Gītā* also expounded the Śāṅkhya system, though he tries to make a hodgepodge of it by saying that Vedānta is Śāṅkhya and Śāṅkhya is Vedānta—claiming that the *Gītā* does not postulate ‘many *Purushas* (souls).’ A Madras Professor declared in Pachaiyappa’s Hall that the *Gītā* is silent on some of the specific doctrines of the Vedānta, such as the doctrine of *Māyā* and the identity of the human soul and the Supreme Soul, etc. And our colleague at the *Brahmavādin* also affirms in his editorial on ‘*Māyā*,’ dated August 15, 1896—after stating that the word *Māyā* scarcely occurs in the principal *Upanishads*, and where it does occur, it seems to be used mostly in the old Vedic sense of power or creative power—declares that “on the whole, the attitude of the *Bhagavad Gītā* toward *Māyā* is similar to that of the *Upanishads*; and it is rather difficult to evolve out of it the later Vedāntic sense,” of illusion or delusion.

And when it is also admitted that the Buddhists were the first to develop the Māyā theory of illusory nothings—who on that account were called Māyāvādins by the other Hindus—and that Śaṅkara only refined this idea, transforming it from meaning an illusory nothing into meaning a phenomenal something, though some of his later followers even went so far as to forget Śaṅkara's teaching and revert to the Buddhist idea of a blank negation and hence were called *crypto-Buddhists* (*Prachchanna Bauddhas*), (see p. 297, Vol. *Brahmavādin* and Max Müller's lectures on *Vedānta*); and since our brother's opinion is merely that in the Vedas, Upanishads, and *Gītā*, we have only the germs of the later system of thought from which the *Vedāntic theory of Māyā* was elaborated—a process of double distillation—it is even worth considering whether the *Gītā* has anything to do with the *Vedānta* at all. And it can also be positively proved that it has no such connection. Today we venture to go no further than what is admitted by the other side: that the *Gītā* contains the exposition of other schools of philosophy which, according to Mr. Charles Johnstone, postulate the reality and eternity of matter (*Prakṛiti*) and spirit (*Purusha*), and that the *Purushas* are without number, and that there is one Supreme Spirit different from the souls.

In understanding the word *Śāṅkhya* as used in the *Gītā*, our writer falls into a mistake, like many others, in thinking that it means the philosophy as expounded in the Śāṅkhya School of Philosophy which is attributed to the Sage Kapila. We have shown in our article on 'Another Side' (see pp. 21 to 34) that it meant no such thing, but meant merely a theory, a system, a philosophy, or knowledge. We showed that the *Gītā*, instead of having anything to do with Kapila's *Śāṅkhya*, distinctly repudiates it and goes on to postulate its own differences; and this we showed by quoting several passages, demonstrating that the proper name of the system evolved in the *Gītā* is '*Seshvara Śāṅkhya*,' as distinguished from the *Nīreshvara Śāṅkhya* of Kapila. To say that this philosophy or the other grew out of this or that is pure fallacy, unless we have real historical

You might argue that Theism was next and grew out of Atheism, as materialists (*Lokāyitas*) only admit the eternal nature of matter and would not admit the existence of any other *padārtha* (category). And you might say they came next because they denied the existence of God admitted by Theists. Yet such is the argument hidden in statements frequently made that, of the six systems of Philosophy, one was first and the others arose from it. Some postulate only one *padārtha*, some two, some three, and some none; and are we to talk of reconciling these with one another simply because one of the postulates—very often things and their qualities which could not be denied by anyone—is common to all or some? This is often the kind of writing that passes for sound knowledge, liberalism, and universal philosophy. It should also be noted that the word *Vêdānta* never occurs in the Gītā or other Upanishads as meaning *Śaṅkara's* system, and the *Brahmavādin* has, as such, adopted a broader platform by properly including under the term both the *Advaita* of *Śaṅkara*, and the *Dvaita* and *Viśishṭādvaita* systems. We now hear of Advaita Vedānta, Dvaita Vedānta, etc., although the Western habit of calling *Śaṅkara's* system *Vêdānta* is still used in a rather confusing way by people, as in the passage we quoted above from the *Brahmavādin*: "the later *Vêdāntic* sense." (The other Indian schools, it should be noted, do not indeed call *Śaṅkara's* system *Vêdānta* or Advaita, but have other names for it).

Mr. Johnstone certainly says that Kṛishṇa quotes directly from many Upanishads (one writer is so carried away by his veneration for the Gītā that he says the Upanishads quote from the Gītā!) and a number of verses, notably in the second book (we would very much like to know what they are), which have the true ring of the old sacred teachings and yet are not in them (in which?) as they now stand. He then presents his theory that *Vêdānta* is the peculiar birthright of the Kshatriyas and not of the Brāhmans. The reason why these unattributed quotations are found in the Gītā and other similar books is that every Brāhman in the olden days had memorized the whole of the Vedas and *Vêdānta* (Upanishads). As such, when they wrote and when they spoke, these old thoughts and verses naturally flowed from their pens and their mouths, and it is never the habit of the Indian scholar to quote his authority, chapter, and verse. We also come to the fact that the whole of chapters 9, 10, and 11 of the Gītā is a mere reproduction and a short abstract of that central portion of the entire Vedas called the *Śatarudriya* of the *Yajur Veda*. What is called transfiguration is the *Viśvasvarūpa Darśana*, or the—

the vision of the Lord as the All, as manifested in the entire universe. Every object in the whole universe—good and bad, *sat* (being) and *asat* (non-being), high and low, animate and inanimate—is named in succession. God is identified with all of these, yet it is pointed out that He is not merely these things but is above them all: “the soul of all things, the creator of all things, the one who pervades all things” (*Viśvātmanē Viśva sṛije viśvam avṛitiya tishṭhatē*). This *Śatarudriya* <note>A sacred Vedic hymn to Rudra/Shiva.</note> ought to be known to every Brahman to some extent, and it is the portion of the Vedas which is recited in the temples every day. The praise of the *Śatarudriya* occurs throughout the *Mahabharata*, most notably in the *Drona* and *Anushasana Parvas*. These chapters, dealing as they do with various visions of God’s universal form (*Vishvarupa Darshana*) as granted to the sages Upamanyu, Vyasa, Narada, Kapila, and Krishna himself on other occasions, contain similar reproductions of the *Śatarudriya* as seen in chapters 9 to 11 of the *Gita*. What is more important to note is that, in the case of Krishna, he had received the teaching from the great sage Upamanyu. After initiation (*Diksha*) into this mystical teaching and the performance of austerities (*tapas*), he witnessed the *vision* himself, and he describes it as follows (see pages 87 to 91 of the *Anushasana Parva*, P. C. Roy’s translation):

> “The hair on my head, O son of Kunti, stood on its end, and my eyes widened with wonder upon beholding Hara, the refuge of all the deities and the dispeller of all their griefs. .........................Before me that Lord of all the Gods, namely Sarva, appeared seated in all his glory. Seeing that Ishana had shown Himself to me by being seated in glory before my eyes, the whole universe, from Prajapati to Indra, looked at me. I, however, did not have the power to look at Mahadeva. The great Deity then addressed me, saying, ‘Behold, O Krishna, and speak to me. You have adored me hundreds and thousands of times.’”

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between Krishna and Arjuna and refutes the theory that the divine transfiguration (Vishvarupa) was derived from aboriginal South Indian traditions, instead tracing it to the Vedas.</meta>

# THE UNION OF INDIAN PHILOSOPHIES.

"There is no one in the three worlds who is dearer to me than you." And the praise by Krishna which follows is almost what Arjuna himself sang about Krishna. Vyasa, meeting Ashvatthama after his final defeat, also tells him that Krishna and Arjuna had worshipped the Lord hundreds and thousands of times. And does this not explain Krishna's own words in the Gita that he and Arjuna had innumerable births (iv. 5)?

What we wish to point out is that this transfiguration scene, with its gruesome description which Mr. Johnstone wants to trace to *Puranic* legends preserved from South Indian aborigines, is—by express text and by the authority of Krishna himself—traced to the second Veda. To say that the Yajur Veda—the central portion of the Vedas—should copy the holiest portion of all the Vedas (as believed by the contemporaries and predecessors of Krishna) from the demonology of the South Indians could only be a parody of the truth. If this were true, this demonology of the South Indians, instead of being something repugnant, must have been glorious indeed to be copied by the *Brahmavadins* of the Yajur Veda days. Western scholars have only misread and misunderstood the nature of this transfiguration and *Vishvarupa* mystery, just as they have misread the mystic personality of Rudra or Shiva himself, whose ideal these scholars say was also copied from the aborigines. To the credit of Mrs. Besant, it should be said that she has understood both of these mysteries better than any other European. Shiva’s whole personality, with his eight forms, the Ashtamuhurtams (see page 220 of the *Siddhanta*

<meta>This page continues the description of Shiva's eight forms (Ashtamurtis) and the terrifying yet majestic nature of the Supreme Being as revealed in the Gita and other scriptures, refuting the idea that these concepts originated from aboriginal traditions.</meta>

(*Dīpikā* Vol. I, for a full description) earth, fire, air, etc.; and his three eyes as *Sōma*, *Sūrya*, and *Agni*; and His Head as *Ākāśa*; and his eight arms as the eight cardinal points; his feet as *Patala*; and the sky as his garment, *Digambara*. He himself is a *Nirvāni* living in cemeteries, and yet he is with his *Šakti*, *Umā*—a *Yōgi* yet a *Bhōgi*. All these provide a conception of the supreme majesty of the Supreme Being which, undoubtedly, no one can look at directly. Does any ordinary person dare to look into nature's secrets and nature's ways in the processes of destruction, creation, and sustenance? If so, he would be a bold and great man. Strip nature of its smooth and fragrant outer cloak, and what do you see inside? The picture is ugly, dirty, and gruesome. Yet the scientist perceives all this with perfect equanimity—indeed, with very great pleasure. A small drop of water reveals under microscopic examination multitudes of living germs, and these fight with one another and devour each other with great eagerness. We drink the water. Plants drink the water. Animals eat the plants, and insects and animals devour one another. Man, the greatest monster, devours everything. Thus, there is a constant struggle of life and death going on in nature. And when nature is thus exposed to view in the transfiguration, and Arjuna sees this havoc before him in the Person of the Supreme as the Destroyer (the "Devourer" of the *Katha Upanishat*)—and it should be remembered that this *Visvasvarūpa Darshan* is undoubtedly more gruesome in the *Gītā* than similar ones presented in the *Anušāsana Parva*, as Kṛishṇa's entire burden of advice in the *Gītā* is simply to force Arjuna to fight and kill his enemies and to conquer his repugnance—a remark that it is derived from *Purāṇic* legends and aboriginal practices is altogether out of place. We hope to pursue this subject on a future occasion.

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Bhagavat Gītā, transitioning from a description of the terrifying divine form (Visvasvarūpa) to a scholarly analysis of the Gītā's place within the various schools of Indian philosophy.</meta>

# THE UNION OF INDIAN PHILOSOPHIES.*

In ancient India, as elsewhere, the minds of the leading men were of many complexions; so we have great idealists, profound thinkers of the atomic school, absolute nihilists, and great teachers of doctrines that are wholly agnostic. It is the custom to group a certain set of these teachings together under the title of the Six Philosophies; while all others, considered heterodox, are outside the circle of sympathy and are, therefore, ignored. Most prominent among the outcast philosophies is the doctrine of Prince Siddhārta, also called Šākya Muni and Gautama Buddha. Regarding the others, it would be difficult to find many students of more than three—namely, the Vêdānta, Šākya <note>Likely a reference to Sāñkhya, as indicated in the following paragraph</note>, and Yōga; while the Vaišēshika, Nyāya, and the first Mimāmsa are little more than names, even to dedicated students of Indian thought. They certainly have their followers; but there has not been found one among them with enough intellectual force to give them a modern expression or to show that they carry any message for the modern world. We shall speak here only of the three most popular among the orthodox schools, and this chiefly in connection with a single noteworthy book—the Bhagavat Gītā, or "Songs of the Master." If we were asked spontaneously which of the three schools the Bhagavat Gītā belonged to, we would most likely answer that it was undoubtedly a textbook of the Vêdānta, and indeed one of the most important works of the Vêdānta School. For is it not commented upon by the Great Šañkara, the greatest light of the Vêdānta, and does he not quote from it as a divine authority, a fully inspired scripture?

Yet, despite all this, I think there are other aspects of the Bhagavat Gītā which show that this answer is too simple. While the Songs of the Master undoubtedly form a bulwark of Vêdāntic orthodoxy, there is very much in them which belongs to the Sāñkhya, and even more that is the property of the Yōga School. It seems quite certain that the Bhagavat Gītā has grown up gradually, beginning with a ballad about Kṛishṇa and Arjuna, much of which is preserved in the first book, and which suggests throughout the core of Kṛishṇa's advice: *Therefore fight, O son of Kunti!* It seems likely that the next element in the structure of the Bhagavat Gītā is drawn from the great Upaniṣhats, the Katha Upaniṣhat more especially. And this suggests a very interesting—

# THE UNION OF INDIAN PHILOSOPHIES.

thought; side by side with many direct quotations from the *Upanishads* in our possession, there are a number of verses, notably in the second book, which have the true ring of the ancient sacred teachings, and yet are not in them as they now stand. And this suggests that we have only fragments; that there was once much more, in the form of verses and stories, which made up the mystery teaching of the Rajput Kings—that secret doctrine spoken of so clearly in the *Upanishads* themselves as the jealously guarded possession of the Kshatriya race. The fourth book of the *Bhagavad Gita* fully endorses this idea, since Krishna traces his doctrine back through the Rajput sages to the solar King, Ikshvaku, to Manu, the Kshatriya, and finally to the sun, the guiding spirit of the Rajput race. And this is in connection with that teaching of successive rebirths, which, we know from the two greatest *Upanishads*, was the central point of the royal doctrine. So we are inclined to suggest that we have in many verses of the *Bhagavad Gita* additional portions of the old mystery doctrine, parts of which form the great *Upanishads*. And it is quite credible that Krishna—whom we believe to be as truly historical as Julius Caesar—as an initiate in these doctrines did actually quote to Arjuna a series of verses from the mystery teaching, and that these verses are faithfully preserved for us to the present day. However that may be, there the verses are: a series of verses from the *Upanishads*, followed by a second series, entirely resembling these in style and thought. As a third element in the *Bhagavad Gita* we have the Puranic episode of the transfiguration, and, we must say, it reproduces all that grim and gruesome ugliness of many-armed gods, with terrible teeth, which the Puranas have preserved most probably from the wild faiths of the dark aboriginals and demon worshippers of Southern India.

Finally, there is a very important element, into the midst of which the episode of the transfiguration is forcibly wedged; and of this element we shall more especially speak. It consists of the characteristic Sankhya doctrine of the three potencies of Nature completely developed along physical, mental, and moral lines. A word about this doctrine, which we may, with great likelihood, refer to Kapila himself, the founder of the School. His conception seems to be this: there is the consciousness in us, the spirit, the perceiver: and, over against this, there is Nature, the manifested world. This duality of subject and object has a great gulf fixed between its two elements, whose characteristics are wholly and irreconcilably opposed. Of the subject, the spirit, consciousness, we can only say that it perceives. To attribute to consciousness any characteristic drawn from our experience of objects, such for instance as mortality, beginning, or end, is to be guilty of a cardinal error. Of Nature, the

<summary>The author argues that the Bhagavad Gita contains lost fragments of an ancient "mystery teaching" belonging to the Rajput Kings and identifies three distinct elements within the text: Upanishadic verses, Puranic imagery, and the Sankhya philosophy of Kapila.</summary>

<keywords>Bhagavad Gita, Upanishads, Rajput Kings, Krishna, Kshatriya, Puranic transfiguration, Sankhya doctrine, Kapila, duality of subject and object</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the different philosophical components integrated into the Bhagavad Gita, moving from the Puranic elements to the dualistic system of Kapila and the practical discipline of Yoga.</meta>

# THE UNION OF INDIAN PHILOSOPHIES.

As the opposite element of existence, Kapila’s teaching, it seems, was something like this: Nature may be divided into three elements: the substance of phenomena, the force of phenomena, and thirdly the dark space or void in which phenomena take place. Take a simple illustration. The observer, with closed eyes, is the spirit or consciousness, not yet involved in Nature. He opens his eyes and, instead of the dark space or void, sees the world of visible objects or substance, and there is perpetual movement among the things thus observed. This is force. Thus we have the three elements of Nature—the three qualities, as they are generally called—which make up the central idea of Kapila’s cosmic system, and which are not to be found in that form in any of the oldest <term>Upanishats</term>; they are, therefore, no part of the <term>Vèdānta</term> properly so called, but distinctively <term>Sāṅkhya</term> teachings. Now, these distinctive teachings form a very important part of the <term>Bhagavat Gītā</term> and are woven into many passages, besides the chief passages already referred to in the seventeenth and eighteenth books. Thus, as early as the second book, we have a reference to the <term>Sāṅkhya</term> teachings: “The <term>Vêdas</term> have the three Nature-powers as their object; but you, Arjuna, become free from the three powers.” It is unnecessary to quote the many passages that refer to the same teaching: the divisions of the knower, the knowing, and the known; the doer, the doing, and the deed; the gift, the giving, and the giver; and so forth, according to the three Nature-powers. All this is carried out with much intellectual skill and dialectic acumen, but it has nothing in the world to do with the main motive of the book—Arjuna’s action under the calamity of civil war, and Krishna’s assertion of the soul as the solution to Arjuna’s dilemma.

There is also a very important element in the <term>Bhagavat Gītā</term> that is equally characteristic of the <term>Yōga</term> school, whose final exponent (though probably not its founder) was <term>Patañjali</term>, the author of the commentary on <term>Pāṇini</term>’s grammar, who lived, it is believed, some three centuries before our era. We do not regard the directions for choosing a lonely place, a fawn-skin seat over sprinkled kusha grass, and the fixing of the attention on the tip of the nose as necessarily or most characteristically belonging to the <term>Yōga</term> school, though they are undoubtedly important elements in that teaching. What seems more vital is the moral concept of action with disinterestedness—of action without attachment, according to the primary motion of the will. This teaching, it seems to us, is both characteristic of the <term>Yōga</term> system and foreign to the spirit of the <term>Upanishats</term>; for the <term>Upanishats</term>, so high is their ideal, are not greatly concerned with fallen man or the means of his redemption. They look on man as an immortal spirit, already free and mighty, and

<summary>This page discusses the integration of Sāṅkhya and Yōga philosophies into the Bhagavad Gītā, contrasting Kapila's "three qualities" and Patañjali's concept of disinterested action with the older Upanishadic view of the soul.</summary>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the integration of various Indian philosophical schools within the Bhagavad Gītā, specifically focusing on the transition from the concept of a spirit independent of Nature to one requiring redemption through the will.</meta>

# THE UNION OF INDIAN PHILOSOPHIES.

...therefore needing no redemption. Man, needing to be redeemed, is a later thought; one springing from a more self-conscious age.

Now the connection of this thought with the <term>Sāṅkhya</term> philosophy is obvious. It regards man, the spirit, as ensnared by Nature, and consequently as needing release; and, for the <term>Sāṅkhya</term> school, this release comes through an effort of intellectual insight. But this concept, man saved by intellect, is essentially untrue to life, where man lives not by intellect alone, or even chiefly, but by the will; and it became necessary, granting our fall, to find a way of salvation, of redemption through the will. This way is the <term>Yoga</term> philosophy. It is the natural counterpart and completion of the <term>Sāṅkhya</term> and has always been so regarded. The pure spirit of the over-intellectual <term>Sāṅkhya</term> becomes Lord of the more religious <term>Yoga</term>—using religion in the sense of redemption to the will. But, though thus complementary, the two systems might easily come to be considered as opposing each other; and it seems to be part of the mission of the <term>Bhagavat Gītā</term>—or rather, of certain passages forcibly imported into it—to reconcile the <term>Sāṅkhya</term> and the <term>Yoga</term> once for all, and to blend these two with the <term>Vēdānta</term>.

We need only quote two passages, which are obviously due to the <term>Sāṅkhya</term>-<term>Yoga</term> reconciler. The first is dragged into the middle of the following sentence, and evidently has no true place there: “If slain, you shall attain to heaven; or conquering, you shall inherit the land. Therefore rise, son of Kuntī, firmly resolved for the fight. Holding as equal good and ill fortune, gain and loss, victory and defeat, prepare yourself for the fight, and you shall not incur sin. And thus there shall be no loss of ground, nor does any defeat exist; a little of this law saves from great fear;”—the law, namely, that those slain in battle go to Paradise. Now into the midst of this complete and continuous passage has been inserted this verse: “This understanding is declared according to <term>Sāṅkhya</term>; hear it now, according to <term>Yoga</term>.” Needless to say, the last part of it has as little to do with the <term>Yoga</term> philosophy as the first has with the <term>Sāṅkhya</term>. Then again, in the next book, the third: “Two rules are laid down by me: salvation by intellect for the <term>Sāṅkhya</term>; salvation by works for the followers of <term>Yoga</term>.” So that one part of the <term>Bhagavat Gītā</term> is devoted to the reconciliation of these two complementary though rival schools.

The following passages in the book of Genesis have reference to the subject in hand. "And out of the ground the Lord God made every tree grow that is pleasant to the sight and good for food; *the tree of life also in the middle of the Garden, and the tree of knowledge of good and evil*" (ii. 9). "And the Lord God commanded the man, saying, 'Of every tree of the garden you may freely eat. But of the tree of knowledge of good and evil, *you shall not eat of it; for in the day that you eat of it, you shall surely die*'" (ii. 16 and 17). "And they were both naked, the man and his wife, and were not ashamed" (ii. 25). "And the serpent said to the woman, 'You will not surely die. For God knows that in the day you eat of it, your eyes shall be opened and you shall be like gods, knowing good and evil.' And when the woman *saw the tree was good for food, that it was pleasant to the eyes, and a tree to be desired to make one wise*, she took of its fruit and ate, and also gave to her husband with her, and he ate. And the eyes of both of them were opened and they knew that they were naked" (iii. 4 to 7). "To the woman he said, 'I will greatly multiply your sorrow and your conception.' 'In sorrow shall you eat of it all the days of your life'" (iii. 16 and 17). "And the Lord God said, 'Behold, the man has become like one of us, to know good and evil; and now, lest he reach out his hand and take also from the tree of life, and eat and live forever.' Therefore the Lord God sent him out from the Garden of Eden" (iii. 22 and 23).

And now we ask, what are we to understand by this story? Are we to take it literally, as many would suggest, or are we to leave it as a mystery too deep for words to explain? And

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the biblical "Tree of Knowledge," interpreting it through the lens of spiritual liberation (moksha) and worldly bondage (Bhaṇḍa).</meta>

### TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

Yet this is the mystery of mysteries, the original mystery by which we came to be born and to die. If we can here get a clue to our birth and death, can we not thereby unravel secrets by which we can surely prevent our death and rebirth, and gain everlasting life? And surely there must be an explanation, for the words "Tree of life" and "Tree of knowledge of good and evil" cannot be mistaken in their real meaning, and these cannot be identified with any earthly tree actually in existence. The Tree here is clearly a metaphor signifying the soul’s True Being in freedom (<term>moksha</term>) and its false life in <term>Bhaṇḍa</term> <note>bondage</note>, the light and shadow of our human existence. As bound up in the world, the sum of our existence consists in our knowledge of likes and dislikes, of what leads to our pleasure and what gives us pain, and our memory of both; and as Doctor Bain would define it, the sense of similarity, difference, and retentiveness. That is to say, our human knowledge is built up from our very birth from a series of acts and experiences which give us pleasure or pain, or make us indifferent, and our sense of them; and Desire and Will are also slowly built up. The greater the pleasure we imagine a certain act or experience gives us, the more we *desire* its repetition or continuance; the greater the pain we fear from an act, the more we hate its repetition or continuance. But it happens also that the greater the pleasure or the pain, and the longer its continuance, or the more often it is repeated, the pleasure itself palls and we grow indifferent to the pain. Life may therefore be divided into a series of acts, or a sequence of them, one flowing from another and close upon each, each yielding a certain result or experience or fruit, whether it be pleasure or pain, good or evil. And God’s injunction was that we should not eat the fruit of the knowledge of good and evil or experience the pleasure or pain which will flow from our acts of good and evil in this tree of worldly life.

And one can ask why it is we should not seek the direction of our inclination, why we should not secure the good in life and the pleasure and happiness thereof, and avoid the evil and the pain and suffering thereof, and the best knowledge that will

--- Page 218 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the biblical Tree of Knowledge, examining the difficulty humans face in distinguishing true good from evil and the misunderstanding of the self or "I." The text picks up from a sentence regarding the search for a clue to birth and death.</meta>

...secure for us the attainment of these ends? And God’s command appears even stranger when it is seen that there is not only a command not to try to know evil, but also a command that we should not know good. And to know good—if not to know evil—must at least appear to us to be our duty. And all our moral textbooks and lessons and sermons are intended to teach us this duty. And yet, the fruits or acts resulting from our knowledge of both good and bad are forbidden to humanity, and the punishment for disobeying this Law or Word of God is said to be death itself, with the further penalty of being excluded from partaking of the everlasting Tree of Life.

And of course, there may be no wrong in our *knowing* what is good for *us* and what is bad, and in our *desiring* to seek the one and avoid the other—provided we *can know* what is *really* good and what is bad, provided we can obtain what we *desire*, and provided also that we *can know* what it is that we mean by the 'us' or '*I*'. Do all people understand what will *really* bring them good and what will bring them evil? Is every act that gives pleasure automatically a good, and every act that gives pain a wrong? When a child cries for sweets and struggles hard against swallowing a bitter potion, is it really seeking its own good and avoiding evil? When a schoolboy chafes under school discipline and desires to "sow his wild oats," is he really avoiding pain and seeking pleasure? Does the man of the world, when he seeks power and wealth and resorts to all sorts of ways to gain that end, really seek his own good? Or when he chafes in a prison as a result of his previous actions, does he think that it is for his good? And then again, when we seek pleasure beyond our means, does that not really bring us suffering? Above all, how many of us rightly understand the '*I*' to which we want to minister? For the great majority, the '*I*' means nothing more than the bare body and the external senses; and is not the whole world engaged most strenuously in satisfying its bodily wants and appetites? How many are there who understand that they have a moral nature? How many understand that they have a spiritual nature? Even when we

# TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

...do know that we have a moral nature and a spiritual nature, how many try to live up to the requirements of their moral and spiritual nature, while being more or less dragged down and constrained by their worldly *desire*! In our ideas of good and bad, don't we confuse our various natures? Don't we confuse what is good for the soul with what is good for the body? To most of us, the world and our physical appetites are our God and nothing more.

Where, therefore, does this difference in people's likes and dislikes come from? Where does their inability to match means to ends, their ignorance of their real selves, and their mistaking of one thing for another come from? Does it not show that there is an original lack of understanding, a lack of power, and a lack of real knowledge—a serious defect in all types and conditions of people? And when, because of this lack of knowledge, the first wrong step is taken and the first mistake is made, does it not lead to a series of failures and a succession of mistakes? And does man not commit more mistakes in his ignorance when he tries to fix one error than when he leaves it alone?

We do not propose to answer the question of where this defect or ignorance in humanity came from, or what its nature is, etc. For our current purpose, it is enough to know and recognize that this defect exists within each and every one of us; that we are all full of faults and liable to make mistakes at every step. And these defects were in Eve, the original woman, who represents the lower nature of man (with Adam meaning the higher life of man, pulled down by the lower part of him). And when Eve saw *the tree was good for food*, it means she only thought of what would give pleasure to her body and satisfy her appetite, regardless of the consequences, just as a child wants to grab sweets from a candy shop. She saw that *it was pleasant to the eyes*: that is to say, she mistook what was not good for something that was good. <gloss>*</gloss> She saw it was

மருளானாம் மாணாப் பிறப்பு.
The delusion by which people believe that which is not the truth to be the truth—that is the cause of a miserable birth.

# TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

...a *tree to be desired to make one wise.* <note>Desire, it is said, is the seed that produces eternal birth for all living beings at all times. (Tirukkural 361)</note> And when that most learned of scholars, full of his own knowledge and wisdom, wanted St. Meykaṇḍān to inform him of the nature of Āṇava, or Ahaṅkāra, or Egoism, what was the reply he received? The True Seer replied that the Āṇava, or Ignorance, or Egoism stood disclosed before him. One desires to be wise, as Eve desired, then learns much and thinks himself wise; this is the highest type of Egoism or Ignorance.

So it is clear that before Eve ate the forbidden fruit, she was ignorant and filled with Egoism or Āṇava. To say that the serpent or the Devil misled her is to carry the problem back one step. If she were wise, she would not have been misled by the tricks of the tempter. If she had known beforehand what was to happen to her, she would not have yielded to the words of the serpent and disobeyed the word of God. She therefore had no knowledge and no foresight. She was weak and ignorant even before the temptation. Being ignorant and weak, the moment the fruits of pleasure and pain were placed before her, she was dazzled, she was attracted, and she seized them at once. And the devil vanishes from the scene. We take it that the devil merely represents this inherent weakness, or ignorance, or Āṇava in man, and nothing more. Adam and Eve represent the mere infants of human creation. There is something in the youngest infant that makes it desire to live, learn, and know. It tries to put everything into its mouth, whether a piece of bread or a piece of chalk, and it wants to understand the anatomy of every toy it handles by pulling it to pieces. Can any amount of warning and advice prevent a baby from touching the flame of a burning candle? The loving parent no doubt gives the warning, "Don't touch, don't touch," but the advice is all useless, and the wise father usually allows it to get a singeing—enough for it to know the good and evil, the pain and pleasure of it—and he takes care that the baby is not burnt. # TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

...a brilliantly colored and glowing fruit of the strychnine tree; the baby will seize it and try to bite it, but the ever-watchful father will take care to see that the baby does not swallow it. It is our love that prompts us to give instruction, advice, warning, and even discipline, but all this will be wasted if the soil itself is not good. And in our wisdom, we recognize that all this is of no use, and that the wayward child should be allowed to gain peace by tasting the bitterness "of sorrow in all the days of its life." So, too, the All-loving Father in Heaven told Adam and Eve what was not good for them: not to taste or desire the fruits of both good and bad acts—that is, the pleasures and pains of this world. But they would not keep it in mind nor listen. Did God not know that they would be tempted, and did he try to save them from the Devil? No; he permitted them to be tempted. Indeed, he willed them to taste the fruit just as a father would allow a child to touch a candle flame ever so slightly. "Whom the Lord loves he disciplines," "நல்லபரமசிவன் நம்மை வருத்துவது கொல்லவல, பொல்லாக்குணந் டோக்க்" <note>"The good Lord Shiva afflicts us not to kill us, but to remove our evil nature."</note>. And the misery and suffering that flow from our tasting of the fruit of good and evil acts are merely for our discipline and purification, and this can only be achieved in this existence and no other; thus, the whole purpose and scheme of creation becomes evident. (*Śivajñānabodha* first Sūtra 'மலத்துதைதாம்' <note>"Because of the existence of the three impurities (Malas)..."</note>.)

It is for the purpose of removing this defect, weakness, <term>Āṇava</term>, or egoism in man that this life is given to him, and every means which a loving Father can devise for his improvement is afforded him. But all such means do not influence each individual in the same way. The best education, the purest home influence, and the holiest associations seem actually wasted on some people. They have a disposition of their own, their own individuality, and this asserts itself under all guises and under all covers. This contradicts the theory that the human mind is a mere *tabula rasa*. As the saying goes, youth and white paper take impressions easily. Evolutionists look to heredity to explain it. But it is now acknowledged that heredity does not explain everything. <meta>This page continues the allegorical interpretation of the Tree of Knowledge, moving from the necessity of suffering for purification to the explicit connection between the biblical Fall and the Indian philosophical doctrine of Karma.</meta>

# TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

...the most wicked of children. Neither Western theologians nor their scientific peers have explained this aspect of the matter, and we must admit that this is the only weak point in modern Christianity that its best defenders have been unable to fortify. It does not require much thought to see that this story of man's first disobedience, and of his tasting the fruit of that forbidden tree, is nothing more than the Doctrine of <term>Karma</term> as taught by all the Indian schools of philosophy, including the Buddhists.

The knowledge of good and evil is good and bad <term>Karma</term> (நல்வினை and தீவினை), and its fruits are the pleasures and pains derived from such acts. There is no harm in performing good and bad acts, but these acts should not be performed for the sake of their fruits, or out of selfish desire or dislike. The moment these acts are performed with such desire, the thirst (அவா, <term>Trishna-Tanha</term>) for such enjoyment increases, and the bonds of worldly existence are tightened further. The fruits of both are bad and are compared to gold and iron fetters; Saint <term>Tiruvaḷḷuvar</term> calls them இருள்சேர் இருவினை, meaning "the two kinds of <term>Karma</term>, covered in darkness." It is significant how, in the Indian schools of philosophy, the phrase வினைப்பயன் நுகர்தல்—meaning "eating the fruits of <term>Karma</term>"—is the most common expression and one that exactly corresponds to eating the forbidden fruit of good and evil in the Biblical accounts. Furthermore, the tree of good and evil fruits—a single tree from which both fruits are produced—is a common figure in the Upanishads and in the Tamil <term>Siddhāṇta</term> works.

The following passages in <term>Muṇḍaka Upanishat</term> 3.1–4, which are repeated in the <term>Kaṭha</term> and <term>Śvetaśvatra</term> Upanishads and are derived from the <term>Rigveda</term>, explain the matter fully:

> 1. Two birds, inseparable friends, cling to the same tree; one of them eats the sweet fruit, and *the other* looks on without eating.

> 2. On the same tree, man (<term>anīśa</term>) sits grieving, overwhelmed by his own helplessness. But when he sees the *other* Lord (<term>Īśa</term>) contented and knows His glory, then his grief passes away.

<summary>The author identifies the biblical story of the forbidden fruit with the Indian doctrine of Karma, noting that the "eating of fruit" is a common metaphor for experiencing the results of one's actions in both traditions. He supports this by citing the Upanishadic allegory of two birds on a single tree, representing the soul and the Lord.</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Christianity, Buddhism, Mundaka Upanishad, Tamil Siddhānta, Tiruvaḷḷuvar, Trishna-Tanha, allegory, good and evil, nalvinai, tīvinai, anīśa, Īśa</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the Tree of Knowledge and the nature of the soul's liberation, moving from the comparison of Karma to Biblical themes into a translation and commentary of Upanishadic verses regarding divine realization and the indwelling of God.</meta>

# TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

3. When the seer sees the brilliant Maker and Lord of the world, and himself as if in the womb of God, then he is wise; and *shaking off good and evil*, he reaches the highest oneness, free from passions.

4. He is surely the Life who flames through all creation. The wise man, knowing Him, speaks of nothing else. He sports in God, finds his delight in God, yet he performs deeds (truthfulness, penance, meditation, etc.); he is the best of God’s universe.

5. This God is to be reached by truth alone, and meditation, by knowledge, and pure and constant discipline. He is in the midst of the body, made entirely of Light, translucent; whom disciplined men, with their sins washed away, behold.

6. That heavenly-bright being, of a nature transcending thought, shines out both vast and rarer than the rare; farther than the far, yet also close at hand, right here in all who see, nestling within the heart.

7. He is not grasped by the eye, nor by speech, nor by the other powers, nor by mere meditation, or even holy deeds. By calm wisdom, pure in essence, then and only then does one behold Him, who is free from parts, in ecstasy.

The second <term>mantra</term> is thus commented on by <term>Śrīkaṇṭhāchārya</term> (see <term>Siddhānta Dīpikā</term> Vol. 2, p. 74). The traditional interpretation of this passage is given as follows:

> “The <term>Jiva</term>, bound by the shackles of beginningless <term>Karma</term>, having entered into many a body made of <term>Māya</term> (physical matter)—each suited to the enjoying of a particular fruit—is subjected to much incurable misery; and unable to ward it off on account of his impotence, he does not know what to do and grieves. He is thus immersed in the ocean of grief, caused by his great delusion. When, however, by the *Lord’s grace*, he intuitively sees Him, who as the Impeller dwells within himself, who is gracious to all, and who is ever associated with <term>Umā</term> (Love and Light), then he attains to the unsurpassed greatness of the Lord, free from all grief. Therefore, though <term>Śiva</term>, who is independent and who has been free from <term>samsāra</term> from time without beginning, is in contact with the body, he is not subject to its evils, as the <term>Jiva</term> is. This is why the <term>Jiva</term> and <term>Parameśvara</term> are said to be in the cave of the heart.”

St. <term>Tirumūlar</term> has the following stanza:

->வம்பு பழுத்த மலர்பழ மொன்றுண்டு<-

> Meanwhile, the mighty heavenly stream
> Rises and rushes, crowned with bubbles of delight,
> Eddies around, and dashes against the bank of our ‘embodiment,’
> *And our twofold deeds, growing from age to age,*—

> It rushes through the cleft of the high hills,
> Is imprisoned in the encircling lake,
> Where the wide-open, fragrant flowers grow—
> In the tank, where the smoke of the *agil* rises and beetles hum;
> And as it swells with ever-increasing joy,
> The devotee-ploughmen in the field of worship
> Sow the seed of love in rich abundance!
> Hail, CLOUD-LIKE GOD,* who is hard to reach in this universe!
