<meta>This page continues the discussion on the metaphorical "Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil," transitioning from the poetic descriptions of St. Māṇicka vāchakar to the philosophical elaborations of St. Paṭṭinattār and the distinctions between Buddhist and Siddhānta theology regarding the cessation of the ego.</meta>

# TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

...and St. Paṭṭinattār has a much more elaborate passage regarding the uprooting of this (நஞ்சுமாமரம்) poisonous Mango tree in *Tiruvidai Marudūr Mummaṇik-Kovai* (10).

The tree of knowledge of good and evil is the Karmic Life of the individual, made up of the accumulated acts performed by him that remain in a perfect and unchangeable chain of causes and effects, following the man closely like his shadow, as distinguished from the tree of life which is the light within him. It is this Karmic existence—this tree of shadow—which the Buddhists postulated, rather than anything like the tree of Life or the true soul postulated by the theistic Hindu Schools; they recognized nothing higher than this impermanent though continuous (like a stream) Karmic Life. To them, all existence seemed only as sorrow and evil, and the complete cessation or annihilation of this Karmic existence, through the attainment of mere knowledge, constituted their highest end. To them, there was no joy in life and no means of attaining such joy, as they would not recognize the all-loving Powers of the Supreme Lord, who could grant them such Joy out of His immeasurable Grace. The Siddhānta no doubt agreed with the Buddhist that his body (birth and death) must cease, his feelings must cease, his life must cease, his understanding must cease, and that his egoism must cease. But how and by what means could this cessation be brought about? The means are set forth succinctly in the tenth and eleventh Sūtras of *Sivajñānabotha*.

> “ அவனே தானே ஆகிய அந்நெறி
> ஏகனாகி இறைபணி நிற்க,
> மலமாயை தன்னொடு வல்வினையின்றே.”

As the Lord becomes one with the Soul in its human condition, so let the Soul become one with Him and perceive all its actions to be His. Then it will lose all its *Mala*, *Māyā*, and *Karma*.

> “ காணும் கண்ணுக்குக் காட்டு முளம்போல
> காணுவுளத்தைக் கண்டு காட்டலின்
> அயரா அன்பின் அரன்கழல் செலுமே.”

<summary>This page defines the "Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil" as the cycle of Karmic existence and contrasts the Buddhist view of annihilation with the Saiva Siddhānta path of achieving divine union through the Lord's grace. It references the tenth and eleventh Sutras of the Sivajñānabotha to explain how the soul can transcend Mala, Māyā, and Karma.</summary>

<keywords>St. Paṭṭinattār, Karmic Life, Buddhism, Saiva Siddhānta, Sivajñānabotha, Karma, Mala, Māyā, reincarnation, divine grace</keywords>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the "Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil," specifically focusing on how the soul attains union with the Divine (Hara) through Advaita knowledge and devotion, contrasting the Siddhānta path of grace with Buddhist concepts of annihilation.</meta>

# TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

As the soul enables the eye to see and itself sees, so Hara enables the soul to know and itself knows. This *Advaita* knowledge and undying love will unite the soul to His feet.

These means are: becoming one with God, dedicating one's acts to God, and practicing unceasing love and devotion to Him. By such dedication, one brings oneself into harmony with divine law and loses the pride of self-knowledge; consequently, one's own ignorance and Karma cease to operate, and the person's whole being becomes beautiful through the flood of His grace. In clear distinction from Buddhist ethics and psychology, the Siddhāṇti believes that salvation cannot be secured except through such self-renunciation and love for the Supreme.

> “ இருள்சேர் இருவினையும் சேரா இறைவன்
> பொருள்சேர் புகழ்புரிந்தார் மாட்டு.”
> “ வேண்டுதல் வேண்டாமையிலான் அடிசேர்ந்தார்க்கு
> யாண்டுமிடும்பையில.”
> “ பற்றுக பற்றற்றான் பற்றினைப்பற்று
> பற்றுகபற்றுவிடற்கு.”
> “ பிறப்பென்னும் பேதமை நீங்கச் சிறப்பென்னும்
> செம்பொருள் காண்ப தறிவு.”
> “ உணர்ந்தமா முனிவரும் பரோபரரொழிந்தார்
> உணர்வுக்கும் தெரிவரும் பொருளே
> யிணங்கிலி யெல்லா வுயிரட்கு முயிரே
> யெனைப் பிறப்பறுக்கு மெம்மருந்தே
> திணிந்ததோரிருளிற் றெளிந்ததூ வெளியபே
> திரட்பெருந் தரையறை சிவனே
> குணங்கடாலில்லாவின்பமே யுள்ளத்துக்
> குறிகின்ற தென்ன குறைவே.

He is the one not comprehended by the gods and the wise. He is the life of all life. He is the supreme panacea for all the ills of the flesh; and by obeying His law, no one knows death or birth. He is the shining light of our dark existence. He is the singular joy—one not born of physical life, nor of *Prakriti guna*, nor of the world and its transitory nature. By partaking in this joy, our highest desires are completely fulfilled, unlike the—

# TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

...joys of this world which always create a burning desire, a thirst for them, more and more like the unquenchable thirst of a confirmed drunkard. This supreme and irresistible Joy—as shown in other stanzas of the ‘House of God’ (*Kōyiṟ ṟiruppatikam*), கோயிற் றிருப்பதிகம்—fills our hearts, like a flood overflowing its banks, when, in all humility and love, our body and heart melt in His service.

The contrast between the transient world’s joy and the Joy that transcends all states without end, நிலாப்பதங்களியாவையுங் கடந்த இன்பம், is well brought out in the following stanza by the same Saint *Māṇickavāchakar*.

->தினைத்தனை யளவுள்ளதோர் பூவினிற் றேனுண்ணாதே<-
->நினைத்தலுங் காண்டலுந் பேசுந்தோறும் எப்போதும்<-
->அனைத்தெலும் புண்ணெக ஆனந்தத்தேன் சொரியும்<-
->குனிப்புடையானுக்கே சென்றுதாய் கோத்தும்பீ.<-

->Do not taste the flower-borne honey drop as tiny as a millet seed;<-
->Sing instead of Him who showers the honey of bliss<-
->So as to melt one’s very marrow,<-
->Whenever thinking, seeing, and speaking of Him, now and forever.<-

When this joy fills him, then does he rejoice in God and delight in God as the *Muṇḍaka* says; then “does he love God, delight in God, revel in God and rejoice in God,” as the *Chhāndogya* puts it. In this condition of *Svarāj*, when he can exclaim, “I am the most glorious of the glorious,” neither pain, nor the pleasures of this world, nor the fruits of the forbidden tree can touch or attract him. Although he does not stop performing his duties, such as truthfulness, meditation, *tapas*, etc., in this condition, even “if he moves about there, laughing or eating, playing or rejoicing (in his mind), be it with women, carriages, or relatives,” (Chhāndogya viii. 12. 3) these acts will not affect him, just as fire cannot burn a man who is practiced in *agni-stumbha* (see the principle stated in *Śivajñāna Siddhiyār*, X. 5 & 6).

Compare this with the Christian aspiration for divine joy:

> “If to anyone the tumult of the flesh were hushed, if the images of the earth, and water, and air were hushed; if the heavens themselves were hushed, and the very soul were hushed to herself, and by not...”

It describes a state of spiritual independence (Svarāj) where a person remains unaffected by worldly pleasures or pains while remaining centered in God.</summary>

# TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

...thinking on self surmounting self, silencing all dreams and imaginary revelations, every tongue and every sign, and whatever exists only in transition—since if we could hear, all these would say, "We did not make ourselves, but He who remains forever made us." If, then, having uttered this, they too should be silenced, having roused our ears to him who made them, and He alone should speak—not through them, but by Himself—so that we may hear His word, not through any human tongue, nor an angel's voice, nor the sound of thunder, nor in the dark riddle of a metaphor, but might hear Him whom we love in these things; if we might hear His very self without these (as we also now strained ourselves and in swift thought touched on the eternal wisdom which remains over all)—could this be continued, and other visions of a far different nature be withdrawn, and this one ravish and absorb and wrap up its beholder in these inward joys, so that life might be forever like that one moment of understanding which we now sighed for—would this not be "Enter into My Master's joy"? (St. Augustine's Confessions Book ix.)

வான்கெட்டு மாருத மாய்ந்தழல் நீர் மண்கெடினும்

The original fall was caused by disobeying God's law, by opposing our will to His will; the only way to salvation consists in establishing harmony between His will and ours, completely subordinating our will to His own, and allowing His will to be done on earth as it is in heaven.

When we were first created, we were just like children: fresh and innocent, fully trusting and depending on our loving parents without worrying about tomorrow, fully obeying their dictates, and never asserting ourselves or becoming self-willed. But the child preserves this condition for only a short time; it refuses to abide by the loving words of wisdom and warning given to it, wanting to know for itself. Slowly, its desire and self-will develop, and in its ignorance and conceit, it accumulates the load of <term>Karma</term>. Unless we become like children again, living in complete trust and faith in our Beloved Father, we cannot get rid of this sin and sorrow. And unless we are born again, we cannot see the kingdom of heaven, as declared by the same Jesus Christ, whom the world thought "was beside himself"—that is, mad. And our St. Tāyu-

# TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

Thayumanavar likens the nature of the saintly—"பாலரோடு பேயர் பித்தர் பான்மையென நிற்பதுவே சீலமிகுஞானியர் தம் செய்கை பராபரமே" <note>The conduct of the virtuous sages is like that of children, the possessed, and the mad, O Supreme!</note>—to babies, lunatics, and men possessed.

<term>Karma</term> or <term>vinai</term> (வினை) simply means an act, and this act may give pleasure or pain; if it gives pleasure, it is called good, and if it produces pain, it is called evil. Every good act is right and every evil act is wrong, or <term>Punyam</term> or <term>papam</term>, Virtue or sin. *Šivajñāna Siddhiyār* defines <term>punyam</term> and <term>papam</term> as <term>uyirkkitham seythal</term> (உயிர்க்கிதம்செய்தல்), doing good to all sentient creatures, and <term>uyirkkatham seythal</term> (உயிர்க்கதம் செய்தல்), doing evil to all creatures in the largest and broadest sense of the term, in the same way as any modern utilitarian philosopher would define these terms; we have no doubt that the definition is quite correct from any point of view. When we interpose conscience in the middle as a judge of good and evil, right and wrong, it is seen how varying the consciences of men are, and so we must necessarily seek a higher authority or test.

<term>Karma</term>, therefore, signifies acts or a series of acts, or the aggregate of human experience, acting and reacting on each other; and the Law of Karma means the invariable order or <term>Niyati</term> which results in pain or pleasure attaching itself to a doer in accordance with the kind of acts performed by him, in accordance with the maxim: நன்மை விதைத்தால் நன்மை விளையும், தீமை விதைத்தால் தீமை விளையும், "He who sows must reap accordingly" <note>Literally: If you sow good, good will result; if you sow evil, evil will result.</note>.

One result of this law is that the respective fruits have to be enjoyed in a suitable body, and this body is determined by the <term>Karma</term> performed by each (See *Šivajñānabotha* II. 2. ab). If his previous <term>Karma</term> is good, he will get a good body, and if it is bad, he will get a bad body. This accounts for the myriads of physical bodies in every stage of development to the highest—from that of the amoebas to that of a Christ or <term>Māṇickavāchakar</term>—possessed of every varying mental and spiritual characteristic. The more good a man performs, the better and more developed a body he receives, with the accompanying development of mind and heart; the result of this privilege is that he is enabled to get a purer and purer

<summary>The author defines Karma as the ethical law of cause and effect, where virtuous acts (Punyam) and sinful acts (Papam) determine a soul's future physical and spiritual development. He equates this Saiva Siddhanta principle with utilitarianism, explaining that one's current station—from an amoeba to a saint—is the result of past actions.</summary>

# TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

...body, which, the more it becomes pure, will reflect the Light and Glory of God; so that when man reaches his physical and mental perfection, he reaches the spiritual perfection of complete merger in the supreme Light. And of all bodies, the human body is the one in which a man can work out his salvation, and therefore he is urged to "take time by the forelock" <note>To act promptly or seize an opportunity</note> and do good while this body lasts—if not to secure salvation in this birth, at least to secure a better body in which he can carry on the good work.

> “ எண்ணரிய பிறவிதனின் மானிடப் பிறவிதான்
> யாதினும் அரிதரிதுகாண்
> இப்பிறவி தப்பினா லெப்பிறவி வாய்க்குமோ
> ஏதுவருமோ வறிகிலேன். etc.
> Among births beyond number, that of a human
> Is rare, rare indeed;
> If this birth is lost, I know not what will happen.

Hence
> தானந்தவம்தரு மஞ்சந்ததமும் செய்வர்சிவ
> ஞானந்தனையணைய நல்லோர் பராபரமே.
> O You Supreme of the Supreme,
> The good who desire to attain <term>Šivajñāna</term> ever perform good deeds,
> <term>Tapas</term>, and offer gifts.

And so this doctrine of <term>Karma</term>, instead of leading to quietism and indifference, encourages a life of active beneficence, "desiring the welfare of all," and provides as good and sure a basis for perfect ethical conduct as any other system in the world.

But even when doing good works, he is not to have any regard for the result; he is to do it without seeking the rewards, as this tends to bind him to the world still by producing the physical body and will not achieve his final release from this body. After performing both evil and good, he attains to <term>Iruvinai Oppu</term> (இருவினை யொப்பு), becoming balanced in good and evil, pain and pleasure. This does not mean that he should perform actions so that all his good actions will weigh as much as his bad actions, or by doing as much <term>punyam</term> as <term>pāpam</term>; rather, it is attaining a state of viewing deeds, whether good or bad, without either liking or disliking—a condition described as <term>Venduthal Vendamaiyilaan</term> (வேண்டுதல் வேண்டாமையிலான்) <note>One who has neither desire nor aversion</note>. In such a condition, man is not

<vocab>Karma, Šivajñāna, Tapas, punyam, pāpam, salvation, இருவினை யொப்பு, வேண்டுதல் வேண்டாமையிலான்</vocab>

<summary>This page emphasizes the rarity of human birth as a unique opportunity for salvation and explains how the doctrine of Karma promotes active goodness. It describes the spiritual goal of Iruvinai Oppu, a state of equanimity where one performs deeds without attachment to pleasure, pain, or results.</summary>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the concept of Iruvinai Oppu, explaining how spiritual balance leads to selfless action and how the cycle of birth is an act of divine mercy intended for the soul's salvation.</meta>

<page-num>201</page-num>
# TREE OF KNOWLEDGE OF GOOD AND EVIL.

...impelled or attracted by anything which will give him pleasure, he will not be deterred simply because it will cause him pain. Such objects of desire in the world are wealth, health, and gratification, and we hate all those acts which will produce the opposite results. To such a person, wealth and poverty, food and poison, praise and blame, will be equally welcome, and one looks on all these as one looks on dust or chaff, without desire or aversion. It is when a man attains to this condition of <term>Vēṇḍutal Vēṇḍāmai</term> (வேண்டுதல் வேண்டாமை) or <term>Iruvinai Oppu</term> (இருவினை யொப்பு) that he is led in pursuit of the highest ideals, to do the greatest acts of heroism and the most magnanimous acts of self-sacrifice, and to suffer the greatest martyrdom. The story of the Churning of the Ocean is full of this meaning. The gods, who were pained at their poverty and desired wealth, came to reap the fire of the poison which arose as a result of their own self-seeking; and the Supreme Being, who appeared there not for the sake of any reward but for the sole purpose of saving the distressed gods, was not affected by the poison which he swallowed.

So, when God willed to create this earth and the heavens, it was not the result of a mere whim or play; it was not for his own improvement or benefit; it was not for his self-glorification or self-realization. Instead, he willed it out of his infinite love and mercy toward the innumerable souls who were rotting in their bondage, enshrouded in <term>Āṇava mala</term>, without self-knowledge and self-action, so that they might be awakened out of their <term>kēvala</term> (கேவல) condition and move into the cycle of evolution, <term>sakala</term> (சகல), births and deaths, by which alone they can achieve their salvation. Being helped toward this by being given bodies, faculties, etc., out of matter, they begin to act and accumulate <term>karma</term>, which must be fully "eaten" <note>Experienced or exhausted through living</note> before <term>Iruvinai Oppu</term> (இருவினை யொப்பு)—the indifference to pain and pleasure—can be gained. In the process of eating the "bitter fruits" and gaining <term>oppu</term> (balance), one gathers experience, wisdom, and the knowledge of Truth. And unless this Truth is gained, the soul's salvation is a mere myth and nothing more.

# THE FOUR PATHS.

---

### RELIGION THEORETICAL AND PRACTICAL.

A good deal of attention has been paid lately to the theoretical aspects of our Hindu religion, and most people are familiar with the various systems of Hindu philosophy—specifically the Dvaita, Visiṣhtādvaita, and Advaita perspectives. In such a study, one is likely to lose sight of the practical aspect of the religion, and it is to this aspect that I wish to draw your particular attention today.

### DIFFICULTIES IN UNDERSTANDING HINDU RELIGION.

To the average foreigner, Hinduism appears as a fantastic combination of the grossest superstitions and the most dreamy speculations. Even the sympathetic student of our religion, though prepared to admire and appreciate particular aspects of our philosophy, looks down with pity on our so-called errors. One Christian friend asked me whether we have any real and practical religion in Hinduism. Of course, to the observer, the contrast between temple worship and its attendant festivals and the more austere practices of the Sanyāsins, the ritual washings and pūjah of pious people, and the ‘Tatvamasi’ and ‘Ahambrahmasmi’ meditations of others, cannot help but be bewildering. Even some of us are inclined to view the significant labor and money spent on temples and in temple worship as a waste, or we are ready to dismiss these practices as being only for the "illiterate lower orders," as we like to call them. Can all these various practices have any real meaning and purpose, or can they not? Can all these be reduced to certain definite principles or not? These are the questions I propose to discuss in this paper.

<summary>The author introduces a discussion on the practical application of Hinduism, moving beyond theoretical philosophy to address common misunderstandings of rituals and temple worship.</summary>
<keywords>Hinduism, philosophy, Dvaita, Visiṣhtādvaita, Advaita, temple worship, Sanyāsins, pūjah, practical religion, religious practice</keywords>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the practical application of Hinduism, moving from the general contrast between ritual and philosophy to a specific examination of the four spiritual paths and various modes of worship.</meta>

# THE FOUR PATHS.

### DIFFERENT PATHS AND UPASANAS.

Of course, we have read and heard people talk about <term>Karma-mārga</term>, <term>Bhakti-mārga</term>, <term>Yōga</term>, and <term>Jñāna-mārgas</term> as though there is little or no <term>bhakti</term>, or as if <term>bhakti</term> is not required in other paths. They speak as if there are no actions or duties attached to the others, or that all those who do not follow the <term>Jñāna-mārga</term> are simply ignorant people. Does a person's smearing themselves with ashes and <term>nāmams</term>, or repeating God’s names, constitute <term>bhakti</term>? Does not the relieving of the poor, the infirm, and the sick constitute part of one’s religious duties? Is it the highest duty of the <term>Yōgi</term> and <term>Jñāni</term> to consider himself superior to others, to think that he will be polluted by the mere touch of others, and to think that he has achieved a great thing if he has simply injured no one?

And then we have heard of different <term>Upāsanas</term> and <term>Vidyās</term>—<term>Sandilya</term>, <term>Dahara</term>, <term>Sakala</term> and <term>Nishkala</term>, and <term>Saguṇa</term> and <term>Nirguṇa</term>; and there are people who would advocate for the <term>Saguṇa</term> against the <term>Nirguṇa</term> and the <term>Nirguṇa</term> against the <term>Saguṇa</term>.

To begin stating my views: Hindus hold it as an axiom that no study is of any benefit unless it can lead one to the worship of the supreme One.

> “ கற்றதனாலாய பயனென்கொல் வாலறிவன்
> நற்றாள் தொழா ரெனின். ”

And we cannot be rid of the "ills that flesh is heir to," cross the sea of births and deaths, and attain everlasting joy unless we reach the feet of the Supreme Lord.

> “ வேண்டுதல் வேண்டாமை யிலானடி சேர்ந்தார்க்கு
> யாண்டு மிடும்பையில. ”
>
> “ பிறவிப் பெருங்கடல் நீந்துவர் நீந்தார்
> இறைவனடி சேராதார். ” (*The Kural*).

To get rid of our ills and to attain His joy is our goal.

That this human birth is given to us to work out our salvation in this mundane world is admitted by all religions, Christianity included:

<summary>The author critiques the perceived separation of the four spiritual paths and the superficiality of certain rituals, arguing that all religious practice and study must ultimately lead to the worship of the Supreme Lord to achieve salvation.</summary>

# THE FOUR PATHS.

> ->புவனியிற்போய் பிறவாமை யிஞ்ஞானம்<-
> ->போக்குகின்றே மவமேயிந்தப் பூமி<-
> ->சிவனுய்யக் கொள்கின்ற வாறென்று நோக்கித்<-
> ->திருப்பெருந் துறையுறைவாய். (*Tiruvāchaka*).<-

How then can we reach this goal? This is the focus of Practical Religion. Our systematic treatises devote significant space to the treatment of this question. This is the subject of the chapter on <term>Sādana</term> in the <term>Vedānta Sūtras</term> and in the <term>Śivajñānabhodha</term>.

As a necessary introduction to this, the nature of the Deity and of the Soul must be discussed.

->NATURE OF GOD.<-

According to the greatest sage of our Tamil land, Saint <term>Tiruvalluvar</term>, He is <term>Adi</term> (ஆதி) and <term>Iraivan</term> (இறைவன்), our Supreme Lord and Master, the author of our existence and spiritual rebirth. He is Pure Intelligence and the Transcendent One, <term>Vālarivan</term> (வாலறிவன்) and <term>Tanakkuvamai-yillādān</term> (தனக்குவமையில்லாதான்). He is without likes or dislikes, <term>Vēndutal-vēndāmai-yillān</term> (வேண்டுதல் வேண்டாமையிலான்); He dwells in our hearts, <term>Malarmisai-ēkinān</term> (மலர்மிசைஏகினான்), and He is the ocean of love and mercy, <term>Aravāli-antanan</term> (அறவாழி அந்தணன்).

The <term>Upanishads</term> speak of Him as "the highest great Lord of lords, God of gods, King of kings, the supreme abode, as God, the Lord of the world, the adorable." "He is the one God hidden in all beings, all-pervading, the <term>antarātma</term> (inner self) of all beings, watching over all works, dwelling in all beings, the witness, the perceiver, the only one, the <term>Nirguṇa</term> (attribute-less) being. His High Power (<term>Śakti</term>) is revealed in many forms, as inherent, acting as both force and knowledge."

"He is <term>Śiva</term> (the Happy and Blissful). He brings good and removes all evil; He is the Lord of Bliss, dwelling within the <term>Ātma</term> (soul), the immortal, the support of all."

"No one has grasped Him from above, across, or in the middle. His form cannot be seen; no one perceives Him with the eye."

"That God, the maker of all things, the <term>Paramātmā</term> (Supreme Soul), always—"

<vocab>Tiruvāchaka, Sādana, Vedānta Sūtras, Śivajñānabhodha, Tiruvalluvar, Upanishats, antarātma, Nirguṇa, Śakti, Śiva, Ātma, Paramātmā, ஆதி, இறைவன், வாலறிவன், தனக்குவமையில்லாதான், வேண்டுதல் வேண்டாமையிலான், மலர்மிசைஏகினான், அறவாழி அந்தணன்</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the practical religious methods (Sādana) for spiritual attainment and defines the nature of God using the teachings of Saint Tiruvalluvar and the Upanishads. God is described as a transcendent, all-pervading, and merciful Supreme Intelligence who dwells within all beings as the immortal support.</summary>

dwelling in the heart of man, is perceived by the heart, the soul, and the mind. Those who know It become immortal.”

“Those who, through heart and mind, know Him thus abiding in the heart, become immortal,” “*Satyam Jñānam Anantam Brahma Ananda Rūpam Amritam YadVibhuti Shantam Šivam Advaitam.*” “He is the *sat, chit and ānand.*”

In the *Gītā* also, He is spoken of as the Lord of Lords, *Išhvara* and *Mahešvara*, the spectator and permitter, supporter and enjoyer, the *Paramātman*, the supporter of elements, as devourer and cause. It is the light of lights and is said to be beyond darkness (*Tamas*). Wisdom to be known, wisdom to be gained, centered in every heart.

In the *Advaita Siddhānta Šāstras*, He is called அந்தமாதி one with His *Šakti*, the ‘*Šiva Sat.*’

உலகெலாமாகி வேறாய் உடலுமாய் ஒளியாயோங்கி
அலகிலா வுயிர்கள் கன்மத்து ஆணையினமர்ந்து செல்லத்
தலைவனாய் இவற்றின் தன்மைதனக் கெய்தலின்றித்தானே
நிலவுசீர் நின்மலனாகி நின்றனன் நீங்காதெங்கும்.

One with the world, and different, and both; the light transcendent;
The Lord who guides countless souls, in obedience to His Will (*Ājñā*) and each one’s <term>karma</term>;
The <term>Nirmala</term> Being, untouched by the defects of His creatures;
Supreme He stands, without a second, pervading all.

சிவனொரு வருவுமல்லன் சித்தினோடு அசித்துமல்லன்
பவமுதற்றொழில்கள் பண்ணிடுவானு மல்லன்
தவமுதல் யோகபோகம் தரிப்பவனல்லன்றானே
இவைபெற வியையந்தமொன்று மியைந்திடா இயல்பினானே.

*Šiva* is neither a <term>Rūpi</term> nor an <term>Arūpi</term>. He is neither *chit* nor *Achit*. He does not create, sustain, nor perform other functions. He was never a <term>Yogi</term> nor a <term>Bhōgi</term>. Although present in and pervading all these inseparably, yet He is of a nature different from all these.

சத்திதன் வடிவேதென்னில் தடையிலா ஞானமாகும்
உய்த்திடு மிச்சை யெட்டி திவை ஞானத்துளவா மென்னில்

<summary>This page defines the nature of the Divine through various Hindu scriptures, describing God as the immortal light dwelling in the heart and as a supreme, transcendent being who guides souls according to their karma. It emphasizes that while God pervades all things, He remains distinct from the material world and untouched by the limitations of His creatures.</summary>

எத்திற ஞான முள்ளதத்திற மிச்சையுஞ் சத்தி
வைத்திடு மறைபின்ஞானன் மருவிக்கம் கிரியையெல்லாம்.

The form of this Šakti is Pure Intelligence. If it is asked whether Supreme Will and Power are also found in this Supreme Intelligence, the answer is yes. Wherever there is intelligence, there is will and power. As such, power and will are also manifested by the Supreme Chit Šakti.

அருளது சத்திகாகும் அரன்தனக் கருளையின்றி
தெருள்சிவ மில்லையந்தச் சிவமின்றிச் சத்தியில்லை
மருளினை யருளால்வாட்டி மன்னுமியிற் களிட்டன்கண்கட்
இருளினை யொளியாலோட்டும் இரவியைப்போல வீசன்.

Hara has Grace for His Šakti. Except for this Supreme Love and Grace, there is no Śiva. Without Śiva, there is no Šakti. Īša removes the hate of the souls with his love and grants them bliss, just as the Sun dispels the darkness that shrouds the eyes with its light.

This supreme statement was reached in the famous lines of the great Tirumūlar:

அன்பும் சிவமும் இரண்டென்பர் அறிவிலார்
அன்பே சிவமாவதாரு மறிகிலார்
அன்பே சிவமாவதாரு மறிந்தபின்
அன்பே சிவமா யமர்ந்திருந்தாரே.

"God is Love." And that great agnostic teacher of science who died a sincere believer in God had stated truly, "What has all the science or all the philosophy of the world done for the thought of mankind, to be compared with the one doctrine 'God is Love'?"

God is, as such, all Knowledge and all Love.

->NATURE OF THE SOUL.<-

To talk of the means to attain this great goal will be futile if we do not understand the nature of man. From the statements in the first chapter of the Kuraḷ, it may be deduced that man is ignorant and subject to births and deaths, has likes and dislikes, and does sin and suffer; therefore, he cannot be compared to God in any way. The following texts bring out the distinction quite plainly.

It concludes by introducing the nature of the soul, noting that unlike the divine, man is characterized by ignorance, suffering, and mortality.</summary>

> "The one who knows (God) and the one who does not know (the soul) are two, both unborn; one is the Lord, the other is not the Lord (anīśa)."
>
> "Patim Vivasy-ātmeswaram (Lord of the soul) Sāsvatam Śivam achyutam."
>
> "He who dwells in the soul and within the soul, whom the soul does not know, whose body the soul is, who rules the soul from within—He is your soul, the ruler within, the immortal."

> Since I surpass the kshara (perishable), and even excel the akshara (imperishable), I am known as the Purushottama."

### A DIFFICULTY IN REACHING THE GOAL.

And here we encounter statements that God is unknowable and imperceptible to our senses. He is beyond all thought and speech.

> ->உரைபதொன்றை யுரைசெய்யு மூமர்காள்<-
> ->கரையற்ற தொன்றைக் கரைகாணலாகுமோ. (*Tirumantra*).<-

And yet the Upanishads say that only when men can roll up the sky like a hide could there be an end to pain without knowing Śiva.

And St. Arul Nanḍi Śivāchāryar states the difficulty thus:

> "If God is unknowable, then there can be no benefit from Him. He can never pervade us, nor can we unite with Him in Moksha. He cannot perform the pañcha-krityas for our benefit. His existence would be like that of the flowers of the sky or a rope formed from the hair of a tortoise.

And yet it must stand to reason that we cannot possibly know Him if His nature is as we have described above. The moment we assert that we can know Him, we assert that He becomes an object of our cognition; and as all psychologists, Hindu and European, agree, all objects of cognition are what is called Achit or Asat, or matter. Here is St. Arul Nanḍi's

<vocab>anīśa, Patim Vivasy-ātmeswaram, Sāsvatam, Śivam, achyutam, Paramātma, kshara, akshara, Purushottama, Tirumantra, upanishats, Śiva, St. Arul Nanḍi Śivāchāryar, Moksha, pañcha-krityas, Achit, Asat</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the dual nature of God and the soul, highlighting the theological challenge that if God is entirely unknowable, salvation and divine interaction would be impossible.</summary>
<keywords>Purushottama, Paramātma, Tirumantra, Arul Nanḍi Śivāchāryar, epistemology, Moksha, Achit, Asat, Saiva Siddhanta</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion regarding the nature of God as an object of knowledge, contrasting the definitions of Achit and Sat, and exploring the soul's relationship to the divine as the inner ruler.</meta>

<page-num>208</page-num>
<header>THE FOUR PATHS.</header>

statement: “If you ask whether God is an object of knowledge or not, then know: if He is an object of knowledge, He will become <term>Achit</term> and <term>Asat</term>. All objects of cognition are <term>achit</term>; all objects of cognition come into being and are destroyed (being bound by time). They divide themselves into the worlds, bodies, and organs (being bound by space) and enjoyments. They are identified at one time by the intelligence as itself (<term>bandha</term>) and at another time (in <term>moksha</term>) are seen as separate; and they are all products of <term>Māyā</term>. Hence, all such things are <term>achit</term>, or non-intelligent, or <term>Asat</term> (other than <term>sat</term>).”

As God is spoken of as the inner Ruler and Soul of the Soul, whose body the Soul is, the knowing Soul is itself in the position of an object to the True subject, God; and the thinking mind cannot itself think of thought, much less can the object perceive or think of the subject.

And if He cannot be known, He must be a non-entity, argues St. Arul Nandi. And this is exactly the position that Paul Carus takes in his pamphlet on the “Idea of God.” His argument is exactly that of Saint Arul Nandi: that if God is knowable, He can only be known as an object, like matter, which would be absurd. But Paul Carus would, however, retain God as an idea or ideal—an abstract thing like redness or whiteness, a beautiful fantasy that will be useful. But against this view, it is positively asserted by Saint Arul Nandi that He is not a non-entity and that He is <term>Sat</term> and <term>Chit</term>. As He is <term>chit</term>, He is not knowable in the traditional sense, and yet He is a positive fact.

How, then, is this psychological difficulty to be overcome?

### THE FIRST POSSIBILITY OF OVERCOMING THE DIFFICULTY.

In the first place, it will be futile to think of knowing Him as something different from ourselves, like an object. Says St. Arul Nandi: “As God is not different from the soul, as He is in the soul, as He is the thinker of all the soul’s thoughts, and as in Him there is no distinction of ‘I’ and ‘mine,’ God cannot be perceived by the soul’s intelligence as something different.” “God is not different from you

<summary>The text argues that God cannot be an object of cognition because objects are temporary and insentient (Achit), whereas God is the ultimate subject and inner ruler of the soul. It refutes the idea of God as a mere abstract ideal, asserting His reality as Sat and Chit while explaining that He cannot be known as an entity separate from the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Achit, Asat, Sat, Chit, Māyā, St. Arul Nandi, Paul Carus, cognition, inner ruler, Moksha, Bandha, soul and God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion from page 208 regarding the nature of God as Sat and Chit, specifically focusing on how the soul can be said to be one with Śivam through inseparable association and the removal of the ego.</meta>

<page-num>209</page-num>

...either as He is inseparably associated with you, and transcends all discriminating intelligence. Since He is always the *inside* of the soul, the soul can be said to be Śivam.”

Therefore, the first possibility of our becoming Him lies in the fact that we are inseparably associated with Him and must think of ourselves as one with Him. We must not create distinctions between ourselves and Him, nor interpose our own will and thought, or the feelings of ‘I and mine.’ Only then will our will and thought come into rapport with Him.

> “ ஏகனாகி யிறைபணி நிற்க
> மலமாயை தன்னொடும் வல்வினையின்றே ”.
> “ திரையற்ற நீர்போல் சிந்தை தெளிவார்க்கு
> புரையற்றிருந்தான் புரிசடையோனே ”.

மனதே கல்லாலெனக்கன்றோ—தெய்வ
மௌனகுருவாகி வந்து கைகாட்டி
எனதாம் பணியறமாற்றி—அவன்
இன்னருள் வெள்ளத்திருத்தி வைத்தான்டி.

O mind, was it not for my sake that God came under the banyan tree as a silent teacher? With a silent gesture of His hand, He cured me of the acts I called *my* acts and placed me in the blissful ocean of His grace.

அருளா லெவையும் பாரென்றான்—அத்தை
அறியாதே சுட்டி யென்னறிவாலே பார்த்தேன்
இருளான பொருள்கண்டதல்லால்—கண்ட
என்னையுங் கண்டிலனென்னடி தோழி.

“Behold all things through grace,” He said. Not understanding this, I looked with my own intelligence and made distinctions. I saw only darkness. I did not even see myself, the seer. What is this, sister?

என்னையும் தன்னையும் வேறா—உள்ளத்
தெண்ணாத வண்ணமிரண்டற நிற்கச்
சொன்னதுமோ வொருசொல்லே—அந்தச்
சொல்லால் விளைந்த சுகத்தை யென்சொல்வேன்.

<summary>This page explains that the soul becomes one with Śivam by recognizing its inseparable association with Him and abandoning the ego-driven concepts of "I and mine." It uses Tamil devotional poetry to illustrate how God, acting as a silent teacher (Dakshinamurti), leads the soul into the ocean of divine grace.</summary>

> “Do not think in your heart of ‘me’ and ‘you’ as two. Stand without making distinctions.” When He uttered this one word, how can I tell, dear, the bliss that grew immediately from that word?

->(From Saint Tāyumānavar’s ஆனந்தக்களிப்பு ‘Revel in Bliss’—translated by P. Arunāchalam Esq. M.A., of Colombo.)<-

### THE SECOND POSSIBILITY.

The second possibility lies in the fact that God is not knowledge alone. If He were so, we could not know Him for certain. But as we have stated above, He is also *all Love*. It is upon this supreme fact that our salvation is based. This Love is in us and surrounds us on all sides—above, below, and all around us. His Love for us surpasses that of a mother, says Saint Māṇikkavāçagar.

> “சட்டோ நினைக்க மனத்தமுதாம் சங்கரன்”
>
> “உருகிப்பெருகி உளங்குளிர முகந்துகொண்டு
> பருகற்கினிய பரங்கருணைத் தடங்கடல்”
>
> “நினைந்தொறும் காண்டொறும் பேசுந்தொறும் எப்போதும் அனைத்
> தெலும் புண்ணெக ஆனந்தத்தேன் சொரியும் குனிப்புடையான்”
>
> “தப்பாமே தாளடைந்தார் நெஞ்சுருக்கும் தன்மையினான்”
>
> “அகண்டாகார சிவபோகமெனும் பேரின்பவெள்ளம்,
> பொங்கித் ததும்பிப் பூரணமாய் ஏகவுருவாய்க்கிடக்குதையோ
> இன்புற்றிட நாமெடுத்ததேகம் விழுமுன் புசிப்பதர்க்குச்
> சேரவாரும் சகத்திரே.”

No selfish want prompts His love. His Love was always with us from our first beginning to the very end.

> “அருளெங்குமான வளவையறியார்
> அருளே நுகரமுதானதும் தேரார்
> அருளெங்கருமத்ததி ருக்கமுன்னார்
> அருளெங்கும் கண்ணானதா ரறிவாரே.”

Who knows the power of this <term>Aruļ</term> <note>Divine Grace</note> by which omnipresence is secured? Who understands that this Love transmuted Herself into delicious ambrosia?

Who thinks that this Love—permeates subtly the five great operations (<term>Pañchakṛitya</term>)?

> “ அருளிற்பிறந்திட்டு அருளில் வளர்ந்திட்டு

> “Born in Love, Bred up in Love,
> Changing, and resting in Love,
> Fed in the Supreme ambrosia of Love,
> The Nandi entered me as Love.”

A mother’s love will not fail, even if the child misbehaves and does not deserve it. If we will therefore return His love, then our salvation is secured.

> “ அயரா அன்பின் அரன்கழல் செலுமே,”

(With undying love, one reaches the Feet of <term>Hara</term>)

St. <term>Tirumūlar</term> sums up these preceding facts in a beautiful verse.

> ஒன்று மிரண்டு மிலதாய் யொன்றாக
> நின்று சமய நிராகார நீங்கியே
> நின்று பராபரை நேயத்தைப் பாதத்தால்
> சென்றுசிவமாதல் சித்தாந்த சித்தியே.

> Becoming one, without being either one or two,
> Becoming freed of <term>Samaya Nirākāra</term>,
> Ascending by the Grace of our Loving mother,
> And becoming <term>Śivam</term> is <term>Śiddhānta Siddhi</term>.

### LOVE, THE GREATEST THING IN THE WORLD.

Now let us realize for ourselves how it is that to know Him and become one with Him, we must love Him. Let us consider our human relations. Is it by birth and caste, wealth and possessions, learning and knowledge, that one is brought nearer to another? Are not all these barriers dividing one from

<summary>This page explores the concept of God as absolute Love through the teachings of Saint Tirumūlar, explaining that union with the Divine (Śiddhānta Siddhi) is achieved through undying love and Grace rather than status or intellect.</summary>
<keywords>Pañchakṛitya, Nandi, Hara, Tirumūlar, Samaya Nirākāra, Śivam, Śiddhānta Siddhi, Divine Love, Grace, Aruļ, salvation</keywords>

--- Page 243 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how social and intellectual barriers separate individuals, contrasting them with the unifying power of Love which leads to the soul's union with the Divine.</meta>

<page-num>212</page-num>
<header>THE FOUR PATHS.</header>

another? By all these means, one regards themselves as raised above all other less favored individuals. It is learning that puffs up a person. The "I-ness" and "mine-ness" become more and more developed in these people. So these means can never lead one person nearer to another. Then what other means do we have? It is love—love in all its gradations, from pity upward. This is "the greatest thing in the world," as <term>Prof. Drummond</term> truly said. It is the ideal of both theistic and atheistic systems of the world.

Love is the basis of all human society, the rock on which it is built. This is evident from the mere chapter headings in <term>இல்லறம்</term> (Domestic Virtue) in the sacred <term>Kural</term>. It is the one thing which binds person to person, parent to child, friend to friend, and wife to husband. When love prevails, the distinctions created by birth, possessions, and learning all cease. It is this which impels the servant to engage in his master’s service, the mother to sacrifice herself for the child, the friend to give his life for his friend, and the lover to forget himself in the beloved. All the noblest acts of heroism, philanthropy, and martyrdom arise from this one source. It is this love which, as we have seen, gives rise to the other great fact in Being, namely, Sacrifice. Even naturalists have discovered the connection between these two facts, Love and Sacrifice, even in the case of lower animals. And should this law not hold good in a higher realm than the animal and social? It is toward this end that we have been striving all along.

### KNOWLEDGE NECESSARY.

And in this place, the importance of knowledge cannot be ignored. One has only to enter a railway platform and watch one of the ever-recurring scenes.

The compartments are more or less crowded. New passengers try to rush into them. The people already inside, naturally motivated by their own comfort, resist the intrusion. Actual fights ensue. Some of them try to get in somehow. They stand for a while.

He then begins to illustrate the role of knowledge in human interaction using the metaphor of a crowded railway carriage.</summary>

--- Page 244 ---
<meta>This page continues the analogy of a railway journey to illustrate how knowledge and proximity overcome prejudice, leading to the theological conclusion that serving humanity is the true worship of God.</meta>

<page-num>213</page-num>
<header>THE FOUR PATHS.</header>

Those who have comfortable seats are moved by their own hard hearts, and they begin to pity and relent. A small space is found for the person who stands. Naturally, they soon after fall into conversation. They soon discover their mutual friends and relations, and by the time they leave the train, they become the most affectionate of people, and their parting becomes a sorrow. How was this mutual hate turned into love? It was through knowledge. We are all ignorant of how intimately we are related to each other. We are all God's servants—His children, in fact—and perhaps we can share in His fellowship. The whole world is ensouled by Him. "We are members of His body," says Śrīkaṇtha.

## THE TRUE WORSHIP.

> "Therefore, the whole universe is ensouled by Shiva. If any living being whatsoever is subjected to constraint, it will be quite repugnant to the eight-bodied Lord; of this, there is no doubt. Doing good to all, showing kindness to all, and providing shelter to all—this they hold to be the worship of Shiva."

Here, in this last sentence by Śrīkaṇtha, we reach the real essence of all religion. What is Shiva? He is Love. What is the worship of Him? It is loving Him. How can we love Him whom we do not know? Indeed, we can and do know Him. We do not perceive each other's souls or minds, and yet we love each other. It is the body we know, and it is upon each other's bodies that we manifest all our love. We willingly serve only the bodies of our elders, masters, teachers, and parents. It is upon those bodies we love that we lavish all our wealth and labor. So, we can worship and love Him by loving His Body, which is the entire universe of <term>Chētana</term> (the conscious) and <term>Achētana</term> (the unconscious).

->பூதங்கள்தோறு நின்றாயெனின்ல்லால்<-

> "You dwell in all the elements," it is said; and yet
> "You neither go nor come"; the sages thus have sung

> To contemplate You is difficult for human thought. To us,
> Come into our presence! Cut off our ills! In mercy, make us Yours!
> Our mighty Lord, arise from Your couch in grace!

As I pointed out above, knowledge is an essential requirement for our love. As knowledge grows, love will grow. The more we understand our nearness to each other and to God, the more our love will grow. The knowledge and love existing between master and servant is weaker than between father and son; between friends it is higher, and in the case of lovers, it is highest.

### THE THIRD POSSIBILITY.

I must here point out a psychological law which I may state as the basis of this experience and which I may describe as the third possibility.

It is the unique nature of the soul or mind by which it identifies itself with the thing to which it is united. This aspect alone is fully discussed in the *Siddhānta Shastras*. *Saint Meykaṇḍān* calls it அது அது ஆதல் and in the commentaries as சார்ந்ததன் வண்ணமாதல். *Saint Tāyumānār* paraphrases it as யாதொன்றுபற்றின் அதன் இயல்பாய் நிற்றல். The human soul is a mirror—a crystal. It becomes dark when darkness covers it. A man can be judged by his associates. He can be good or bad as his associates are. In union with the world, the soul has become identified with the world and lost its individuality. In God, it has become *Śivam*, losing its individuality. In the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul’s unique nature, moving from its tendency to identify with its surroundings to the specific process of its union with the Divine through the metaphor of a mirror and reflection.</meta>

In the full glare of the midday sun, I challenge anyone to look into the mirror. What one will see, if he has courage enough to look at it, will be the full radiance of the glorious sun, which will blind him instantly.

Professor Henry Drummond says: "All men are mirrors; that is the first law on which this formula is based. One of the most apt descriptions of a human being is a mirror."

Professor Drummond states this Law as the Law of Reflection and Assimilation, the Law of Influence, or the Law of Identity as we may call it, *adu adu adal* அது அது ஆதல். He gives the example of iron which becomes magnetized and turns into a magnet, "as the magnet attracts the iron" இரும்பைக்காந்தம் இழுக்கின்றவாறெனை; a mirror, getting rid of its dust, reflects the glorious light and becomes merged with it and lost in it.

### HOW THE SOUL MERGES AND LOSES ITSELF.

Just a word about the meaning of the words "merging" and "losing" before I continue my train of thought. I quote from a science textbook:

> "When a river enters the sea, it soon loses its individuality and becomes *merged* in the body of the ocean; it loses its current and, therefore, has no power to keep in suspension the sediment which it had brought down from the higher lands."

Please reread those lines in this way and the application will become clear: "When the soul *loses* its individuality (its sense of 'I' and 'mine'), <term>Ahaṅkāram</term> or <term>Āṇavam</term>, it becomes *merged* in God when it loses its <term>karma</term>, and when, therefore, it has no power to keep in suspension its <term>mala</term>, with which it was associated from the beginning. This losing of the self is the real sacrifice, brought about by love. It is this sacrifice <term>pali</term> பலி, we are asked to make as we enter the temple precincts; the moment we make it, our animal nature <term>pasutvam</term> பசுத்துவம் will leave us, and we will become pure <term>suddha</term> சுத்த, the blissful <term>Śivam</term>."

We compared the soul to a mirror; consider the following passages from the Upanishads.

> “As a metal disc (mirror), tarnished by dust, shines brightly again after it has been cleaned, so is the incarnate person satisfied and freed from grief after he has seen his own true nature.” “And when by his own true nature he sees, as if by a lamp, the true nature of the Brahman, then—having known the unborn, Eternal God who transcends all <term>tattvas</term>—he is freed from all <term>pāša</term>.”

> “From meditating on Him, from joining Him, and from becoming one with Him, there is ultimately a complete cessation of all <term>Māyā</term>.” In Drummond’s language, these verses read: “See God, reflect God, and become God.”

Students of Darwin will have noted how powerful the law of association and assimilation (or identity) is in animal and human evolution. People who are always associated with pigs develop pig-like faces, and those associated with horses develop horse-like faces. In the case of a husband and wife who have been perfectly loving, it has been found to cause a complete assimilation of their facial features சாரூபம் <note>Sārūpam: attainment of the divine form</note>. Such is the power of the human mind; it can lower itself to the very depths of a brute, or it can raise itself to the very height of Godhood. This law is referred to in our textbooks as the law of <term>garuḍadhyānam</term>.

This brings us to the very end of our subject.

We cannot truly know God through all our religious rites and performances, the repetition of prayers and formulas, or through <term>saguṇa</term> or <term>nirguṇa</term> worship—with or without idols—or even through the highest <term>yōga</term>, except when His grace and Love fill us completely and we lose ourselves in this Love.

Look at how St. <term>Meykaṇḍān</term> ridicules this idea of the Yogi who thinks that he knows God:

> “If it can be meditated upon, then as an object of our senses, it becomes <term>Asat</term>. If you regard it as inconceivable by our organs (internal and external), even then it is of no use. If you contemplate it as being beyond contemplation, even then it gives you

<summary>The author explains that the soul attains divinity through the "law of association," where contemplating God leads to becoming one with Him, a process more powerful than any ritual or yoga. This transformation is compared to biological assimilation and the philosophical concept of Sārūpam.</summary>

"...is of no benefit as it is a mere fiction. If you contemplate Him as yourself, this is also fiction. Giving up these fictitious ideas of God, the only way to know Him is by understanding through His <term>Arul</term> or Grace.”

### THE FOUR PATHS.

Therefore, all our understanding of Him until the final goal is reached will be merely fictitious—or, to use a better word, symbolic. The conception, whether that of the <term>Bhakta</term> or <term>Yogi</term>, Hindu or Christian, will only be symbolic. We introduce a real element into it when we introduce love into our conception of God. And this conception naturally divides itself into four forms: that of master and servant, parent and child, friend and friend, and lover and beloved. All other conceptions can be reduced to these four. There is love and knowledge in all these different forms of <term>Bāvana</term> or <term>Sādana</term>. As our Lord and master, we offer Him and His <term>bhaktās</term> loving service, obedience, and reverence. In the master, we lose our own identity. To the father and mother, obedience, service, reverence, and love are exhibited to a greater degree. To the friend, we can say ‘I am he,’ ‘he is myself,’ ‘all mine are his,’ and ‘all his are mine.’ In real life, this ideal of friendship is rarely manifested. Our people could hardly appreciate the act of the saint who gave his wife to the <term>bhakta</term> who demanded her from him. How would you like the portrayal by Hall Caine of the lowborn and illiterate Manxman who loved, and continued to love more and more, the highborn and cultured aristocrat who betrayed him, cheated him, and robbed him of his betrothed, and forfeited all claims to regard and respect? It was because his own friendship was sincere and true.

It is this ideal of friendship and the <term>Bāvana</term> required under it which reveals the meaning of the formulas <term>Tatvamasi</term> and <term>Aham Brahmāsmi</term>, given as the <term>mantras</term> to be practiced by the <term>Yōgi</term>. In <term>Yōga</term>, the identity of <term>Bāvana</term> is fully reached. When we understand this fully, we can understand all the episodes in the life of <term>St. Sundara</term>, who was of the

...the very image of <term>Sōma Sundara</term> and whom God chose as His own "friend."

ஏழிசையாய் இசைப்பயனாய் இன்னமுதாப் யென்னுடைய
தோழனுமாய் யான்செய்யும் துரிசுகளுக்குடனாகி
மாழையொண்கண் பரவையைத் தந்தாண்டானை மதியிலா
எழையேன் பிரிந்திருக்கேன் என்னாரூரிறைவனையே.

He is the seven notes and the fruit of those notes, the sweet nectar, and my very friend who stays with me even in my faults. He is my Lord who gave me my beautiful-eyed <term>Paravai</term>, my Lord of <term>Ārūr</term>; how can I, a poor fool, remain separated from Him?

In life, have you felt even a hundredth part of this love for your friend—the gnawing pain in your heart when you were separated and the boundless joy when you met?

These, then, are the four paths or <term>mārgas</term>: <term>Charyā</term>, <term>Kriyā</term>, <term>Yoga</term>, and <term>Jñāna</term>, otherwise called <term>Dāsa</term>, <term>Satputra</term>, <term>Saha</term>, and <term>Sanmārga</term>. The various duties assigned under each are only those that our love for a master, father, friend, or lover will lead us to demonstrate as tokens of our love. These duties are meaningless except as tokens of our love and as a means of disciplining us to love God and His creatures more and more.

என்பே விறகாயிறைச்சி யறுத்திட்டு
பொன்போற் கனலில் பொறியவறுப்பினும்
அன்போடுருகி யகங்குழைவார்க் கன்றி
என்போல் மணியினை யெய்தவொண்ணாதே.

Even if one uses their bones as firewood,
And tears their flesh to strips to be burnt like gold in the fire,
God is not accessible,
Except to those who inwardly melt with Love.

These duties are for the <term>Dāsa Mārgi</term>:

எளிவன தீபமிடல்மலர் கொய்தல்

பளிமணி பற்றல் பன்மஞ்சன மாதி

Lighting the lamps, gathering flowers,
Joyfully cleaning and sweeping the temple floors,
Singing praises, wearing garlands of sacred beads,
And performing various ritual baths and services—
These are the works for the <term>Dāsa</term> (servant).

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the four spiritual paths (Mārgas) in Shaiva Siddhanta, specifically detailing the duties associated with Dāsamārga (the path of the servant), Satputramārga (the path of the son), and Sahamārga (the path of the friend).</meta>

<header>THE FOUR PATHS.</header>

The simple duties—lighting lamps, picking flowers, sweeping and washing the temple, praising God, assisting in His service of <term>abhisheka</term> <note>ritual bathing of a deity</note>, and cooking food—constitute <term>Dāsamārga</term>.

Our Christian friends who observe our temple-building and our spending on ornaments and flowers will scarcely realize why vast sums of money are spent on churches and church decorations. The money spent on flowers for Easter and Christmas festivities in churches amounts to a million or more each year. Christ rebuked the man who held the common purse and who objected to Mary wasting that precious scented oil on Christ's feet. It was not the value of the oil that was worth anything, but the love that prompted that sacrifice was worth everything.

But it is not through costly gifts alone that we can manifest our love.

The duties of a <term>Satputra-mārgi</term> are as follows:

->பூசித்தல் வாசித்தல் போற்றல் சபித்திடல்<-
->ஆசற்ற நற்றவம் வாய்மை யழுக்கின்மை<-
->நேசித்திட்டன்னமுஞ் சந்திசெய்தல் மற்று<-
->ஆசற்ற சற்புத்திர மார்க்கமாகுமே.<-

-><term>Puja</term>, reading, and reciting prayers,<-
-><term>Japa</term>, true <term>tapas</term>, and truth,<-
->Purity, love, and offering food<-
->Constitute <term>Satputramārga</term>.<-

->ஆதாரசோதனை யாறுடி சுத்திகள்<-
->பேதாதி யீரெண் கலந்து மின்னொளி<-
->போதாலயத்துட் புலன்கரணப் புத்தி<-
->சாதாராணங்கெடலாஞ் சகமார்க்கமே.<-

Purifying oneself through <term>Ādhāra</term> and <term>Nāḍi Sōdhana</term>, becoming possessed of eighteen <term>Shaktis</term>, entering the Temple of <term>Jñānākāśa</term> (<term>Chidambaram</term>), and getting rid of one's senses and mind is <term>Sahamārga</term>.

The eight forms of <term>Yoga</term> referred to are <term>Yama</term>, <term>Niyama</term>, <term>Āsana</term>, <term>Prāṇayāma</term>, <term>Pratyāhāra</term>, <term>Dhāraṇā</term>, <term>Dhyāna</term>, and <term>Samādhi</term>; we note here only the definitions of <term>Yama</term> and <term>Niyama</term>.

<term>Yama</term> is <term>Ahimsa</term>, <term>Satyam</term>, refraining from theft, celibacy or chastity, mercifulness, being devoid of deceitfulness, and contentedness,

courage, moderation in food, and purity. <term>Niyama</term> consists of performing <term>tapas</term>, <term>japam</term>, and <term>vratam</term>, believing in God, worshipping Him, reading and meditating on the <term>Śastras</term>, being cheerful, fearing evil, and being intelligent.

The duties of <term>Sanmārga</term> are stated as follows:

->பசுபாச நீக்கிப்பதியுடன் கூட்டிக்<-
->கசியாதநெஞ்சங் கசியக் கசிவித்துத்<-
->தெரியாத உண்மைச் சொரூபோதயத்துற்று<-
->யசைவான தில்லாமை யானசன்மார்க்கமே.<-

Casting off one’s <term>paśutvam</term> <note>animal nature</note> and <term>Pāśa</term> <note>bondage</note>, becoming one with <term>Pati</term> <note>the Lord</note>, softening the heart which never melts through love, entering the True Presence which one can never otherwise know, and standing steadfast there—this is <term>Sanmārga</term>.

These four <term>sādanas</term> <note>spiritual practices</note> are arranged so that one may lead into the next. The forms and symbols in each are chosen so that as one reaches a higher path, new meaning, beauty, and life burst forth as one's own intelligence and love ripen to receive that fresh life.

The temple built of brick and mortar becomes the very soul and heart of the <term>Yōgi</term>, and the <term>Śivalinga</term> becomes the loving Presence and Light of the Supreme. The food <term>bali</term> <note>offering</note> presented by the devotee gradually comes to represent the sacrifice of <term>āṇava</term> <note>egoism</note> or <term>tarpōtam</term> <note>self-conceit</note>.

The beauty of such books as the <term>Tiruvāçaga</term>, <term>Devāra</term>, and <term>Tiruvāimoḷi</term> lies in the fact that they provide the necessary mental and spiritual nourishment for both the illiterate and the most cultured minds.

It is easily perceived that these four paths are natural divisions. The world’s great religions may be categorized under one or another of these headings. <term>Islam</term> and ancient Judaism fall under the first division. It was the merit of Jesus Christ that he brought the Fatherhood of God into greater prominence. The following quotations from the Bible will show that Jesus Christ recognized the other paths as well.

When I spoke of these higher aspects of Christ's teaching to a missionary, he remarked to me that it had only recently occurred to him that fellowship with God was a higher spiritual state than the fatherhood of God. Among us, the <term>Mādhwa</term> system may be considered pure <term>Dāsamārga</term> <note>the path of the servant</note>. The <term>Rāmānuja</term> system, in its popular aspects, is <term>Dāsamārga</term> and <term>Satputramārga</term> <note>the path of the good son</note> and a little more. <term>Śaṅkara</term>'s system would be <term>Sahamārga</term> <note>the path of the friend</note>. However, a mistake is made in failing to understand that these truths are only symbolic; otherwise, they are prone to becoming dogmatic. I have seen Christian friends argue that God is our literal father, just as <term>Vedāntins</term> and <term>Yōgis</term> may declare that there is no other God but the self.

A true and universal religion will combine all these various paths, which are required and made necessary by the varying degrees of humanity’s intellectual and spiritual development.

I only need to quote one verse to you from the <term>Gitā</term>, where all these four paths are set out.

More than all this, I wish to emphasize the fact that love is the essence of all real religion, and real worship of God is the worship of God's creatures and loving them one and all without distinction of caste or creed, as observed by <term>Srī Kaṇṭha</term>. Unless this is fully recognized and practiced, no real spiritual progress is possible.

## ACCORDING TO THE <term>ŚAIVA SIDDHĀṆTA</term>.*

It is interesting to note that it was about 12 years ago that we published our first work in English on the <term>Śaiva Siddhāṇta</term> Philosophy from <term>Tiruppattūr</term>, and we have continued ever since to work hard at it. Our translations of ‘<term>Śiva-jñānabodham</term>,’ ‘<term>Śivajñānasiddhiyār</term>,’ and ‘<term>Tiruvaruṭpayaṉ</term>,’ along with our contributions to the *<term>Siddhāṇta Dīpikā</term>* during the last ten years, and Dr. <term>G. U. Pope</term>’s ‘<term>Tiruvāçagam</term>,’ constitute the only bibliography on the subject in English. We are pleased to note that, within the last few years, considerable interest in the subject has been awakened, and several European missionaries have made a special study of the subject and have discussed it before missionary societies and in the public press. We quote the latest opinion from the *Christian College Magazine*, Vol. XX, 9, written by the Rev. <term>W. Goudie</term>.

> “There is no school of thought and no system of faith or worship that comes to us with anything like the claims of the <term>Śaiva Siddhāṇta</term>.
>
> “This system possesses the merit of great antiquity. In the religious world, the <term>Śaiva</term> system is the heir to all that is most ancient in South India; it is a religion of the <term>Tamiḷ</term> people, compared to which every other form is of a relatively foreign origin.
>
> “In the size of its following, as well as in regard to the antiquity of some of its elements, the <term>Śaiva Siddhāṇta</term> is, beyond any other form, the religion of the <term>Tamiḷ</term> people and ought to be studied by all <term>Tamiḷ</term> missionaries.

> "We have, however, left the greatest distinction of this system until the last. As a system of religious thought, as an expression of faith and life, the <term>Śaiva Siddhānta</term> is by far the best that South India possesses. Indeed, it would not be rash to include the whole of India and to maintain that, judged by its intrinsic merits, the <term>Śaiva Siddhānta</term> represents the high-water mark of Indian thought and Indian life—apart, of course, from the influences of the Christian Gospel."

And we remarked in our introduction to ‘<term>Tiruvaruṭpayan</term>’ or ‘Light of Grace’: "And there can be no doubt that we have, in these works, the brightest and largest gems, picked from the diamond mines of the Sanskrit <term>Vēdāntic</term> works, washed, polished, and arranged in the most beautiful and symmetrical way in the diadem of Indian thought."

Due to a lack of active promotion through lectures and conferences, the subject is not properly brought to the attention of the English-educated public or appreciated by them as it deserves to be; we are, therefore, very grateful to the editor for allowing us to contribute a paper on the subject.

Despite the opinion of a few European and Indian scholars who would trace <term>Śaiva Siddhānta</term> to a purely South Indian source, we have always maintained that <term>Śaiva Siddhānta</term> is nothing but ancient Hinduism in its purest and noblest aspects. It is not a new religion or a new philosophy, and it can be traced back to the earliest <term>Vēdas</term> and <term>Upanishats</term>. We do not hear of anyone ever introducing <term>Śaivaism</term> into India, and the majority of Hindus have remained <term>Śaivaites</term> since before the days of the <term>Mahābhārata</term>.

The ideal of the Highest God has, from the beginning, centered around the person of <term>Rudra</term> or <term>Śiva</term>. In the <term>Ṛig Veda</term>, we find him described as the "Lord of Sacrifices and Prayers," and we find this maintained in the days of <term>Vālmiki</term>, even when beliefs in other deities were slowly gaining ground.

Consistent with this position in the <term>Ṛig Veda</term>, the <term>Yajur Vēda</term> declares that "There is only one <term>Rudra</term>; they do not allow

"...a second," "Eka-eva-Rudrō Nadvitiyāya tasteḥ" (kāṇḍa 8, 6, 10). "He who is one is called Rudra," "Ya Ekō Rudra Uchyati." And St. Tirumūlar declares accordingly:

> ஒன்றவன்றானே யிரண்டவளின்னருள்
> நின்றனன் மூன்றினுள் நான்குணர்ந்தானைந்து
> வென்றனன் ஆறுவிரிந்தனனேழும்பார்ச்
> சென்றவன்றானிருந் தான் உணர்ந்தெட்டே.

He is the only One; the second is His Sweet Grace (**Šakti**).
He stood in the Three; He spoke the four (**Dharmas**).
He conquered the five (**Senses**); He expanded as the six (**Ādhāras**).
He stood transcendent as the seventh, knowing the eighth.

> தானான வேதாந்தம் தான் என்னும் சித்தாந்தம்
> ஆனாதுரியத்து அவன்றன் தனைக்கண்டு
> தேனார் பராபரஞ்சேர் சிவயோகமாய்
> ஆன மலமற்றருஞ் சித்தியாதலே.

'Sōham' is Vēdānta; Siddhānta is the One alone (without a second).
In the imperishable Turiya, after seeing the self (**Atmadaršan**),
You unite with the Parabrahman in Šivayōga.
You can attain the rare Siddhi by losing mala.

"God is only one." "Siddhānta declares there is God alone without a second."

It should be noted that the first mantra is not as well known as the mantra "Ekamēvādvitīyam Brahma", which occurs in an Upanishat of the Śāma Veda. Max Muller has shown that the use of words such as Rudra, Hara, and Śiva to denote the Highest God is much earlier than the use of words like 'Brahman', 'Ātman', and 'Paramātman'. In fact, these words do not occur in the Ṛig Veda at all to denote the Highest God. We may also point out that the word 'Nadvitiyam' occurring in the Yajur Veda is certainly a more ancient and original form of the word than 'Advitiyam', which was formed by the omission of the letter 'n'.

And St. Meykaṇḍān comments on this mantra in the following verse:—

> “ ஒன்றென்றது ஒன்றேகாண் ஒன்றேபதி, பசுவாம்
> ஒன்றென்றநீபாசத்தோடு ளைகாண்—ஒன்றின்றால்
> அக்கரங்களின்றாம் அகரவுயிரின்றேல்
> இக்கிரமத்தென்னும் இருக்கு.”

“The Vedic text means there is only one Supreme Being without a second. And this one is the Lord. You who say ‘there is one,’ are the <term>Paśu</term> (soul) bound up in <term>Pāśa</term> (bondage). The word ‘second-less’ means that, besides God, nothing else can exist, just as we say that there would be no other letters (consonants) if the vowel did not exist.”

No consonant sounds can possibly be formed unless the vowel sound is uttered at the same time; and this justifies us in stating that the vowel is alone, without a second; and yet the vowel is not the consonant, nor is the consonant the vowel. When we utter the consonant sound (மெய் எழுத்து *or* உடல் எழுத்து), the vowel and the consonant are linked in a peculiar, inseparable, and eternal manner. This is the link or relation between our own human body and the mind (உடல் *or* மெய் *and* உயிர்). And from analogy, we say there is a similar link between God and the world (including souls). And this link or relation is called, in the <term>Śaiva Siddhānta</term>, ‘the <term>Advaita</term>,’ and the philosophy postulating this peculiar link between God and man is called the ‘<term>Advaita Siddhānta Philosophy</term>.’

But how does the One link Himself to the many, become the many, and divide Himself among the many, as it were? St. Tirumūlar postulates: “ஒன்றவன்றானே இரண்டு அவன் இன் அருள்” — “He is the one; the second is His Grace (<term>Aruḷ</term>).” This division of Him is brought about because He is also Grace or Love. His Second is His <term>Śakti</term>. He is one with His <term>Śakti</term> or Love.

> அன்பும் சிவமும் இரண்டென்பர் அறிவிலார்
> அன்பே சிவமாவதாரு மறிகிலார்
> அன்பே சிவமாவ தாரு மறிந்த பின்
> அன்பே சிவமா யமர்ந்திருந்தாரே.

<summary>This page explains the Śaiva Siddhānta concept of Advaita, using the analogy of vowels and consonants to describe the inseparable relationship between God and the world. It also identifies God's second aspect as His Grace or Śakti, which is synonymous with Love.</summary>
<keywords>Meykaṇḍān, Paśu, Pāśa, Śaiva Siddhānta, Advaita, Tirumūlar, Aruḷ, Śakti, God and Soul analogy, Tamil theology</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of God's nature, specifically exploring the relationship between God's unity and His Grace or Love, as taught by St. Meykaṇḍān and St. Tirumūlar.</meta>

-># THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.<-

> “The ignorant say, Love and God are different.
> None know that Love and God are the same.
> When they know that Love and God are the same,
> They rest in God as Love.”

And accordingly, also, St. Meykaṇḍān postulates his second Sūtra, in which he declares that God is one with, yet different from, the world and the souls, as He is one with His Ājñā-Śakti, which is all Power, all Intelligence, all Will, and all Love. And in the final argument, he shows that because God is Pure Intelligence, this oneness, union with the world, or omnipresence is possible. If He were not intelligent, but material or <term>jaḍam</term>, this would not be possible.

As such, *Śivajñānabōdam* contains the shortest definition of God as Śiva-Sat, or Chit-Sat, or Sat-Chit. Sat denotes God as a Pure Being, an aspect in which He can never reach us; Chit or <term>Aruḷ</term> or Love denotes His aspect in which He can reach us, and we can know Him. Sat is the sun, which we can never fully comprehend. Chit is the Light, a single ray of which is enough to remove our darkness and enlighten us; and without that one ray of light, we can never know the Sun.

All other conceptions of God follow from this essential definition of God as ‘Sat-Chit’ and, if true, must conform to it. If not, they must be rejected as false.

From the fact that He is intelligent, it follows also that God wills and acts.

> “ சத்தியின்வடிவேதென்னில் தடையிலா ஞானமாகும்,
> உய்த்திடுமிச்சை செய்தி யிவை ஞானத்துளவோவென்னின்,
> எத்திரஞானமுள்ள தத்திறமிச்சைசெய்தி,
> வைத்தலான் மறைப்பின் ஞானன் மருவிக்கிங்கிரியையெல்லாம்.”
> “The form of this Śakti is Unlimited Intelligence.
> If asked whether supreme Will and Power are also found in this Intelligence,
> We answer: yes. Wherever there is intelligence, there are Will and Power.
> As such, Power and Will will also be manifested by this Chit Śakti.”

-># THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.<-

And as He wills to create the worlds, He creates them, dissolves them, and reproduces them again and again. He could not do this without purpose or out of His mere whim and pleasure; and, as we know He is all love, He could do it only out of such love, to help lift up erring and ignorant souls by giving them their bodies and senses. This allows them to will and act for themselves, to taste the bitter fruit of the tree of knowledge of good and evil, to be chastened and purified by suffering and sorrow, and to learn to submit their will to the Will of the Supreme.

And Kālidāsa, in his *Kumāra Sambhava*, declares:

> “No selfish want ever prompts a deed of mine:
> Do the forms—eight varied forms—I wear,
> Not declare the truth of this to all the world?”

And these eight forms he mentions in his invocation in *Śākuntala*:

> “May Īśa preserve you! He who is revealed
> In these eight forms, perceptible to man.—
> *Water*, the first of all creation’s works;
> The *Fire* that bears the sacrifice on high,
> Presented with solemnity to Heaven;
> The *Priest*, the holy offerer of gifts;
> The *Sun* and *Moon*, those two majestic orbs,
> Eternal marshals of day and night;
> The *Subtle Ether*, the vehicle of sound,
> Diffused through the boundless universe;
> The *Earth*, called by sages the place of birth
> Of all material essences and things;
> And *Air*, which gives life to all that breathe.”

> அருநிலைய திங்களாய்நாயிறாகி
> ஆகாசமாயட்ட மூர்த்தியாகிப்

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

->பெருகமுந்நூற்றமும் பெண்ணுமாணாய்<-
->பிறருருவுந்தம்முருவும் தாமேயாய்<-

->நிமிர்புன்சடைப்படிக்கள் நின்றவாறே."<-

> "As Earth, Fire, Air, and the Sacrificer (Ejamān), as Moon, the Sun, and Space (Ākāś), as the Eight-Formed (Ashtamūrti), as goodness and evil, as male and female, Himself the form of every form, as yesterday, today, and tomorrow, my Lord with the braided hair stands supreme."

St. Māṇikkavāçagar has written the following verse:

> “ நிலம்நீர் நெருப்புயர் நீள்விசும்பு நிலாப்பகலோன்
> புலனாயமைந்தனோடு எண்வகையாய்ப்புணர்ந்துநின்றான்.
> உலகேழெனத்திசை பத்தெனத்தானொருவனுமே
> பலவாகிநின்றவாதோ ணோக்கமாடாமோ. ”

> Earth, Water, Air, Fire, Sky, the Sun and Moon,
> And the sentient man—these eight forms He pervades.
> Within the seven worlds and ten directions, He, the *One*
> And the Many, stands thus; let us sing.

He pervades these eight forms; they constitute His eight bodies, and therefore Śiva is called <term>Ashtamūrti</term>.<note>By pervading these forms, He also receives eight names. The following verse is usually quoted, but its source is unknown.</note> By this, His inner governance (<term>Antaryāmitvam</term>), His Omnipresence, or His immanence in all of nature is established, as He is pure Consciousness (<term>Chit</term>). Yet He is beyond all these forms, beyond all nature, and beyond man.

---

> “Prithivyō Bava, Āpah Sarvah, Agné Rudraḥ, Vāyūr Bhīmah
> Ākāśasya Mahādevah, Sūryasya Ugrah, Chandrasya Somah, Ātma
> naḥ Paśupatih.”

**Srikaṇthaśivāchārya comments on these names in his commentary (<term>Bhāshya</term>) on I. i. 2. as follows:**

Regarding Brahman being the subject of an eightfold naming: The Supreme Brahman is the Being signified by the eight names: *Bhava, Sarva, Īśāna, Paśupati, Rudra, Ugra, Bhīma, and Mahādéva*. Although He is signified by all words, He is specifically designated by *Bhava* and similar words because they indicate His highest being; it does not follow that He is not designated by words other than these eight.

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues the examination of the names of Brahman as discussed by Srikaṇthaśivāchārya, providing the etymological and scriptural basis for the titles Bhava, Sarva, Īśāna, Paśupati, and Rudra to demonstrate God's immanence and sovereignty.</meta>

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

The famous passage in the 17th Brāhmaṇa of the 3rd chapter of the Bṛihadāraṇyaka Upanishad deals with God being immanent in nature and in man.

Brahman is called *Bhava* because He exists everywhere at all times, with the root "bhū" meaning *sattā* or existence. We are taught that Brahman is the Existent, pervading all things. Accordingly, the Sruti says:

> "Existent alone, my dear, this was at first—one only, without a second." (Chhā. Up. vi, 2)
> 
> "Truth (Existence), Wisdom, Endless is Brahman." (Tait. Up. ii, 1.)
> 
> "He who is existent, who delights in Prāṇa, whose joy is in manas." (Tait. Up. i, 6.)
> 
> "The ineffable glory" (Mahānārāyaṇa Up. 24)

and so on. As He pervades all things—as, for example, "the jar *existing*" or the "cloth *existing*"—it is evident that Brahman, the existent, constitutes the *upādāna* or material cause of all. The jar, for instance, being always associated with clay, is said to be made out of clay; that is, it has clay for its *upādāna*. Thus Brahman, the existent, is designated by the word *Bhava*.

Brahman, the all-destroyer, is designated by the word *Sarva*, derived from the root "Śṛi," meaning to destroy. Brahman is spoken of as the destroyer in the following passages:

> "Hail! hail! therefore, to the Destroyer, to the Great Devourer" (Atharvaśiras Up.)
> 
> "To whom the Brāhmaṇas and Kshatriyas are, as it were, but food" (Katha. Up. ii, 25.)

Brahman is denoted by the word "*Īśāna*," the Ruler, as one endowed with unconditioned supreme sovereignty, as revealed in the passage: "Who rules these worlds with His powers of ruling." (Atharvaśiras Up.)

Since the Īśvara or Ruler must have subjects to rule over, Brahman is denoted by the word *Paśupati*, Master of *Paśus* or subject beings (souls). Thus, the Sruti says:

> "Whom—the four-footed as well as two-footed souls (paśus)—Paśupati, the Lord of souls, rules." (Taittirīya Saṃhitā III. i, 4.)

As *Paśus* (souls) are called such because of *pāśa* (the bond), the term *Paśu* stands for both the soul and the bond. By this title, Brahman is shown to be the Ruler of *chit* and *achit*, or spirit and matter.

Brahman is called *Rudra* as He expels the affliction of *samsāra*, as we are told in the passage:

> "The knower of Ātman crosses beyond grief" (Chhā. Up. vii. 1.)

<summary>This page explains the scriptural and etymological origins of several names for Brahman, identifying Him as the material cause of existence, the universal destroyer, and the supreme ruler of both spirit and matter. It highlights God's role as Paśupati, the master of souls, and Rudra, the remover of worldly suffering.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of Brahman's nature and names, specifically focusing on the attributes of Ugra, Bhīma, and Mahādeva, while establishing Brahman as the material and efficient cause of the universe through the lens of Śiva.</meta>

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

Beginning with the verse, "He whose body is the earth, etc.":

> "He who dwells in the earth, and is within or distinct from the earth, whom the earth does not know, whose body (*śarīra*) the earth is, and who rules the earth from within—He is your *Ātmā*, the Ruler within, the Immortal," ¹

and providing similar statements:

Brahman is called *Ugra* or Fierce because He cannot be overpowered by other luminaries, as taught in the passage:

> "The sun does not shine there, nor the moon and stars." (Śvetāśvatara Upanishad vi. 14.)

As the regulator and the source of fear for all sentient beings, Brahman is known by the name of *Bhīma* or Terrible. The Śruti says:

> "From fear of Him, the wind blows." (Taittirīya Upanishad ii. 8.)

As Great and Luminous, Śiva is called *Mahādeva*. Similarly, the Atharvaśiras Upanishad says:

> "For what reason, then, is He called Mahādeva? Because, having abandoned all things, He is adored for His *Ātma-Jñāna* (spiritual wisdom) and for His yogic glory; therefore He is called Mahādeva."

That Being called Śiva—known as free from all taint of *Samsāra* and as the repository of all that is good—is, because of His nature, the cause of the birth and existence of the whole world. Since a Being of such greatness can be the twofold cause of the world, He (the Being called Śiva), endowed as He is with such greatness, is called Brahman. He has also been proved to be the seat of Bliss and other such attributes; therefore, it is useless to ask whether Bliss and other attributes can constitute Brahman individually. From the passage "one should know *Māyā* as *Prakṛiti*," it may be seen that *Māyā* is the *Prakṛiti* or cause; that *Māyā* is essentially *Īśvara*, as taught in the concluding part of the sentence:

> "And know *Īśvara* as the possessor or the seat of the *Māyā*." (Śvetāśvatara Upanishad iv. 10.)

Brahman, associated with the *sūkshma* (subtle) *chit* and *achit*, is the cause; and Brahman, associated with the *sthūla* (gross) *chit* and *achit*, is the effect. Therefore, the *Siddhānta* (demonstrated conclusion) is that the birth and existence of the universe form the distinguishing marks of Brahman.
