Regarding water, air, fire, etc. * * * it ends with: "He who dwells in *Vijñāna* (the soul) and is within or *distinct* from *Vijñāna*, whom *Vijñāna* does not know, whose body *Vijñāna* is, who rules *Vijñāna* from within—He is your *Ātmā*, the Ruler within, the Immortal."

That God is different from all nature and man is further brought out by the famous "Neti, Neti" (Not this, Not this) verse of this same Upanishad (3-9-26), which Parañjoti Munivar translates and expands in the following lines:

> அல்லநீதல்லவே யீதெனயன்மைச் சொல்லினொற்றுதித்தி
> ளொக்குமிச்சுந்தரன்.

> பாதக்கண்ணாவளை யிந்த ணாதி பகர் வாரையாயு மவரே."

"The Sages declare, 'He is not the five elements, not the senses, nor sensations, nor the *Andakaraṇas* (inner faculties), nor the soul; He is the deceitful void <note>The word 'Veli' in Tamil means a 'void space' and corresponds to the Telugu word 'Bayilu,' which the sage Vēmana is very fond of using.</note> which the *Vedas* fail to discover."

The Supreme is adored as the Creator, Hara; as Protector, Sankara; as Destroyer or Reproducer, Rudra; and as Bliss-giver, Śiva. God is called "எண்குணத்தான்" <note>St. Tiruvalluvar: Just as the senses are useless if they do not enjoy their proper sensations, so the head is useless if it does not bow to the Lord with eight attributes.</note> as possessing—

"Admitted that birth, etc., as attributes inherent in the universe, do not pertain to Brahman; still, they rightly constitute the defining marks of Brahman as one closely connected with the universe. The Entity called Śiva, possessed of the attribute of omniscience and so on, and"

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

<sig>30</sig>

eight attributes and they are as follows:—Self-dependence, Purity, Self-knowledge, Omniscience, Being Ever Free from Sin, Supreme Graciousness, and Unlimited Bliss.

[Brahman], denoted by these eight names, is said to be the cause of the universe; and to that Entity alone, Bliss and all other similar attributes point. The attributes referred to are Omniscience (*Sarvajñatā*), Ever-contentedness (*nityatriptatā*), Beginningless Wisdom (*Anādibodhata*), Independence (*Svatantratā*), Never-failing Power (*Nityāluptaśaktitā*), and Infinite Power (*Anantaśaktitā*).

*Omniscience* (*Sarvajñatā*) consists of all things becoming objects of direct perception—of pure intuitive experience—independent of all external organs of sensation. It is known to exist within Brahman from such passages as: “Who perceives all and who knows all, whose essence consists of knowledge.” (Muṇḍaka Upanishad I. i. 4). Thus, the cause of the universe is Brahman, who knows the appropriate ways and means of creating the various bodies suited to all sentient beings so they may receive the results of their many actions.

*Ever-contentedness* (*nityatriptatā*) consists of being filled with supreme Bliss, in which there is not the slightest trace of distress. Therefore, the revelation says, “Bliss is Brahman” (Tait. Up. iii. 6). That Bliss (*ānanda*)—which is introduced with the words, “There is yet another *Ātman* who is composed of Bliss” (Tait. Up. ii. 5), and reaches the highest point of supreme Bliss through repeated multiplication in the passages beginning with “Here follows the measurement of Bliss” and ending with “that is the unit of Brahman's Bliss” (Tait. Up. ii. 8)—is an attribute of Para-Brahman. It is figuratively described as Brahman Himself in the passage “Bliss is Brahman,” because of the abundance of Bliss in Him. Brahman, who delights in enjoying such Bliss, is said to be ever-contented. The enjoyment of this mighty Bliss on the part of Brahman is achieved through the mind (*manas*) only, not through external organs of sensation. Therefore, the passage says:

> “There is Brahman who is *ākāśa-śarira* (whose body is light), *satyātma* (Himself the existent), *prāṇārāma* (whose joy is life), *mana-ānanda* (delighted in the mind), *śānti-samṛiddhā* (perfect in peace), and *amṛita* (immortal).” Tait. Up. i. 6.

Here, by *ākāśa*—literally, that which shines all around, the Light—is meant the *chit-ambara*, the ether of the spirit or the spirit-light; but not the material *ākāśa* or ether, because the latter cannot serve to distinguish Brahman from other things in nature. The *chit-ambara* referred to here is that Supreme Power (*Parama-Śakti*), that highest cause, that ocean, as it were, from which spring up all the hosts

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

Then follow questions whether God should be said to possess form or no form, whether He should be regarded as *Saguna* or *Nirguna*, Personal or Impersonal, and so on.

---

of bubbles, the mundane eggs of all groups. Brahman, whose form is that supreme light, is spoken of in the *śruti* as "*ākāśa-śarīra*." That *chid-ākāśa* is the highest cause is known from such passages as the following:

> "All these beings take their rise from Ākāśa and return into Ākāśa." (Chhā. Up. III. ix. 1).

> "He who is called Ākāśa is the revealer of all forms and names" (Chhā. Up. VIII. xiv. 1.)

*Satyatman* : He who is the *Sattā* or existence. *Praṇarāma*, He who delights in *Prāṇa*, the *chit-ambara-śakti*, the Power of Spiritual light, the Basis of all, constituting Brahman's own essential nature. *Mana-ānanda* : He whose joy is in *Manas* (mind), not in the external organs of sensation. Here, too, "ānanda" refers to the spirit-light, the *chit-ambara*, the Prakriti or cause. Accordingly the *Śruti* says:

> "Who could breathe if that Bliss, that Light, existed not." (Tait. Up. III. vii, 1.)

*Sānti-samṛiddha* : He who has attained to *Śivatā*, to Śiva's condition.
*Amṛita* : He who has been free from time without beginning.

Thus, it is seen that Brahman, who is essentially Existence, Intelligence, and Bliss, and whose essential nature is the Supreme Light, enjoys the Bliss of His essential nature by mind alone, independent of external organs of sensation, as implied by the epithet "*Mana-ānanda*." This epithet also implies that the emancipated souls who have attained the state of Brahman possess the *antaḥ-karaṇa* or mind, the organ which acts independently of external organs, and by which they experience the unsurpassed bliss of their essential nature. Therefore, Brahman is *nityatripta* or ever-contented, enjoying the infinite Bliss of His essential nature by *manas*, which is pure *bodha-śakti* itself—the faculty of knowledge which can act independently of external organs. That is to say, for Him there is no necessity for the slightest joy of the world (*saṃsāra*) external to Himself.

The possession of unsurpassed knowledge—which is *svatās-siddha*, self-existing or inherent—constitutes what is called *anadibodhatva* or beginningless wisdom. Indeed, the *antaḥ-karaṇa*, *jñāna* or knowledge, which is the organ by which He enjoys the Bliss of His own essential nature, exists through eternity. Therefore, Brahman is one of beginningless wisdom, since knowledge which repels *saṃsāra* exists through...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Brahman, moving from His existence as spiritual light and joy to the specific attributes of His personality, including His independence and infinite power.</meta>

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

In regard to the question of form or no form, the Siddhānta is certain that God is neither *Rūpi* (possessing form), nor *Arūpi* (formless), nor *Rūpārūpi* (possessing both form and formlessness). “ சிவன் அருவுருவுமல்லன் சித்தினோடே சித்துமல்லன் ” “God is neither form nor formless, neither soul nor matter.” It recognizes that all *Rūpa* (form) and *Arūpa* (formlessness) are only forms of matter that are perceivable to our senses, and God can never be perceived as an object by us, and cannot possess any of these material forms or bodies. The nature of matter is to limit, and God is the boundless and can never be [limited by] eternity; He is always free from the evil of *samsāra* <note>The cycle of death and rebirth</note> and is spoken of in the *Śruti* as “perfect in peace and immortal.”

*Independence* (*svatantratā*) consists of freedom from servitude to others and from other marks of inferiority, and in all things other than Himself being brought under His own control. The independence of Brahman as the driving force of the universe of matter and spirit is taught in passages such as the following:
> “There are two, one knowing (*Īśvara*), the other not knowing, both unborn, one strong, the other weak.” (Śvetā. Up. i, 9).
> “By knowing the enjoyer, the enjoyed, and the ruler etc.” (Śvetā. Up. i, 12).
> “But he who controls both knowledge and ignorance, is another.” (Śvetā. Up. v, 1).

It is evident that because of His independence in all matters, Brahman is the author of all.

Never-failing potency (*Nityaluptasaktitva*) consists of all powers being inherent in His own nature. Accordingly, the *Śruti* says “His Higher Power (*Parā-śakti*) is revealed as manifold, as inherent, acting as force and knowledge.” (Śvetā. Up. vi, 8). From this it follows that the powers of the universe of spirit and matter are inherent in Brahman and that He is never without these specific attributes.

The possession of unlimited potentialities is what is called Endless Potency (*anantaśaktita*). It is by virtue of these endless powers that Brahman is the producer and the ruler of the world. Accordingly it is revealed to us that:
> “There is only one Rudra—they do not allow a second—who rules all the worlds by His powers”; (Atharvasiras Upanishat).
> “Who rules all these worlds by His supreme powers of ruling and producing.” (Atharvasiras Upanishat).

As one who possesses endless powers, Brahman can be the material cause of the infinite universe.

<summary>This page explains that God transcends material form and formlessness, emphasizing His absolute independence and His inherent, endless powers as the creator and ruler of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Brahman, Siddhānta, Rūpi, Arūpi, svatantratā, Nityaluptasaktitva, Parā-śakti, anantaśaktita, Rudra, Śvetāśvatara Upanishad, Atharvasiras Upanishat</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Brahman, specifically addressing the transition from God's independence and power to why His spiritual forms are distinct from material existence.</meta>

found by any material forms. Some would say God is <term>Arūpi</term> <note>Formless</note>, not realizing that matter is also formless, like air, and nothing is gained by calling Him <term>Arūpi</term>. The fact to be clearly borne in mind is that God cannot be objective to us, nor can He possess a material form.

But if it is pointed out that the <term>Śaiva Siddhāṇta</term> religion recognizes forms of God and His appearances and acts, it is replied that these forms of His are not material. Instead, they are purely spiritual forms created from His great love and grace, to be perceived not by the human mind but through divine grace: 'அவன் அருளாலே அவன்றாள் வணங்கி' <note>"Worshipping His feet by His own grace."</note>. <term>St. Arulnandi</term> says:—

> உருமேனி தரித்துக்கொண்ட தென்றலு முருவிறந்து
> வருமேனி யதுவுங் கண்டோ மருவுரு வானபோது
> திருமேனி யுபயம் பெற்றோம் செப்பிய மூன்றுநந்தம்
> கருமேனி கழிக்கவந்த கருணையின் வடிவுகாணே.

"All these forms of His are assumed out of His supreme grace for destroying our evil bodies." And how this is possible is shown in the following:—

"As He does not possess the defect of being an object of perception, and as He possesses absolute intelligence and power, and as He does not possess likes and dislikes, the <term>Nirmala</term> <note>Pure/Stainless</note> God can assume any form out of His grace." And these forms are described in the following verse: "His form is Love; His attributes and knowledge are Love; His five functions are Love; His organs like arms, feet, etc., and His ornaments like the crescent moon, etc., are also Love. These things are assumed by the <term>Nirmala</term> God, not for His own benefit but for the benefit of mankind." Compare this with the following verse from the <term>Taittirīya Upanishat</term>:—

> "His head is surely Love; joy is His right wing; delight His left;
> Bliss is His Self, Brahman whereon He rests."

The following beautiful hymn from <term>St. Appar</term>, and the text from the <term>Maṇḍūkya Upanishat</term> may also be read:—

> மைப்படிந்த கண்ணாளும் தானும் கச்சி
> மயானத்தான் வார்சடையான் மாசொன்றில்லான்

<summary>This page argues that God's forms in Śaiva Siddhāṇta are spiritual manifestations of pure Love and Grace rather than material objects, intended solely for the salvation of mankind. It supports this with citations from St. Arulnandi, the Taittirīya Upanishat, and St. Appar.</summary>

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD

ஒப்புடையனல்லன், ஒருவனல்லன்,
ஒருவனல்லன் உருவ மில்லி
யப்படியன் அவ்வுருவன் அவ்வண்ணத்தன்.
அவனருளே கண்ணாகக் காண்பதல்லால்
இப்படியன் இவ்வுருவன் இவ்வண்ணத்தன்
இவனிறைவ னென்றெழுதிக் காட்டொணாதே.

> "The Lord with braided hair lives in the <term>Kāñchi</term> burial ground with His beautiful <term>Umā</term>, who has finely shaped eyebrows. He is without sin. He is not like mortals and cannot be compared to any of them. He is beyond any specific place and is incomparable. We can perceive Him, His form, and His nature only through His grace; otherwise, no one can depict Him in His true form and nature."

> "This <term>Ātmā</term> cannot be reached through explanation, intellectual understanding, or frequent listening. *He is obtained only by the one whom He chooses.* To that person, God reveals His true form." (Maṇḍūkya iii, 2, 3.) <note>This citation refers to the Mundaka Upanishad 3.2.3.</note>

It should also be noted that the various forms found in temples are merely earthly symbols. In our view, they are necessary for the ordinary human mind to understand and follow divine ideals until the soul has reached a very advanced stage. A missionary friend of ours wrote that he found the use of symbolism necessary for educated people, but he was unconvinced of its beneficial effect on the illiterate. This opinion contradicts the common view on the subject, yet it remains true in its claim that symbols are necessary even for the highly educated.

We regard the various ways of conceiving God—as "He," "She," and "It"—as concepts derived from material forms. As such, they do not belong to His real essence; however, these forms are necessary for us to easily conceive of God:

" பெண்ணணலியாகிப் பிறங்கொளிசேர்
மண்ணாகி விண்ணாகி யித்தனையும் வேறாகி "

> "He is male, female, and neuter; He is earth and heaven, and yet He is none of these."

<summary>This text explores the incomparable and spiritual nature of God, asserting that He can only be perceived through His own grace. It further explains that religious symbols and gendered conceptions of the divine are necessary aids for human understanding rather than descriptions of His true essence.</summary>

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

> "பெண்ணணலிபெணும் டேற்றிபன் டோற்றி"
> "Praise be to Him who is female and male and neuter."

Further, the words <term>Saguṇa</term> and <term>Nirguṇa</term> are usually translated as "personal" and "impersonal," and we have often pointed out how vaguely and loosely these words are used and protested against this translation. We will first consider the words <term>Saguṇa</term> and <term>Nirguṇa</term>. They literally mean "with <term>guṇa</term>" and "without <term>guṇa</term>." One school of people would interpret them as meaning "with *good qualities*" and "without *bad qualities*"; the absurdity of this is seen from the fact that the two words are made to mean the same thing. The word "<term>Guṇa</term>," however, does not mean any good or bad quality, but is a technical word as used by the <term>Sāñkhya</term> and <term>Vedānta</term> schools and as it occurs in the <term>Upanishads</term>, <term>Gītā</term>, etc. It means the three <term>guṇas</term>—<term>Sattva</term>, <term>Rajas</term>, and <term>Tamas</term>—the qualities of <term>Prakṛiti</term> or <term>Pradhāna</term> (matter); and as such, the words mean "with *material qualities*" or "without *material qualities*." St. <term>Tirumūlar</term> uses the phrase "முக்குணநிற்குணம, <term>mukkuna-nirguṇam</term>," so that no mistake may be made regarding the word <term>Nirguṇam</term> itself.

->சாத்திகமெய்து நனவெனச் சாற்றுங்கால்<-
->வாய்த்த விராசத மன்னுங்கனவென்ப<-
->ஒப்பத்திடும் தாமத முற்ற சுழுத்தியாம்<-
->மாய்த்திடும் நிற்குண மாசில் துரியமே.<-

> "<term>Satva</term> is the condition of wakefulness (<term>Jāgrata</term>); <term>Rajas</term> is the dream-condition (<term>Svapna</term>); <term>Tamas</term> is <term>Sushupti</term>; the stainless <term>Turiyam</term> is <term>Nirguṇa</term>."

So also the <term>Gītā</term> speaks of "<term>Thraigunyo Nirguṇaha</term>," and it stands to reason that God cannot be "<term>Saguṇa</term>," clothed in matter or material qualities, and must therefore be non-material, <term>Nirguṇa</term>. The Supreme God is, therefore, described in the <term>Upanishads</term>, the <term>Gītā</term>, and the <term>Śivajñānabodham</term> as <term>Nirguṇa</term> and not as <term>Saguṇa</term>, as in the following passages:—

"This one God is hidden in every <term>bhūta</term>, pervading all, the inner <term>ātmā</term> of every <term>ātmā</term>, the Inspector of all deeds (spectator) in

<summary>This page defines Saguṇa and Nirguṇa as the presence or absence of material qualities (guṇas) rather than personal or impersonal traits. It argues that God is Nirguṇa, meaning He is non-material and exists beyond the three states of consciousness (wakefulness, dreaming, and deep sleep).</summary>

...in whom everything dwells (the supporter), the witness, the pure Intelligence and <term>Nirguṇa</term> Being; the <term>Išvara</term> of <term>Išvaras</term>, the <term>Mahešvara</term>, the Supreme God of Gods, the king of kings, the supreme of the supreme, the “<term>Išā</term>” of the universe (<term>Švetāšvataropaniṣad</term>).

> “Beginningless, <term>Nirguṇa</term>, <term>Paramātman</term>, Imperishable—though seated in the body, O <term>Kaunteya</term>, He does not work, nor is He stained (<term>Gītā</term> 13:31). <note>Rāmānuja explains <term>Nirguṇa</term> as being destitute of <term>sattva</term> and other material qualities.</note>

> “Will not the Lord—who is <term>Nirguṇa</term>, <term>Nirmala</term>, Eternal Happiness, <term>Tatparam</term> (transcending all things), and beyond comparison—appear to the soul when it gets rid of its <term>tattvas</term> such as <term>ākāš</term>, etc.? Will He not appear as a far transcending wonder and an inseparable light of its understanding?” (<term>Šivajñānabōdham</term> ix. 2. a.) But certain deities are stated to be <term>Saguṇa</term>, as they are clothed with pure <term>Sattva</term> or <term>Rajas</term> or <term>Tamas</term>; they should not be confused with the <term>Turiya mūrti</term> or the Fourth (<term>chaturtha</term>), the supreme <term>Brahman</term>. These <term>Saguṇa</term> beings are merely certain souls from among the <term>Sakalars</term> wielding very high powers and still possessing material bodies.

->“ *Šāntam šivam advaitam chaturtham* ” (<term>Rāmatāpini Upaniṣad</term>)<-

The word <term>Nirguṇa</term> is the same as the word “<term>guṇātita</term>,” meaning “beyond <term>guṇa</term> or matter.” The word, therefore, implies a non-material nature and therefore pure <term>chit</term> (consciousness). Christian missionaries, therefore, need not shy away from this word, and they should certainly drop the word “<term>Saguṇa</term>,” which technically means material. From the passages quoted above, especially from the verse from <term>Šivajñānabōdham</term>, it will be seen that although God is called “<term>Nirguṇa</term>,” intelligence, rationality, and consciousness are not denied to Him. This is made further clear in the following verses from St. <term>Meykaṇḍān</term> and St. <term>Tirumūlar</term>.

> ஏகமாய் நின்றே யிணையடிகள் ஒன்றுணரப்
> போகமாய் தான்விளைந்த பொற்பினான்—ஏகமாய்
> உள்ளத்தின் கண்ணானான் உள்குவா ருள்கிற்றை
> உள்ளத்தாற் காணாரோ வுற்று.

“When the soul, becoming one with God, feels Him,”

It distinguishes the supreme, non-material Brahman from "Saguṇa" deities who possess material forms and are categorized alongside high-ranking souls.</summary>

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

He becomes the Supreme Bliss as God becomes one with the soul. So, by understanding Him, will he not know through the soul what is understood by the soul?

> காணறிந் தன்றே யிருக்கின்ற தீசனை
> வானறிந்தார் அறியாது மயங்கினர்
> ஊனறிந் துள்ளே உயிர்க்கின்ற வொண்சுடர்
> தானறியான் பின்னை யாரறியாரே.
>
> "The day I knew my God, the same was not understood by the Gods. It is said that the bright radiance lighting the inside of my soul and body does not know! If He does not know, who else can?"

Of course, it is also said in these works that God "cannot know" ("<term>உதிப்பிலரியா உணராமறவா</term>"). It is pointed out by *Śiva-jñāna Svāmigal* in his *Drāviḍa Mahā Bāshya* that this only means that God's consciousness is not like the consciousness of an individual man. Human consciousness is limited and cannot become conscious unless it forgets, and it can only understand things in relation to other things ("change is essential to consciousness"—Bain). This human consciousness is called <term>சுட்டுணர்வு</term> (*suṭṭuṇarvu*). God does not possess this limited <term>சுட்டுணர்வு</term>. His consciousness is that which transcends all limitation and all relation and is absolute; as in His <term>Akandākāra</term> (Infinite Form), there is no distinction between "this" and "that," and there is nothing outside of Him: "<term>போக்கிலன் வரவிலன்</term>" (He has no going or coming).

Regarding the question of whether God is personal or impersonal, we are not quite sure in what sense our Indian writers use these words, but they mostly take them to mean *Saguṇa* (with qualities) and *Nirguṇa* (without qualities). There is some difference of opinion regarding the connotation of these words among European writers. Some use "personal" to imply individuality and limitation; others use it in a way that does not mean individuality, and this is the more prevalent and learned opinion. We take the following definitions from a vocabulary of Philosophy:

**Person:** A being who is intelligent and free; every spiritual and moral agent, every cause that possesses responsibility and consciousness, is a person. In this sense, God, considered as a creating cause, is a person.

<summary>This page explains that God's consciousness is absolute and non-relational, unlike limited human understanding, and begins defining the "personality" of God in the context of Indian and European philosophy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of God's consciousness, moving from the definition of Nirguṇa to the distinction between human and divine understanding, while addressing whether God can be considered a "person."</meta>

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

> “The intimate relation of God, as Being, to all His attributes and to all His essence, constitutes the Divine *Personality*; which for God is His entire Being. God exists only for Himself, in an infinite and absolute manner. God relates entirely to Himself; for there is no being outside of Him to which He can have a relation. His whole essence is for Himself, and this relation is altogether internal. The divine consciousness or personality embraces all that is in God, of all of which He is the reason. ‘Person,’ as applied to Deity, expresses the definite and certain truth that *God is a living being*, and not a dead material energy.”

Emerson says that personality signifies true being (*Sat*), both concrete and spiritual. It alone is original being. It is not limited. It is that universal element that pervades every human soul and which is at once its container and fount of being. Distinction from others and limitation by them results from individuality (Ahaṅkāra or Āṇava), not personality (*Sat*). Personality pertains to the substance of the soul, and individuality to its form. Another Christian writer (Rev. J. Iverach) points out that for the absolute and unconditioned Being to be Personal is not a contradiction in terms, such as a round square, but that it is as true as when we say a white or crimson square.

> “When we speak of the absolute, we speak of it as a predicate of pure being; we simply mean that the Absolute Personal Being is and must be self-conscious, rational, and ethical; it must answer to the idea of spirit. Why may the Absolute Being not be self-conscious? To deny this to Him would be to deny Him one of the perfections which even finite beings can possess.”

St. Meykaṇḍān and St. Tirumūlar had stated the same question long ago, as we have shown. This self-consciousness, <term>இயற்கையுணர்வுணர்தல்</term>, and <term>முற்று முணர்தல்</term>, as we have shown above, is not to be confused with the limited <term>சுட்டறிவு</term> of the soul.

As it is, Personality clearly means *Sat* and *Chit*, and neither *Saguṇa* nor *Nirguṇa*. Personality is opposed to *Achit* or

It aligns Western philosophical perspectives from Emerson and Iverach with the ancient teachings of Tamil saints like St. Meykaṇḍān and St. Tirumūlar.</summary>

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

<term>Jaḍa</term>, or irrational matter, relates to substance, while <term>Saguṇa</term> and <term>Nirguṇa</term> relate to form, whether individual or otherwise. God can never become individualized as a man, woman, or animal; the limitation of these latter classes of beings arises from their union with matter or <term>Guṇa</term> (<term>Saguṇa</term>). From this view, "impersonal" would clearly mean irrational, unintelligent, and material; I do not believe any Indian writer would desire to use this word in relation to the Deity if they only understood its meaning.

From the statement that God is <term>Nirguṇa</term> and not <term>Saguṇa</term>, it follows that God can have neither birth nor death. This is one of the central doctrines of <term>Śaiva Siddhānta</term>, and in this respect, it differs from all existing forms of faith, whether Hindu or otherwise—except, perhaps, Islam and the Unitarian form of Christianity.

->பிறப்பிலி பிஞ்ஞகன் பேரரு ளாளன்<-
->இறப்பிலி யாவர்க்கு மின்ப மருளும்<-
->துறப்பிலி தன்னைத் தொழுமின் தொழுதால்<-
->மறப்பிலி மாயா விருத்தமு மாமே.<-

> "The unborn one with braided hair and supreme grace, the undying one who bestows bliss upon all—worship Him! If you worship Him, your <term>Māyā</term> will undoubtedly vanish." (Saint Tirumūlar.)

Of course, it must stand to reason that our soul itself is neither born nor can it die. What is born or what dies is the material body formed of <term>Māyā</term> or <term>Guṇa</term> associated with the soul from the beginning. These repeated births and deaths occur because of the peculiar link existing between the soul and matter; therefore, the souls comprising all <term>Sakalars</term> are called <term>Saguṇa</term>. This same peculiar link does not exist between God and matter, and hence, He is <term>Nirguṇa</term>. Thus, God can neither be born in the womb nor die. This unique doctrine of <term>Śaiva Siddhānta</term> is what should elevate it to the highest rank of philosophy, and the latest discoveries in science could not shake its foundation.

<vocab>Jaḍa, Saguṇa, Nirguṇa, Śaiva Siddhānta, Islam, Saint Tirumūlar, Māyā, Sakalars</vocab>

<summary>The author explains that God is Nirguṇa (without material qualities) and therefore immune to birth and death, unlike souls (Sakalars) which are linked to matter. This distinction positions Śaiva Siddhānta as a superior philosophy that aligns with rational and scientific thought.</summary>

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

Before we close this paper, we will mention one other feature of <term>Śaiva Siddhāṇta</term> regarding the Godhead. This is that the supreme <term>Brahman</term> of this school, called <term>Śiva</term> or <term>Śivam</term>, is not to be confused with the Hindu Trinity. God is specifically designated by the words <term>Śivam</term>, <term>Saṅkara</term>, <term>Sambhu</term>, and <term>Rudra</term> (he who removes sorrow), as they express the most spiritual nature of God as Love and all-beneficent. That this is no sectarian conception of the Deity, and that the God of the <term>Śaiva Siddhāṇtis</term> is the universal God of all nations and all religions, is finally demonstrated by <term>St. Aruḷnaṇḍi Śivāchāriyār</term> in his very first verse in *<term>Śivajñāna Siddhiyār</term>*.

->அறுவகைச் சமயத்தோர்க்கும் அவ்வவர் பொருளாம் வேறாங்<-
->குறியது உடைத்தாய் வேதாகமங்களின் குறியிறந்தங்<-
->கறிவினி லருளான் மன்னியம்மையோ டப்பனாகி<-
->செறிவொழியாது நின்ற சிவனடி சென்னிவைப்பாம்.<-

> "Let me place upon my head the feet of <term>Śiva</term>, who stands as the goal of each of the six forms of religion, and who exists in the various forms conceived by the various internal schools of the <term>Śaiva</term> faith, yet stands beyond the reach of all <term>Vēdas</term> and <term>Āgamas</term>, filling all intelligences with His love, becoming my Heavenly Father and Mother, and inseparably filling each and every one."

To sum up, according to the true <term>Vēdaṇta Siddhāṇta</term> philosophy, God is <term>Sat</term>, <term>Chit</term>, and <term>Ānaṇda</term>. He is not material nor enveloped in matter; He is <term>Nirguṇa</term> and Personal, ever blissful and entirely Love, and all His acts, such as creation, etc., are prompted by such Love. He is neither "he," "she," nor "it," nor does He have any material <term>Rūpa</term> (form) or <term>Arūpa</term> (formlessness), and He can reveal His grace and majesty to those who love Him. He can neither be born nor die, and as such, He is indeed the Pure, Absolute, and Infinite Being, able to lift up humanity struggling in the bonds of <term>mala</term>, <term>māyā</term>, and <term>karma</term>. To know Him as our true Heavenly Father and Mother and to love Him as such is the only remedy for all the evils of wandering mankind.

<vocab>Śaiva Siddhāṇta, Śiva, Śivam, Saṅkara, Sambhu, Rudra, Hindu Trinity, St. Aruḷnaṇḍi Śivāchāriyār, Śivajñāna Siddhiyār, Vēdas, Āgamas, Vēdaṇta Siddhāṇta, Sat, Chit, Ānaṇda, Nirguṇa, Rūpa, Arūpa, mala, māyā, karma</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the God of Śaiva Siddhāṇta as a universal, absolute being who exists beyond the Hindu Trinity and material form. It characterizes God as a personal entity motivated by love, serving as a spiritual parent to lift humanity out of the bonds of karma and matter.</summary>

# <term>ADVAITA</term> ACCORDING TO THE <term>ŠAIVA SIDDHĀṆTA</term>.

In a previous paper contributed to *The New Reformer*, we discussed the Personality of God as understood in the <term>Šaiva</term> School; now, we intend to discuss at length the <term>Advaita</term> Philosophy, as explained by the <term>Siddhāṇta</term> writers. We request the earnest attention of all students of Indian Philosophy, and we confidently hope that as this philosophy is understood more and more, it is bound to win its way into the hearts and hopes of all sincere people of every religion. As we pointed out in our last paper, this philosophy has only been presented to the world at large, no longer hidden from view, within a short time; there is a great future before it, when it can shine like a beacon from the summit of the highest hill.

First, we must point out that the word ‘<term>Advaita</term>’, in its simplest form, is used to describe their philosophy by all <term>Siddhāṇta</term> writers; and the word *<term>Višishṭādvaita</term>* never appears in <term>Siddhāṇta</term> literature. People who hear of this philosophy for the first time immediately categorize it as *<term>Višishṭādvaita</term>*, without pausing to inquire into its real aspects. But, as we said above, all <term>Šaiva Siddhāṇtis</term> call themselves strict <term>Advaitis</term>. <term>Saint Meykaṇḍān</term> uses the word ‘<term>Advaita</term>’ in his commentary on the second <term>Sūtra</term> of *<term>Šivajñānabōdham</term>* in the passage “அத்துவிதமென்ற சொல்லே அந்நிய நாத்தியையுணர்த்து மாயிட்டு,” and explains his system of <term>Advaita</term>. In another place, “அத்துவிதமாதல் அகண்டமும் தைவமே அத்துவிதியன்பாற்றெழு,” he calls his system ‘<term>Advaita</term>’ and addresses his pupils as ‘<term>Advaiti</term>.’

<term>Saint Umāpati Šivāchārya</term> uses the term in the following introductory verse in *<term>Šivaprakāšam</term>*:—

> புறச்சமயத்தவர்க்கிருளாய் அகச்சமயத்தொளியாய்
> புகலளவைக்களவாகி பொற்பணிபோல பேதப் .

<summary>The author introduces the Saiva Siddhanta interpretation of Advaita, distinguishing it from Vishishtadvaita and citing the authoritative use of the term by saints Meykandan and Umapati Sivacharya.</summary>
<keywords>Advaita, Saiva Siddhanta, Vishishtadvaita, Saint Meykandan, Saint Umapati Sivacharya, Sivajnanabodham, Sivaprakasam, Indian Philosophy</keywords>

# THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

->பிறப்பிலதா யிருள்வெளி போற்பேத முஞ்சொற்பொருள்போற்<-
->பேதாபேதமுமின்றிப் பெருநூல் சொன்ன<-
->அறித்திறனால் விளைவதா யுடலுயிர்க் கண்ணருக்கண்<-

->சிறப்பினதாய் வேதாந்தத் தெளிவாஞ் சைவ<-
->சித்தாந்த திறனிங்குத் தெரிக்க லுற்றாம்.<-

> "We expound here the beauty of <term>Śaiva Siddhānta</term>, the essence of the <term>Vedānta</term>, *whose excellent merit consists in its exposition of <term>Advaita</term>*. It postulates an inseparable relation like body and soul, the eye and the sun, or the soul and the eye. This is supported by the <term>Dharma</term> of the highest authoritative books and is unlike the <term>Bheda</term>, <term>Bhedābheda</term>, and <term>Abhēda</term> relations—illustrated, respectively, by light and darkness, word and meaning, and gold and ornament—as set forth by other schools. This system is further supported by perfectly logical methods; it is light to truth-seekers and darkness to others."

Saint <term>Tāyumānavar</term> uses the word freely and has this verse in praise of his <term>Parama Guru</term>, Saint <term>Meykaṇḍān</term>:

->பொய்கண்டார் காணாப் புனிதமா மத்துவித<-
->மெய்கண்ட நாதனடி மேவுநா ளெந்நாளோ.<-

> "Oh, for the day when I can reach the feet of my lord, who found the truth of the pure <term>Advaita</term>, which could not be comprehended by those dwelling in untruth."

There is another verse of his as well in which he uses the word twice, illustrating and explaining the meaning of the word itself, which will be discussed later on:

->ஆணவத்தோடத் துவிதமானபடி மெய்ஞ்ஞானத்<-
->தாணுவினோடத்துவித மாகுநாளெந்நாளோ"<-

> "Oh, for the day when I will be in <term>Advaita</term> relation with God, just as I am now in <term>Advaita</term> relation with <term>Āṇava</term> (the world)."

Returning to the word itself, it occurs in the following passages of the <term>Vedas</term> and <term>Upanishads</term>, and in a few other places:

"Eka eva Rudro Nadvitīyāya tasthe" (<term>Yajur Veda</term>, 1. 8. 6.)
"Ekhohi Rudro Nadvitīyāya tasthe" (<term>Svetās Upanishat</term>, 3, 2).

# ADVAITA ACCORDING TO THE ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA.

> “Ekamevādvitīyam” (Chhāndog Upanishat, 6, 2, 1.)
> “Sāntam Śivam Advaitam Chaturtham Ātmā” (Māṇḍukya Upanishat, 7.)
> “Amatras chaturtavya vakārika prapunchopasamas Śivodvaitavave.”
The partless fourth, incomprehensible, that ends all going out. Śiva-advaitam. (Māṇḍukya Upanishat, 12.)

The first two texts provide the word in its original form, ‘Nadvitīyam,’ and the word now in use has been formed by the omission of the initial ‘n’; and ‘na’ is the negative prefix. Therefore, the word literally means "no two," or "not two." The word, as used in the texts quoted above and read within the context, would not convey all the philosophical meaning which has been attached to it by the teachers (<term>Acharyas</term>) of various schools. It simply meant there was no other person except the one mentioned at the time. In the first text, it meant that there was only one God and no second God. However this may be, we have to deal with the word as it has been brought into use by the various schools. The negative prefix has been interpreted in various ways. This prefix is said to connote (1) இன்மை or <term>Abhava</term> (positive negation), (2) அன்மை <term>Sadṛisyam</term> (non-difference), and (3) மறுதலை or <term>Virodha</term> (the opposite). Both in Sanskrit and in English, the same prefix or word is used to denote the first two meanings, but in Tamil we have two different words, *illai* (இல்லை) and *allai* (அல்ல), to denote these two different meanings. If in the word ‘Advaitam,’ the first meaning is taken, it would mean that one or the other of the two, or both, would be non-existent, and it would mean only one out of the two, or neither. Śivajñānasvāmigaḷ points out that almost all the teachers of other schools, including Śaṅkara, Rāmānuja, and Mādhva, take it to mean ‘one,’ adopting the ‘Abhava’ meaning. If the <term>Sadṛisya</term> meaning is taken, it would mean non-different or non-dual. This meaning is best explained and illustrated by taking the first stanza of chapter 36 of the sacred <term>Kural</term>—entitled “How to Perceive Truth.”

->பொருள் அல்லவற்றைப் பொருளென் றுணரும்<-
->மருளானா மாணாப் பிறப்பு<-

> The delusion by which men believe that to be the truth which is not,
> That is the cause of miserable birth.

<summary>This page analyzes the etymology of the word "Advaita," arguing that it literally means "not two" rather than "one." It distinguishes between different types of negation, explaining that Śaiva Siddhānta views Advaita as "non-difference" rather than the "non-existence" of a second entity.</summary>

# ADVAITA ACCORDING TO THE ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA.

Here the word used is அல்ல (*alla*—"not that"), and the meaning is that of the two things before us—say a copy of the *Kural* and a copy of the *Śivajñāna-bōdham*—if one mistakes one book for the other, this would be delusion, <term>Mityajñānam</term> (false knowledge), <term>Avidyā</term> (ignorance). In this case, the existence of the two books is not denied. But if the words used were பொருள் இல்லவற்றை (*porul illavatrai*—"things which are not") instead of பொருள் அல்லவற்றை (*porul allavatrai*—"things which are not those"), then the meaning would be altogether altered. It would mean that, even though there is no copy of the *Kural* before us at all, we imagine there is a copy of the *Kural* before us. In the former case, the reality of the objects before us is not questioned. In the latter case, the reality of the object presented before us is denied. 

In the familiar example of the shell and the silver, both the objects and the ideas are real, and we could never have these conceptions unless both were real and distinct. The delusion arises from the fact that we mistake one thing (the shell) for the silver *which is not there*. This also occurs because of the resemblance that exists between these objects, such as the shell and silver, or the two books. If there were no shell before us, but silver presented itself, this would illustrate the <term>Abhava</term> (non-existence) meaning; however, ordinarily, no such object or idea will present itself. This same difference will be felt throughout the workings of the two systems. One system holds the world, including the body and the soul, as real; but ordinarily, we often mistake the body for the soul and attend to its needs instead of seeking the soul’s salvation. And so, too, we mistake the soul for God. If we only understood the true nature of each of these, understood the transient nature of the pleasures of the body, and gave them up for the eternal bliss of union with God, our path would be clear. Saint Tiruvalluvar follows this view and states in his second stanza:

->இருள்நீங்கி இன்பம்பயக்கு மருள்நீங்கி<-
->மாசறுகாட்சி யவர்க்கு.<-

> "Darkness departs (with which we have previously identified ourselves), and rapture springs forth to those who see the pure mystic vision, free from all delusion." In this view, no

<summary>This page explains the philosophical distinction between mistaking one real thing for another (delusion) and imagining something that does not exist (non-existence). This logic is used to argue that the world and soul are real but often misunderstood in their relation to God.</summary>
<keywords>Advaita, Śaiva Siddhānta, Kural, Śivajñāna-bōdham, Mityajñānam, Avidyā, Abhava, Tiruvalluvar, delusion, soul, reality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Advaita within the Śaiva Siddhānta tradition, moving from the definition of negation to how these concepts define the relationship between God, the soul, and the world.</meta>

### THE PERSONALITY OF GOD.

An attempt is made to deny the reality of the world, sin, ourselves, and God, but one is instead asked to distinguish one thing from the other. In the other view, there is no world, no sin, and no soul, and all these fantasies arise. But there is no reply to the question, "How?"

However, let it be premised that the Siddhānta writers take the negative prefix to mean not *Abhava* (non-existence) but *Sadrisya* (non-identity); and we will proceed to show how they develop their system.

Count Tolstoy defines religion as "a certain relation established by man between his separate personality and the endless universe or its source; and morality as the perpetual guiding of life which flows from this relation." And Siddhānta writers attempt to trace this same relation between God, man, and the world, and thereby discover the means or <term>Sādana</term> for our guidance by which we can get rid of all pain and sin. The first postulate is contained in two words in the second Sūtra of *Śivajñānabodham*.

> “அவையே தானேயாய்”
>
> “God is one with them, and *different*.”

And Saint Aruṇandi Śivāchārya adds another relation, "one-and-different." Here, then, are involved "<term>Abhēda</term>," "<term>Bhēda</term>," and "<term>Bhēdābhēda</term>" relations. But other schools postulate one or another of these relations, and the similes used are "gold and ornament" to denote the <term>Abhēda</term> relation, "darkness and light" to denote the <term>Bhēda</term> relation, and "word and meaning" to denote the <term>Bhēdābhēda</term> relation. And there can be no reconciliation between these views, and no meeting place between them. The Siddhānta postulates all these different relations, but by other similes, such as "body and soul" to denote <term>Abhēda</term>, "eye and the sun" to denote <term>Bhēda</term>, and "soul and the eye" to denote <term>Bhēdābhēda</term>, as set forth above in the stanza quoted from Saint Umāpati-Śivāchārya, and yet in a way that is not contradictory. There must, therefore, be something peculiar in this view which makes it possible to admit all these different relationships or aspects.

<summary>This page discusses the Śaiva Siddhānta view of the relationship between God, man, and the world, contrasting its definitions of negation and identity with other philosophical schools. It introduces the tripartite relation of Abhēda, Bhēda, and Bhēdābhēda, using similes like body and soul or eye and sun to explain how God can be simultaneously one with, different from, and both one-and-different from the soul.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Advaita within the Śaiva Siddhānta tradition, specifically focusing on Saint Meykaṇḍān's interpretation of the term as "non-different" rather than "one," using the analogy of vowels and consonants.</meta>

# ADVAITA ACCORDING TO THE ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA.

...and yet not be self-contradictory, appearing as one harmonious whole. This peculiar relation, which cannot be easily defined or described, is denoted by the word '*Advaita*.'

Saint Meykaṇḍān accordingly discusses this word in his first argument. "The word *Advaita* cannot mean oneness or *Ekam*; no one can think of themselves as one, as the very thought implies two things. The word simply denies the separateness of the two, *Anyanāsti*, and thus God is said to be one with the souls." That is to say, *Advaita* is *Ananya* or non-different. The relation is such that, although there is a difference in substance, no separation is possible; the word is used to emphasize this non-different character. He cites the example of a person and their body. Although these are different, the person identifies themselves with the body because of the inseparable connection between the two; therefore, they are practically one or non-different. Similarly, the soul identifies itself with God, even though God is not the soul and the soul is not God; hence, God is one, yet not identical with the soul. In the second stanza, he develops this argument, analyzing and illustrating the text '*Ekamevādvitīyam*.'

> "ஒன்றென்றது ஒன்றேகாண் ஒன்றேபதி பசுவாம்
> ஒன்றென்ற நீபாசத்தோடுளைகாண்--ஒன்றின்றால்
> அக்கரங்க ளின்றாம் அகரவுயி ரின்றேல்
> இக்கிரயத் தென்னு மிருக்கு."

> "In the Vedic text, '*Ekam*' means that there is only one, and that one is the <term>Pati</term> (Lord). You who say 'There is one' are the <term>Paśu</term>, bound up in <term>Pāśa</term>. The word '*Advaita*' means that apart from God nothing else will exist, just as we say that there would be no other letters (consonants) if the vowel 'A' did not exist."

The meaning will be clear when the illustration is fully understood. The illustration is that of a vowel and consonants—that is, <term>uyir</term> (soul) and <term>mey</term> or <term>udal</term> (body).

> "உடல்மேலுயிர்வந்து ஒன்றுவது இயல்பே."

"The vowel becoming one with the consonants is a natural union," is the *Nannūl sūtra*.

->32<-

<vocab>Advaita, Śaiva Siddhānta, Meykaṇḍān, Ekam, Anyanāsti, Ananya, Ekamevādvitīyam, Pati, Paśu, Pāśa, Nannūl sūtra, உயிர் (uyir), மெய் (mey), உடல் (udal)</vocab>

<summary>This text explains the Śaiva Siddhānta definition of Advaita as a relationship of non-difference (Ananya) rather than simple oneness, illustrated by the inseparable connection between a soul and its body or a vowel and its consonants. It emphasizes that while God and the soul are distinct in substance, they are inseparable in their shared existence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Advaita within the Śaiva Siddhānta tradition, elaborating on the relationship between the soul and God by using the analogy of the body and the mind, while drawing comparisons with contemporary Western theological thought.</meta>

# ADVAITA ACCORDING TO THE ŠAIVA SIDDHĀNTA.

And the illustration of body and mind or soul was what was stated in the first stanza.* So we have two

---

* We are pleased to include the following excerpt from Mr. Armstrong’s book, *God and the Soul*, in which he presents the same analogy.
> “But I would much rather put it in this way: the relation of the physical universe to God is, within certain limits, analogous to the relation of my body to myself. The movement of my tongue as I speak, of my eyes as I glance at my friend, of my hand as I write these words, comes from that stream of conscious energy which you may call my mind, my soul, my spirit, my will, or myself— Instantaneously the command of my unseen self flows through my seen self and modifies its attitudes, its gestures, its several and separable parts. But the intimate connection between myself and my body does not imply that I am my body or that my body is myself, the ‘Ego.’ If they are in absolute alliance, they are also in absolute antithesis. Nor, even if you went on to imagine my body as the absolute product of my own will—and its automatic and reflex actions (breathing, the circulation of the blood, the beating of the heart, the growth of the hair and nails) to be the effect of my will, and my consciousness to be perpetually engaged in conducting these processes—would you be one step nearer to identifying me, the ‘Ego’ or the self, with this body. I would still be other than the body, above and beyond it, transcending it, and of a nature belonging to a superior order, on another and higher plane. Press the analogy home, and you have a safeguard against Pantheism. The universe may be thought of as the body of God; but just as it is crude to confuse the body with the man, so it is crude to confuse the universe with God. The soul is in the body only in the sense that its energies flow through the body; a man’s soul (that is, the man) is not in the body in any physical sense. The body is its organ and its instrument.
>
> But why do we shrink from Pantheism? Not from the fear of losing the physical universe in God, but from the fear of losing our own souls in God. Pantheism only becomes deadly to vigorous religion and morality when it makes the man’s soul, the man’s self, a portion of God. Theism claims that the human soul is a free cause, a separate island of individual will in the midst of the great ocean of the Divine Will. Leave us man confronting God, not absorbed in Him, and the conditions are preserved for the ethical life of the individual, and also for the communion of the soul with God as another than itself, the very possibility of which is destroyed if a separate personality is wiped out. On this matter of the otherness of man from God, I hope to say more in a later chapter.”

<summary>This page discusses the analogy of the body and soul to explain God's relationship with the universe, emphasizing that while God acts through the universe, He remains distinct from it. This distinction is used to safeguard the philosophy against pantheism and preserve the individual's ethical and spiritual identity.</summary>

# ADVAITA ACCORDING TO THE ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA.

illustrations to describe the relation of God to the world, and these two illustrations going by the same name show that the relation between mind and body is what exists between vowels and consonants.

Viśishṭādvaita writers have undoubtedly used the illustration of mind and body, but nowhere do they discuss the nature of this relation; much less do they seem to have understood the analogy of vowels and consonants. Doctor Bain discusses this question in his book on *Mind and Body*, and we wrote on the subject in the *Siddhānta Dīpika*, Vol. II, page 13, and this is reproduced on pages 52-63 of this book.

So, whatever word we may use, the nature of this relationship is clear. If the Vedic texts postulate oneness, it is in a higher sense than what is understood in current philosophies. In this position, a higher and truer Monism is reached. We have shown how true it is that St. Meykaṇḍān stated that there is no other letter but ‘A.’ So it is that we can state, "There is nothing else but God," and "Only one, without a second." This comes as the result of the highest experience, or Jñāna, or Svānubhava, or Śivānubhava. And this is stated in the central stanza of the *Tiruvāçagam*, its *Hṛidaya sloka*:

> ‘ இன்றெனக் கருளி இருள்கடிந் துள்ளத்
> தெழுகின்ற ஞாயிறே போன்று
> நின்ற நின்தன்மை நினைப்பற நினைந்தே
> நீயலாற் பிறிது மற்றின்மை
> சென்று சென்றணுவாய்த் தேய்ந்து தேய்ந்து ஒன்றாம்
> திருப்பெருந் துறையுறை சிவனே
> ஒன்று நீயல்லை யன்றி யொன்றில்லை
> யாருன்னை யறியகிற் பாறே. ’’
>
> “This day, in Your mercy toward me, You drove away the darkness and stood in my heart like the Rising Sun.
> Of this, Your way of rising—there being nothing else but You—I thought without thought.
> I drew nearer and nearer to You, wearing away atom by atom, until I was one with You,
> O Śiva, Dweller in the great holy shrine.”

This mystical oneness is illustrated through a stanza from the Tiruvāçagam, describing the soul's union with Śiva.</summary>

# ADVAITA ACCORDING TO THE ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA.

> You are not anything in the universe; nothing is there except You.
> Who can know You?
->—(*from P. A.’s Translation*).<-

As man nears God, he wears away atom by atom, so that at the moment of union, nothing of him is left; what remains is the Presence of the Supreme One only and the feeling of His Presence, with no feeling or consciousness of himself or others. This feeling of the Presence and Bliss of God is One and <term>Advaita</term>, and there is no consciousness of such oneness or Bliss; duality will certainly arise the moment man regains consciousness. So what he is said to lose, in fact, atom by atom, is his various conscious selves.

> “ வான்கெட்டு மாருதமாய்ந்து அழல்நீர் மணகெடினும்
> தான்கெட்ட லின்றிச் சலிப்பறியாத் தன்மையனுக்கு
> ஊன்கெட்டு உயிர்கெட்டு உணர்வுகெட்டு என் உள்ளமும்போய்
> நான்கெட்ட வாபாடித் தெள்ளேணங் கொட்டாமோ.”
> 
> “ Though Ether, Wind, Fire, Water, and Earth should fail,
> His Constant Being does not fail and knows no weariness!
> In Him, my body, soul, thought, and mind were merged (lost);
> How all of myself was lost, let us sing and beat <term>Tellēnam</term>.”
->(*from Rev. Doctor G. U. POPE’s Translation*).<-

His bodily consciousness, his life-consciousness, and his mental consciousness—all these alone constitute his individuality, the feeling of "I" and "mine." This "I-ness," "நான்," is what must be gotten rid of. So when this "I-ness" or individuality is lost, "how I was lost" (நான் கெட்டவா); he becomes <term>Śivam</term> or God, "how I was lost and became Śivam" (நான் கெட்டு சிவமானவா). What perishes, of course, is the soul's individuality or the consciousness of "I-ness" that induces duality; but what subsists even in <term>Mōksha</term> is the soul’s personality, which has <term>Svānubhava</term> or <term>Śivānubhava</term>, identifying itself with God.

The soul in union with God becomes a pure object (God), as it were, which is the true <term>Monism</term> of Science. Hence, Saint <term>Meykaṇḍān</term> states this paradox (xi. 2. c.): “When becoming one with God, if the soul perished, there would be nothing to unite with God, as it has perished. If it did not perish,

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the soul's union with God, specifically how the soul loses its individual "I-ness" (individuality) while maintaining its substance or personality to experience divine bliss, illustrated through the analogies of salt in water and a river entering the sea.</meta>

# ADVAITA ACCORDING TO THE ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA.

It cannot become one with God. Just like salt dissolved in water, the soul, after losing its <term>mala</term>, unites itself with His feet and becomes the servant of God (loses its "I-ness" or individuality). Then it will have no darkness (as separation). The salt in its crystalline condition constitutes its individuality. In that condition, it is distinguished from water. But after it is dissolved in water, what is lost is its individual character and not itself, its substance, or its personality.

The following sentence from a science textbook will show how exact our language is: "When a river enters the sea, it soon loses its *individuality*; it becomes *merged* with the body of the ocean when it loses its current, and when, therefore, it has no power to keep in suspension the sediment which it had brought down from the higher lands." If re-read as follows, its application will become clear: "When the soul loses its individuality (feeling of "I-ness," <term>Ahaṅkāram</term> or <term>Āṇavam</term>), it becomes merged in God when it loses its <term>Karma</term>, and when, therefore, it has no power to keep in suspension its <term>mala</term> with which it has been associated from the beginning." And this is the exact figure and language used by St. <term>Meykaṇḍān</term> in viii. 4. A. This losing of self is the real sacrifice brought about by love. It is this sacrifice, <term>பலி</term>, we are asked to make as we enter the Temple, and the moment we make it, our <term>பசுத்துவம்</term> (<term>Paśutvam</term>) will leave us, and we will become the *Naṇḍi*, the Blissful <term>Śivam</term>.

That the <term>Siddhānta</term> marks the highest standard of monistic truth is what is also brought out by St. <term>Tirumūlar</term> in his famous line "<term>தானான வேதாந்தம் தான் என்னுஞ் சித்தாந்தம்</term>." <term>Vēdānta</term> postulates "<term>Aham Brahmāsmi</term>," "I am <term>Brahman</term>"; <term>Siddhānta</term> postulates "<term>Tat</term> (one) alone." That is to say that the <term>Siddhānta</term> appeals fully and finally to only one experience—the Bliss of God and one alone—whereas the <term>Vēdānta</term> refers to the <term>Sōham</term> paths by which this experience is gained. And anyone can perceive that the <term>Sōham</term> experience is a conscious one and a dual one, or <term>Dvaita</term>. In this sense, <term>Siddhānta</term> is <term>Advaita</term> and <term>Vēdānta</term> is <term>Dvaita</term>. And what are considered as the

<summary>The author explains the Śaiva Siddhānta concept of union with God using the analogies of salt dissolving in water and a river merging into the sea, where individuality is lost but the soul's essence remains. He contrasts the experiential bliss of Siddhānta (Advaita) with the conscious paths of Vēdānta (Dvaita).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's union with God, transitioning from the analogy of salt in water to the philosophical interpretation of Advaita. It addresses how the Śaiva Siddhānta school interprets the "great sayings" of the Vedānta in a way that preserves both the distinction and the union between the soul and the Divine.</meta>

# ADVAITA ACCORDING TO THE ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA.

The strongholds of Vedānta held by the followers of Śankara are easily interpreted by the Siddhāntis. As pointed out by Śivajñāna Yōgi, the question did not simply concern whether <term>padārthas</term> were one or two. It arose in connection with the famous <term>Mahāvākya</term> texts, such as "Aham Brahmāsmi," "Tattvamasi," and others. He says:—

மற்றென்னையோ அத்துவிதமென்னும் சொற்குப் பொருளெனிற் சித்தாந்த சைவருரைக்குமாறு காட்டுதும், 'அது நீயாகின்றாய், எ-ம், அது நானாகின்றேன், எ-ம், 'அதுவிது வாகின்றது,' எ-ம், மூவிடம்பற்றி நிகழும் தத்துவமசி முதலிய மகாவாக்கியங்களைக் கேட்டவழி அதுவென்பது ஒருபொருள், நீயென்பது ஒருபொருளாகலின், ஒருபொருள் மற்றொரு பொருளாமாறியங்ஙனம் என்னும் ஐய நீக்குதற்கு எழுந்ததாகலின் அதுவிது வாதற் கேதுவாய், அவ்விரண்டிற்கு முளதாகிய சம்பந்த விசேட முணர்த்துவதே யத்துவித மென்னும் சொற்குப்பொருளென்றுணர்ந்துகொள்க.

> "If you ask what the meaning of the word *Advaitam* is, I will show how Śaiva Siddhāntis explain it. Upon hearing the great texts called <term>Mahāvākya</term>, such as <term>Tatvamasi</term>, which are used in the three grammatical persons, we see that these sentences speak of 'that' as one substance and 'Thou' as another, and we inquire how one can become the other. The answer is provided to remove this doubt by stating *how one can become the other and what relation exists between these two*; the word <term>Advaitam</term> is used to express this peculiar relation."

The word does not mean "one" or the non-existence of two or more entities; rather, it is used to express the unique relation that exists between two distinct things that can become one. We called attention to this meaning long ago in our very first work, even before we had any chance of seeing this brilliant exposition by Śivajñāna Yōgi. We observed (see <term>Śivajñānabōdham</term>, p. 17):

> "Although an identity is perceived in all these cases, a difference in substance is also felt. It is this *relation* which could not be easily expressed in words but which may perhaps be conceived, and which is seen as two (<term>Dvaitam</term>) and at the same time as not two (<term>Advaitam</term>); it is this relation which is called *Advaitam*, 'a unity in duality,' and the philosophy which proposes it is the Advaita philosophy."

<summary>This page defines the Śaiva Siddhānta interpretation of "Advaita" not as numerical oneness, but as a specific relation of "unity in duality" between God and the soul. It uses the teachings of Śivajñāna Yōgi to explain how the great Vedāntic texts (Mahāvākyas) describe this unique connection.</summary>

### ADVAITA ACCORDING TO THE ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA. 255

Of all the mass of Vedic and theosophic literature that has come into existence during the last two or three decades, none equal the writings of Professor Kunte for real insight into the nature of Hindu philosophy and critical acumen. His summary, added at the end of the first <term>pāda</term> of the first <term>Adhyāya</term> of his translation of the <term>Brahma Sūtras</term>, is most beautiful and original. Wonderful as it may seem, both <term>Śivajñāna Yōgi</term> and Kunte propound exactly the same questions and give the same answer. He shows that there are texts in the <term>Upanishats</term> which support both the dualistic and monistic views. The mainstay of the monists are the <term>*Mahā Vākya*</term> texts, and these texts are the great stumbling block in the path of dualists. He shows that their interpretation cannot withstand examination because the texts evidently do not admit of it, and all that they say is simply beside the mark.

> "What is to be done? There are doubtless a few texts in the <term>Vēda</term> which support <term>Pantheistic</term> views. Most, however, support <term>Theistic</term> principles. But as long as <term>Pantheistic</term> texts are not explained, the proposition that the <term>Vedas</term> do not teach <term>Pantheism</term> cannot be accepted. Again, the adjustment and the interpretation proposed by the <term>Theists</term> cannot be accepted because they are far-fetched and forced. But we do not see how the few pantheistic texts stand in the way of <term>Theism</term>, because we believe that even if they are interpreted as the <term>Pantheists</term> do, they still support <term>Theism</term>. How can this be?"

He proceeds to show how this can only be understood in the light of <term>Yōga</term>. After giving examples of the various forms of <term>Bhakti</term> (<term>Charya</term> and <term>Kriya</term>), he says: "But there is a special feature of such adoration—a feature not included in any of these. It is the ecstatic condition of the spirit, a condition which can neither be explained nor understood without an illustration. Let the reader realize the love a mother has for her child. A mother or her child sometimes experiences a state of mind, an indescribable state. Only what either of them expresses can convey an idea of their feelings when they are in the ecstatic condition. The mother directly addresses the child thus: 'Oh,

"My piece of gold, oh, my soul, oh, my life, can I eat you up?" 'தின்பன்கடிப்பன் திருத்துவன்தானே.' Under these circumstances, the mother forgets that her body is distinct from that of her child, who experiences the same feeling. Such an identity is the form of the ecstatic condition of the mind. This is a special feature of adoration. The <term>Yōgis</term> feel this sort of ecstatic identity. Therefore, in the <term>Vēda</term> and in the <term>Upanishats</term>, the <term>Pantheistic</term> doctrine of the identity of the human spirit and the Supreme Spirit, *if expressed, is expressed in this way*. Furthermore, the <term>Brahma Sūtra</term> of <term>Bādarāyaṇa</term> does not teach it." And he explains further below: "The characteristic feature of the Indian <term>Vēdāṇṭa</term> is its recognition of spirit-power, as it is explained in the <term>Yōga Sūtras</term> which systematically lay down the following propositions: That the Supreme Spirit or God is *related* to the human spirit; that the human spirit has very great *potential powers*; and that if certain methods of living are adopted, it can summon its powers and become *actually* able to *know the past and the future*; and that the spirit, once freed from the flesh, is ultimately absorbed *in one sense* into the Supreme Spirit. The <term>Yōga</term> system is properly the backbone of the <term>Vēdānta</term>."

And we had pointed out in another place, *Personality of God* (pp. 223—243), that the <term>Yōga Pāda</term> is not merely the backbone of <term>Vēdāṇṭa</term>, but it is <term>Vēdānta</term> itself.

It is not well understood that the word <term>Upanishat</term> really means the same thing as '<term>Yōga</term>.' <term>Yōga</term> means the <term>Sādana</term> required for bringing the Soul and God into Union; and the <term>Upanishat</term> is also the teaching of the <term>Sādana</term> whereby man comes nearer and nearer to God by destroying the bonds that bind him. The root meaning (upa = near, ni = completely, sad = to perish) is captured perfectly in the famous line in <term>Tiruvāçagam</term> quoted above. "The House of God," 7th verse, சென்று சென்று அணுவாய்த் தேய்ந்துதேய்ந்து ஒன்றாம், "*nearer and nearer to Thee I drew, wearing away atom by atom, till I was one with Thee.*" And in the passage in (<term>Chāndogya Upanishad</term>, I. I. 10) and in several others, the word <term>Upanishat</term> is used as a synonym for <term>Yoga</term>. And this derivation

This really explains the scope of an <term>Upanishat</term>, a misunderstanding of which has led to no end of confusion. The <term>Siddhāṇti</term> takes the <term>Upanishat</term> as the textbook of the <term>Yōga Pāda</term> or School.

The higher stage or <term>Pāda</term> being the <term>Jñāna Pāda</term>, the words <term>Upanishat</term>, <term>Vedānta</term>, <term>Yōga</term>, <term>Saha-Mārga</term> or <term>Sōhamārga</term> or <term>Hamsa-Mārga</term> are all synonymous; and as <term>Vedānta</term> strictly means <term>Yōga</term>, the words <term>Vedānta</term> and <term>Siddhāṇta</term> are contrasted, <term>Siddhāṇta</term> meaning the <term>Jñāna-Mārga</term> or <term>Pāda</term>, though it embraces all the remaining <term>Pādas</term>: <term>Charya</term>, <term>Kriya</term>, and <term>Yōga</term>. The practice involved in the <term>Mahāvākya</term> texts is this <term>Sōham Bhāvana</term> or <term>Śivōham Bhāvana</term>, and when this practice is matured, the soul stands in complete allegiance to the Supreme One, renouncing all idea of self and self-action; then can the soul say: “I am all the world,” யானே யுலகென்பனின்று. (<term>Śivajñānabödham</term>, 2-1-4). “In me everything originated, in me everything is established, in me everything merges. That secondless-Brahman am I. (<term>Kaivalya Upanishad</term> 21).”

As Professor <term>Kunte</term> speaks of the *potential power of man* by calling it out so he can become one with God; <term>Śivajñāna Yōgi</term> dwells at great length, and very frequently, on this special characteristic or power of man by which man can be said to become God; and this power is the power of the soul to *become that to which it is united*, அதுவதுவாதல், in the language of St. <term>Meykaṇḍān</term>, or சார்ந்ததன் வண்ணமாதல் and யாதொன்றுபற்றின் அதன் இயல்பாய் நிற்றல் in the language of St. <term>Tāyumānavar</term>, and this power is likened to that of a crystal or mirror.

Says Professor <term>Henry Drummond</term>:—

> “All men are mirrors—that is the first law on which this formula (of sanctification or corruption) is based. One of the aptest descriptions of a human being is that he is a mirror.”

This illustration is to be originally found in the <term>Upanishats</term> and <term>Gītā</term>.

> “As a metal disk (mirror) tarnished by dust shines bright again after it has been cleansed, so the one incarnate person is satisfied and free from grief after he has seen his own real nature. And when by his own real nature, he sees as by a lamp, the real nature of the <term>Brahman</term>, then having become the unborn eternal God who transcends all <term>tattvas</term>,

he is freed from all <term>pāśa</term> <note>bondage</note>." (Śvetāśvatara Upanishad ii. 14, 15). "From meditating on Him (<term>abhidhyānāth</term>), from joining Him (<term>yojanāth</term>), from becoming one with Him (<term>tatvabhāvāt</term>), there is further cessation of all <term>Māyā</term> in the end." (Śvetāśvatara Upanishad i. 10.) "As a flame is enveloped by smoke, as a mirror by dust, as an embryo is wrapped by the womb, so this (soul) is enveloped by it (desire)." (<term>Gītā</term> iii. 3.)

And St. <term>Meykaṇḍān</term> has this stanza (viii. 3. a.) <gloss>பன்னிறமே</gloss>. The principle of it receives its exposition in the <term>Sāṅkhya</term> and in the <term>Yōga Sūtras</term> by means of this illustration of mirrors and colors.

> "Though it (the soul) is unassociated, there is still a staining (by reflection) through non-discrimination; [for there is not a real stain in that which is unassociated (with color or anything else), yet there is, as it were, a stain; hence the stain is treated simply as a reflection by those who discriminate the stain from the soul to which it delusively seems to belong].
> 
> "As is the case with the hibiscus and the crystal, there is not a stain, but a fancy that there is such." *Sāṅkhya aphorism vi. 27-28—Garbe's Translation.*

In the words of Professor Max Müller, this is how the subject is treated in the <term>Yōga Sūtras</term>: "Now if we ask what is the result of all this, we are told in Sūtra 41 that a man who has put an end to all the motions and emotions of his mind obtains, with regard to all objects of his senses, a conformation grounded on them, or steadiness and consubstantiation—the idea being that the mind is modified or changed by the objects perceived, '<gloss>அது அதுவாதல்</gloss>' (i. 41). As a crystal, when placed near a red flower, becomes really red to our eyes, in the same way, the mind is tinged by the objects perceived" (*Six Systems*, p. 453).

This principle of the mind identifying itself with the objects perceived is stated in the following passages of the <term>Upanishad</term> also.

> "Now a man is like this or that; according to how he acts and according to how he behaves, so will he be: a man of good acts will

> “He who desires the <term>Ātman</term>, being <term>Brahman</term>, goes to <term>Brahman</term>. That <term>Ātman</term> is indeed <term>Brahman</term>.” (<term>Brihat</term> IV, iv. 5. 6.)

Similar passages are found in the <term>Mahābhārata</term> and the familiar statement of it in Sanskrit is:
‘*Yat Bhāvam tat Bhavati*.’
Herbert Spencer calls this union one of absolute identity. And this is almost the language used by St. <term>Meykaṇḍān</term>:
‘ அது அது ஆதல் ’.

As the <term>Upanishad</term> writers, <term>Sāṅkhyans</term>, <term>Yōgins</term>, and <term>Siddhāntins</term> state this principle and base their scheme of salvation upon it, so also does Professor Henry Drummond in his remarkable address entitled “The Changed Life,” based on the text from St. Paul.

> “We all, with unveiled face, reflecting, as a mirror, the glory of the Lord, are transformed into the same image, from glory to glory, even as from the Lord the Spirit.” He paraphrases the sentence as follows: “We, all reflecting, as a mirror, the character of Christ, are transformed into the same image from character to character—from a poor character to a better one, from a better one to one a little better still, from that to one still more complete, until by slow degrees the perfect image is attained. Here the solution of the problem of sanctification is compressed into a sentence, ‘reflect the character of Christ, and you will become like Christ.’ Or, as we would say, reflect the image of God in yourself, and you will become God-like, or God.

But how is the poor character to be made better and better, or the reflecting image clearer and clearer? It is by cleansing the mirror (the soul) of more and more dirt, and bringing it more and more in line with the effulgent light, that this can be achieved;

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the soul as a mirror, drawing parallels between Śaiva Siddhānta and the theological writings of Professor Henry Drummond regarding how the soul reflects and assimilates its environment.</meta>

and when the mirror is absolutely perfect and nearest, the light shines brightest and so overpowers the mirror that the mirror is lost to view, and the Glory and Light of the Lord are felt. For, as the learned Professor truly observes, “What you are conscious of is ‘the glory of the Lord.’ And what the world is conscious of, if the result is a true one, is also the glory of the Lord. In looking at a mirror, one does not see the mirror or think of it, but only of what it reflects. For a mirror never calls attention to itself—except when there are flaws in it.” These flaws are the colors of the <term>Siddhānti</term> who compares them to the <term>māyā</term> or body. In union with the body, it is the body alone that is perceived, and not the <term>mirror-like soul</term>. In union with God, the Glory and Light alone are perceived and not the <term>mirror-like soul</term> either; and the Professor declares, “All men are mirrors—that is the first law on which this formula (of <term>sanctification</term> or corruption) is based. One of the aptest descriptions of a human being is that he is a mirror,” and we must urge our readers to go through the whole pamphlet to note how beautifully he develops this parallel.

He notes the second principle which governs this process, namely, the <term>law of assimilation</term> or <term>identification</term>. “This <term>law of assimilation</term> is the second, and by far the most impressive truth which underlies the formula of <term>sanctification</term>—the truth that men are not only mirrors, but that these mirrors, so far from being mere reflectors of the fleeting things they see, transfer into their own inmost substance and hold in permanent preservation the things that they reflect. No one can know how the soul can hold these things. No one knows how the miracle is done. No phenomenon in nature, no process in chemistry, no chapter in necromancy can even help us begin to understand this amazing operation. For think of it, the past is not only focused there in a man’s soul—it is there. How could it be reflected from there if it were not there? All things he has ever seen, known, felt, or believed of the surrounding world are now within him, have become part of him, and in part are him—he has been changed into their image.”

These two principles in fact underlie our <term>Maṇtra</term> and <term>Taṇtra</term>, our <term>Upāsana</term> and <term>Sādana</term>, <term>Bhāvana</term>, and <term>Yōga</term>; and our books cite the case of the snake-charmer chanting the <term>Garuḍa Maṇtra</term> to illustrate this second principle of assimilation or identification. The Professor provides examples from Darwin of how, in the working out of this principle of association and assimilation or identity in human and animal evolution, persons constantly associated with pigs develop pig-like faces, and those associated with horses, horse-like faces. In the case of a husband and wife who have been perfectly loving, it has been found to result in a complete assimilation of their features. Such is the power of the human mind, which is both a disadvantage and an advantage; it can lower itself to the very depths of the beast, or it can rise to the very height of Godhood. This law is referred to in our textbooks as the law of ‘<term>Garuḍadhyānam</term>.’ The author of the book “Spiritual Law in the Natural World” (Purdy Publishing Company, Chicago) observes that all “who have studied the cause of all things have become so at one with it as to possess causative power, for it is an invariable rule that *we become like what we study or are closely associated with.* We become so similar to people with whom we live constantly that the expression of the face and the sound of the voice grow similar, and even features grow alike. Sometimes a child will look more like its nurse than its mother.” And the entire book is an exposition of this principle, and it presents as a <term>Sādana</term> for spiritual elevation that a person should firmly believe there is no world, no untruth, no sin, no sickness, and no death, and that he is a child of God; that there is only ‘Truth, Power, Love, and Presence' in this universe and nothing but this; that he is the reflection of God, the image and likeness of God, and then he can truly conquer sickness and death and become truly the son of God. This is exactly the <term>Sōhambhāvana</term> or <term>Śivōhambhāvana</term>. And the following verse by **St. Aruṇandi Śivāchārya** sums up the entire teaching:—

> “ கண்ட விவையல்லே னானென்ற கன்று காணக்
>      கழிபரமு நானல்லேனெனக் கருதிச் சிந்தை
> தொண்டினொடு முளத்தவன் றன்னின்ற கலப்பாலே
>      சோகமெனப் பாவிக்கத் தோன்றுவன் வேறின்றி

> “Say, ‘I am not the world, and I am separate from it.’ Say also, ‘I am not the Unknowable Supreme One.’ Then unite with Him indissolubly by loving Him in all humility, and practice <term>Sōham</term> (‘I am He’). Then He will appear to you as yourself. Your <term>mala</term> will all cease, just as poison is removed by <term>Garuḍadhyāna</term>, and you will become pure. Thus, the ancient <term>Vedas</term> teach us to practice this <term>mantra</term>: ‘<term>Aham Brahmāsmi</term>,’ ‘I am <term>Brahman</term>’.”

As this correct knowledge of the non-difference and difference between ourselves and God and the universe is essential for our salvation, <term>Śrīkaṇṭha</term> discusses these questions in his <term>Bhāshya</term> on the <term>Sūtras</term> (II. i. 21-23). We quote these passages in full, where he cites and beautifully reconciles the numerous <term>Bhedā Śrutis</term> with the <term>Mahāvākya</term> texts:

> “The <term>Sūtrakāra</term> raises and refutes an objection to the preceding theory:
> 
> (The <term>Jīva</term>) being mentioned as one with the other, there follows an incongruity such as the neglect of what is good (II. i. 21).
> 
> (Objection): Because in the words ‘That thou art’ and ‘this <term>Ātman</term> is <term>Brahman</term>,’ the <term>Jīva</term>—the effect—is mentioned as being one with <term>Brahman</term>, for it has been shown that they are not distinct from one another. In that case, it would follow that the all-knowing and all-pervading <term>Paramēśvara</term> dissolves the universe for His own <note>benefit</note> and creates it for His own <note>harm</note>. Then it may be asked: how is it that <term>Īśvara</term>—who is all-knowing, possesses an unfailing will, and knows that the pain of the <term>Jīva</term> (who is none other than Himself) is His own pain—engages Himself in the creation of the universe? Since this creation leads to <term>Saṃsāra</term>, which is an evil, why does He not abstain from creation for His own good? Accordingly, once it is proved that the <term>Jīva</term> and <term>Paramēśvara</term> are one, this incongruity follows: that <term>Paramēśvara</term>, though all-knowing, is guilty of a lack of sense because He abstains from what is good for Himself and engages in what leads to His own harm. Therefore, it does not stand to reason that the <term>Jīva</term> and <term>Īśvara</term>—the cause and the effect—are one.”

<summary>This page discusses the practice of Sōham meditation to achieve purity and addresses a logical objection in Śrīkaṇṭha's commentary: if God and the soul are one, God would be acting against His own interest by subjecting Himself to the sufferings of the universe.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical defense of the Śaiva Siddhānta position on Advaita, specifically addressing the distinction between the cause (Brahman/Śiva) and the effect (the universe/Jīva) while maintaining their inherent non-separation.</meta>
