(Answer): In reply, we say the following:
But the Cause is superior, because of the mention of a distinction. (II. i. 22.)
Though the cause and effect are one, the cause is declared in the <term>Śruti</term> to be superior to the effect—to the sentient and insentient universe—in such passages as the following:
> “Superior to the universe is <term>Rudra</term>, the Mighty Sage.”
So, a distinction is also made between <term>Jīva</term> and <term>Paramēśvara</term> in the following passages:
> “But he who controls both—<term>Vidyā</term> and <term>Avidyā</term>—is another.”
> “The one God rules the perishable (<term>Pradhāna</term>) and <term>Ātman</term>.”
> “Thinking that the <term>Ātman</term> is different from the Mover (the Lord).”
> “Two birds, inseparable friends, cling to the same tree.”
> “Two Brahmans ought to be known, the superior and the inferior.”
> “There are two, one knowing, the other not knowing: both unborn; one strong and the other weak.”
> “He is the eternal among eternals, the sentient among the sentient.”
> “Having entered within, He is the ruler of the creatures.”
> “Know then <term>Prakṛiti</term> is <term>Māyā</term>, and the great Lord the <term>Māyin</term>.”
> “From that, the <term>Māyin</term> sends forth all this; in that, the other is bound up through that <term>Māyā</term>.”
> “When he sees the other, the Lord is contented... then his grief passes away.”
> “He is the master of nature and of man, the Lord of the three qualities.”
> “Of these creatures, <term>paśus</term>, the <term>Paśupati</term> is the Lord.”
Therefore, <term>Brahman</term>, otherwise called <term>Śiva</term>, is entirely superior to the universe.

(Objection): By establishing non-duality in II. i. 15, and duality in II. i. 22, you have only proved the duality and non-duality of <term>Brahman</term> and the universe.

(Answer): No: we do not establish that sort of <term>Viśiṣṭādvaita</term> which takes the form of duality and non-duality. We are not the advocates of an absolute distinction between <term>Brahman</term> and the universe, such as that between a pot and a cloth, because that would oppose the <term>Śruti</term> declaring that they are not entirely distinct from each other. Neither are we the advocates of an absolute identity, like that of mother-of-pearl and silver where one of them is illusory; for that is opposed to the <term>Śruti</term> which points to a difference in the inherent attributes of <term>Brahman</term> and the universe. Nor do we hold to duality and non-duality together, which is opposed to the nature of things. On the other hand, we maintain that the unity of <term>Brahman</term>—as the cause and—

<summary>This page argues that while Brahman and the universe share a unity as cause and effect, Brahman remains superior and distinct in its attributes. It rejects absolute dualism, absolute monism (illusionism), and the simple combination of the two, seeking a middle path in Śaiva Siddhānta.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that Brahman (Śiva) is superior to the universe while remaining inseparable from it, utilizing the analogies of body and soul or cause and effect to explain this non-dual relationship.</meta>

<page-num>264</page-num>

the effect—is like that of the body and of the embodied, or like that of the substance and its attribute. By the unity of <term>Brahman</term> and the universe, we mean their inseparability, like that of clay and the pot as cause and effect, or like that of the substance and its attribute. A pot, indeed, is not seen apart from clay, nor is the blue lotus seen apart from the color blue. Similarly, apart from <term>Brahman</term>, no potentiality of the universe can exist; nor is <term>Brahman</term> ever known apart from something else; the former must ever be conditioned by the latter, and this latter is naturally one with the former.

Therefore, <term>Brahman</term>, who is in no way separable from the universe, is said to be one with the other. And there is a natural distinction between the two, so that the Supreme <term>Brahman</term> is ever higher than the universe. As to their distinction as the cause and the effect, it has been already explained in II. i. 9. Therefore, this theory is quite consistent with the <term>Śrutis</term> declaring distinction as well as non-distinction.

And as in the case of stone, etc., it is incongruous (II. i. 23).

(Objection): Under all conditions, <term>Jiva</term> and <term>Iśvara</term> are one, because of the <term>Śrutis</term> declaring non-duality.

(Answer): No, because of an incongruity. <term>Jiva</term> and <term>Iśvara</term> cannot be identical because, like the insentient stone, timber, grass, etc., the <term>Jiva</term> also is—on account of ignorance, etc.—said to belong to quite a distinct class from the <term>Iśvara</term>, who is possessed of such attributes as omniscience. Therefore <term>Iśvara</term> is a distinct entity from <term>Jiva</term>. Thus even the <term>Jiva</term>, sentient as he is, cannot be identical with <term>Iśvara</term> owing to this difference: that the latter is superior. Much less can the insentient existence, which is essentially different, be identical with <term>Iśvara</term>. From all points of view, by <term>Śruti</term>, <term>Smriti</term>, and Reasoning, we see that the omniscient and omnipotent <term>Paramêśvara</term> is quite superior to the whole universe, sentient and insentient, though, as His own emanation, it is not altogether distinct from Him.

And he brings out the non-difference further by means of the simile of soul and body in his commentary on I. ii. 1.

> “All this is <term>Brahman</term>, as beginning, and breathing in Him; and therefore let a man meditate on Him.”

This passage may be explained as follows: The origin, existence, and end of all this depends on <term>Brahman</term>. All this, both the sentient and the insentient existence, is truly <term>Brahman</term>, and therefore let a man meditate on <term>Brahman</term> with a tranquil mind. Just as water bubbles which have their origin, existence, and end in the ocean are found to be only forms of that ocean, so, too, that which depends for its origin, etc., on <term>Brahman</term> associated with <term>Śakti</term> must be made up of <term>Brahman</term> and nothing

It specifically refutes the absolute identity of the individual soul (Jiva) and God (Isvara), citing the soul's ignorance as proof of its subordinate status.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that Brahman (Śiva) is inseparable from the universe, asserting that nothing exists distinct from Him and that the world is a manifestation of His inherent energy (Śakti).</meta>

else. Nothing distinct from Him is ever perceived. Accordingly, in the <term>Atharvaširas</term>, it has been declared by <term>Isāna</term> as follows:

> “Alone I was at first, (alone) I am and shall be; there is no one else distinct from Me.”

And then it was declared by Him, in the words “I am <term>Brahman</term>,” that the whole universe is His own form. And in the words “He entered the more hidden from (or than) the hidden one,” etc., His entering into the universe is given as the reason for the whole universe being His own form. Thus, since this universe has no origin, existence, or end outside of <term>Brahman</term>, it is not a completely distinct thing from <term>Brahman</term>. Accordingly, the learned say:—

“His <term>Šaktis</term> or energies form the whole world, and the <term>Mahēša</term> or the Great Lord is the energetic one (<term>Šaktimān</term>). Energy can never exist distinct from the energetic. The unity of these two is eternal, like that of fire and heat, inasmuch as inseparability always exists between energy and the energetic. Therefore, the supreme energy belongs to the Supreme <term>Ātman</term>, since the two are related to each other as substance and attribute. The energy of heat is not conceived to be distinct from fire,” and so on. The <term>Vāyu-Samhita</term> also says:

> “From <term>Šakti</term> down to the earth, (the whole world) is born of the principle of <term>Šiva</term>; by Him alone it is pervaded, just as a jar, etc., is pervaded by clay. His multifaceted Supreme <term>Šakti</term>, whose form is knowledge and bliss, appears as one and many, like the light of the sun.”

The following passages of the <term>Šruti</term> speak of <term>Para-Brahman</term> as possessing infinite powers of creating, ruling, and maintaining the world, all inherent in Him:

> “His supreme <term>Šakti</term> is spoken of as manifold, inherent, and endowed with the activity of knowledge and life.”

> “Truly, <term>Rudra</term> is one—they were not for a second—who rules these worlds with the powers of ruling.”

In short, on the authority of <term>Śruti</term>, <term>Smriti</term>, <term>Itihāsa</term>, <term>Purāṇa</term>—and the sayings of the learned—the Supreme <term>Śakti</term> is inherent in the nature of <term>Śiva</term>, the Supreme <term>Brahman</term>, and constitutes His own essential form and quality. This <term>Śakti's</term> manifold manifestation is this whole universe of *chit* and *achit*, and Her being is composed of Supreme Existence, Intelligence, and Bliss, unlimited by space and time. Apart from <term>Śakti</term>, He cannot be the Omniscient, the Omnipotent, the cause of all, the all-controlling, the all-adorable, the all-gracious, the means of attaining all aspirations, and the Omnipresent; and, moreover, such grand designations as ‘*Maheśvara*,’ the Supreme Lord, ‘*Mahādeva*,’ the Supreme Deity, and ‘*Rudra*,’ the expeller of pain, cannot

<sig>34</sig>

<summary>This page argues that the universe is not distinct from Brahman (Śiva) but is a manifestation of His inherent energy or Śakti. apply to Him. Thus, it is <term>Brahman</term> whose body is the whole sentient and insentient universe, and who is denoted by all words. Just as the word ‘blue’ denotes not only the blue color, but also the lotus which is blue in color, so does the word ‘universe’ also denote <term>Brahman</term>. Therefore, such passages as “All is truly <term>Rudra</term>” teach that <term>Brahman</term> is denoted by all words. Accordingly, the passage “All this is truly <term>Brahman</term>” refers to <term>Brahman</term>, whose body is the entire sentient and insentient universe. Because the universe is thus a form of <term>Brahman</term> and is therefore not an object of hatred and so on, let everyone be peaceful at heart and worship <term>Brahman</term>. This doctrine is clearly expounded even in the <term>purāṇic</term> texts, such as the following:

> “The body of the God of gods is this universe, both moving and unmoving. This, the souls (<term>Jivas</term> or <term>Paśus</term>) do not know, owing to their mighty bondage. They say consciousness is <term>Vidyā</term>, and the lack of consciousness is <term>Avidyā</term>. The whole universe of <term>Vidyā</term> and <term>Avidyā</term> is undoubtedly the body of the Lord, the Father of all; for the whole universe is subject to Him. The word ‘<term>sat</term>’ is used by the wise to denote the real and the good, and ‘<term>asat</term>’ is used by Vedic teachers to denote the opposite. The whole universe of the <term>sat</term> and the <term>asat</term> is the body of Him who is on High. Just as the branches of a tree are nourished by the watering of its roots, so is the universe—which is His body—nourished by the worship of <term>Śiva</term>. The Self (<term>Ātman</term>) is the eighth body of <term>Śiva</term>, the Supreme Lord (<term>Paramēśvara</term>) who pervades all other bodies. Therefore, since the whole universe is ensouled by <term>Śiva</term>, if any embodied being whatsoever is subjected to harm, it will be entirely repugnant to the eight-bodied Lord; of this there is no doubt. Doing good to all, showing kindness to all, and providing shelter to all—this, they hold, is the worship of <term>Śiva</term>.” And so on.

Since <term>Brahman</term> is all-formed, it is only right to say “all is <term>Brahman</term>” and “let everyone be peaceful and worship <term>Brahman</term>.” Therefore, it is <term>Brahman</term>, who in the opening passage is stated to be the object of worship, who is also spoken of as <term>manōmaya</term> (partaking of the nature of the mind or <term>manas</term>), and so on. Nor should it be supposed that partaking of the nature of the mind is a characteristic mark of a worldly soul (<term>samsārin</term>); for <term>Brahman</term> may limit Himself by assuming a shape that can serve as an object of worship.

The slight difference between the way the subject is treated by <term>Śrīkanṭha</term> and by Saint <term>Meykaṇḍān</term> must be noted. <term>Śrīkanṭha</term>, following <term>Bādarāyaṇā</term>, calls this relation one of cause and effect and describes it as a peculiar <term>Apūrva pariṇāma</term> (unique transformation), in which the efficient cause is not affected by the change, as in

<summary>This page explains the Śaiva Siddhānta view that the universe is the body of Brahman (Śiva), meaning that all things are forms of the Divine. It emphasizes that since everything is part of Śiva's body, worshipping Him requires practicing kindness and doing good toward all living beings.</summary>

...an ordinary case of causation; and yet his illustration of soul and body would seem to bring it, if not entirely, within the realm of causation. <term>Śivajñāna Yōgi</term> distinguishes between two kinds of *<term>Tādātmyam</term>* (identity). In the first kind, one thing appears as two, such as <term>Guṇi</term> (the possessor of attributes) and <term>Guṇa</term> (the attribute), or substance and attribute. Again, two distinct things might be so connected as to be regarded as one. This is also called *<term>Tādātmyam</term>*. This latter relation is what is known as *<term>Advaita</term>*, while the former relation is simply known as *<term>Tādātmyam</term>*. <term>Śrīkaṇṭha</term> would seem to align himself with the first kind of *<term>Tādātmyam</term>* described.

Professor <term>Max Muller</term> does not seem to understand the importance of the distinction between <term>Kapila</term>'s <term>Sāṅkhya</term> and <term>Patañjali</term>'s <term>Yōga</term>, which is also called <term>Śeshvara Sāṅkhya</term>. He says, in his 'Six Systems of Philosophy,' that the <term>Sūtra</term> "Devotion to God" is not very important and is only one of the various means of obtaining <term>Kaivalya</term> (liberation). We have discussed at length the difference between the <term>Nirvāṇa</term> postulated by <term>Buddha</term> and that of the <term>Siddhāṇti</term> in our notes to <term>Śivajñāna Siddhiyār</term>, *Parapaksham*, under the section on Buddhism, and also in our paper on the 'Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil.' Buddhism postulated <term>Pāśatchaya</term>—freedom from desire and pain—but <term>Siddhāṇta</term> postulates, in addition, <term>Patijñāna</term> (the knowledge of God), which is entering into the <term>Brahma-Nirvāṇa</term> or <term>Śivānubhava</term> (the experience of <term>Śiva</term>). We have shown how, despite all our effort, *<term>Pāśatchaya</term>* will not be practicable unless there is <term>Patijñāna</term>.

The matter is best illustrated by the simile of the crystal or mirror and colors, used by both <term>Sāṅkhyans</term> and <term>Yōgins</term>. By placing a red flower next to a mirror, the mirror is tinted by the reflection of the red flower; similarly, the soul, when in relation with the world, is affected by the world, and thus death, birth, and pain arise. According to the <term>Sāṅkhyan</term>, the soul will regain its freedom when it realizes that it is different from the colors reflected within itself and that it is not actually affected by the colors or the reflection. The acquisition of this knowledge is secured for the soul by the action of <term>Pradhāna</term> (nature) itself. But is it possible for the soul to attain this knowledge through its own effort or the effort of the

<summary>This page explains the Śaiva Siddhānta definition of Advaita as a specific kind of identity (Tādātmyam) where two distinct things are regarded as one. It further critiques Max Muller's interpretation of Sāṅkhya and Yōga, arguing that true liberation requires the knowledge of God (Patijñāna) rather than just the cessation of desire.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Śaiva Siddhānta philosophy, specifically critiquing the Sāṅkhya system's view of the soul and its liberation through the analogy of the mirror and the red flower.</meta>

<header>ADVAITA ACCORDING TO THE ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA.</header>

Pradhāna? If so, let us examine the illustration itself. The mirror and red flower came into juxtaposition, and the reflection was caused on the mirror.

But did they come into juxtaposition by the effort of the mirror or the effort of the red flower? Let us assume that they somehow came into juxtaposition and could not help arriving at this position. Having been placed in juxtaposition, how could the reflection now present on the mirror be removed? Could this be done by any effort of the mirror or by any effort of the red flower? Since there is nothing but the Soul and Pradhāna (or mirror and red flower) according to the Sāṅkhyan, how could this release be achieved? It is clear that, under the circumstances stated by the Sāṅkhyans, there is likely no way out of the difficulty, and the juxtaposition must remain fixed forever; there can be no release, no freedom, and no Moksha, except in name. The ever-recurring cycle of births and deaths would go on forever and ever. But noting that the Yogins use the same figure, is there really no way for the mirror to get rid of this reflection? Yes, there is. But this will require a brief examination of how the reflection itself was caused. Suppose the position between the mirror and flower remained fixed as ever; could we see the reflection at night? No. Why not? Because the essential condition for the reflection to be cast on the mirror is the presence of light or the Sun. And it is the essential presence of the Sun that we had forgotten while using the figure of the crystal and flower. Well, at nighttime, when there is no reflection and no knowledge of coloring, this is the *kevala* condition of the Soul. In this condition of the Soul, it is devoid of all *ichcha* and *kriya* and it is not even conscious that it is undergoing pain, without knowing how to get rid of the pain. As the *Sun* dawns, the reflection is felt on the mirror, and by means of this conjunction, the Soul's *ichcha* and *kriya* are aroused and it experiences both pleasure and pain, sins and suffers, and by suffering, gains experience and freedom. As the *Sun* travels over and over, and nearer and

<vocab>Advaita, Śaiva Siddhānta, Pradhāna, Sāṅkhyan, Moksha, Yogins, kevala, ichcha, kriya, Soul, Sun</vocab>

<summary>This page critiques the Sāṅkhya philosophy by arguing that without the presence of a third element—God, symbolized by the Sun—the Soul (the mirror) could never escape the influence of Pradhāna (the red flower). It explains that the Soul's will and action are only activated by this divine light, leading it through the cycle of experience toward eventual freedom.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the analogy of the mirror (the soul), the red flower (worldly bonds), and the Sun (God), explaining how the presence of the Divine light is necessary for liberation.</meta>

<page-num>269</page-num>
<header>ADVAITA ACCORDING TO THE ŚAIVA SIDDHĀNTA.</header>

Closer to the crystal, the shadow of the red flower will grow smaller and smaller until, at noon, when the Sun is at its nearest point to the crystal, it will be covered with such a blaze of light that you cannot see it, and the image of the flower will be lost. In the first position, the mirror was one with the red flower (bondage-oneness or Advaita in Āṇava), and in the second condition, the mirror was one with the Sun (liberation-oneness or Advaita in God). This is the position of the Yogi or the Theistic Sāṅkhya, and the importance of the doctrine of "Devotion to God" will now be manifest, though "Chitta Nirodha" <note>control of the mind</note> should necessarily precede it. [Compare *Dēvāram*]: the Soul, by its own effort or that of Pradhāna, cannot get rid of its *mala* <note>impurity</note>. Neither of them could be energized by their own will and power unless the Supreme Will and Power thought, "May I become many," and so willed all creation and evolution. And freedom from the world and desire, and from the thirst for birth and death, cannot be gained unless the Soul rests its desire in God or becomes devoted to Him. That this is the only way of securing freedom from *mala* is set forth distinctly in the tenth and eleventh sutras of *Śivajñānabodha*, which treat, as they do, of *Pāśatchaya* <note>the removal of bonds</note> and *Patijñāna* <note>knowledge of God</note>. These *sādhanas* <note>spiritual practices</note> are becoming one with God, dedicating one’s acts to God, and unceasing love and devotion to God. By such dedication and devotion, the soul brings itself into harmony with the Divine Law and loses its pride of self and self-knowledge; karma and ignorance cease to operate, and the person’s whole being becomes covered with the flood of His Grace.

This love and devotion to God, who is Love Himself, begets joy and bliss which completely fulfills our highest desire—unlike the joys of the world, which time and again create a gnawing desire and a thirst for more and more, like an unquenched

True freedom from birth and death requires dedicating one's acts and love entirely to the Divine, as described in the Śivajñānabodha.</summary>

...thirst of a confirmed drunkard. This supreme bliss, rest, and joy fill our hearts like a flood that cannot be contained by its banks, when, in all humility and love, our body and soul are devoted to His service.

When this joy fills him, then he revels in God, delights in God, and rests in God, as the *Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad* (III. i. 4) puts it; then he loves God, delights in God, revels in God, and rejoices in God, and becomes a *Svarāj* <note>self-ruler</note>, and lord and master in all the worlds, as the *Chāndōgya Upaniṣad* (VII. 25. 2) puts it.

In this condition of *Svarāj*, when he is fully filled with God, even as he moves about laughing or eating, playing or rejoicing—be it with women, carriages, or relations (*Chāndōgya Upaniṣad* VIII. 12. 3)—these actions will not affect him, just as fire cannot burn a man skilled in *agni-stambha* <note>the yogic power of resisting fire</note> (See the principle stated in *Śivajñāna Siddhiyār* X. 5 and 6).

This position, therefore, must be clearly distinguished from the ethics and psychology of both Buddhists and Sāṅkhyans. The Yogi and the Siddhanti believe that true salvation can be secured only by such self-renunciation and love for God.

That it is only possible to get rid of our *mala* <note>impurity</note> by attaching ourselves to the Supreme Paramēśvara is also brought out by Saint Tiruvaḷḷuvar:

> “ பற்றுக பற்றற்றான் பற்றினையப் பற்றினைப்
> பற்றுக பற்று விடற்கு ”.
>
> “Desire the desire of Him who is desireless.
> Desire His desire so that desire may leave you.”
>
> “ சார்புணர்ந்து சார்புகெட வொழுகின் மற்றழித்து
> சார்தராச் சார்தரு நோய். ”
>
> “He who knows the true support rejects the support he sought before;
> Sorrow that clings shall cease and cling to him no more.”
>
> “ இருள்சேர் இருவினையும் சேரா இறைவன்
> பொருள்சேர் புகழ்புரிந்தார் மாட்டு. ”
>
> “The two kinds of dark karma will cease for one
> who is devoted to the true praise of God.”

->Compare this with the Christian aspiration for Divine joy.<-

> ‘If, for anyone, the tumult of the flesh were stilled, if the images of earth, waters, and air were stilled; if the poles of heaven were stilled, and the very soul itself were stilled, and by not thinking of itself, rose above itself; if all dreams and imaginary revelations were stilled, along with every tongue and every sign, and everything that exists only in transition—since if any could hear, all these would say, "We did not make ourselves, but He who remains forever made us." If then, after saying this, they too should fall silent, having only directed our ears toward Him who made them, and He alone spoke—not through them, but by Himself—so that we might hear His Word, not through a tongue of flesh, nor an Angel’s voice, nor the sound of thunder, nor in the dark riddle of a metaphor, but might hear Him whom we love in these things; if we might hear His very self without these (just as we ourselves just now reached out and, in a flash of thought, touched that Eternal Wisdom which remains over all); if this could be continued, and other visions of a very different kind were withdrawn, and this one vision were to ravish, absorb, and wrap up its beholder in these inward joys, so that life might be forever like that one moment of understanding for which we just sighed—would this not be "entering into the joy of your Master"? (*St. Augustine*).’

We have previously used the illustration of crystal and colors, whether those of the red flower or the variegated clouds. Pure water is crystalline in its nature, and it reflects and refracts light just as a prism does \*. This water is discolored and affected by the dirt within it; when our *<term>ahaṅkāra</term>* and the dirt subside, the water regains its own pure, clear nature. Then, the reflection of the Supreme One fills it with His glory. This is the truth contained in the famous central verse (*<term>Hrĭdaya šlōka</term>*) in St. Tirumūlar’s *<term>Tirumaṇtiram</term>*.

---

\* In the famous spring at <term>Mahāṇandi</term> near <term>Naṇḍyal</term> (<term>Kurnool District</term>), you can see the actual phenomenon of the refraction of light; just as the water ripples in sunlight, all the colors of the rainbow can be seen on the bottom of the spring.

<summary>This page compares the spiritual ecstasy described by Saint Augustine with the Šaiva Siddhānta concept of the soul reaching a state of pure, clear reflection of the Divine once the ego is removed. It uses the metaphor of light refracting through pure water to illustrate how the soul becomes filled with God's glory.</summary>

<page-num>35</page-num>

# THE SAIVA RELIGION

->AND<-

## SAIVA ADVAITA SIDDHANTA PHILOSOPHY. <note>A paper read before the Convention of Religions, at Calcutta, 1909.</note>

Professor Max Müller, in his last great work on the "Six Systems of Hindu Philosophy," remarked as follows:

> "The longer I have studied the various systems, the more I have become impressed with the view taken by Vijnana Bikshu and others that there is, behind the variety of the Six Systems, a common fund of what may be called National or Popular Philosophy—a large Manasa lake of philosophical thought and language, far away in the distant north and in the distant past, from which each thinker was allowed to draw for his own purposes."

And it would have certainly surprised him if someone had told him that one need go neither to the distant north nor to the distant past to discover what this National or Popular Philosophy was, from which each thinker drew his own inspiration. A study of the two popular Hindu religions of modern India—meaning Saivaism and Vaishnavism—will convince anyone that today they inherit all the thought and traditions of bygone ages, just as the modern Hindus themselves linearly represent their ancient ancestors who have been settled in Bharata Khanda since the days of the Rig Veda. Their religion today is a living faith, suited to all sorts and conditions of people, whether peasant or pandit, sinner or saved.

### SAIVAISM IS BASED ON THE VEDAS AND AGAMAS.

Saivaism, comprising in its fold Shaktism and Ganapatyam and the worshippers of the god Subrahmanya, etc., counts among its followers the majority of Hindus, and it accordingly claims to

<summary>This page introduces Saiva Siddhanta philosophy by connecting it to Max Müller's concept of a "common fund" of national Indian philosophy. The author asserts that Saivaism and Vaishnavism are living continuations of ancient Vedic traditions accessible to all levels of society.</summary>

<keywords>Saiva Religion, Saiva Advaita Siddhanta, Max Müller, Saivaism, Vaishnavism, Rig Veda, Agamas, Bharata Khanda</keywords>

--- Page 305 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the Vedic authority of the Saiva religion, asserting that the Vedas and Agamas share the same divine origin, and begins to explore its ancient roots through the lens of Vedic sacrifice.</meta>

<page-num>274</page-num>
<header>THE ŚAIVA RELIGION.</header>

represent the old traditional and parent religion of the days of the Vedas and Upanishads, Agamas or Tantras, and Itihasas and Puranas, and it bases its authority on these ancient revealed books and histories. It claims that God Siva is the author of the Vedas and Agamas. Sri Nilakantha Sivacharya says in his Sutra Bhashya:

<margin>We see no difference between the Veda and the Sivagama. Even the Veda may properly be called Sivagama, because he is its author. Therefore, Sivagama is twofold: one intended for the three castes, and the other intended for everyone. The Veda is for the three castes, and the other is for all. For both, Siva alone is the author. "He is the Lord of all knowledge," "The breath of this great Being," etc., as stated in the Sruti. "Of the eighteen sciences of various paths, the leader and original author is the wise Sulapani (Siva) himself," so says the Sruti.</margin>

> "We see no difference between the Veda and the Sivagama. Even the Vedas may properly be called Sivagama, as Siva is their author. Accordingly, Sivagama is twofold: one being intended for the three higher castes, and the other being intended for all. The Vedas are intended for people of the three castes, and the other for all. Siva alone, as the author of the Veda, is declared in the following passages of Sruti and Smriti."

> "He is the Lord of all Vidyas (knowledge),"

> "(The Veda) is the breath of the Mighty Being."

> "Of these eighteen Vidyas of various paths, the original author is the wise Sulapani Himself. So says the Sruti."

It will therefore be important to trace modern Saivism from the traditions, thought, and language of the past.

### ITS ANTIQUITY.

The supreme system of the Veda is sacrifice. Various gods—Indra, Vayu, Varuna, Agni, Hiranyagarbha, Soma, the Sun, the Moon, Vishnu, and Rudra—are worshipped. Each is addressed as a most powerful deity, and his aid is invoked for all kinds of earthly blessings and freedom from evil. They are all supposed to represent various powers of nature and to idealize man’s aspiration for the Supreme. Then we encounter the text, “Ekam Sat Vipra Bahudha Vadanti” <note>“Truth is one, but the wise call it by many names.”</note>; and who is this one? Was any one God recognized, above all others, as the Chief, as the

God of Sacrifices, as the Pati? We have the following texts from the <term>Ṛig Veda</term>.

“Tasmāt Rudraḥ Paśunāmadhipatiḥ” * (Rig Veda).

“Gāthapatim Mēdhapatim Rudram Jalāsha bhêshajam Tat Samyōḥ Sumnamīmahē.”† (Rig. 1 Ashtaka, 1 Mandala, 26 Anu.)

“We seek from Rudra—the Lord of Songs, the Lord of Sacrifices, who possesses healing remedies—his auspicious favor" (Rig Veda 1. 43. 4.)

As the Pati of all sacrifices, He is the fulfiller of sacrifices: “Yajña Sādham” ‡ (1. 114. 4) and “Rudram yajñānam sadhad-iṣhṭim abasam” (3. 2. 5). As the God of gods, He is said to “derive His renown from Himself” (“Rudrāya Svayaśase”). His glory is said to be inherent; he is an independent or self-dependent God, “Svadhavane” (Rig. 7. 46. 1). He is also called *Svapivata*, which is variously explained as meaning “readily understanding,” “accessible,” “gracious,” “He by whom life is conquered,” “He whose command cannot be transgressed,” or “You by whom prayers (words) are readily received.” He is called the “father of the worlds,” “*Bhuvanasya Pitaram*,” § 6. 49. 10, and the <term>Ṛik</term> story of His becoming the Father of the fatherless Maruts can be recalled in many a <term>Purāṇic</term> story, local legend, and common folklore.

He is referred to in the text “*anter ichchanti*” (8. 61. 3). His form as described in the Rig Veda is almost the same as the image of later days. He is called the Kapardin, with “spirally braided hair.” He is of “Hiranya,” “golden formed,” and “brilliant like the sun,” and “shining like gold,” “Yaḥśukra

And in the Rig Veda, X. 136-1 to 7, He is the "long-haired being" who sustains the fire, water, and the two worlds; who is, in appearance, the entire sky; and who is called this "Light." He is *wind-clad* (naked) and drinks *Visha* (water or poison), and a Muni (sage) is identified with Rudra in this aspect.

Rudra is derived by Sāyana from the roots *Rut drāvayita*,* meaning "he who drives away sorrow." And consistent with this derivation, Rudra is called in the Rig Veda itself the "bountiful" and the "Healer" who possesses various remedies (the later Vaidyanāth), "benign" and "gracious." And the term *Šiva* clearly appears in the following text of the Rig Veda (X. 92-9): "Stōman *va* adya Rudrāya šikvase kshyad-vīraya namasā didishṭāna yēbhiḥ Šivaḥ † svavan ēvayavabhir divaḥ šikshati svayašaḥ nikāmabhiḥ."

> (With reverence, present your hymn today to the mighty Rudra, the ruler of heroes, and to the Maruts—those rapid and ardent deities with whom the gracious (Šivaḥ) and opulent (Rudra), who derives his renown from himself, protects us from the sky.)

If the Gods, Indra and others, personified the different powers of nature individually, in the supreme personality of Rudra all these different powers will be found combined. He is a thunderer and storm-god, the father of the Maruts. He is Agni. He is Vāyu. He is Varuṇa. He is Sōma. He is the Sun and Moon. We have the high authority of Sāyana that Sōma means *Sa-Umā* (with Umā). He deduces the story of Tripuradahana

and Vishapāna from two texts in the Rig Veda. We also have in the Rig Veda the germ of the later Hindu cosmology, in the famous *Nasadiya sūktam*; and this is also the central text of Šiva Šakti worship.

> “In the beginning there was neither *sat* (being) nor *asat* (non-being);
> Then there was neither sky nor atmosphere above.
> What then enshrouded all this teeming universe?
> In what receptacle was it contained?
> Was it enveloped in the profound gulf of water?
> Then there was neither death nor immortality;
> Then there was neither day, nor night, nor light,
> Nor darkness; only the Existent One breathed without 
>        breath, self-contained.
> Nothing else but he existed, nothing else above or
>        beyond.
> Then first came darkness, hidden in darkness, gloom in
>        gloom;
> Next all was water, all an undifferentiated chaos,
> In which the one lay void, shrouded in nothingness.
> Then turning inwards, he, by self-developed force
> Of inner fervor and intense abstraction, grew.
> First in his mind was formed Desire (*Ichchā-šakti*), the
>        primal germ,
> Productive, which the Wise, searching profoundly, say
> Is the first subtle bond, connecting *Sat* with *Asat*.”

In the Rig Veda, we also find the famous text which is repeated in the Atharva Veda and subsequently in the Švetāšvatara Upanishad and also in the Kaṭha and Muṇḍaka Upanishads, and which forms the chief stronghold of Indian Theism against Idealism. “Two birds, inseparable friends, cling to the same tree. One of them eats the sweet fruit; the other, ‘Anya,’ looks on without eating.”

### YAJUR VEDA.

In the Yajur Veda, the position of Rudra becomes more established as Pašupati and Lord of sacrifices, and as The One without a second.

> "Pašunām sarma asi sarma yajamānasya sarma me yacha
Eka Eva Rudro Na Dvitīyāya Tasthe Ākhusthe Rudra
Pašuḥ Tam Jushasva. Esha tê Rudra Bhāgaḥ Saha Svašra
Ambikāya tam Jushasva Bheshajam Gave Asvāya Purushāya
Bheshajam."

This text is repeated in the <term>Švetāšvatara Upanishat</term> and is the original source of the famous text in the <term>Chhāndōgya Upanishat</term> '<term>Ekamevādvitīyam Brahma</term>.' *Nadvitīyam* is a more ancient form than *Advitīyam* or <term>Advaitam</term>. As we know, this is the central text of the <term>Advaita</term> philosophy. In this Veda, His supreme majesty is fully developed, and He is expressly called <term>Šiva</term> by the name 'Šivo namaši' (Yaj. S. 3-63). The famous mantra, the <term>Pañchākshara</term>, is said to be placed in the very heart of the three Vedas (the name occurs in Tait. S. IV. 5, 1-41: "namah šāmbhave cha mayobave cha namah Šaṅkarāya cha mayāskarāya cha NAMAHŠIVĀYA cha Šivatarāya cha"). The famous <term>Šatarudrīyam</term>, which is praised in the Upanishads and in the <term>Mahābhārat</term>, also forms the central portion of this central Veda. This is a description of God as the all, the all in all, and transcending all—'<term>Višvadevo</term>, <term>Višvasvarūpo</term>, <term>Višvādhiko</term>.' Anyone can see that the famous passage in the <term>Gītā</term> in chapters 10 and 11 merely parodies this other passage; these two chapters are respectively called <term>Vibhūti Vistāra Yoga</term> and <term>Višvarūpa Sandaršana Yoga</term>, which matches the exact character of the <term>Šatarudriya</term>. The Yogi who has reached the highest state "sees all in God and God in all." In the <term>Šatarudriya</term> and throughout the Veda, <term>Rudra</term> is called <term>Šiva</term>, <term>Šaṅkara</term>, <term>Šambhu</term>, <term>Išāna</term>, <term>Iša</term>, <term>Bhagavān</term>, <term>Bhava</term>, <term>Sarva</term>, <term>Ugra</term>, <term>Sōma</term>, <term>Pašupati</term>, <term>Nilagrīva</term>, <term>Giriša</term>, <term>Mahādeva</term>, and <term>Mahešvara</term>.

The word '<term>Pura</term>'* in the Upanishad technically means 'the body.' <term>Tripura</term> means the triple bond (of the soul), and <term>Tripura samhara</term> means the destruction of our human bondage by the grace of God.

The story of <term>Tripurasamhāra</term> is much more fully set forth in the <term>Yajur Veda</term> (6th <term>Kaṇḍa</term>, 2nd <term>Prašna</term>, 3rd <term>Anuvāka</term> and 12th <term>Mantra</term>).

*Mahāpuram Jayantiti ta ishum Samas Kurva to gnium
anikan Soman Salyam Vishum Tejanam the bruvan ka imam
asishatita Rudra iti aleruva rudro vai Krura; Somya twiti,
sobravit Varam Vrina aham eva Pašunām Adhipatirasāniti
tasmāt rudraḥ Pašunām adhipatishṭam rudrova Sṛijat Satisra
Puro bhitva ebhyo : lokebhys Suran pranudata.*

> "There were three cities of iron, silver, and gold belonging to the Asuras. The gods, not being able to win them by battle, wished to win them by siege. The great say that he (the Brahmin, etc.) who knows what ought to be known, and he (the non-Brahmin, etc.) who does not know—they are able to win by siege the great city which cannot be overtaken by battle. Then the gods made an arrow composed of Agni as the bottom hilt, Soma as the middle iron, and Vishṇu as the top, and they deliberated on who would discharge it, and determined that Rudra was able. He (Rudra) said the boon was granted: 'I am the Lord of the Pašus (both the two-footed and four-footed).' So Rudra, the Lord of the Pašus, discharged it, broke up these three cities, and blew them all away from these worlds." *

***

\* It is clear from the <term>Yajur Veda</term> that there is a closer link between the Lord's aspect as <term>Tripurasamhāra</term>, as the burner of the three cities, and His other aspect as <term>Pašupati</term>, the Lord of the <term>Pašus</term>. In fact, it is from the Lord's aspect as <term>Tripurasamhāra</term> that we come to know of His other aspect as <term>Pašupati</term>. The whole story of <term>Tripurasamhāra</term>, with the earth becoming a chariot, the Sun and Moon forming its wheels, the four <term>Vedas</term> becoming its horses, the <term>Kshêtrajña</term> Brahma its driver, and Vishṇu, Agni, and Sōma becoming the portions of the arrow held in the hands of the Lord, etc., is on the face of it symbolic. That this story is symbolic can be further gleaned from an earlier portion

The importance of this lies in the fact that in the chief festival of each temple, called the <term>Brahmotsava</term>, the central event is the chariot festival in which the charioteer is the four-headed Brahma. This recalls and represents the ancient story referred to in the <term>Rig Veda</term> and <term>Yajur Veda</term>.

The <term>Yajur Veda</term> is the central Veda and is a most important one; as such, more than ninety percent of the Brahmins today are <term>Yajur Vedis</term>. The occurrence of the words <term>Pati</term>, <term>Paśu</term>, and <term>Pāśam</term> should be noted, as their significance will be referred to later on.

...of the <term>Yajus-Samhitā</term>. The following texts occur in the 5th <term>Kaṇḍa</term> of the <term>Yajur-Veda</term>:

> रुद्रोवाएषयदग्नि: तस्यतिस्र: शरण्या: प्रतीची, तिरश्ची, अनूची, etc., यत् तेपुर: धनु:
> तद्वात: अनुवातुते तस्मै ते रुद्रसंवत्सरेण नम: करोमियत्ते रुद्रदक्षिणाधनु: तत् वात: अनुवातु-
> तेतस्मैते रुद्रपरिवत्सरेणनम:करोमि । यत्तेरुद्रपश्चाद्धनु: तत्वात:अनुवातुते । तस्मैतेरुद्रइदावत्स-
> रेणनम: करोमि । यत्तेरुद्र उत्तरात् धनु: तत् वात: अनुवातु ते तस्मै ते रुद्रइदुवत्सरेणनम:
> करोमि । यत्तेरुद्रउपरिधंनु: तत्वात: अनुवातुतेतस्मै ते रुद्रवत्सरेण नम: करोमि.

Here all the quarters (that is, space) are said to be the arrows of Rudra, and the bows are the various periods of time—that is, Eternity. Rudra Himself is the Spirit dwelling within all things, bound by Space and Time.

> यो रुद्रो अग्नौ य अप्सु ओषधिषु यो रुद्रोविश्वा भुवनाविवेश तस्मै रुद्राय नमॊ अस्तु ।

(Yajus, <term>Kaṇḍa</term> 5). In the <term>Karṇaparva</term> of the <term>Mahābhārata</term>, we are expressly told that Time is represented by the Bow of the <term>Tripuraghna</term>.

> विचित्रंकर्तुभि: षड्भि: कृत्वा संवत्सरं धनु: ।

And what is Eternity? It is that which transcends the periods of past, future, and present.

> यच्चान्यत्रिकालातीतं तदप्योंकारएव ।

Hence the same Bow is described as <term>OMKĀRA</term>: सकृत्वाधनुरोंकारं सावित्रीज्यां- महेश्वर: "The Supreme God made a bow of <term>Omkara</term> and a string of <term>Sāvitrī</term>."

Regarding what the granting of the boon means, the following text makes it clear:

> स्वतस्सिद्धंपतित्वं मे युष्माकंपशुतापिच ।
> तदेतद्दर्शयाम्यद्य वरवन्धविनोदने ॥

“My Lordship (<term>Patitvam</term>) is as natural to Me as the state of dependence (<term>Paśutvam</term>) is to all of you; it is this fact that is shown by My playing with you regarding the granting of the boon.” It is like a father playing with his children, requiring them to say, “Call me father.” It is the acknowledgment of utter dependence upon the Lord.

### THE <term>UPANISHATS</term> AND <term>ĀGAMAS</term>.

As we noted above, the religious system of the <term>Vedas</term> was the performance of sacrifices. This continued during the <term>Upanishat</term> period, and the <term>Brāhmaṇas</term> elaborated the rituals. But at the same time, the worship of many gods was being abandoned in favor of the one God, and the effectiveness of sacrifices in general was being doubted. A more spiritual form of worship was being substituted in its place, and the first departure is noted in the story given in the <term>Kêna Upanishat</term>.

#### <term>KENA UPANISHAT</term>.

> “<term>Brahman</term> obtained the victory for the <term>Dêvas</term>. The <term>Dêvas</term> became elated by the victory of <term>Brahman</term>, and they thought, ‘this victory is ours only.’ <term>Brahman</term> perceived this and appeared to them. But they did not know it, and said:
> “‘What <term>yaksha</term> <note>a celestial spirit</note> is this?’ They said to <term>Agni</term> (fire): ‘O <term>Jātavēdas</term>, find out what spirit this is.’ ‘Yes,’ he said. He ran towards it, and <term>Brahman</term> said to him: ‘Who are you?’ He replied: ‘I am <term>Agni</term>, I am <term>Jātavēdas</term>.’ <term>Brahman</term> said: ‘What power is in you?’ <term>Agni</term> replied: ‘I could burn everything there is on earth.’ <term>Brahman</term> put a straw before him, saying: ‘Burn this.’ He went towards it with all his might, but he could not burn it. Then he returned from there and said: ‘I could not find out what spirit this is.’ Then they said to <term>Vāyu</term> (air): ‘O <term>Vāyu</term>, find out what spirit this is.’ ‘Yes,’ he said. He ran towards it, and <term>Brahman</term> said to him: ‘Who are you?’ He replied: ‘I am <term>Vāyu</term>, I am <term>Mātarišvan</term>.’ <term>Brahman</term> said: ‘What power is in you?’ <term>Vāyu</term> replied: ‘I could lift up everything there is on earth.’ <term>Brahman</term> put a straw before him, saying: ‘Take it up.’ He went towards it with all his might, but he could not take it up. Then he returned from there and said: ‘I could not find out what spirit this is.’ Then they said to <term>Indra</term>: ‘O <term>Bhagavān</term>, find out what spirit this is.’ He went towards it, but it disappeared from before him. Then in the same <term>Ākāš</term> (ether), he came towards a woman, highly adorned: it was <term>Umā</term>, the daughter of <term>Himavat</term>. He

<summary>This page describes the transition from Vedic sacrificial rituals to a more spiritual form of monotheism in the Upanishads. It illustrates this shift through the Kena Upanishat's story of Brahman appearing to the gods Agni, Vayu, and Indra to humble them and reveal their power as secondary to the Divine.</summary>

said to her: 'Who is that spirit?' She replied: 'It is <term>Brahman</term>. It is through the victory of <term>Brahman</term> that you have thus become great.' After that, he knew that it was <term>Brahman</term>."

This is a step further than the position in the <term>Ṛig Vēda</term>, where the <term>Ekam Sat</term> or <term>Rudra Paśupati</term> is identified in a way with all the Gods. Here, He is not <term>Indra</term> or <term>Varuṇa</term>, <term>Vāyu</term> or <term>Agni</term>. He cannot be understood by the Gods, though He is before them, and it was left to *Umā Haimavati* to point out the Supreme <term>Brahman</term> as her consort. This story is frequently repeated in the <term>Purāṇas</term> <note>See *Vayu Samhita* II Chapter 3 and *Suta Samhita*.</note> and the person of <term>Rudra-Śiva</term> is introduced as <term>Umā's</term> Lord.

This is called the <term>Brahami Upanishat</term>, and it introduces the grand thought: "he by whom <term>Brahman</term> is not thought of, by him it is thought of; he by whom it is thought of, knows it not."

This departure from the old system of the <term>Vēdas</term> to the worship of the One Supreme <term>Brahman</term>, <term>Umā's</term> Lord, will be found illustrated further in the <term>Purāṇas</term> by the stories of <term>Daksha's</term> sacrifice and the <term>Dārukavana Ṛishis</term>. <term>Daksha</term>, son of <term>Brahma</term> (<term>Śabda Brahma</term> or the <term>Vedas</term>), simply means sacrifice, and <term>Dākshāyaṇi</term> meant the spirit of sacrifice. And as long as this spirit of sacrifice was devoted to the One Supreme <term>Brahman</term>, <term>Śiva</term>, it was beneficial. But once this sacrifice was divorced from the worship of the One Supreme <term>Brahman</term>, represented in the person of <term>Śiva</term>, the consort of <term>Dākshāyaṇi</term>—as <term>Daksha</term> tried to do—then this sacrifice was of no use. When the spirit of sacrifice was divorced from the word, then <term>Dākshāyaṇi</term> died and was reborn as <term>Umā Haimavati</term>, the bearer of <term>Brahma Jñāna</term> <note>divine knowledge</note>, and was reunited to <term>Śiva</term>. This reunion or rebirth of the ancient <term>jñāna</term> is what is celebrated in every temple in the important feast of <term>Tirukkalyāṇa</term>, and is depicted in the oldest sculptures in the <term>Elephanta</term> and <term>Ellora</term> cave-temples. In the <term>Dārukāvana</term> story, the Vedic sacrifice was also divorced from the worship of the One Supreme <term>Brahman</term>. The <term>Vedas</term> represented the

<term>Śabda Brahman</term>, and the <term>Ṛishis</term> thought that no God was required, and the worship of the <term>Śabda Brahman</term> alone was sufficient for securing salvation. The bleating of the <term>Śabda Brahman</term> represented by the deer (மான்மறை) was found in no way to reach God.

The <term>Śvētāśvatara Upanishat</term>, the greatest authority of the <term>Śaiva</term> School, repeats the text of the <term>Yajur Veda</term> “Eka Eva Rudrō Nadvitiyāya Taste,” and the philosophy of <term>Advaita Siddhāṇta</term> is fully expounded in this Upanishat. This Advaita is neither the <term>Śāṅkhya</term> nor the <term>Yōga</term>, neither <term>Dvaita</term> nor Advaita, as ordinarily understood. Hence, Oriental Scholars like Monier Williams, Professor Macdonnell, and Garbe regard this Upanishat as the oldest representative of the ancient eclectic* school of Hindu philosophy. With this book, they couple the <term>Bhagavat Gītā</term>.

The highest conception of the one God, ‘Eko Dēva,’ is given here which, as Max Müller says, corresponds to the conception of God in Christian theology. “He is the one God hidden in all beings, all-pervading, the <term>Antar Ātmā</term> of all things, watching over all works, dwelling in all beings, the witness, the perceiver, the only One <term>Nirguṇa</term>.” “He is the eternal and infinite, unborn being, partless, actionless, tranquil, without taint, without fault, the highest bridge to immortality.”

“He is the causeless first cause, the all-knower, the all-pervader; the creator, sustainer, and liberator of the world, the end and aim of all religion, and of all philosophy. He is the <term>Iśvara</term> of <term>Iśvaras</term>, <term>Mahēśvara</term>, the God Supreme of Gods, the king of kings, the supreme of the supreme, the <term>Iśa</term> of the Universe. The sun does not shine there, nor the moon and the stars, nor these lightnings, and much less this fire; everything shines after Him; by His light all this is lightened.” God is <term>nirguṇa</term>; and as I have shown elsewhere, <term>nirguṇa</term> does not mean impersonal,

Furthermore, <term>Saguṇa</term> should not be translated as "personal." <term>Nirguṇa</term> simply means existing beyond the three <term>Guṇas</term>—<term>Sattva</term>, <term>Rajas</term>, and <term>Tamas</term>—and <term>Saguṇa</term> means being united with these three. Personality, as Emerson and other Christian writers interpreted it, means "pure spiritual being" (<term>Sat</term>); thus, God can be both personal and <term>Nirguṇa</term> (absolute). It also follows that God cannot be born, as He is not joined with matter. The meaning of the <term>Ṛig Veda</term> <term>Sūkta</term> we quoted above is explained in the following verse: "When there was neither darkness, nor day, nor night, neither being (<term>Sat</term>) nor non-being (<term>Asat</term>), then <term>Śiva</term> alone existed (<term>Śiva Eva Kevalaḥ</term>). That is the absolute; that is the adorable state of the Lord. From that source also came forth the ancient wisdom (<term>Jñānāśakti</term>)." <note>यदातमस्तन्नदिवानरात्रि: नसन्नचासशिवएवकेवलः । तदक्षरंतत्सवितुर्वरेण्यं प्रज्ञाचतस्मात्प्रसृतापुगणी ॥ (<term>Śvētāśvatara Upanishat</term>)</note> After repeating the text about the two birds, the passage proceeds as follows: "On the same tree, man (<term>Anīśa</term>) sits grieving, overwhelmed and bewildered by his own helplessness. But when he sees the other, the Lord (<term>Īśa</term>), and is content in knowing His glory, then his sorrow departs." <note>समानेवृक्षेपुरुषोनिमग्नोऽनीशयाशोचतिमुह्यमानः । जुष्टंयदापश्यत्यन्यमीशमस्य महिमानमितिबीतशोकः ॥ (<term>Śvētāśvatara Upanishat</term> 4.7)</note> That this is the highest teaching of the <term>Ṛig Veda</term> is pointed out in the next verse: "What use is the <term>Ṛig Veda</term> to one who does not know that Indestructible Being (<term>Akshara</term>)—that highest Ether (<term>Parama Vyōmam</term>) in which all the Gods reside? Only those who know It find contentment." <note>ऋचाऽक्षरेपरमेव्योमन् यस्मिन्देवाअधिविश्वेनिषेदुः । यस्तन्नवेदकिमृचाकरिष्यति य इत्तद्विदुस्तइमेसमासते ॥ —(<term>Ṛig Veda</term> 2nd Ashṭaka, 3rd Adhyāya)</note> The otherness of God (<term>Anyatā</term>) referred to in the <term>Ṛig Veda</term> <term>Mantra</term> is also fully illustrated in the following verses: "Indeed, that unborn one (the <term>Aja</term>-soul) sleeps in the arms of the unborn one (nature or <term>Pradhāna</term>), enjoying her (nature, characterized as red, white, and black), who brings forth many offspring like herself. But once her charms have been enjoyed, the soul leaves the side of <term>Prakṛiti</term> for the other unborn one, the Lord (<term>Anyatā</term>)."

"In the imperishable and infinite highest <term>Brahman</term>, where the two—<term>Vidyā</term> (<term>Vijñāna-Ātmā</term>) and <term>Avidyā</term>—are hidden, the one, <term>Avidyā</term>, perishes; the other, <term>Vidyā</term>, is immortal; but He who controls both <term>Vidyā</term> and <term>Avidyā</term> is another (<term>Anyatā</term>)." <note>* द्वे अक्षरे ब्रह्मपरे त्वनन्ते विद्याऽविद्ये निहिते यत्र गूढे । क्षरं त्वविद्या ह्यमृतं तु विद्या विद्याऽविद्ये ईशते यस्तु सोऽन्यः ॥ (Śveta 5—1).</note> And in the subsequent verses, this "another" is clearly pointed out to be the "only one God, without a second, the ruler of all, the generator of all, and the supporter (ripener) of all." This forms the subject of discussion by <term>Bādarāyaṇa</term> in I. ii, 21. And the famous passage in the <term>Bṛihadāraṇyaka</term> is referred to: "He who dwells in the <term>Ātmā</term> (<term>Vijñāna</term>) and is *different from the <term>Ātmā</term>*, whom the <term>Ātmā</term> does not know, whose body the <term>Ātmā</term> is, and who pulls (rules) the <term>Ātmā</term> from within, He is your <term>Ātmā</term>, the puller within, the immortal" (III. 7, 22).

(3) The Supreme Mantra of the <term>Veda</term> or the <term>Śabda Brahma</term> is the <term>Praṇava</term> or <term>Omkāra</term>. It is commonly known that <term>Om</term> is a compound of the three letters A, U, and M, and that they represent the deities <term>Brahma</term>, <term>Vishṇu</term>, and <term>Rudra</term>. What is not as well known is that there is a fourth part of this <term>Omkāra</term> called its <term>Ardha Mātra</term> sound; this is called the <term>Chaturtam</term> or <term>Turīyam</term>, and represents the supreme <term>Brahman</term> or <term>Śiva</term>. This is brought out in several of the <term>Upanishats</term>, and in the following verses it is coupled with <term>Śivam</term> and <term>Śambhu</term>.

> "Śivam Śāntam Advaitam Chaturtam Manyante."

(4) The Supreme <term>Upāsana</term> of the <term>Upanishat</term> is the <term>Dahara Upāsana</term> in the <term>Hṛid Puṇḍarīka</term>, in the <term>ākāś</term>, <term>Vyōma Paramālaya</term>. the same mantras, as pointed out by Swami Vivekananda* in his famous address before the Chicago Parliament of Religions, which were used in the old sacrificial worship, were brought into the new system of worship. The offering of the self as a sacrificial oblation was made in the place of animal sacrifice. The *Pashu* was the animal nature within man, and when it was offered as a sacrifice in the fire of knowledge (*Jnana Agni*), it became the *Nandi* or Shiva.

### PURANAS.

The Puranas are the earliest interpreters of the Vedas and the Upanishads. Entire passages from the Upanishads are quoted and explained. Their principles are illustrated by stories and parables, and the Vedic stories themselves are further elaborated. All of these explain the difference between the old and new systems of worship and thought, bring out fully the distinction between the Supreme Brahman Shiva and man, and illustrate the paths to salvation. These stories include the Daksha sacrifice, the churning of the milky ocean, the destruction of Tripura (*Tripura Samhara*), Durga Puja, and others. The *Linga Purana* specifically deals with the birth of the *Jyotir Linga*. The majority of the Puranas are Shaivite, and the oldest of them is the *Vayu* or *Shiva Purana*, as noted by Wilson. The *Chandogya Upanishad* traces ancient wisdom back to Skanda Sanatkumara (तपसस्पारं दर्शयति भगवान् सनत्कुमारस्तं स्कन्द इत्याचक्षते—"The Lord Sanatkumara shows the way beyond darkness; they call him Skanda"—*Chandogya*, 26th Kanda), and the *Skanda Purana* accordingly deals with the same subject. The *Uttara* (later) portions of some of the Puranas are clearly later interpolations, showing the rise of new sects and faiths.

---

<note>\* "The Tantras, as we have said, represent Vedic rituals in a manifold form; before anyone jumps to the most absurd conclusions about them, I would advise them to read the Tantras in connection with the Brahmanas, especially the *adhvaryu* portion. Most of the mantras used in the Tantras will be found taken word-for-word from these Brahmanas. As for their influence—aside from the *Shrauta* and *Smarta* rituals—all other forms of ritual observed from the Himalayas to Cape Comorin have been taken from the Tantras, and they direct the worship of the Shaktas, Shaivas, and Vaishnavas alike."</note>

<summary>This page explains the transition from animal sacrifice to the spiritual offering of the self in Shaivism and describes the Puranas as the primary interpreters of Vedic wisdom through parables. It highlights the importance of the Shiva Purana and the Skanda Purana, and notes the Tantric influence on various Hindu rituals.</summary>

### ITIHĀSA.

The only worship universal in the days of the *Mahābhārata* was that of Shiva and the Shiva Linga, and we refer to the stories of Krishna's and Arjuna's *Tapas*, and the discussion between Aśvatthāma and Vyāsa. Most of the temples mentioned in the *Āraṇya Parva* are temples dedicated to Shiva.

### MAHĀBHĀRATA.

Oriental scholars point out that the superior castes in the days of the *Mahābhārata* followed the worship of Shiva, and we quote the following passage from the *Anushasana Parva*, which explains at the same time Rudra's different aspects—the beneficent and the apparently terrible forms—as the Creator, Protector, and Destroyer.

Lord Krishna says: “Large-armed Yudhishthira, understand from me the greatness of the glorious, *multiform*, many-named Rudra. They call him Mahādeva, Agni, Ishāna, Maheshvara, the one-eyed, Tryambaka, the *Universal-formed*, and Shiva. Brahmans versed in the *Veda* know two bodies of this God, one awful and one auspicious, and these two bodies have, in turn, many forms. The dire and awful body is fire, lightning, and the sun; the auspicious and beautiful body is virtue, water, and the moon. Half of His essence is fire and the other half is called the moon. The one which is His auspicious body practices chastity, while the other, which is His most dreadful body, destroys the world. Because He is the Great Lord, He is called Maheshvara. Since He consumes, since He is fiery, fierce, glorious, and an eater of flesh, blood, and marrow, He is called Rudra. As He is the greatest of the Gods, as His domain is wide, and as He preserves the vast Universe, He is called Mahādeva. From His smoky color, He is called Dhurjati. Since He constantly prospers all men in all their acts by seeking their welfare (Shiva), He is therefore called Shiva.”

And it can be shown that the picture of God as the fierce and terrible is not altogether an un-Christian idea. The following paragraphs we extract from a book called “The woodlands in

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Shaiva religion, moving from the scriptural evidence in the Mahābhārata to an analogy from a Christian source that illustrates the nature of Shiva as Destroyer, Creator, and Preserver.</meta>

Europe" intended for Christian Readers, and we could not produce better arguments for the truth of our conception of the Supreme Shiva—the Destroyer, the Creator, and the Preserver (see p. 6. *Shivajnanabodham*, English Edition).

> "And how about the dead leaves which, season after season, strew the ground beneath the trees? Is their work done because when their bright summer life is over, they lie softly down to rest under the wintry boughs? Is it only death and nothing beyond? No, if it is death, it is death giving place to life. Let us call it rather change, progress, transformation. It must be progress when the last year's leaves make the soil for the next year's flowers, and in so doing serve a set purpose and fulfill a given mission. *It must be transformation when one thing passes into another, and instead of being annihilated, begins life again in a new shape and form.*

> "It is interesting to remember that the same snow which weighs down and breaks those fir branches is the nursing mother of the flowers. Softly it comes down upon the tiny seeds and the tender buds and covers them up lovingly, so that from all the stern vigor of the world without, they are safely sheltered. Thus they are getting forward, as it were, and life is already swelling within them. So that when the sun shines and the snow melts, they are ready to burst forth with a rapidity which seems almost miraculous.

> "*It is not the only force gifted with both preserving and destroying power, according to the aspect in which we view it.* The fire refines and purifies but it also destroys, and the same water which rushes down in the cataract with such overwhelming power, falls in the gentlest of drops upon the thirsty flower-cup, and fills the hollow of the leaf with just the quantity of dew which it needs for its refreshment and sustenance. And in those higher things of which nature is but the type and shadow, the same grand truth holds good, and from our Bibles we learn that *the consuming fire and the love that passes knowledge are two different sides of the same*

<sig>37</sig>

<vocab>Shiva, Shivajnanabodham, Destroyer, Creator, Preserver, transformation, consuming fire, nature</vocab>

<summary>This page uses analogies from nature and Christian literature—such as decaying leaves, protective snow, and the dual nature of fire and water—to explain the Shaiva concept of God as a simultaneous force of destruction, creation, and preservation. The author argues that destruction is not annihilation but a necessary transformation that enables new life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Shaiva religion, moving from a metaphorical description of God's dual nature to a formal theological analysis of scriptural passages regarding the trembling of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>290</page-num>

### THE SHAIVA RELIGION.

God. "*Just and yet merciful, who will by no means clear the guilty, yet showing mercy unto thousands.*"

Bādarāyaṇa also touches upon this subject in I. iii. 40, and we quote below the *Pūrvapaksha* and *Siddhānta* views on this question from the commentary of Śrīkaṇṭha.

"Because of trembling (I. iii. 40).

"In the Kaṭhavallis, in the section treating of the thumb-sized *purusha*, it is said as follows:

> 'Whatever there is, the whole world when given forth (from the Brahman) trembles in the *breath*. (It is) a great terror, like a thunderbolt uplifted; those who know it become immortal' (Cit. 6, 2).

"Here a doubt arises as to whether the cause of trembling is the Paramēśvara or some other being.

"(Pūrvapaksha):—Here the Śruti speaks of the trembling of the whole universe through fear caused by the entity denoted by the word *breath*. It is not right to say that the Paramēśvara, who is so sweet-natured as to afford refuge to the whole Universe and who is supremely gracious, is the cause of the trembling of the whole Universe. Therefore, since the word thunderbolt occurs here, it is the thunderbolt that is the cause of trembling. Or it is the vital air which is the cause of trembling, because the word *breath* occurs here. Since the vital air causes the motion of the body, this whole world—which is the body, as it were—moves on account of the vital air. Then we can explain the passage, 'whatever there is, the whole world, when given forth (from the Brahman) trembles in the *breath*.' Then we can also explain the statement that it is a great terror, the thunderbolt uplifted, inasmuch as lightning, cloud and rain—the thunderbolt which is the source of great terror—are produced by the action of the air itself. It is also possible to attain immortality by a knowledge of the air, as the following Śruti says:

> 'Air is everything itself and the air is all things together. He who knows this conquers death.' (Bri. Up. 5. 3. 2).

<summary>This page discusses the theological paradox of a merciful God who also causes the universe to tremble in fear. It presents a preliminary argument (Pūrvapaksha) suggesting that the "breath" and "thunderbolt" causing this trembling might refer to physical air or weather rather than the Supreme Lord.</summary>

<keywords>Bādarāyaṇa, Pūrvapaksha, Siddhānta, Śrīkaṇṭha, Paramēśvara, Śruti, Brahman, Kaṭhavallis, purusha, vital air, immortality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological argument that Paramēśvara (the Supreme Lord) is the source of cosmic order and the cause of the "trembling" of the universe, transitioning from the commentary of Śrīkaṇṭha to the evidence found in the Bhagavad Gītā and the Rāmāyaṇa.</meta>

<page-num>291</page-num>

“(Siddhāṇta): As against the previous argument, we say that Paramēśvara himself is the cause of the trembling. It is possible that as the Ruler, Paramēśvara is the cause of the trembling of the whole universe; by fear of his command, we all abstain from prohibited actions and engage in our prescribed duties. It is by fear of his command that Vāyu and others perform their respective duties, as may be learned from such passages as the following:

> ‘Out of fear of him, Vāyu (the wind) blows’ (Tait. 14. 2. 8).

“Though gracious in appearance, Paramēśvara becomes awe-inspiring as the Ruler of all. Hence the Śruti says:

> ‘Hence the King’s face must be awe-inspiring’ (Tait. Bra, 3. 8. 23).

“Therefore, as the master, Īśvara himself is the cause of the trembling of the whole universe.”

### THE GĪTĀ.

The Bhagavad Gītā epitomizes the philosophy of the Śvētāśvatara Upanishad. Oriental scholars link the two together as expounding an eclectic school of Hindu philosophy. In it, the words Īśvara, Īśa, Mahēśvara, and Paramēśvara are used; and in the Uttara Gītā, the word Śiva is used not to denote the lower Brahman, but the Supreme Brahman.

### THE RĀMĀYANA.

In the Rāmāyaṇa, Rudra’s position as the Lord of sacrifices is affirmed despite some dissenting voices, showing the rise of new faiths. The worship of Śiva and the Śiva-Liṅga was

### THE SŪTRAS.

All the Sūtrakāras recognize Iśvara as the Supreme God and Purusha. Śrī Nīlakaṇṭha’s Bhāshyam* on the Brahma Sūtras is the earliest commentary now extant; as such, it is entitled to the greatest weight and will be found to be the most accurate and reliable interpreter of the Vedāṇta Sūtras, and Śrī Nīlakaṇṭha is the accepted authority by the Southern Śaiva School.

<note>English translation published in volumes 1 to 7 of *Siddhānta Dīpikā*.</note>

It is now proved by Thibaut and admitted by Max Muller that the interpretation of Śaṅkara is not correct. Doctor Thibaut says:

> "If now I am to sum up the results of the preceding inquiry as to the teaching of the Sūtras, I must give it as my opinion that they do not set forth the distinction of a higher and lower knowledge of Brahman; that they do not acknowledge the distinction of Brahman and Iśvara in Śaṅkara's sense; that they do not hold the doctrine of the unreality of the world; and that they do not, with Śaṅkara, proclaim the absolute identity of the Individual and the Highest Self."

> "The Upanishats no doubt teach emphatically that the material world does not owe its existence to any principle independent from the Lord, like the Pradhāna of the Sāṅkhyas; the world is nothing but a manifestation of the Lord’s wonderful power and hence is insubstantial (Asat) if we take the term substance (Sat) in its strict sense. And again, everything material (Achit) is immeasurably inferior in nature to the highest spiritual principle from which it has emanated and which it now hides from the individual Soul. But neither insubstantiality nor inferiority of the kind mentioned constitutes unreality in the sense in which the Māyā of Śaṅkara is unreal. According to the latter, the whole world is nothing but an erroneous appearance as unreal as the snake for which a piece of rope is mistaken by the belated traveler, and"

<summary>This page argues that the Brahma Sūtras and early commentaries, particularly that of Śrī Nīlakaṇṭha, identify Iśvara as the Supreme God and do not support Śaṅkara's doctrine of the world's unreality. It cites Dr. Thibaut’s scholarship to assert that the Sūtras view the material world as a manifestation of the Lord's power rather than a total illusion.</summary>

Śrī Nīlakaṇṭha Śivāchārya, in his Bhāshya, quotes with approval this beautiful text from the Upanishats: “*Apivāyas chaṇḍālaḥ Śiva iti vācham vadēt tēna saha samvadēt, tēna saha samvasēt, tēna saha bhuñjīt*” <note>\* अपिवायश्वण्डालः शिवइतिवाचं वदेत् तेन सह संवदेत् तेन सह संवसेत् तेन सह भुंजीत.</note> which means: “Even if a person is a Chandala, if he utters the name Śiva, converse with him, live with him, and dine with him.”

> “Therefore the whole universe is ensouled by Śiva. If any embodied being whatsoever is subjected to constraint, it will be quite repugnant to the eight-bodied Lord; as to this there is no doubt. Doing good to all, showing kindness to all, and affording shelter to all—this they hold to be the worship of Śiva.”

During the Buddhist and Jaina period, it was Śaivaism that was able to rise above the onslaught of these two creeds and vanquish them. The rise of the great Āchāryas—St. Jñāna-Sambandar, St. Appar, St. Sundarar, and St. Māṇikka vāçagar—occurred in this period. By the close of the ninth century, both Buddhism and Jainism had become inert and dead.

The next few centuries saw the rise of the great teachers Śrī Śaṅkara, Śrī Rāmānuja, and Śrī Madhvāchārya. Following close behind them came the great Santāna Āchāryas, including St. Meykaṇḍān. <note>‡ The author of *Śivajñānabodham*.</note>

It also chronicles the historical triumph of Śaivaism over Buddhism and Jainism through the influence of various great Āchāryas.</summary>

St. Aruṇandi,* St. Maṛai-jñāna-Sambaṇdar, and St. Umāpati Śivāchāryar§ and modern Śaivaism may be said to commence from that time.

We will now begin the study of Modern Śaivaism. Its form of ritualism and philosophy is determined in the South by the Āgamas or Taṇtras, 28 in number, from Kāmika to Vātula, called the Dakshiṇa or Right-handed; and the different temples in Southern India follow the rules prescribed in one Āgama or another, though there are still some temples like the one at Chidambaram where the pure Vēdic Rituals† are followed. This Āgama Philosophy has also been greatly developed and systematized in Tamil by a line of Teachers beginning with St. Tirumūlar,‡ St. Meykaṇḍān, St. Aruṇandi Śivāchāriyar, St. Maṛai-jñāna-Sambaṇdar, and St. Umāpati Śivāchāryar. Both in the rituals and in the philosophy, the same mantras, forms, and words derived from the old Vedic Times are used. For instance, the temple represents the old Yajña-śālā, symbolizing the human body. Dr. Ananda K. Coomaraswamy, in his paper read before the Historical Congress of Oriental Religions (in which he shows that it is the least anthropomorphic of symbols), takes the place of Rudra Pasupati. Its form is that of the Pranava,\* and there is the Balipitha at the entrance to the temple with the Yupastambha† and the Pasu, or animal offered in sacrifice, in the form of the Bull. Every Brahmotsava still commences with a sacrifice (blood sacrifice is altogether abandoned in South Indian Temples), and the Pasu, represented by a cloth effigy, is tied to the Yupastambha and taken down after the festival. The position of the bull or Pasu will be found to be on the other side (the side of God) of the Balipitha and Stambha; it is not called Pasu but Nandi (Blissful) or God. Because, according to the terminology of Shaivism, the Jiva or soul, once it has become freed, is no longer called Jiva but Shiva or Brahman. What had to be offered in sacrifice was not an animal but the Jiva; the soul, also called the Ejaman of the sacrifice, had to offer his Jivatvam—his animal part, his individuality, Ahankara, Avidya, or Ignorance. The Naivedyam in all temples is now interpreted as this Pasutvam, or Pasubhodham as it is called. As soon as he enters the temple, he is asked to prostrate in front of the Yupastambha. This represents his attainment of the High Devahood of Sri Shankara. In the Atharva Veda Samhita, the sacrificial cows are also praised with the attributes of the Brahman. In the Linga Purana, the same hymn is expanded into stories meant to establish the glory of the great Stambha and the superiority of Mahadeva. Later on, he says, the explanation of the Shiva Lingam as a phallic emblem began in India during her most thoughtless and degraded times.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Shaiva philosophy, specifically explaining how self-sacrifice (Arpana) and the fundamental categories of God (Pati), the soul (Pashu), and bondage (Pasha) form the basis of salvation.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>

offering of himself as a sacrifice; and self-sacrifice thus becomes the center of Hindu and Shaivaite Philosophy, upon which the entire process of salvation depends. This is the *Arpana* or *Shivarpana* referred to in Verse 57, Chapter 18 of the *Gita*.

The philosophy also retains the ancient language for its technical terms. While newer systems use technical terms such as "*Chit, Achit, Ishvara*" and "*Jagat, Jiva*, and *Para*," the Shaiva-Siddhanta technical terms used to denote these *Padarthas* (categories) are *Pati* (God), *Pashu* (soul), and *Pasha* (bondage).

In the hymn to the unknown God in the tenth *Mandala* of the *Rig Veda*, God is termed the *Pati*—which means Protector or Savior, coming from the root *Pa*, to protect. It strictly corresponds to the English term Providence. Even the term *Isha* or *Ishvara*, which simply means Ruler, does not express the Inner Nature of the Lord, which is Love. See also the *Brahma Sutra* text पत्यादिशब्देभ्य: because of the term *Pati* and others. *Pashu*, as Shrikantha Yogi explains, involves bondage in *Pasha*—“पाशसम्बन्धेन पशुत्वव्यवहारात्”—and *Pasha* in its root meaning simply means "that which binds." It means a noose or a cord only in its figurative sense. A man bound to a pole by means of cords—his hands, feet, neck, and back being tied to it—can have no liberty, and he is said to suffer pain. *Pasha*, therefore, does not simply mean "limitation" but is a limitation that involves pain, or pain to the core. The *Agamas* explain the noose or cord held in one of the ten hands of Sadashiva’s form as पाशमायास्वरूपं and “मलमाया कर्मपाशश्च”.

In Shaivism, the soul is symbolized as an animal tied by a rope to a pole. This implies the existence of a master for it. The *Vayu-Samhita* states:

> ब्रह्माद्या:स्थावरान्ताश्च पशव:परिकीर्तिता: ।
> पशूनामेवमेतेषां प्रोक्तमेतन्निदर्शनम् ।
> यएषबध्यतेपाशै: सुखदु:खाशन:पशु: ।
> लीलासाधनभूतश्च मीश्वरस्यनिसृष्टय: ।
> अज्ञोजन्तुरनीशोय मात्मन:सुखदु:खयो ।
> ईश्वरप्रेरितोगच्छेत् स्वर्गंवाश्वप्रमेववा ।

Beings from Brahma down to immovable things are termed *Pashus*. These are the characteristics of all *Pashus*: that they are bound or tied by means of ropes; that they chew the cud of *Sukha* and *Duhkha* (pleasure and pain) arising from their own acts; and that they form an instrument for the Lord to

Pasha is the rope with which the Pashu is tied to the sacrificial stake, and this is also the word most commonly used in the Upanishads to describe man’s bondage or Mala. “The wise man burns the Pasha” (*Kaivalya Upanishad*).

The Pati is accordingly described in the text-books as follows:

This Pati is Supreme (*Param*), neither Form (*Rupa*) nor Formless (*Arupa*), without attributes (*Nirguna*) and without mark, without impurity (*Nirmala*), One (*Eka*), Eternal, the Intelligence of Intelligence (*Chit* of *Chit*), Unmoving (*Achala*), Infinite, Bliss (*Ananda*), the unapproachable, the Goal, the smallest of the small, and the greatest of the great, *Tat* and Shiva (*Shivaprakasha I*).

### SHAIVA IS NIRGUNA AND PERSONAL.

We have only to notice that the God postulated by Shaiva Siddhanta is not *Saguna*, but *Nirguna*,* which as we have pointed out above means only being above the three *gunas*—*Sattva*, *Rajas*, and *Tamas*—that is, above *Prakriti*, or non-material and *Chit*.

### NIRGUNA NOT TO BE TRANSLATED AS IMPERSONAL.

We have condemned very often the translation of the words *Nirguna* and *Saguna* into Impersonal and Personal and

---

play with His *Panchakrityas* [five acts], just as a cow is tied or released, so that it has no wider vision (*Agam*) and is not master of itself (*Anisa*), and it is led by a *master*, or *Ishvara*, to heavenly regions or other places.” — Hence the soul is symbolized as a *Pashu*. Shri Haradatta says:

->आत्मान्तराणिपशवः परतन्त्रभावात् ।<-
->स्वातंत्र्यतःपशुपतेः पतिरीश्वरम ॥<-

> “Owing to dependence upon its master, the *Atmans* are *Pashus*, and independence is the mark of the *Pati* and *Ishvara*.”

The term *Mala* applied to the corrupting element has been more in vogue in classic works. Thus Patanjali has in his *Yoga Sutras*, “Then the knowledge from which all covering impurities (*Mala*) are removed,” and in the *Manu-smriti* we have: “One should stand for the morning twilight prayer to remove the sins of the night, and sit for the evening prayer to destroy the impurity (*Mala*) created during the day.” (Manu Chap. 2). And what is *Mala*? “*Mala* is what intrinsically covers the *Chit* or the intelligence of the soul.”

\* These three *gunas* stand for the three states *Jagra*, *Svapna*, and *Sushupti*, and *Nirguna* therefore means *Turiya* or *Chaturta*. “*Jagra* is *Sattva*, *Rajas* is *Svapna*, *Tamas* is *Sushupti*. *Nirguna* is therefore *Turiya*.” — *Tirumantiram*.

<sig>3</sig>

<summary>This page defines the nature of God (Pati) in Shaiva Siddhanta as Supreme and Nirguna (beyond the three material gunas) while clarifying that the soul (Pashu) is defined by its dependence and its covering of impurity (Mala). It emphasizes that Nirguna refers to a state beyond material qualities (Turiya) rather than being "impersonal."</summary>
