<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of God in Shaiva Siddhanta, specifically addressing how the "Personal" aspect of the Supreme corresponds to the concept of Pure Being (Sat) and how divine forms are manifestations of Grace rather than material attributes.</meta>

<page-num>298</page-num>
<header>THE SHAIVA RELIGION.</header>

...thus scaring away Christians from the highest conception of the Supreme. "Personal" is explained to mean "Pure Being," the absolute, by Emerson, Lotze, and other Christian writers, and would correspond to our word *Sat*. Therefore, I have shown that God can be both *Nirguna* and personal.

God neither has form nor is formless like air—ideas which are all derived from matter—but He can assume any form suited to the conception of His *Bhakta*. These forms are not material; rather, as the text says, "His form is produced out of Divine Grace or Love." God is therefore not to be called *Saguna* simply because He is spoken of as *Uma-sahaya*, *Nilakantha*, *Sambhu*, *Umapati*, *Ambika-Pati*, etc., Lord of *Kailas*, as *Shiva*, *Hara*, or *Rudra*.*

God is neither he, nor she, nor it, but He can be thought of in all these forms—as male, female, and neuter. All specific names of *Shiva* are declinable in all three genders without

<note>* "It has been said, for instance, that the *Shvetashvatara Upanishad* is a sectarian *Upanishad* because, when speaking of the Highest Self or the Highest *Brahman*, it applies such names to Him as *Hara* (I, 10), *Rudra* (II, 17. III, 2, 4. IV, 12, 21), *Shiva* (III, 14. IV, 10), *Bhagavat* (III, 14), *Agni*, *Aditya*, *Vayu*, etc. (IV 2). But here it is simply taken for granted that the idea of the Highest Self was developed first, and after it had reached its highest purity, it was lowered again by an identification with mythological and personal deities. The question of whether the conception of the Highest Self was formed once and once only—whether it was formed after all the personal and mythological deities had been merged into one Lord (*Prajapati*), or whether it was discovered behind the veil of any other names in the mythological pantheon of the past—has never been raised. Why would an ancient *Rishi* not have said: 'What we have hitherto called *Rudra* and what we worship as *Agni* or *Shiva* is in reality the Highest Self,' thus leaving much of the ancient mythological phraseology to be used with a new meaning? Why should we at once conclude that the later sectarian worshippers of mythological gods replaced the Highest Self, after their fathers had discovered it, with their own sectarian names? If we adopt the former view, the *Upanishads* that still show these *Rudras* of the ancient temples would have to be considered even more primitive than those in which the idea of the *Brahman* of the Highest Self has reached its utmost purity."—*Max Müller*.</note>

<summary>This page argues that God in Shaiva Siddhanta is both Nirguna and personal, possessing forms made of Divine Grace rather than matter. It also features a commentary by Max Müller suggesting that divine names like Rudra and Shiva in the Upanishads represent the Highest Self rather than merely sectarian figures.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the nature of Śiva, specifically focusing on His distinction from the Hindu Trinity and the theological impossibility of Him taking human birth (avatāra) in the Śaiva school.</meta>

<page-num>299</page-num>
<header>THE ŚAIVA RELIGION.</header>

...change of meaning: Śiva, Śivaḥ, and Śivam;* Śambhu, Śāmbhavi, and Śāmbhavam; Īśa, Īśaḥ, and Īśānam, etc.

### ŚIVA IS NOT ONE OF THE TRINITY.

The Pati or Śiva of the Śaiva religion is not one of the Trimūrtis—Brahma, Viṣṇu, and Rudra—and scores of texts could be quoted from the popular Tamiḷ hymn books conveying the same idea as in the hymn of the *Mahimna Stōtra* quoted above. God is "Śivam Advaitaṁ Śāntam Chaturtam."

### GOD CANNOT BE BORN IN MAN.

As Śiva is Nirguṇa and Turīya, the Supreme absolute Brahman, it follows that God cannot be born as a man through the womb of a woman. It is generally known that Śiva had no avatāras or births.† This is the greatest distinction of ancient Hindu philosophy and of the Śaiva school, making it a purely transcendental religion, free from all anthropomorphic conceptions. It was the late Mr. T. Subba Rao who, in his "Notes on the Bhagavat Gītā," entered a vigorous protest against the conception of the Supreme Brahman having human avatāras; we regret that, in all the mass of current writings, no writer has thought fit to bring this view to prominence. But this absolute nature of Śiva does not prevent Him from being personal at the same time and appearing as a Guru and Saviour in the form of a man, out of His great love and feeling for the sin and sorrow of mankind, helping them to rid themselves of their bondage.

### REASON FOR CREATION.

And this is the reason, as shown in Sūtra I of the *Śivajñānabōdham*, why God creates the universe and dissolves it: for the purpose of making souls eat the fruit of the "Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil"§ (good and bad karma) and attain salvation.

---

<note>* This noun form occurs rarely in Sanskrit, but in Tamiḷ it is very commonly used as a synonym for the masculine form "Śiva."</note>
<note>† नजन्ममरणतप्तनकांक्षितमकांक्षितम्। He has neither birth nor death, nor likes nor dislikes.—*Vāyu-Saṁhitā*.</note>
<note>§ See pages 185–201 above.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Śiva, transitioning from His transcendental state to the practical necessity of a Guru and the doctrine of Divine Grace in achieving salvation.</meta>

<page-num>300</page-num>
<header>THE ŚAIVA RELIGION</header>

### THE NECESSITY FOR A GURU.

The necessity for human effort is assumed, but without God’s appearance as the Divine Guru in human form and His Divine Grace, final salvation is not possible. Man can only try to remove the cataract covering his eye, but his ability to enjoy the light of the Sun (<term>Śiva Sūrya</term>) is independent of his own effort. Without the hope of reaching this Light (<term>Śivānubhūti</term>), a man would have little incentive to get rid of his cataract (Desire, <term>trishṇa</term>, the seed of birth), which veils him by forcing him to undergo all the trouble and effort (<term>tapas</term>, etc.). It would be as if a doctor forbade a patient from seeing the Light after he regained his sight; and darkness cannot vanish unless Light enters.<note>To those who would deny this <term>Śivānubhūti</term>, Swami Vivekananda replied by saying, "He jests at scars that never felt a wound."</note>

### DOCTRINE OF GRACE.

The doctrine of Grace and Love is the distinguishing feature of Shaivism, and God is accordingly defined by Saint Tirumūlar in the following terms:<note>‡ परिपूर्णस्यसर्वाद्यैनात्मनोस्तिप्रयोजनम् । परा्नुग्रहएवास्यफलं सर्वस्यकर्मणः ॥ 
All His actions are the result of His love toward souls wallowing in sin. What use would the acts of creation and others be to Him who is <term>Paripūrṇa</term> (eternally contented), except for the sake of those who need them? —*Vāyu Samhitā*</note>

### GOD IS LOVE.

> “The ignorant think that Love and Śiva are different; none know that Love and Śiva are the same. When everyone knows that Love and Śiva are the same, they will rest in Śiva as Love.” —*Tirumantiram*.

### GOD'S IMMANENCE.

As God gives life to the Universe of Nature and of Man, Śiva is called the <term>Ashṭamūrti</term>—the eight-bodied Lord—and He is named according to how He dwells in earth, water, air, fire, ether (<term>ākāś</term>), sun, moon, and the soul (<term>ātman</term>).

<summary>This page explains that while human effort is necessary, salvation requires God's grace and His appearance as a Guru. It emphasizes the Shaivite doctrine that God is synonymous with Love and is immanent throughout the universe in eight forms.</summary>

"Prithivyōbhavaḥ, āpassarvaḥ, Agnērudraḥ, Vāyōr Bhimaḥ, Ākāśasya Mahādēvaḥ, Sūryasyōgraḥ, Chandrasya Sōmaḥ, Ātmanaḥ Paśupatiḥ."<note>* Śarva, Bhava, Rudra, Ugra, Bhīma, Paśupati, Īśāna, and Mahādēva are the eight well-known forms. Through these eight forms—earth, water, fire, wind, ether, the soul (the "field-knower"), the sun, and the moon—the Great Lord sustains the world, which consists of both moving and non-moving things, as He is the soul of the universe. (*Vāyu-Samhitā*, Chapter 3).</note>

The famous passage in the 7th Brāhmaṇa of the 3rd Chapter in the <term>Bṛihadāraṇya Upanishad</term> gives more forms than these as the <term>Śarīra</term> (body) of the Brahman, but in the Āgamas and Purāṇas, these are reduced to eight, as comprising all other forms.

### GOD'S TRANSCENDENCE.

As God is immanent in the <term>Chētana</term> (conscious) and <term>Achētana</term> (unconscious) <term>Prapañcha</term> (universe) as the soul of all, He is identified with the Universe as the *All*, and yet His transcendence is also brought out by such statements as '<term>Antas</term>,' '<term>Antara</term>,' '<term>Anyata</term>,' and '<term>Nēti Nēti</term>.' "They are in me, not I in them." "You are not anything in the Universe, and nothing is there except You."

### GOD IS SATCHIDĀNANDA.

Śiva is <term>Sat-Chit-Ānanda</term>,<note>† See Bishop Westcott's definition of God, the Holy Spirit, and Christ as Spirit, Light, and Love.</note> <term>Sōmāskanda</term> (<term>Sa-Umāskanda</term>);<note>‡ Umā literally means Light or Wisdom.</note> Being, Light, and Love. As Pure Being, the absolute, God is unknowable;<note>✱ Through the three powers of knowledge, action, and will, the Lord, being powerful, presides over the universe through His pervasiveness.</note> and as Light and Love, He links Himself to man; and it is possible for man to approach Him through Love.

### GOD'S ŚAKTI IS LIGHT AND LOVE.

This Light and this Love are therefore called His <term>Śakti</term>,<note>§ See *Brahma Sūtra*: "Because He is seen to be endowed with all powers," and also the *Śvetāśvatara* text: "His supreme power is revealed as manifold, and His essential nature is knowledge, strength, and action." ... "The supreme power of the Supreme Soul is described as a part of Him."</note> and

This <term>Śuddha Māyā</term> is the <term>Kuṭila</term> or <term>Kuṇḍalinī Śakti</term> of the <term>Yogis</term>, of which <term>Mūlaprakṛiti</term> (also called <term>Kuṇḍalinī</term>) is the densest form. ### MĀYĀ DISTINGUISHED FROM AVIDYĀ.

This <term>Māyā</term> is again to be distinguished from <term>Āṇava Mala</term> <note>The technical term used to denote the impurity (<term>Mala</term>) called <term>Āṇava</term>. पशुत्वपशुनीहारमृत्युमूर्च्छामलंजनैः । अविद्याऽवृतिशक्तग्लानिपापमूलक्षयादिभिः <term>Mṛigēndrāgama</term>.</note> (the technical term in the <term>Agamas</term> for <term>Ahaṅkāra</term>, <term>Avidyā</term>, <term>Ajñāna</term>, or ignorance), and the definition and distinction are stated in the following verses by St. <term>Aruṇanḍi</term>:

### AVIDYĀ OR ĀṆAVA MALA DEFINED.

> “<term>Āṇava Mala</term>, with its many <term>Śaktis</term>, is one, pervading the countless souls (<term>Jivas</term>), like the impurity in copper <note>नीहारोनादिरकोमनुज सहभवस्ताम्रकाकालिकेव । स्यात्रीकोणन्तनाशी प्रतिपुरुषदृगावारकाऽनेकशक्तिः ॥ <term>Siddhānta Sārāvali</term>.</note>; it hinders their knowledge (<term>jñāna</term>) and action (<term>kriyā</term>). It also provides them with the capacity for experience and is always the source of ignorance.”

### DISTINCTION.

> “Do you say, ‘There is no other entity such as <term>Mala</term> (<term>Āṇava</term>); it is only the effect of <term>Māyā</term>’? Understand well that <term>Māyā</term> causes desire (<term>Ichcha</term>), knowledge (<term>Jñāna</term>), and action (<term>Kriyā</term>) to arise in the <term>Jivas</term>, but <term>Āṇava</term> causes these same things to disappear. <term>Āṇava</term> is inherent in the <term>Jivas</term>, but <term>Māyā</term> is separate from them (just as one’s ignorance and body can be called inseparable and separate, respectively); and besides manifesting itself as the universe, <term>Māyā</term> forms the body, senses, worlds, and enjoyments.”

### KEVALA, SAKALA AND ŚUDDHA OR NIRVĀṆA CONDITION OF THE SOUL.

According to the <term>Pūrvapakshin</term>, <term>Māyā</term> is the cloud that hides the light of the sun. But the <term>Siddhāntin</term> answers, “You

One cannot speak of the sun being hidden by the clouds unless there is a seer. The cloud has no capacity to hide the sun, but it has the power to hide the seer’s eye. This sun is <term>Śiva</term>. The cloud or cataract in one’s eye is the <term>Āṇava Mala</term>; the seer or his eye is the <term>Jīva</term> or <term>Ātmā</term> or soul. When the soul is enshrouded by <term>Āṇava Mala</term> and is without action, will, and intelligence, it is its night—the <term>Kēvala State</term>.<note>From start to finish, life consists of a series of awakenings until the final goal is reached. अबुद्धश्चैवबुद्धश्च बुध्यमानस्तथैव च ।
प्रबुद्धस्सुप्रबुद्धश्च पञ्चापिकथयामिते ॥
<term>Abuddha</term>, <term>Buddha</term>, <term>Budhyamāna</term>, <term>Prabuddha</term>, and <term>Suprabuddha</term> (Unawakened, awakened, being awakened, highly awakened, and perfectly awakened; I tell you of all five).</note> When God, out of His great love, sets it in evolution, giving it the body and the worlds out of <term>Māyā</term> for its enjoyment and experience, by which its <term>kriyā śakti</term>, etc., are aroused, this is called its <term>sakaḷa</term> condition. <term>Āṇava Mala</term> is night and darkness, and <term>Māyā</term> acts as the lamp-light—the power of a million arcs is the <term>Śuddha Māyā</term>—in darkness. But when the sun rises, all darkness and night vanish and there is no need for any lamp, however powerful, and the soul is fully enveloped in that Supreme Splendour, that “Light of Truth that, entering body and soul, has melted all faults and driven away the false darkness.” This is the soul’s <term>Śuddha</term> or <term>Nirvāṇa</term> condition.

> “This day in Your mercy toward me You did drive away the darkness, and stand as the Rising Sun:
> Of this, Your way of rising—there being nothing else but You—I thought without thought.
> I drew nearer and nearer to You, wearing away atom by atom, until I was one with You.
> O Śiva, dweller in the great Holy Shrine,
> You are not anything in the Universe, yet nothing is there except You. Who can know You?” <note>From St. <term>Māṇikkavāçagar</term>’s <term>Tiruvāchakam</term>.</note>

The above expresses the kernel of <term>Śaiva Advaita Siddhāṇta</term>. This leads us naturally to the discussion of the nature of <term>Advaita</term> postulated by the Śaiva School, and before we do so,

<page-num>306</page-num>

We will look at the nature of the <term>Jivātmā</term> or soul itself, as this is essential to understanding the <term>Advaita</term>.

### SOUL DISTINGUISHED FROM SAT AND ASAT.

The <term>Sānkhyans</term>, <term>Yōgins</term>, and <term>Vēdāntins</term> admit that the <term>Purusha</term>, <term>Ātmā</term>, or soul is distinct from <term>Prakriti</term> and superior to <term>Buddhi</term> and the 23 <term>tattvas</term>. Confusion arises when trying to establish its relationship to God. The soul is not a reflection, a particle, or a spark of the indivisible and changeless <term>Brahman</term>,* nor is it one with Him. God is *other* than the soul. Even where the <term>Sūtrakāra</term> postulates <term>Parināma</term>, he does so only in reference to <term>Māyā</term>, but he maintains the difference between the human soul and the Supreme Soul. No harm would arise if we regarded <term>Māyā</term> as being one with <term>Brahman</term> as His inseparable <term>Śakti</term>, but all religion and morality are sure to die if we regard the soul as being the same as God.† There would be no way to account for the presence of evil or ignorance in the world, even when we try to whittle it down—

---

<note>* पृथगात्मानंप्रेरितारंचमत्वा । जुष्टस्ततस्तेनामृतत्वमेति ॥ <term>Švēt. Up.</term>
विद्याविद्येशतेयस्तुसोन्त्यः । (<term>Švēt. Up.</term>)
जुष्टंयदापश्यत्यन्यमीशम । (<term>Švēt. Up.</term>)
द्रासुपर्णासयुजासखाया समानंवृक्षंपरिषस्वजाते ।
तयोरन्य:पिप्पलस्वाद्वत्त्य नश्नन्नन्योऽभिचाकशीति ॥
->(Rig-Veda 1st Maṇḍala 22nd Anuvāka.)<-
आत्मानंचपृथङ्‌मत्वा प्रेरितारंततःपृथक् ।
असौजुष्टस्तत्तेन ह्यमृतत्वाय कल्पते ॥ (<term>Vāyu-Samhitā</term>.)</note>

<note>† This is pointed out by a Christian writer in the following words:
> “But why do we shrink from Pantheism? Not from the dread of losing the physical universe in God, but from the dread of losing our own soul in God. Pantheism only becomes deadly to vigorous religion and morality when it makes the human soul—the person’s self—a portion of God. Theism claims that the human soul is a free cause, a separate island of individual will in the midst of the vast ocean of the Divine Will. Leave us man confronting God, not absorbed in Him, and the conditions are preserved for the ethical life of the individual and also for the communion of the soul with God as ANOTHER than itself—the very possibility of which is destroyed if a separate personality is wiped out. On this matter of the OTHERNESS of man from God, I hope to say more in a later chapter.”

(‘*God and Soul*’ by <term>Rev. Mr. Armstrong</term>).</note>

<summary>This page defines the nature of the Jivātmā (individual soul), asserting its distinction from both material nature (Prakriti) and the Supreme Being (Brahman). It argues that identifying the soul with God undermines morality and fails to account for the existence of evil.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the distinct existence of God, souls, and matter, specifically addressing the philosophical challenges posed by the concept of illusion and the interpretation of non-dualist Mahāvākya texts.</meta>

<header>THE ŚAIVA RELIGION.</header>
<page-num>307</page-num>

away as an illusion, delusion, or myth, the presence of this delusion itself must be accounted for. Delusion is a conscious experience, and the question arises: who is experiencing this delusion? If the soul is distinct from God and distinct from <term>Māyā</term>, and is in <term>bandha</term> (bondage), then the necessity for the creation of the world becomes understandable. We therefore postulate three <term>Padārthas</term> (categories), three planes of existence, or three centers: the plane of matter, the plane of souls, and the plane of God. In the language of Euclid, God is the point—that which has no parts or magnitude, and which is everywhere, inside and out, above and below. The soul is the center of the circle, and the circumference is the <term>Māyā</term> that bounds it. When this center can rise up to the Point, then its <term>Nirvāṇa</term> is possible.

### MAHĀVĀKYA TEXTS.*

But what should we do with the <term>Mahāvākya</term> texts: "That Thou art," "I become That," "I am That," and so on? It should be noted that these texts are not discussed by the <term>Sūtrakāra Bādarāyaṇa</term> in the first <term>Adhyāya</term> relating to <term>Pramāṇa</term> (Proof) of the nature of the <term>Padārthas</term>, where he distinctly established their difference. Rather, they appear in the chapter on <term>Sādana</term>, which relates to the means of salvation. The Teacher tells the pupil to practice the <term>Sādana</term>, informing him that he is God (<term>Tattvamasi</term>), and the pupil accordingly practices <term>Soham bhāvana</term> or <term>Śivohambhāvana</term> by repeating the mantra "<term>Aham Brahmāsmi</term>." In this state, there is consciousness—specifically a consciousness of duality involving two <term>Padārthas</term>: <term>Aham</term> (I) and <term>Brahma</term> (God). This is <term>Dvaitam</term> (duality), the <term>Yōga</term>, <term>Upanishad</term>, or <term>Vēdānta Pāda</term>. When, through this practice of <term>Śivoham</term>, consciousness itself disappears, then the soul can become one with God, and the Knower (<term>Jñāthru</term>), the Knowledge (<term>Jñāna</term>), and the Known (<term>Jñēya</term>) all disappear; this is the <term>Jñāna</term> or <term>Siddhānta Pāda</term>. The question arises: how can this oneness be reached? How can the two become one? This becomes possible because of—

---

<note>अहमात्माशिवोह्यन्यः परमात्मेतियःस्मृतः ।
एवंयोपास्तेमोहात् नशिवत्वमवाप्नुयात् ॥
यशिवस्सोहमेवेति अद्वयंभावयेत्सदा ।
अद्वैतभावनायुक्ताः सर्वत्वात्मनिसंस्थिताः ॥
(Whoever thinks, "I am the soul and Shiva, the Supreme Soul, is someone else," and worships in this way out of delusion, will not attain the state of Shiva. One should always meditate on the non-dual truth that "Shiva is I myself." Those who are united with this non-dual meditation are established in the Self of all.)
—<term>Sarvajñānōttarāgama</term>.</note>

<summary>The author argues for a three-fold reality of God, soul, and matter, explaining that the non-dual "Mahāvākya" statements are meditative practices for salvation rather than descriptions of the soul's original state. Through the practice of identifying with Shiva, the soul eventually transcends the consciousness of duality to reach union.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul’s relationship with God, transitioning from the refutation of total illusion to the specific definitions of the soul found in the Yoga Sutras and Śaiva Siddhānta.</meta>

<header>THE ŚAIVA RELIGION.</header>
<page-num>308</page-num>

...the peculiar nature of the soul and its relation to God. This peculiar nature of the soul alone is discussed in the <term>Yoga Sūtras</term> and in <term>Śaiva Siddhānta</term> textbooks. And the peculiar relation between God and the Soul is called <term>Advaita</term>.

->NATURE OF THE SOUL.<-

This nature of the soul consists in its becoming one with whatever it is united to, losing its own individuality, and its inability to exist independently except in union with one or the other. It can only be united to the world or to God. It can reach God only when it leaves the world. It cannot serve God and Mammon at the same time. It is the caterpillar of the <term>Upanishads</term>, which leaves one leaf-stalk to gain another. And when it is united to one thing or the other—like the mimicking caterpillar again—it is indistinguishable from the one or the other. It is the shadow of the one (<term>Māyā</term>) or the light of the other (God) that completely hides the soul's individuality. So, when in union with matter and with the body, it is so lost in the nerve centers and so on that the scientific agnostic fails to discover the soul, even by the closest analysis. In union with God, the pure idealist finds no soul there. The soul identifies itself absolutely with the body or God, and its individuality or identity disappears, but not its personality or being (<term>Sat</term>).

->MAN IS A MIRROR OR A CRYSTAL.<-

This law of the human mind, called the Law of <term>Garuḍadhyāna</term>, is stated in the terms that *we become like what we are associated with*, and may be called the Law of Association or Identity. Professor Henry Drummond calls it the Law of Reflection and of Assimilation, and likens man to a mirror or a crystal. "*All men are mirrors.* That is the first law on which this formula (of sanctification or corruption) is based. One of the most apt descriptions of a human being is that he is a mirror."

And we will find that this is exactly the simile used by the <term>Upanishads</term> and the <term>Siddhānta</term> writers, and the following extracts contain the illustration and the formula of sanctification.

<summary>This page describes the soul's nature as being defined by its associations, noting its tendency to lose its distinct identity by becoming indistinguishable from either the material world or God. It utilizes the analogy of a mirror or a caterpillar to explain how the soul reflects or attaches itself to its surroundings.</summary>
<keywords>Advaita, Nature of the Soul, Māyā, Sat, Garuḍadhyāna, Law of Association, Henry Drummond, Upanishads, Śaiva Siddhānta</keywords>

### THE FORMULA OF SANCTIFICATION.

> “As a metal disk (mirror) tarnished by dust shines brightly again after it has been cleansed, so the incarnate person is satisfied and freed from grief after he has seen his own true nature; and when by his own nature he sees, as by a lamp, the true nature of the Brahman, then having known the unborn eternal God who transcends all <term>tattvas</term>, he is freed from all <term>pāśa</term>.” (<term>Śvetāś. Up.</term> ii. 14, 15).

> “From meditating (<term>abhidhyānāt</term>) on Him, from joining (<term>yojanāt</term>) Him, from becoming (<term>tattvabhāvāt</term>) one with Him, there is a further cessation of all <term>māyā</term> in the end.” (<term>Śvetāś. Up.</term> i. 10).

And St. Meykaṇḍān has this stanza (viii. 3. a.)

> “The soul, reflecting that the knowledge derived from the senses is only material *like the colors reflected on a mirror,* and that these color-like sensations are different from itself, and after perceiving false knowledge as false, understands the Truth and will become one with God, who is different from itself.”

The formula stated in plain terms would read: “I see God, I reflect God, I become God-like, Godly, God, I am God.”

The crystal or the diamond—unlike the Sun’s Light which it reflects, though in its inner core it is pure—possesses the defect of being covered by dirt, <term>mala</term> (<term>māyā</term>). It is luminous (<term>Chit</term>) in a sense, but unlike the Self-Luminous Sun (<term>Para-Chit</term>); and either in darkness or the full blaze of the Sun, the identity of the mirror cannot be perceived,

### ADVAITA DEFINED.<note>See pages 244, 272 before.</note>

We now come to the definition of <term>Advaita</term>. And we may say at once that all the <term>Śaiva Siddhānta</term> writers describe their system as ‘Advaita’ pure and simple, yet people who hear it casually described call it <term>Viśiṣṭādvaita</term> and fail to note its

<summary>This page outlines the process of sanctification through which the soul, compared to a mirror being cleaned of dust, realizes its true nature and achieves union with God. It introduces the Śaiva Siddhānta definition of Advaita, distinguishing it from other philosophical interpretations.</summary>
<keywords>Śaiva Siddhānta, Advaita, Sanctification, Brahman, Śvetāśvatara Upanishad, Meykaṇḍān, pāśa, māyā, mala, mirror analogy</keywords>

--- Page 341 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul's relationship with the Divine, specifically defining the term "Advaita" within the Śaiva Siddhānta framework as "inseparable" (Ananya) rather than a simple numerical "one" (Eka).</meta>

->THE ŚAIVA RELIGION.<-
310

special features. Advaita is defined by St. Meykaṇḍān as meaning *Anyō nāsti* or *Ananya*, meaning inseparable; and his disciple describes the relationship as being "neither one nor two." <term>Advaita</term>, literally meaning "not two," simply denies the separability or duality of God, soul, and matter, but does not postulate Oneness by denying the existence of one or another <term>Padārtha</term>, nor by suggesting their mutual convertibility as in causation, etc. Mind (the unextended) is not matter (the extended); yet they are always inseparable and found as one. How the unextended is present in the extended is the puzzle and the contradiction stated by Doctor Alexander Bain. And the illustrations of mind and body, or vowels and consonants, are used to denote the <term>Advaita</term> relation of God to the universe of nature and of man. God is the Soul, whose body (<term>Śarīra</term>) is the universe of nature and man, as is so clearly and forcefully expressed in the *Bṛihad-āraṇya Upanishat* texts referred to above, moving from the Earth to the <term>Ātmā</term>.

> “He who dwells in the earth, other than the earth, whom the earth does not know, whose <term>śarīra</term> (body) the earth is, who rules the earth within, He is your <term>Ātmā</term>, the inner controller, the immortal.”

> “He who dwells in the <term>Ātmā</term> (<term>Vijñāna</term>), *other than the Ātmā*, whom

<column-break/>

अद्वैतमागमशिरोभिरुपासनायां उक्तंतवेतिपरमार्थतयान्वाच्यम् ।
भेदःस्फुटीगरुडमन्त्रिकयैरिवानीकं तादात्म्यभावनमयापिविषंप्रमाष्टि ॥
ब्रह्मोच्यतेपरमसौपरमं च तत्वंज्योतिः परंचपरमेश्वरपद्मनाभः ।
त्वद्भावनाैकविषयः त्वदनन्यभावात् मान्त्रीयथागरुडभावनया गरुत्मान् ॥ (Haradatta.)
अनन्येनैवयोगेणमां ध्यायन्तउपासते । (Bhagavad Gitā.)

---

M. N. Dvivedi in his ‘Monism or Advaitism’ also points out that <term>advaita</term> does not mean *Eka* (one) or *Abhinna* (undifferentiated), but *Ananya* (inseparable), and that this is the view of the <term>Sūtrakāra</term>.
